## 📚 Paper List

- **[GHySSeg: Real-Time Geometric Ground and Road Segmentation for Hybrid Solid-State LiDAR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21507)**  `arXiv:2611.21507`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Martín Palos, Fernando García_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hybrid solid-state LiDAR sensors are making their way into production vehicles, yet the data they produce differs structurally from that of the established spinning mechanical sensors behind today's autonomous-driving datasets: it is ring-free, dense, cluttered, and concentrated in a frontal field of view. The classical geometric methods that autonomous vehicles rely on for drivable space computation and navigation assume the ordered ring-and-column structure of a 360-degree spinning LiDAR and degrade or fail on this data, while learned alternatives are computationally heavy, hard to certify, and trained almost exclusively on spinning-LiDAR data. We demonstrate that the same dense, per-curve geometry is instead an opportunity to close the divide between the industry's preferred sensors and autonomous-driving research. We present GHySSeg, a purely geometric method that segments ground and drivable road from a single hybrid solid-state LiDAR frame by tracing each scan curve and reading two geometric signatures, a step in height and a fold-back in range, with no training and no graphics processing unit. To evaluate it, we release HySoLiD-Drive, the first public benchmark dedicated to ground and road segmentation on an automotive hybrid solid-state LiDAR, comprising 100 manually labeled and 7000 unlabeled frames recorded by two vehicles in real driving. On this benchmark, GHySSeg surpasses both geometric and learned state-of-the-art baselines across ground and road segmentation, reaching an intersection-over-union of 0.907 for road and 0.896 for ground at over 0.96 precision, while running in real time at about 22 milliseconds per frame on a single core.
  </details>

- **[Robotic Reward Models in the Foundation-Model Era](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20741)**  `arXiv:2611.20741`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Guoqiang Liang, Yiqi Lin, Shangchen Zhou, Wenzheng Zeng, Yanzhe Chen, Zechen Bai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robotic reward models (RRMs) use foundation models to replace per-task reward engineering with reusable, task-conditioned evaluation. Yet under one name, their outputs answer different questions, from whether a task succeeded to which execution is better. This survey organizes 114 methods into five semantic families by the question their native outputs answer: Outcome, Absolute Progress, Relative, Temporal Value, and Hybrid. Within this taxonomy, we review how model design determines what an evaluator can judge, how raw experience becomes supervision, and how existing evaluation practice tests the result. The review exposes a gap: agreement with demonstration order and downstream policy gains do not show whether a reward tracks task fulfillment. We therefore introduce RRM-Bench-1K, 1,117 real-robot videos of closed-loop executions with task-state and camera-evidence labels, and four contrast suites that separate the credited condition from its usual cues. A unified study of 27 evaluators shows that the strongest final scores rank whole executions well enough for selection and filtering. Dense credit, however, follows the progression of an execution more closely than its task state, and no evaluator signals when the decisive view is missing. How much fidelity a reward model needs therefore depends on the decision it drives. We outline directions from real-failure supervision to metrics that separate task state from chronology, and open-source RRM-Hub, a toolbox in which 14 existing RRMs can be readily applied and new evaluators tested on the same capabilities.
  </details>

- **[JEPA4R: Embodied JEPA World Models with 4D Point Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19771)**  `arXiv:2611.19771`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Jiarui Dong, Zhiheng Liang, Yanjun Li, Zeyu Zhang, Heejeong Nam, Haoyu Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Joint-embedding predictive architecture (JEPA) world models predict future embeddings instead of pixels and plan in latent space. Two challenges remain: 2D images leave the metric geometry that manipulation acts on implicit and entangled with appearance, and what these models imagine stays hidden, as they are mostly judged in simulation by short-horizon latent planning. We present JEPA4R, a JEPA world model that observes single-depth-camera point clouds, predicts and plans in their latent space, and acts through a policy head on its frozen latents; we call this 4D point planning. A decoder on the frozen world model shows what it imagines as 4D point clouds, with a mean point error within 1.1 cm over 200 steps. Under the policy-learning protocols of OGBench and CortexBench, JEPA4R leads pixel JEPA models on OGBench Cube-Single, Cube-Double, Cube-Triple and Scene, and ranks first on MetaWorld and Adroit Relocate with 98.4% and 88.0%, above the runner-up, R3M, at 96.0% and 65.3%. Trained only in a digital twin, JEPA4R and its policy heads run zero-shot on the real arm with both a gripper and a dexterous hand.
  </details>

- **[WorldOracle: Benchmarking Action-Conditioned World Models against Real-World Executions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18826)**  `arXiv:2610.18826`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Haoyi Duan, Liliang Chen, Nan Wang, Yang Zhang, Yuhang Lu, Xiaotong Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce WorldOracle, a benchmark specifically designed to evaluate action-conditioned world models (ACWMs) against paired real-world executions. Given the same initial observation and low-level action sequence, WorldOracle asks whether a predicted rollout reproduces what happens in the real world, including physical consequences and task-state transitions. We evaluate models along four complementary dimensions: physical state correctness, interaction spatial alignment, key interaction area fidelity, and multi-view action coherence. To support this evaluation, we curate real-world training and evaluation suites that cover different interaction dynamics and embodiments. The evaluation suite contains long, multi-segment episodes with both successful and failed executions, enabling evaluation across varied action outcomes and behavior distributions. Experiments on seven representative ACWMs reveal distinct model strengths and failure modes. WorldOracle provides a systematic foundation for measuring and advancing action-conditioned world modeling for manipulation. We release the datasets, evaluation code, and leaderboard:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Navi-Agent: Unlocalized Monocular Navigation Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20388)**  `arXiv:2609.20388`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Wenyuan Xie, Mengyang Hong, Yongzhong Wang, Yanbiao Ji, Yijin Zhou, Shaokai Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language Navigation in Continuous Environments (VLN-CE) requires an embodied agent to execute long-horizon instructions in unknown environments. Existing zero-shot VLN-CE systems typically maintain spatial states through geometric localization or coordinate-based representations. Recent geometry-constrained navigation removes depth and globally consistent coordinates, but maintaining persistent spatial awareness for place confirmation, progress verification, and recovery remains challenging. We present Navi-Agent, a zero-shot VLN-CE agent that constructs a coordinate-free spatial state from visual observations and executed motion histories. Navi-Agent organizes this state as a navigation topology, where nodes represent visual places and edges represent motion transitions. This representation enables observation-based approximate self-localization, task progress verification, and visual revisitation-based recovery. Navi-Agent performs closed-loop navigation by decomposing instructions into sub-goals, executing local visual navigation, and verifying visited places through the constructed spatial state. Experiments on zero-shot VLN-CE benchmark and real-world robot platforms show that Navi-Agent achieves state-of-the-art performance among geometry-constrained methods while remaining competitive with approaches relying on geometric localization.
  </details>

- **[From Uncertainty to Determinism: Coarse-to-Fine Visual Floorplan Localization without Ray Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26817)**  `arXiv:2607.26817`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Shiyong Meng, Bolei Chen, Ping Zhong, Yang Wan, Rongzhi Wang, Jiazhi Xia, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual Floorplan Localization (FLoc) has emerged as a promising solution for indoor localization by matching egocentric images against minimalist structural maps. However, due to cross-modal information asymmetry and repetitive indoor layouts, visual FLoc is fundamentally challenged by multimodal pose distributions, where visually identical observations map to distinct, spatially separated locations. Existing ray-matching-based methods tackle this by explicitly predicting sparse geometric or semantic rays, which inherently incur information loss and demand resource-intensive preprocessing alongside exhaustive matching during inference. In this paper, we bypass the intermediate ray-matching paradigm and propose a coarse-to-fine visual FLoc framework that progresses from uncertainty to determinism. In the coarse stage, we design an image-conditioned pose diffusion model to parameterize the continuous multimodal pose distribution, effectively routing stochastically initialized pose particles toward distinct candidate modes. In the refinement stage, we propose a localized refiner that predicts bounded sub-meter pose residuals from candidate-centered floorplan crops, where structural ambiguities are largely eliminated. Our method effectively balances global multi-hypothesis tracking and local sub-meter refinement without requiring any offline map preprocessing or test-time lookup tables. Comprehensive results on the S3D (full) and ZInD benchmarks demonstrate that our approach achieves state-of-the-art accuracy and robustness.
  </details>

- **[TARGO and TARGO-Net: Benchmarking Target-driven Object Grasping under Occlusions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.06168)**  `arXiv:2407.06168`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Yan Xia, Ran Ding, Ziyuan Qin, Guanqi Zhan, Kaichen Zhou, Long Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Predicting 6-DoF grasp poses from a single RGB-D frame has recently achieved impressive accuracy, yet performance can degrade significantly when the target object is heavily occluded by clutter. In this paper, we establish TARGO, the first benchmark dataset for TARget-driven Grasping under Occlusions, and introduce TARGO-Net, a grasp prediction model designed for visual occlusion. Our main contributions are fourfold: 1) We identify the visual occlusion challenge in single-view RGB-D 6-DoF grasping and show that current state-of-the-art models still suffer under severe occlusion. 2) We propose the TARGO dataset, which supports training and testing 6-DoF grasp models across different visual occlusion levels and enables robustness evaluation in real-world scenarios. 3) We devise TARGO-Net, a transformer-based grasping model with a target completion module and target-scene cross-attention, which achieves the most robust performance across all visual occlusion levels. 4) We show that target completion, number of occluders, and target minimum dimension also relate to grasp success in addition to visual occlusion.
  </details>

- **[Learning-based Autonomous Oversteer Control and Collision Avoidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15275)**  `arXiv:2505.15275`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Seokjun Lee, Seung-Hyun Kong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Oversteer, wherein a vehicle's rear tires lose traction and induce unintentional excessive yaw, poses critical safety challenges. Failing to control oversteer often leads to severe traffic accidents. Although recent autonomous driving efforts have attempted to handle oversteer through stabilizing maneuvers, the majority rely on expert-defined trajectories or assume obstacle-free environments, limiting real-world applicability. This paper introduces a novel end-to-end (E2E) autonomous driving approach that tackles oversteer control and collision avoidance simultaneously. Existing E2E techniques, including Imitation Learning (IL), Reinforcement Learning (RL), and Hybrid Learning (HL), generally require near-optimal demonstrations or extensive experience. Yet even skilled human drivers struggle to provide perfect demonstrations under oversteer, and high transition variance hinders the accumulation of sufficient data. Hence, we present Q-Compared Soft Actor-Critic (QC-SAC), a new HL algorithm that effectively learns from immature demonstration data and adapts rapidly to new conditions. To evaluate QC-SAC, we introduce a benchmark inspired by real-world driver training: a vehicle encounters sudden oversteer on a slippery surface and must avoid randomly placed obstacles ahead. Experimental results show that QC-SAC attains near-optimal driving policies, significantly surpassing state-of-the-art IL, RL, and HL baselines. Hardware-in-the-loop simulation (HILS) validation with physical steering hardware further demonstrates that QC-SAC can operate beyond a software-only simulation, providing an intermediate validation step toward real-vehicle implementation. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first work that achieves safe autonomous control of vehicles in oversteer while simultaneously avoiding obstacles.
  </details>

- **[Multi-CONDOR: Learning Multistable Motion Primitives from Demonstration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21059)**  `arXiv:2611.21059`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Gabriele Giuseppe Di Marzo, Mariano Ramírez Montero, Maximilian Stölzle, Rodrigo Pérez-Dattari, Cosimo Della Santina_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dynamical-system-based motion policies provide a framework for learning reactive robot motions with stability and convergence properties. Many manipulation tasks, however, admit several distinct goals or a continuous set of acceptable outcomes, requiring motion primitives that preserve these alternatives. Accommodating both goal structures within a unified framework with continuous, autonomous dynamics remains an open challenge. We propose a CONDOR-based framework, multi-CONDOR, for learning motion primitives with either multiple discrete attractors or a continuous attracting goal contour. The approach combines imitation learning with losses that encourage agreement between the learned task dynamics and latent reference systems encoding the desired attraction structure. We provide a Lyapunov-based analysis of the convergence properties of the learned motion dynamics. We evaluate the framework on simulated benchmarks with discrete goals, qualitative examples with continuous goal contours, and robotic grasping experiments. In simulation, multi-CONDOR reduces trajectory RMSE by approximately 72% compared with separately learned single-goal motion primitives selected by a state machine. For tasks with two and three goals, respectively, it reduces the fraction of evaluated initial states ending outside the goal tolerance from StableNODE's 0.31% and 7.29% to 0% in both settings. Experiments with a Franka Emika robot arm demonstrate successful grasping of a container by either of its two handles using motions learned from demonstrations. Together, these results show multi-CONDOR combines motion reproduction with the flexibility to converge to alternative valid outcomes.
  </details>

- **[NF-VLA: A Normalizing-Flow Baseline for Vision-Language-Action Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20314)**  `arXiv:2611.20314`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Ruichen Deng, Qinyu Zhao, Yong-Hyun Park, Mutian Tong, Liang Zheng, Jiatao Gu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autoregressive normalizing flows (NFs) provide a tractable density for learning and sampling continuous robot actions. We show that a single maximum-likelihood objective can train vision--language--action (VLA) policies competitive with leading methods on manipulation benchmarks. We present NF-VLA, which combines conditional autoregressive normalizing flows with score-based action refinement. Its deep--shallow architecture concentrates visual, language, and proprioceptive prefix conditioning in a high-capacity deep block, while unconditioned shallow blocks provide a shared invertible action transformation. The vision--language backbone and flow are jointly trained by exact maximum likelihood on Gaussian-smoothed action chunks. The same learned density supplies a single score-based correction at inference, refining generated actions to account for training-time smoothing without a separately trained denoiser. Trained on LIBERO from a pretrained vision--language backbone without robot pretraining, NF-VLA achieves 98.35\% success across the four standard suites and 72.6\% on LIBERO-Plus using the same checkpoint without further fine-tuning. Ablations show that deep-only prefix conditioning and score-based refinement both improve closed-loop success. Together, these results establish carefully structured autoregressive flows as a competitive approach to VLA control, with one learned density supporting policy training, action generation, and refinement.
  </details>

- **[HospitalBench: Diagnosing Where and Why Embodied Agents Fail on Long Hospital Task Chains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19274)**  `arXiv:2611.19274`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Gaytri Jena, Kapil Wanaskar, Aman Chadha, Vasu Sharma, Vinija Jain, Amitava Das_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon embodied tasks fail in ways that end-task success cannot see. HospitalBench is a diagnostic benchmark of ten hospital chores in NVIDIA Isaac Sim, each two unscored warm-up steps followed by a causal chain of five scored sub-tasks in one continuous simulation: the state one step leaves (an object pose, a choice, a fact seen once) determines what the next step must do, unlike benchmarks that place sub-tasks side by side. Vision-language agents act through skill-level tools from camera images. A scripted robot that makes only the calls a model gets is the solvability check of a chore. From each trajectory the benchmark reads where a chain breaks, what the agent did after a failed call, and how far its own position estimate drifts from the true one. This version reports first results only: 15 of 60 planned runs (three models at high effort, one run each on chores T1 and T10 from both start states and T3 from one), with scripted-robot proofs on three of the ten chores. Of the 15 runs, 2 passed all five sub-tasks. Runs still going after sub-tasks 1 to 5 were 15, 8, 4, 2, 2. Of 189 failed calls, 168 were followed by a different call and 111 by a call that did not fail. These are first results and not a full benchmark. We will release the scenes, task chains, scripted robot, evaluation harness and trajectories.
  </details>

- **[ReuseMem: One Memory, Multiple Goals in Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21115)**  `arXiv:2611.21115`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yoonseok Kang, Dayeon Jung, Seunghoon Jeon, Wonjib Kim, Dongchul Park_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robots that work alongside people should be able to use what they have seen when new requests arise. The same experience may later support identifying an object or returning it to its original location, although these uses were unknown during observation. ReuseMem learns to use one visual memory for multiple later manipulation goals. It records visual features independently of future goals, retrieves complementary evidence for each request, and combines it with the current scene through a lightweight action interface. Retrieval is supervised by how well candidate evidence supports expert-action prediction across different current states, linking memory selection to its use in manipulation. Repeated visual features are stored once, and memory computations that remain unchanged during action generation are reused. Experiments on four simulation tasks and two physical scenarios demonstrate reuse of a common recording across different requests. With matched action adaptation, ReuseMem improves joint goal completion on the benchmark tasks by 24.5 percentage points over uniform memory. These findings show that one memory can support multiple later manipulation goals as the scene changes.
  </details>

- **[ForceHarness: Turning Force into Action and Physics for Frozen Frontier Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21099)**  `arXiv:2611.21099`  `cs.RO`  
  _Ke Sun, Kevin Yuchen Ma, Yike Zhang, Yanzhe Chen, Mike Zheng Shou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Frontier multimodal models can now control robots zero-shot from images, yet many manipulation tasks depend on contact forces that images cannot show. Bringing force to an API-only frozen model raises two challenges. First, contact evolves much faster than an API model can decide, so the system must close the loop on force beneath the model. Second, a frozen model cannot interpret raw force streams, so force must be returned in a form the model can reason about. Therefore, we present ForceHarness, a harness that lets frozen models use force without demonstrations or finetuning. After calibrating hand force from a wrist sensor or joint torques alone, ForceHarness addresses the first challenge with self-terminating force primitives that regulate force on their own and return at a contact event. To address the second challenge, a physics translation layer turns each force trace into physical quantities with uncertainty as the tool result. To evaluate ForceHarness against expert force controllers and learned policies under identical conditions, we also build HiddenPhys, a seven-task MuJoCo benchmark with hidden physical variables. On a real arm without a wrist force sensor, ForceHarness raises the zero-shot success rate of GPT-6 Astra from 3.3% to 90.0%. In simulation, it adds 17.5 percentage points on the public ForceBench suite and 34.3 percentage points on HiddenPhys, surpassing force-aware policies trained on demonstrations on both benchmarks and matching hand-written expert controllers on HiddenPhys. Ablations further show that smaller models make significantly better decisions when given physical quantities rather than raw force values.
  </details>

- **[FlowRow: Learning Navigation Behaviors in Agricultural Environments with Long-Horizon Goal-Conditioned Flow Matching Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21035)**  `arXiv:2611.21035`  `cs.RO`  
  _Utkarsh Bajpai, Cédric Pradalier, Stéphanie Aravecchia_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous monitoring and intervention in semi-structured agricultural environments, such as orchards and vineyards, require mobile robots to navigate to locations across diverse field configurations. This entails performing navigation behaviors including in-row driving, row turning and skipping, and inter-row traversal. Recent learning-based approaches have enabled robust in-row navigation from onboard perception, but navigation across rows typically requires a priori knowledge of field geometry to specify and tune row-transition maneuvers, preventing arbitrary transitions between rows. We address this challenge by framing navigation in these environments as a goal-directed navigation task, where a long-horizon goal is expressed relative to the robot and a planner generates local paths based on the goal and the robot's perceptual observations. We present FlowRow, a flow-matching-based local planner which models the distribution of diverse agricultural navigation behaviors as $\mathrm{SE}(2)$ paths using the long-horizon goal and depth perception. To enable systematic evaluation, we introduce RowNavBench, a navigation benchmark for semi-structured agricultural environments developed in Isaac Sim, comprising 900 episodes across grape vineyards, lemon orchards, and apple orchards with varying row lengths, row and plant spacings, plant positions, and weed growth. FlowRow achieves 87% success, compared to 44% for the strongest generative behavior cloning baseline retrained on the same demonstrations. We will release our benchmark, data, and code upon acceptance.
  </details>

- **[Inverse Kinematics in Endoscopic Scenarios using MoveIt](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20978)**  `arXiv:2611.20978`  `cs.RO`  
  _Marc Bestmann, Timon Engelke, Rebecca Rodeck, Gerko Wende_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Inverse kinematics are critical in many robotic applications, yet standard MoveIt-based solvers struggle in confined scenarios. This paper provides a benchmark of the available solvers and compares multiple practical modifications for improving the performance of the solvers. Splitting the IK problem into two parts results in the best performance, leading to a 100% success rate under 100 ms in an endoscopic tank scenario.
  </details>

- **[TaF-Foundation: Wrench-Grounded Cross-Sensor Tactile Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20718)**  `arXiv:2611.20718`  `cs.RO`  
  _Jiaming Jiang, Yuzhe Huang, Baokun Yang, Hao Liang, Li Gao, Haoran Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tactile sensing is essential for contact-rich manipulation, yet heterogeneous sensing mechanisms and observation formats hinder cross-hardware transfer. Since physical contact is governed by Newtonian mechanics, we ground tactile representations in six-dimensional wrenches, establishing a common physical reference across sensing mechanisms. Specifically, we pair tactile observations from all sensors with wrench measurements, enabling shared physical supervision without requiring direct tactile-to-tactile pairing between sensors. To support learning at scale, we develop a dedicated acquisition platform and collect over 100 million paired tactile observations and wrench measurements from 24 sensors spanning four families of sensing principles. Building on this dataset, our framework combines sensor-specific input adaptation with a shared tactile encoder. By learning to reconstruct wrenches, the encoder maps observations from different sensors into a shared, continuous feature space grounded in contact physics. To leverage these representations for manipulation, we introduce TaF-Policy, which integrates tactile features with visual observations and robot states to generate action chunks. We evaluate the learned representations through wrench estimation, cross-sensor retrieval, few-shot adaptation to unseen sensors, and robotic manipulation. On the core benchmark, our method reduces single-frame force RMSE by 17.1% and improves cross-sensor retrieval R@1 by 13.11 percentage points over the strongest baseline. In manipulation experiments, TaF-Policy achieves average success rates of 51.33% across three simulated tasks and 46.25% across four real-world tasks, compared with 47.33% and 23.75%, respectively, for the strongest baselines evaluated on the same task sets. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Stochastic Self-Distillation for Flow-Based Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20460)**  `arXiv:2611.20460`  `cs.RO`  
  _Jingru Guo, Alessandra Russo, Joey Bose, Christos Ziakas_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Flow-based vision-language-action (VLA) models achieve strong performance in robotics through imitation learning, yet fine-tuning them with on-policy reinforcement learning (RL) is limited. In group-based RL methods like Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), every rollout step requires multi-step ODE integration, and the sampler's limited stochasticity leads to rollout groups with low variance. We propose VLA-SSD, which addresses both high inference latency and limited stochasticity. In particular, our method self-distills a pretrained flow-matching action head into a stochastic one-step policy by conditioning a single-jump generator on a Brownian path. We derive a conditional action density via our stochastic one-step policy, enabling efficient post-training via likelihood-ratio RL algorithms. We show that distilling VLA-SSD from the $\pi_0$ VLA reduces the action head's forward-pass time by a factor of 7.7, at the cost of a 5.5% relative decrease in the success rate on the LIBERO benchmark. However, by leveraging the increased sample diversity of VLA-SSD during group generation, post-training a $\pi_{0.5}$ VLA using GRPO yields a 20.6% relative improvement.
  </details>

- **[DiffWAM: Learning World Action Model from Consecutive Visual Difference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19904)**  `arXiv:2611.19904`  `cs.RO`  
  _Xinheng Zhang, Haoyu Xiong, Chao Jing, Xiaohan Zhang, Linshan Li, Hongbang Fan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World Action Models (WAMs) have emerged as an important paradigm in robot learning, jointly predicting future scene states and robot actions from current observations and instructions. However, future states entangle task-related changes with large amounts of task-irrelevant content, such as static backgrounds, which weakens the model's ability to capture changes relevant to task execution. To address this, we propose Difference World Action Model (DiffWAM), a Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) framework that enhances world representations by jointly learning future-state prediction and consecutive visual difference prediction. We define consecutive visual differences as differences between the latent representations of consecutive observations, providing an explicit learning signal for temporal variation. The framework comprises a World Expert for future frame prediction, an Action Expert for action generation, and a Diff Expert for predicting these latent differences along the trajectory. A structured unidirectional attention mask conditions the Diff, World, and Action Experts solely on the current-frame representation while blocking attention between experts. This design allows both the World and Diff Experts to be removed during inference, introducing no additional inference overhead. DiffWAM achieves state-of-the-art performance on the LIBERO and RoboTwin 2.0 benchmarks and substantially improves over FastWAM on real-world manipulation tasks. Specifically, it attains average success rates of 98.8%, 92.65%, and 88.3%, outperforming the state-of-the-art baseline (i.e., FastWAM) by 1.75, 1.55, and 15.0 percentage points, respectively.
  </details>

- **[DynLimit: A Simulation Benchmark for Dynamics-Critical Planning and Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19785)**  `arXiv:2611.19785`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yang Xiao, Yongzhi Liu, Sunan Zhang, Kang Wang, Guodong Yin, Weichao Zhuang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Experiments near vehicle handling limits must keep three questions apart: what a maneuver requests, what the closed-loop system executes, and whether the task is completed. We present DynLimit, a simulation platform that organizes this process end to end. Scenarios are composed from road, friction, obstacle, and reference objects; planners and controllers attach through a shared observation and control interface; a vehicle adapter runs either a license-free four-wheel model or an external dynamics solver; and synchronized records feed task evaluation, replay, and response inspection. The platform carries a task-oriented evaluation method that pairs a prescribed reference demand with a sampled passing set and event-aligned diagnosis. Across eight task families, three friction environments, and three reference-assisted baseline chains we executed 450 runs: 360 nominal, 18 locally refined, and 72 with prescribed initial offsets. Method differences depend on the task family, equal aggregate pass counts arise from different passing cells, and local refinement narrows two sampled transitions without locating a continuous boundary. Two case comparisons show why sideslip or tire-utilization peaks cannot replace task constraints. The three views therefore stay distinct in every reported run: an outcome can be traced from the prescribed demand, through the executed response, to the task rule that decided it. Code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[M-CROSS: A Multisensor Simulation Dataset for SLAM across Water--Land Transitions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19765)**  `arXiv:2611.19765`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yan Dong, Tianyi Wu, Junyu Yang, Shaoqiang Qiu, Zefeng Yan, Tao Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Water--land cross-domain robots require continuous localization across land, transition and underwater phases, where sensor characteristics and availability change abruptly. Existing datasets and conventional metrics are not designed to capture transition-induced degradation and failures. We present \textbf{M-CROSS}, to the best of our knowledge, the first multisensor simulation dataset and benchmark for SLAM across continuous water--land transitions. It provides synchronized LiDAR, sonar, stereo camera, IMU, DVL, and depth measurements; 6DoF pose ground truth; sensor-wise medium-state annotations; and medium-dependent sensor degradation models. We establish a transition-aware protocol comprising the Trajectory Completion Rate (TCR), Piecewise Absolute Pose Error (PW-APE), Transition End Error (TEE), and Transition Survival Period (TSP), which measure trajectory completion, single-domain accuracy, transition-induced error, and localization survival under prolonged partial immersion, respectively. Experiments with 6 representative SLAM systems show that existing methods fail to maintain both localization accuracy and trajectory continuity across the full set of water--land transitions, exhibiting transition-induced drift, trajectory discontinuities, and tracking failures. M-CROSS is publicly availablethis https URL
  </details>

- **[ResWAM: Learning What Changes, Not What Stays, for World-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19559)**  `arXiv:2611.19559`  `cs.RO`  
  _Chongyu Fan, Gaowen Liu, Ramana Rao Kompella, Jason Corso, Sijia Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World-action models (WAMs) augment vision-language-action policies (VLAs) with future prediction so that the policy can anticipate how its actions will affect the physical world. Existing WAMs supervise complete future observations. However, observations typically change little over an action chunk, so the future loss is dominated by copying static background content rather than learning task-relevant robot and object motion, limiting policy learning. Moreover, existing WAMs rely on large video generation models or separate world models, incurring heavy training and inference compute that hinders closed-loop deployment. To improve policy performance, we propose visual residual learning, supervising only the feature change from the current frame to the future frame in a frozen DINOv3 space. Compared with complete future features, this residual learning target yields a higher feature rank with richer and more diverse representations of scene changes, and spatially concentrates on the robot arm and manipulated objects rather than the background. To further improve computational efficiency, we present ResWAM, a residual world-action model with 2.3B parameters in total, including a lightweight 0.5B dual-branch DiT that jointly trains an action branch and a residual branch via conditional flow matching. Without robot pretraining (i.e., pretraining on external robot action trajectories beyond the benchmark demonstrations), ResWAM achieves top average success rates on LIBERO-Plus (81.1%), RoboTwin 2.0 (93.8%), and RoboDojo (9.40% success, 13.41 score) at the lowest training (2.7-4.0 TFLOPs) and inference compute (1.0-1.4 TFLOPs), and leads real-robot Open, Put, and Stack (80.0% average, +10.0% over $\pi_{0.5}$) at 92.7 ms latency.
  </details>

- **[Long-Horizon Causal Inertial Odometry in Multistory Buildings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19387)**  `arXiv:2611.19387`  `cs.RO`  
  _Tianzi Zhang, Yan Wang, Hao Qiao, Jian Kuang, Xiaoji Niu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In indoor 3D localization inside multistory buildings, an elevator or an escalator accelerates for a few seconds and then cruises at constant velocity for tens of seconds. Gait excitation disappears, the specific force degenerates into a constant gravity reaction, and instantaneous velocity becomes unobservable within a 1-2 s window. The output of short-window learned inertial odometry consequently degrades toward the conditional mean of the training set, and vertical displacement is systematically underestimated. We propose Local-Token Causal Attention (LTCA), a long-horizon architecture that compresses each 1 s inertial window into a 1 Hz motion token and sustains, over a bounded 60 s causal cache, the motion state established at the onset event. We further introduce a pluggable barometer branch that, when online, supplies a direct elevation constraint to the vertical channel through local differential height observations, and we train it with paired online/fallback conditional training so that the barometer also regularizes the representation shared by the two modes during training. On MSIO, our benchmark of nine buildings, the RoNIN and TLIO instances reach 3D trajectory errors (ATE-3D) of 2.83/4.18 m and 2.44/5.12 m on the Seen and Unseen splits, respectively, with the barometer online. With the barometer disabled at inference, the same weights fall back to pure inertial inference and reach 3.24/4.28 m and 2.64/5.23 m, still below every native short-window baseline. We further validate the framework on the public RoNIN and TLIO benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[TMA: Sim-to-Real Forceful Humanoid Manipulation via Tactile-Driven Motor Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18976)**  `arXiv:2611.18976`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yiting Chen, Kelin Yu, Sarthak Kaingade, Bryan Trinh, Kaiyu Hang, Roberto Martin-martin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent humanoid loco-manipulation in articulated environments has largely focused on geometry-dominant interactions, where success is primarily determined by geometric alignment and motion execution. In contrast, forceful multi-finger manipulation requires the robot to adapt to real-world uncertainties in contact and interaction dynamics. We present Tactile-Driven Motor Adaptation (TMA) and an integrated whole-body system for zero-shot sim-to-real forceful humanoid manipulation, and study round-doorknob opening as a challenging benchmark requiring stable multi-finger frictional grasping, sufficient torque generation without slip, force-regulated pushing, and whole-body stability. TMA structures policy learning around interactions observable through tactile feedback, uses cone geometry as an inductive bias for multi-finger contact representation, and adapts motor behavior online from tactile history. An upper-body manipulation policy is trained alongside a pretrained general locomotion controller to coordinate dexterous interaction and whole-body stabilization. Trained entirely in simulation, TMA transfers zero-shot to a Unitree G1 humanoid with BrainCo Revo2 multi-fingered hands and substantially outperforms standard Rapid Motor Adaptation (RMA) and reactive tactile policy baselines across four commercial round doorknobs in the real world.
  </details>

- **[XBot-Omni: An Omni-Modal Model for Embodied Understanding and Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17160)**  `arXiv:2611.17160`  `cs.RO`  
  _Kerui Gu, Xing Fu, Shuoyang Sun, Cheng Peng, Jingru Duan, Huayu Gu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Embodied interaction requires an agent to integrate multimodal perception with verbal and nonverbal behaviors to effectively communicate its intentions, including speech, body motion, and facial expressions. We present XBot-Omni, a Thinker--Talker--Actor architecture that extends the Omni paradigm to robotic modalities, including body motion and facial expressions, with ``X'' representing the extensibility to robotic-specific modalities. The Thinker processes text, images, audio, and video to formulate contextually appropriate responses, which are then realized by the Talker and Actor through speech and coordinated bodily expressions, respectively. In this report, we innovatively introduce the concept of ``Actor'' as a dedicated component for embodied agents: a coordinated, streaming module that leverages the native representations of the Thinker and Talker within an end-to-end pipeline, introducing only modest latency overhead. This unified architecture enables multimodal generation conditioned on omni-modal inputs directly to prevent information loss in staged designs. We comprehensively evaluate XBot-Omni across seven benchmarks, demonstrating strong multimodal understanding capabilities on both general-domain and robot-centric videos, as well as high-quality, natural, and well-coordinated motion generation. Furthermore, our results validate its capability to generate embodied multimodal responses directly from omni inputs, meeting the diverse requirements of embodied interaction. XBot-Omni enables robots not only to perceive the omni world but also to express themselves through speech and bodily behaviors, extending Omni models from conversational interaction to embodied communication in physical environments.
  </details>

- **[VLALagBench: Understanding When and Why VLA Inference Latency Matters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03103)**  `arXiv:2611.03103`  `cs.RO`  
  _Qi Wen, Borui Xu, Hussein Amro, Hongshi Tan, Hanmin Lin, Chaoyi Ruan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language-action (VLA) models offer a promising path toward general-purpose robot manipulation. While inference latency can affect task success in a constantly evolving physical world, the relationship between latency and task success rate remains poorly understood. In this paper, we introduce VLALagBench, a comprehensive benchmark for studying VLA inference latency sensitivity across task types, object speeds, execution modes (synchronous and asynchronous), and VLA policies. Using VLALagBench, we find that even a modest inference latency of 100 ms can nearly halve task success on moving targets, with sensitivity varying substantially across tasks and policies. These differences reflect task-specific requirements for precision and continuous motion. By overlapping inference with action execution, asynchronous execution can recover some of the lost success at the cost of more frequent inference requests. Building on these findings, we develop an empirical model that predicts task success from limited calibration data, achieving root-mean-square prediction errors of only 5.7-7.8% across various VLA policies. Given an inference system's latency and per-robot throughput, these predictions guide the selection of system-specific VLA algorithm configurations to maximize task success. We believe VLALagBench and these findings provide a solid foundation for future robotics algorithm-system co-design.
  </details>

- **[Veloxity: Towards Memory-Safe UAV Embedded Firmware using the Rust Programming Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41053)**  `arXiv:2610.41053`  `cs.RO`  
  _Derek Ward, Brian Bascom, Tyler Larsen, James Usevitch, Phil Tokumaru, Tim McLain_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent years have witnessed increased momentum towards memory-safe programming languages for safety-critical systems. Memory-safe languages can eliminate entire classes of common software errors that have historically required extensive analysis and expenditure to avoid. Nevertheless, memory-safe language adoption in aerospace remains fragmented and incomplete, particularly for unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs). Recent Rust UAV projects either run only in simulation, are closed source, or have not been integrated with an established autonomy ecosystem and tested against existing C/C++ firmware. To address this gap, we present Veloxity: an open-source embedded UAV autopilot written in the memory-safe Rust programming language. Veloxity lowers the barrier to using memory-safe languages within modern ecosystems by providing a minimal, modular foundation for autonomous UAVs. We compare Veloxity to equivalent autopilots written in C++ in simulation, with hardware testbenches, and via outdoor waypoint-following flight tests, demonstrating functional parity with improved performance. These results indicate that modern memory-safe languages are a viable option for reliable and performant firmware for autonomous aerospace systems.
  </details>

- **[One from Infinity: Actualizing Futures from Pretrained World Models into Robot Actions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36413)**  `arXiv:2609.36413`  `cs.RO`  
  _Bang Du, Yichen Xie, Shuqi Zhao, Yuxin Chen, Menglin Wu, Masayoshi Tomizuka_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A pretrained video world model admits many plausible futures for a scene, but a robot must realize the exact task-conditioned one. To turn world models into executable robot policies, existing methods fine-tune the heavy world model backbone using large-scale robot data and computational resources. Challenging this status quo, we argue that the expensive part has already been paid in the world model pretraining since the representation space of a video world model encoder lays out the diverse potential futures. In this case, what remains is to determine the future that accomplishes the task and to read out the actions that realize it. We formalize this task as actualization, which learns a task-conditioned realization on top of a prior supplied by a frozen world model encoder. This can be solved by a tiny actualizer model. We implement RoboActualizer with as few as 60M parameters on top of a frozen world model encoder. The actualizer is composed of two lightweight DiT experts that jointly predict future latents and actions by flow matching. The model can be trained entirely on a single GPU with 32 GB peak memory. With up to 100x fewer trainable parameters than existing WAMs and VLAs, RoboActualizer reaches great performance on simulation benchmarks including LIBERO, LIBERO-Plus, RoboTwin 2.0 and five tasks on two real-world platforms, with a low latency of 39 ms that allows real-time control.
  </details>

- **[CommitFlow: Semantic Commitment Verification and Local Correction for Long-Horizon Robot Manipulation VLA Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21908)**  `arXiv:2609.21908`  `cs.RO`  
  _Zixiang Zhao, Yansong Feng, Jibao Yuan, Yang Yang, Chaoyu Wang, Haoran Xiao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Although vision-language-action (VLA) policies have advanced rapidly, long-horizon execution may still progress to the next task stage before the required physical effect has been established. We call this a mismatch between semantic commitments, physical conditions that a stage must establish or maintain, and the actual physical state. Because an action command alone cannot confirm such a condition, local deviations can propagate and cause task failure. To address this problem, we present CommitFlow, a closed-loop execution framework that combines commitment monitoring with local correction while keeping the base policy frozen. CommitFlow integrates three components. A Semantic Commitment Monitor (SCM) compares stage requirements against current state evidence and holds back dependent actions when a required condition is unmet or violated. BoundaryFlow then generates a local correction conditioned on the current state and base action, and Relation and Gain Calibration (RGC) selects the smallest correction strength that satisfies the relevant constraints. Across the ten common RoboTwin 2.0 benchmark tasks, CommitFlow achieves a mean success rate of 75.9 percent, improving on the base policy pi0.5 by 22.7 percent. Cross-policy experiments show consistent gains, pointing toward reliable long-horizon robot execution.
  </details>

- **[DSSP: Diffusion State Space Policy with Full-History Encoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.14598)**  `arXiv:2605.14598`  `cs.RO`  
  _Zhiyuan Guan, Jianshu Hu, Han Fang, Yunpeng Jiang, Yize Huang, Shujia Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion-based imitation learning has shown strong promise for robot manipulation. However, most existing policies condition only on the current observation or a short window of recent observations, limiting their ability to resolve history-dependent ambiguities in long-horizon tasks. To address this, we introduce \ours, a history-conditioned Diffusion State Space Policy that enables efficient, full-history conditioning for robot manipulation. Leveraging the continuous sequence modeling properties of State Space Models (SSMs), our history encoder effectively compresses the entire observation stream into a compact context representation. To ensure this context preserves critical information regarding future state evolution, the encoder is optimized with a dynamics-aware auxiliary training objective. This high-level context representation is then seamlessly fused with recent state observations to form a hierarchical conditioning mechanism for action generation. Furthermore, to maintain architectural consistency and minimize GPU memory overhead, we also instantiate the diffusion backbone itself using an SSM. Extensive experiments across simulation benchmarks and real-world manipulation tasks show that \ours achieves state-of-the-art performance with a significantly smaller model size, demonstrating superior efficiency of the hierarchical conditioning in capturing crucial information as the history length increases. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[OpenMAS-GApp: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Domain-Adaptive Multi-Agent Application Construction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20997)**  `arXiv:2611.20997`  `cs.MA`  
  _Xunkai Li, Kairui Yang, Jiaxi Li, Yuecong Mao, Zekai Chen, Xinle Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Constructing multi-agent system (MAS) applications across domains requires translating diverse requirements into executable systems, involving task decomposition, agent and tool selection, and execution organization. Existing studies have explored individual aspects of MAS construction, including agent generation, architecture design, workflow generation, and component selection. However, these methods are often developed and evaluated independently, using different task specifications, component resources, and execution settings. This makes it difficult to compare their construction capabilities or determine how they can be applied to different domains. To address this problem, we propose OpenMAS-GApp, a One-for-All benchmark for MAS application construction across diverse domains. It standardizes task inputs, component resources, construction interfaces, and execution environments to support MAS construction under consistent conditions. Within OpenMAS-GApp, we provide Graph Harness as a reference construction method that derives task dependencies, selects compatible agents and tools, and organizes their interactions into executable graphs. We evaluate construction methods across 19 benchmark datasets under 20 evaluation settings spanning four application types and four professional domains.
  </details>

- **[From Vision to Interaction: Evaluating Dynamic Social Intelligence of VLM Agents in ALIVE-World](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20956)**  `arXiv:2611.20956`  `cs.MA`  
  _Yue Xu, Xiaoyu Qin, Sihan Zhao, Ze-en Xiong, Jia Jia_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As AI agents are increasingly deployed in real-world applications and take on social roles, dynamic social intelligence, the ability to continuously perceive multimodal cues, infer latent cognitive states, intervene through embodied actions, and ultimately achieve social goals, has become essential. However, existing evaluations largely fragment social intelligence into text-based abstractions, static reasoning tasks, or embodied simulators that lack social dynamics. These approaches fail to capture the multimodal, continuous, and interpersonal complexity of authentic human interactions. To bridge this gap, we introduce ALIVE-World, an embodied simulation environment supporting dynamic multimodal interactions encompassing egocentric vision, language, and non-verbal action. To provide socially grounded interactive feedback, we populate this environment with counterpart agents driven by a profile-conditioned, dual-system (intuitive-affective and logical-cognitive) architecture. Building upon this platform, we propose ALIVE-Bench to transform the assessment of social intelligence into a trackable quantitative framework. By deeply leveraging underlying environmental states and agents' internal variables, ALIVE-Bench systematically evaluates Vision-Language Model (VLM) agents across four critical dimensions: perception, understanding, influence, and outcome. Extensive evaluations reveal that dynamic social intelligence is fundamentally multi-dimensional and scenario-dependent. We identify a critical bottleneck between upstream perception and downstream social intervention, exposing VLM vulnerabilities in fine-grained proxemic awareness, adaptive intervention, and multi-party coordination. By uncovering these limitations and the diverse interaction strategies they produce, this benchmark provides actionable insights for the future behavioral alignment of multimodal agents.
  </details>

- **[CANDO: Cooperative Agentic Network for Layout Design Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10044)**  `arXiv:2610.10044`  `cs.MA`  
  _Athanasios Masouris, Zheng Jing, Benjamin Sam Chandler, Hadi Jamali-Rad_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Layout generation for real-world facilities is a challenging problem, requiring reasoning over irregular site boundaries, heterogeneous orientations, access-aware placements, and motion-planning feasibility. Yet, most existing layout benchmarks in the generative AI space target simpler placements over rectangular domains and rely on distributional metrics such as FID and IoU that reward conformity to dataset priors, thus discounting design innovation. Motivated by these gaps, we introduce ALPS-Bench, a benchmark of $1,000$ professionally annotated real-world facility layouts paired with an instance-specific scoring protocol grounded in a structured design manual. As a strong baseline for ALPS-Bench, we propose CANDO, a training-free multi-agent framework in which specialized agents iteratively refine layouts through a verification-grounded loop, concentrating reasoning on strategic spatial decisions. We demonstrate that CANDO surpasses state-of-the-art trained and LLM-based baselines on the widely adopted PubLayNet, RICO, and PKU-PosterLayout benchmarks, establishing cooperative agentic design as a broadly effective recipe for constraint-aware layout synthesis. Code and benchmark are available through our project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[UNITE: Consolidating Embodied VLM Specialists via Shared-Anchor One-Bit Factorization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20767)**  `arXiv:2611.20767`  `cs.LG` `cs.CV`  
  _Tao Tao, Anke Tang, Enneng Yang, Zhi Hou, Xiaogang Wang, Dacheng Tao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Embodied capabilities such as visual grounding, spatial reasoning, navigation, and driving understanding are often developed in separate vision-language specialists fine-tuned from a common pretrainedthis http URLthe specialists separately multiplies storage, whereas merging them into one dense model forces conflicting task updates to share parameters and loses part of each specialist'sthis http URLintroduce UNITE, a framework that consolidates such specialists into a compact shared-backbone mixture of experts. UNITE keeps the feed-forward block of each specialist as a routable expert and represents the expert bank through a shared-anchor one-bit factorization: a shared floating-point anchor plus expert-specific, row-scaled binarythis http URLanchor is optimized jointly with the scales and signs through closed-form alternating updates, using only source weights and no calibrationthis http URLadaptation then adjusts token-level expert selection with the backbone and experts frozen, updating fewer than $0.6$M parameters ($<0.01\%$ of the $8.8$B active parameters). Built from four Qwen3-VL-8B specialists, the complete UNITE model occupies $20.3$ GB, $1.16\times$ the storage of one dense specialist and $59.6\%$ below the uncompressed MoE. Across $22$ benchmarks and R2R Val-Unseen, UNITE matches six static merging methods in mean retention of best-specialist performance, leads all of them on Scan2Cap, ScanQA, and all four navigation metrics, and surpasses every source specialist on all five 3D scene understanding benchmarks (up to $+19.1$ on Scan2Cap) and on R2R navigation. Relative to the uncompressed MoE, factorization is nearly lossless on $21$ of $22$ benchmarks; the exception is VSI-Bench, where frame-count experiments suggest impaired use of cross-framethis http URLthus consolidates complementary embodied capabilities near single-model storage.
  </details>

- **[LogT: Logically Think with Images for Visual Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19200)**  `arXiv:2611.19200`  `cs.LG` `cs.CV`  
  _Yanjun Fu, Quanzeng You, Jiadong Guo, Yujie Lu, Sai Charitha Akula, Haiping Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Thinking with images empowers vision-language models (VLMs) to use an external Zoom-in tool for fine-grained visual search. Recent works employ supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning (RL) with tool-specific designs to enable such capabilities. However, achieving logical reasoning alongside accurate and efficient tool use remains a significant challenge. To address this, we shift the focus away from increasing RL complexity and instead identify SFT data quality as a key bottleneck. We then propose LogT (short for Logically Think with Images), a fully automated SFT data engine that explicitly enforces logic-chain-guided reasoning. LogT generates an SFT dataset featuring challenging, visually grounded questions and structured tool-use traces with human-like logic. We validate our approach by fine-tuning two base models, Qwen2.5-VL-7B and InternVL3.5-8B, on the dataset and evaluating them across seven benchmark test sets. We show that LogT-SFT based on Qwen2.5-VL-7B outperforms the state-of-the-art trained with both SFT and RL, despite using 3.1$\times$ less data and $>120\times$ fewer GPU hours. Furthermore, applying a simple RL recipe without tool-specific design on LogT-SFT yields a model that surpasses prior methods with complex RL setups by a substantial 9.59-point margin. In addition to strong performance gains, our model exhibits higher tool-use accuracy and efficiency, as well as more structured and logically consistent reasoning traces, directly validating the effectiveness of our approach.
  </details>

- **[HIMX: mixed-format matrix multiplication based on customized HIFLOAT4 representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18641)**  `arXiv:2611.18641`  `cs.LG` `cs.CV`  
  _Zhiyuan Zhang, Yanzhao Li, Hui Dong, Sifan Cheng, Jingwei Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  4-bit weight-activation quantization (W4A4) is an important approach for efficient large language model (LLM) inference. Recent hierarchical formats such as HiFloat4 (HIF4) improve the representational range of 4-bit quantization through multi-level scaling. However, the same local mantissa representation in HIF4 may not be equally suitable for weights and activations, which exhibit distinct distribution characteristics. To address this limitation, we propose HiMX, a mixed-mantissa framework built upon the hierarchical scaling structure of HIF4. Our key observation is that the two HIF4-compatible mantissa representations, S1P2 and E2M1, exhibit complementary quantization behavior: the denser S1P2 format provides higher resolution for concentrated weight distributions, while the wider-spaced E2M1 format provides complementary quantization characteristics for activation tensors. HiMX enables operand-tailored mantissa representations while converting heterogeneous mantissas into fixed-point integer operands with operand-specific widths, allowing different operand formats to share a unified integer multiply-accumulate (MAC) array. Block-level analysis on Qwen3-8B shows that HIF4-S1P2 is better suited for weights while HIF4 E2M1 is better suited for activations, and their mixed-format combination achieves lower output MSE than homogeneous configurations. Across multiple benchmarks, HiMX reduces the average end-to-end accuracy loss from 2.38 to 1.77 points on Qwen3-8B and from 2.23 to 1.85 points on Qwen3-30B-A3B, demonstrating the potential benefit of heterogeneous mantissa representations for W4A4 LLM inference.
  </details>

- **[The Decision Value of Perception Compute](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35910)**  `arXiv:2609.35910`  `cs.LG` `cs.CV`  
  _Hoang Pham Cong, Ho Viet Duc Luong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Adaptive perception spends extra computation on inputs where perception is expected to improve. When perception feeds a downstream decision system, a better perception output need not produce a better decision. We define the decision value of perception compute as the change in downstream loss from escalating an input from a cheap to an expensive perception mode. Because this value can be negative, the allocation of perception compute should be judged against a budget-constrained decision oracle, with uniform full-fidelity inference as a baseline rather than an upper bound. We introduce DEEP (Decision Evaluation for Escalated Perception), a benchmark that scores pre-escalation allocators against this oracle under selection, latency and energy budgets, charging each allocator for its own computation. With deployed monocular geometry on KITTI and nuScenes, we find that 34--54% of the escalations that change downstream loss make it worse; harmful escalations also occur for the published PDM-Closed planner, evaluated open-loop on nuPlan with real detector outcomes. On nuScenes, perception-level gain frequently disagrees in sign with decision value. This mismatch has practical consequences: choosing among fixed deployable signals by missed-object perception gain rather than by decision value reduces realized test decision gain by 7.4% of the all-cheap loss on average. Learned allocators recover part of the oracle's value by finding beneficial escalations but select nearly as much harm as random, and once their own computation is charged at a 20% latency budget, only the lightweight routers, at about 3.5\% of a full detector pass, still beat random.
  </details>

- **[A Systematic Analysis of Out-of-Distribution Detection Under Representation and Training Paradigm Shifts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.11934)**  `arXiv:2511.11934`  `cs.LG` `cs.CV`  
  _Claudio César Claros Olivares, Austin J. Brockmeier_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present a systematic benchmark of out-of-distribution (OOD) detection confidence scoring functions (CSFs) through a representation-centric lens. Our study spans CNN and ViT backbones, multiple training paradigms, four image-classification source datasets (CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, SuperCIFAR-100, and TinyImageNet), and OOD datasets grouped into near, mid, and far regimes based on CLIP-derived semantic distances. To compare CSFs across these settings, we employ a rank-based pipeline of Friedman and Conover tests that identifies top cliques of statistically indistinguishable winners under threshold-free ranking metrics (AURC and AUGRC). The main empirical finding is that the family of CSFs that are competitive detectors correlates with characteristics of the learned representation. For both CNNs and ViTs, simple probabilistic scores dominate misclassification detection. On CNNs, margin-based scores are strongest in near-OOD regimes, while geometry-aware scores such as NNGuide, fDBD, and CTM become more competitive as shift severity increases. On fine-tuned ViTs, the top cliques are led mainly by reconstruction- and residual-based scores. To interpret these ranking shifts, we analyze the last-layer representation using Neural Collapse (NC) metrics. The resulting picture is consistent across architectures: prototype- and boundary-aware scores become stronger when the representation is more collapsed and better aligned with classifier weights, whereas weaker-collapse regimes favor gradient- and manifold-based scores. Building on these insights, we propose two contributions: a simple PCA-based projection-filtering procedure that improves detector performance, and an approach that uses only the NC measurements of a trained classifier to predict its competitive out-of-distribution detector shortlist, without OOD data for that classifier and without knowledge of the shift.
  </details>

- **[TrainExplorer: Autonomous LLM Post-Training via Plan-and-Decouple Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20430)**  `arXiv:2611.20430`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Xuannan Liu, Tian Lan, Zekun Li, Xing Cui, Zhengfeng Yu, Feihu Jiang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based agents can automate model post-training, accelerating progress toward recursive self-improvement. Despite their promise, existing approaches remain under-explored and face two main challenges: (1) strategy lock-in that limits exploration beyond the initial strategy; and (2) coupled execution that obscures the effects of individual modifications. To address these challanges, we propose TrainExplorer, a multi-agent framework for autonomous post-training, that incorporates strategy planning and execution decoupling, to achieve broaden strategy exploration. First, strategy planning draws on scientific literature to construct a candidate search space of training strategies. Then, decoupled execution enables single-component interventions and attribution to guide strategy exploration. Extensive experiments on PostTrainBench demonstrate that TrainExplorer achieves a weighted aggregate score of 39.02, outperforming Claude Code using the same LLM by 7.32 and surpassing GPT-5.6 Sol-powered Codex, which scores 36.23. Code will be available.
  </details>

- **[When the Cheapest Feasible Router Fails: Auditing Deployment Commitments in Cost-Aware LLM Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20417)**  `arXiv:2611.20417`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Minxuan Hu, Qishi Zhan, Ziheng Chen, Zhengtao Yao, Zihan Dong, Jiayu Yi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cost-aware LLM routing often selects the cheapest policy meeting empirical risk and coverage constraints. Yet a router that passes an audit can violate its fixed service contract on new requests, even when candidate policies are constructed independently. We study this deployment-commitment decision with disjoint construction, audit, and test samples. Across 19 RouterBench task families, the cheapest audit-feasible router deploys in 91.82% of opportunities but violates the held-out contract in 32.22% of deployments. We introduce Menu-Aware Routing Commitment (MARC), which qualifies policies using audit evidence and a serving-cost tolerance to retain the apparent winner, deploy a supported alternative, or defer. Its practical policywise variant (MARC-P) reduces observed conditional failure to 0.73% at 85.79% deployment, with higher serving cost; fixed-margin and formal risk-control procedures provide competitive alternatives. Nested audits show how added evidence changes deployable policy sets, and learned semantic routing exhibits the same commitment failure. These results establish deployment commitment as a distinct operational decision connecting router evaluation, service reliability, and inference cost.
  </details>

- **[Simplify, Agree, Distill: Label-Free Self-Distillation with Stepping Stones](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18916)**  `arXiv:2611.18916`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Dingyang Chen, Jinfeng Xiao, Zhenyu Zhang, William Hu, Ziyu Wang, Yang Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Can a language model teach itself to reason without labels? On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) uses the model as its own teacher, but strengthens that teacher with a ground-truth solution. The natural label-free substitute, voting over the model's attempts, fails precisely on hard problems: attempts disagree, and their most common answer is often wrong. We propose STONE, a label-free alternative that moves agreement to easier related problems. The model writes these stepping stones, keeps those it solves consistently, and shows the teacher their worked solutions, with final answers removed; the student sees only the original problem. No step reads a label, and inference needs no extra context or model calls. Across five math benchmarks, the proposed method achieves the highest average accuracy for Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-8B in thinking mode. Without thinking mode, it gains 13.2 and 22.7 points, 2.5 and 2.7 times the gains of OPSD with ground-truth solutions.
  </details>

- **[Screen Before You Add: Reducing Candidate Inference in Model Ensembles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18647)**  `arXiv:2611.18647`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Davide Mambelli, Alessandro Rozza, Steven Motta_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As new models become available, existing ensembles must be updated to be competitive, making them intrinsically dynamic. Deciding whether to add a candidate model can require inference on an entire annotated dataset. Such a screening becomes prohibitive for models with costly inference especially when they ultimately add no value. Even under simple convex aggregation, relying on benchmark scores can be misleading as strong standalone performance does not guarantee ensemble improvement, and fitted ensemble weights can hurt test performance. In this work, we study candidate screening and show how early rejection can be achieved safely, exploiting residual geometry for stratification and rejecting the candidate when the best possible remaining predictions cannot achieve the necessary improvement. The key result that justifies this approach is that, under convex aggregation and squared or Brier loss, the population optimum improves if and only if a candidate induces a prediction change positively aligned with the residuals of the population-optimal existing ensemble. An optimistic quadratic program (QP) provides certified early rejection, while posterior-scenario sampling offers an alternative heuristic. We evaluate the approach by showing the tradeoff between saved calls and screening accuracy, which refers to recovering the same accept/reject result as for the entire annotated dataset. We assess empirically whether full-dataset decisions predict candidate usefulness on a held-out test set. On two LLM panels, stratified screening with certified QP rejection saves 21-23% of candidate calls while keeping 100% screening accuracy. In our experiments, sampling posterior scenarios further increases call savings to approximately 37%, with a loss of less than one percentage point in screening accuracy.
  </details>

- **[Where Should LLM Agents Adapt at Test Time? In Context, in Weights, or Both](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19700)**  `arXiv:2611.19700`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Parsa Mirtaheri, Mohammadreza Armandpour, Keivan Alizadeh, Iman Mirzadeh, Parshin Shojaee, Aryo Lotfi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents operating repeatedly in the same environment, such as a code repository, a database, or an application suite, encounter tasks that share latent structure. Experience can reveal this structure, allowing agents to reuse environment knowledge and learn from environmental feedback. Agents can adapt at test-time using this experience through two channels: retaining it in context and updating their weights. These channels are usually studied separately, leaving unclear what each contributes and when they complement each other. We compare in-context learning (ICL), reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards using GRPO, and self-distillation using SDPO, alone and in combination, using a shared online protocol and modular framework. Agents encounter matched task streams, are evaluated on each task before adapting to it, and learn only from their own attempts and environment feedback. We evaluate 14 adaptation mechanisms and a stateless baseline with Qwen3.5-9B on 109 tasks from four environments drawn from SWE-bench Verified, AppWorld, ScienceWorld, and BIRD-Critic-SQLite, using five task orders per environment. ICL provides the largest standalone gain: its best policy raises mean success across environments from 28.8% to 49.4%. Adding GRPO to this policy achieves the highest mean success, 51.6%. While adding GRPO improves the overall mean under every ICL policy, SDPO has mixed effects. Trace analyses show fewer lookups and shorter attempts under ICL, consistent with reusing knowledge of the shared structure. However, ICL also frequently skips inspection and verification for the current task. Adding GRPO to ICL helps restore these behaviors while preserving its reduced environment lookups. These observations suggest that reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards can refine how agents use retained experience, motivating the joint study of context management and weight updates.
  </details>

- **[SynthDCB: World Distillation for Scalable Device Control Benchmarking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18639)**  `arXiv:2611.18639`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Zichen Zhu, Dingye Liu, Nan Jiang, Danyang Zhang, Hang Zheng, Shijie Zheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scaling device-control evaluation requires scaling both the worlds agents interact with and the tasks they must solve. We introduce SynthDCB, a scalable benchmark for environment awareness and device control built through world distillation. World distillation transforms knowledge of device behaviors, interaction constraints, and environmental effects into modular executable components, which are composed into interactive worlds with explicit states, actions, and temporal dynamics. A unified capability representation connects tool discovery, world execution, and task synthesis, allowing new capabilities to enter evaluation through a shared construction pipeline. SynthDCB instantiates Home, Car, and Phone domains with 114 device classes, 1,166 functions, and 95 events, supporting five task families with 32 subtypes. The stateful design enables task validation and agent evaluation through state correctness and tool-call traces. We evaluate seven models on 2,560 test tasks; the best achieves 68.2% Pass@1. Error analysis reveals challenges in answer delivery, feasibility diagnosis, and temporal coordination. Construction experiments demonstrate scalability across domains and token efficiency: world distillation reduces token consumption by 0.9-66.0% and 46.2-92.5% relative to Codex with low and xhigh reasoning effort, respectively, while achieving broader capability coverage. SynthDCB makes executable world construction the basis of scalable device-control benchmarking, connecting an expanding space of capabilities and interactions to verifiable agent evaluation.
  </details>

- **[Multi-Head Recurrent Memory Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01523)**  `arXiv:2607.01523`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Jiatong Li, Samuel Yeh, Sharon Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recurrent memory agents extend LLMs to arbitrarily long contexts by iteratively consolidating input into a fixed-size memory window. Despite their scalability, these agents exhibit a well-documented reliability problem: end-to-end performance degrades systematically as context length grows. We diagnose this failure by decomposing performance into two factors---memory capture and memory retention---and quantitatively confirm that retention is the dominant bottleneck. Retention collapses because existing designs maintain memory as a monolithic text block, forcing every update to risk overwriting previously retained content. Motivated by this diagnosis, we propose Multi-Head Recurrent Memory (MHM), a general, training-free framework that partitions memory into independent heads governed by a stage-wise select-then-update strategy. At each step, exactly one head is selected for update while the remaining heads are structurally shielded from overwriting, shifting the burden of retention from model behavior to architectural design. As a lightweight instantiation, we introduce Least-Recently-Updated MHM (MHM-LRU), which guarantees uniform head utilization with zero additional token overhead. Extensive experiments on long-context benchmarks show that MHM-LRU substantially improves both retention and end-to-end accuracy across the 100K--1M token range, where baselines degrade sharply. On RULER-HQA at 896K tokens, MHM-LRU improves the memory retention rate from less than 30% to 73.96%. These gains generalize across model families, scales, and task types, positioning architectural optimization as a practical and cost-efficient path toward reliable long-context recurrent memory.
  </details>

- **[Algorithm Distillation for Safe In-Context Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21524)**  `arXiv:2611.21524`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Minjae Kwon, Amir Moeini, Shangtong Zhang, Lu Feng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Safe in-context reinforcement learning (Safe ICRL) asks a policy with fixed parameters to improve on an unseen constrained task from its interaction history while aiming to keep its cost within a budget. Prior work studied Safe ICRL on five benchmarks in three families: a grid world with a hidden goal and obstacles (DarkRoom), continuous navigation where goals and hazards stay invisible until reached (PointGoal, CarGoal), and locomotion with a hidden velocity limit (HalfCheetah, Ant). Safe Algorithm Distillation (Safe AD), trained on the learning histories of a safe RL algorithm, has shown adaptation failures in prior evaluations. For example, in the locomotion tasks its return stays below zero, and it rarely reaches the goal in CarGoal. We revisit two parts of Safe AD. First, we distill a budget-aware teacher with two phases. It searches for the hidden task, exploring more when more budget remains. After its first reward or cost, an expert policy exploits the task within the remaining budget. We collect the teacher's histories under fixed or dynamic budgets. Dynamic budgets increase the occupancy entropy of the return and budget inputs in every environment, and at test time they improve return and lower cost in DarkRoom and HalfCheetah. Second, we propose A-Lag, a test-time adjustment that reweights the distilled policy's actions by their predicted cost, with an adaptive Lagrange multiplier that is updated from the costs observed. In our experiments, A-Lag had lower mean episode cost than Safe AD in all five environments, with lower mean final return in four.
  </details>

- **[Can AI Mature a Model's Geometry? Training-Free Weight Baking in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21310)**  `arXiv:2611.21310`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Amin Golnari, Saeid Sanei_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Can an AI make a trained language model more mature, in the sense of a better organized latent space, by looking only at the geometry of that space?
