An analyst model reads a fixed-size digest of topological descriptors of the layers of a frozen target and proposes a transformation, which closed-form ridge regression writes into the feed-forward weights without gradient training. Using lightweight models, 126 runs show that category purity rose on unseen words and sentences, benchmark accuracy stayed within sampling error, and the analyst matched a category-aware heuristic with gentler edits. In a five-model round robin approach, 16 pairs raised purity and four left the target unchanged. The results provide a baseline for future work on LLM-guided, training-free model editing without using raw weights.
  </details>

- **[Programmable Semantic Interventions: Learning Causal Carriers from Behavioral Programs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21307)**  `arXiv:2611.21307`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Paolo Muratore, Mackenzie Weygandt Mathis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Causal abstraction explains a frozen network by aligning its activations with the variables of a simpler, interpretable model, and checking that interventions on the two agree. Finding such an alignment currently requires a counterfactual dataset: for every pair of prompts, the answer the network ought to give once the variable has been changed. In practice, those labels are produced by an executable algorithm for the behavior, written before studying the model supposed to implement it. However, the criterion these methods optimize only leverages the answer an intervention should produce. Here, we develop an approach that lets the investigator define that relationship directly: the answers the variable can produce, and which of them an intervention should yield. We call this interface a semantic program and introduce Programmable Semantic Interventions (PSI): a low-rank activation subspace learned so that interchanging it changes the model's answer as the program declares, with the expected answers read from the frozen model itself. On the MIB benchmark PSI performs on par with supervised alignment at matched data and training budget, without curated pairs, and it needs no ground-truth labels; while on natural science questions with gold labels withheld, it tracks the model's own answers better than a subspace trained on gold labels. The declaration also fixes a coordinate system with known semantics, enabling donor-free steering: jointly training PSI for both localization and control realizes 94.8% of held-out requests for answers the investigator chooses, without the need for donor prompts.
  </details>

- **[Conformal Expert Pools: Training-Free Probabilistic Time-Series Forecasting Beyond Seasonality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21267)**  `arXiv:2611.21267`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Valery Manokhin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Conformal Seasonal Pools (CSP) showed that a forecaster with no training at all, built only from past values at the same point in the season and from past forecast errors, significantly outperformed DeepNPTS, Amazon's learned non-parametric forecaster. We propose Conformal Expert Pools (CEP), a training-free successor. CEP replaces the single seasonal-naive forecast by a pool of simple forecasters, builds its predictive distribution from their past forecast errors, and adapts both the pool and the errors to the series being forecast. No model is trained and no parameter is learned across series. On the CSP benchmark of 232 datasets and 24,502 forecast windows, CEP is better than CSP on 78% of datasets by CRPS, on 84% by MSIS and on 83% by Winkler score; on a typical dataset its CRPS is 14% lower and its MSIS 23% lower (both p < 1e-22, paired Wilcoxon test), and coverage of 95% intervals rises from 90.1% to 92.2%. CEP has a lower CRPS than DeepNPTS on 88% of datasets, than NPTS on 89% and than SeasonalNPTS on 83%. The improvement persists on dataset families outside the development set and on 36 additional fev-bench files. We also test the published claims that Laplace, an online ensemble forecaster, "beats (almost) everything" and "beats" CSP. On 228 datasets at multi-step horizons the claims do not hold: CSP outperforms Laplace on 60% of datasets by CRPS and on 64% by interval score, and Laplace ranks sixth of seven methods, with the widest intervals of all (69% wider than CEP's). Its point forecast is biased low and on 54% of datasets is less accurate than seasonal naive, and from a cold start it takes about 7,700 times longer than CSP. CEP is better than Laplace on 82% of datasets by CRPS and on 80% by MSIS, with intervals about 40% narrower, and runs 271 times faster.
  </details>

- **[SemiNovo: Learning Beyond Search-Identified Spectra for De Novo Peptide Sequencing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21079)**  `arXiv:2611.21079`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Pan Liu, Dongxin Lyu, Yusen Tan, Yifan Li, Hai-tao Yu, Yunhua Zhong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sensitive peptide identification is fundamental to protein characterization and novel protein discovery, for which de novo peptide sequencing (DNPS) from tandem mass spectra provides an important database-independent approach. Although deep learning has substantially advanced de novo sequencing, existing models are predominantly trained on high-confidence peptide-spectrum matches obtained through database search. This search-conditioned supervision excludes a large fraction of unidentified spectra, creating a distribution mismatch between the selectively labeled training data and the complete spectrum stream encountered at inference time, thereby limiting peptide identification performance. To address this challenge, we propose SemiNovo, a semi-supervised framework that learns jointly from identified and unlabeled spectra. SemiNovo first constructs DarkSpec, a large-scale quality-controlled corpus of unlabeled spectra collected from diverse public proteomics projects, thereby extending training beyond search-identified spectra and mitigating the resulting shift in DNPS supervision. It then combines stepwise mass-constrained autoregressive decoding with cumulative path confidence to derive reliable supervision from unlabeled spectra. Moreover, SemiNovo introduces head-wise chemical-evidence gating to improve spectral evidence aggregation under noisy and incomplete fragmentation. Experiments show that SemiNovo performs favorably against state-of-the-art methods on multiple benchmarks. Code and dataset are publicly available.
  </details>

- **[Explaining Cross-Label Decision Structure in Multi-Output Tree Ensembles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20996)**  `arXiv:2611.20996`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Sylvio Barbon Junior, Hiago F. F. Oliveira, Rafael Figueira Gonçalves, Jessica Fernandes Lopes, Ricardo Cerri_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A multi-output tree ensemble predicts several binary labels through decision paths that it largely shares across those labels, yet the attribution methods commonly used to explain such models treat each output in isolation. We propose multi-sink Decision Predicate Graphs (ms-DPGs), which extend decision predicate graphs with one sink per label: each leaf contributes to the sink of every label its in-bag positive count for that label. On this representation we define Shared Predicate Flow (SPF), the share of a label's decision mass that passes through conditions active for both labels of a pair, and a contracted view of the graph in which label-specific lifts indicate whether a shared condition raises the probability of both labels or of one at the expense of the other. Controlled experiments show that SPF captures shared decision conditions rather than mere feature overlap. On nine multi-label benchmarks, native multi-output forests share substantially more decision structure than Binary Relevance on eight, including five of the six on which the two are at predictive parity, even at equal hyperparameters, whereas per-label TreeSHAP rankings register the change of architecture without showing whether more is shared. Because a native forest routes every label through the same splits, SPF describes the fitted model rather than the data, and on eight of the nine benchmarks it rises when the feature-label signal is removed; a high SPF is therefore not evidence of shared predictive information. ms-DPG offers a global, predicate-level account of how a multi-output ensemble reuses decision conditions across its labels.
  </details>

- **[Auditing World-Model Explanations in Retail Forecasting: Faithfulness, Accuracy and Price Response](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20811)**  `arXiv:2611.20811`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Kathik Iyer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Forecasting models that read the price and promotion plan explain each forecast as a baseline, what would sell with nothing planned, plus the lift the plan adds. We test such explanations with eight checks, six with a pass rule fixed before any result, and find that passing them does not make an explanation right. In generated store data with known effects, a world model passes the checks of construction, stability, price direction and agreement with sales and with LightGBM, yet its lifts are 1.33 to 1.40 times the truth when merchants plan into high-demand weeks, and 1.23 to 1.42 times in eight variants of that world. It still picks the right weeks to promote, but stock ordered to its lift is off by 0.44 to 0.48 of the true lift. Its stated response to a lasting regular-price change is 4.7 to 6.5 times the true -0.5, and only a measured cause finds this. On Dominick's, 38 episodes in which a store's regular prices sat apart from its zone peers give a noisy benchmark: a pooled 8-week response of -0.33 (95% interval -0.68 to 0.01). For the final tuned world models of our companion paper on nine data sets, baseline and lift add up under each model's own plan, but a deeper price cut lowers the forecast in 29% to 83% of item-week steps on Dominick's (23% to 80% within prices seen in training), and the world model and LightGBM both state about twice the measured response. Across 703 configurations from the settings searches, checks passed were not related to forecast accuracy (Spearman 0.02, 95% interval -0.02 to 0.05); with no known answer for those versions, this concerns accuracy, while whether an explanation is right is measured only in the generated data.
  </details>

- **[LINK: Lightweight and Inductive Node-to-Node Propagation Rule Learning for Traffic Kriging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20521)**  `arXiv:2611.20521`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _You Shang, Yu-Kwan Ching, Weifeng Su, Guanyao Li, Ruiyuan Li, Gary Shueng-Han Chan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spatio-temporal traffic kriging aims to reconstruct traffic time series at unobserved locations with no historical traffic observations by leveraging spatial and temporal dependencies across the road network. Existing Graph Neural Network (GNN)-based approaches primarily rely on neighborhood aggregation to capture spatial dependencies, thereby modeling traffic interactions implicitly and making it difficult to accurately recover traffic magnitudes at locations without historical observations. To address this limitation, we propose LINK, a Lightweight and Inductive Node-to-node propagation rule learning paradigm for traffic Kriging. LINK introduces three key designs: 1) a propagation-centric traffic kriging paradigm that explicitly learns a shared node-to-node transition rule; 2) a context-aware Spectral Propagation Network that performs traffic-state propagation in both the spectral and time domains; and 3) TraffiKrig, a realistic traffic kriging benchmark that preserves heterogeneous road-segment characteristics and road-specific metadata while enabling systematic comparison of representative kriging methods under a unified metadata-enhanced setting. Extensive experiments demonstrate that LINK achieves strong reconstruction accuracy and computational efficiency. Compared with the strongest baseline on each subset, LINK achieves an average MAE reduction of 54.7% over original baselines. LINK also achieves substantial efficiency improvements, reducing training time by 66.5% and inference time by 86.6% compared with the fastest competing methods. Zero-shot experiments further demonstrate the transferability of the learned propagation rule across regions.
  </details>

- **[Explainable Federated Learning via Global-Local Attribution Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20445)**  `arXiv:2611.20445`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Dawood Wasif, Terrence J. Moore, Chang-Tien Lu, Jin-Hee Cho_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Federated learning enables on-device training without centralizing data, yet existing systems still struggle to provide explanations that are both locally faithful and globally consistent under strict privacy and bandwidth constraints. Prior approaches either keep explanations siloed across clients, transmit heavy or sensitive artifacts, or replace expressive task models with interpretable surrogates that sacrifice accuracy. We propose xFedAlign, a model-agnostic framework that decouples task optimization in parameter space from explanation coordination in a compact group space. Each client distills a lightweight surrogate to produce private, per-class top-k attribution artifacts, which are robustly aggregated by the server into a Global Explanation Prior that softly aligns client explanations without constraining task learning. Across image, text, and tabular benchmarks with IID and non-IID partitions, xFedAlign matches FedAvg accuracy while consistently reducing explanation drift and improving deletion and insertion AUC relative to Local-XAI, FedAttr-Agg, and Fed-XAI, with only a few kilobytes of additional communication per round. Privacy and robustness evaluations further demonstrate reduced membership inference advantage and increased resistance to attribution poisoning, enabling consistent and trustworthy explanations in federated learning.
  </details>

- **[Residual-Tail-Informed Flow Matching for Time Series Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20334)**  `arXiv:2611.20334`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Shyam Sundar Saravanan, Tie Luo, Sanjay Madria_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time series anomaly detection (TSAD) models are often trained on nominal data using average-risk objectives, which emphasize dominant normal patterns. However, real time series can contain rare but valid nominal regimes that are underrepresented in the training distribution. We show that data-space flow matching can over-penalize such rare-normal windows and assign them anomaly scores comparable to those of true anomalies. To address this, we propose Residual-Tail-Informed Flow Matching (RT-FM), an unsupervised framework that uses a temporal variational autoencoder to estimate residual latent tailness and converts it into bounded, warmup-annealed weights for training a data-space flow matching model. Experiments on seven TSAD benchmarks show that RT-FM improves rare-normal coverage while preserving strong anomaly discrimination, substantially reducing false alarms on such windows and resulting in improved aggregate detection performance. The code is available at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[MT-RGCN-DTI: A Multi-Task Relational Graph Convolutional Network for Drug-Target Interaction Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20109)**  `arXiv:2611.20109`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Dheeran Sankaran, R Yadunandan, Daniele Santoni, Nimisha Ghosh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Prediction of drug-target interaction (DTI) has become a very crucial part of drug discovery. With the increase in the data volume, it has become very important to improve prediction accuracy by integrating multiple data sources. In this regard, utilising multiple resources in the form of heterogeneous information network to address DTI prediction can be rewarding. In this work, we propose a heterogeneous information network based multi-task DTI prediction model which leverages the advantages of relation graph convolutional network (RGCN). To achieve this, the model employs drug-target interaction network, drug-drug similarity network and protein-protein similarity network to construct a heterogeneous information network. Moreover, we use a knowledge graph embedding scoring functions to model interactions between drug and targets, specifically DistMult and ComplEx as decoders over the learned node representations. We performed extensive experiments on multiple benchmark datasets and compared with state-of-the-art methods. We observed that the proposed model shows competitive performance, thereby confirming the effectiveness of our approach. The codes and datasets are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Federated Dataset Simulation: Inducing Label-Free Heterogeneity Across Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19975)**  `arXiv:2611.19975`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Vasilis Siomos, Lam Ngo, Jonathan Passerat-Palmbach, Giacomo Tarroni_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Federated Learning enables collaborative model training across decentralized clients without sharing raw data. While the field has seen rapid growth, the datasets underpinning FL evaluation have not kept pace: real federated datasets remain scarce, and most studies rely on small-scale centralized classification datasets partitioned via Dirichlet label skew, a one-dimensional proxy for the multi-dimensional heterogeneity present in real federations. Moreover, label-based partitioning is by construction inapplicable to non-classification tasks such as segmentation and detection, for which no principled simulation method exists. We cast the conversion of centralized datasets into federated benchmarks as the task of Federated Dataset Simulation (FDS), and decompose it into a representation function $\Phi$ and an assignment mechanism $\pi$. We provide two label-free instantiations -- geometric partitioning via pretrained model embeddings and semantic partitioning via vision-language model-derived criteria -- applicable to classification and dense prediction tasks alike. We evaluate across four datasets spanning classification, segmentation, and detection, and show that both methods induce heterogeneity that matches or exceeds that of label-skew partitioning, with measurable impact on downstream FL performance. We release fds, a modular open-source package for end-to-end federated dataset simulation, heterogeneity measurement, and FL training, atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[BadGCL: Towards Persistent Backdoor Attack on Graph Continual Learning Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19656)**  `arXiv:2611.19656`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Dong Li, Yufei Han, Xintao Wu, Xujiang Zhao, Wei Cheng, Chen Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Backdoor attacks pose a persistent security threat to machine learning systems, yet their behavior under graph continual learning (GCL) remains underexplored. Conventional graph backdoor attacks often lose effectiveness as models are updated across successive tasks, creating the impression that continual updates naturally weaken malicious associations. This work reveals a contrasting vulnerability: knowledge-preservation mechanisms in GCL can also preserve early injected backdoors when the poisoned update is compatible with the clean-task signal being retained. For graph data, this risk is amplified by structural triggers, which are embedded into the message-passing paths of graph neural networks (GNNs) and can induce stable representation shifts across tasks. To expose this risk, we propose BadGCL, a model-agnostic framework for persistent backdoor attacks under a strict early-stage intervention setting. BadGCL learns topology-aware structural triggers using a gradient mutual-information objective that couples poisoned gradients with clean-task optimization signals, together with a propagation consistency objective that stabilizes trigger-induced effects through graph message passing. We provide theoretical analysis showing that gradient compatibility and propagation stability jointly support long-term backdoor persistence under benign continual updates. Extensive experiments across task-IL and class-IL protocols, four GCL benchmarks, and diverse victim systems show that BadGCL achieves persistent attack effectiveness with limited clean-utility degradation, substantially outperforming graph and continual-learning backdoor baselines and remaining robust against representative defenses.
  </details>

- **[LoopFill: Pipelined Multi-Token Speculative Decoding for Looped Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19649)**  `arXiv:2611.19649`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Siqi Zhu, Suozhi Huang, Yi Zhao, Jiaxuan You_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Looped Transformers improve parameter efficiency by repeatedly applying the same Transformer layers. During autoregressive decoding, however, generating each token requires several sequential passes through these layers, leading to high decoding latency. Pipelined self-speculative methods use shallow-loop predictions to process tokens at different loops in the same batch, but each token must reach the drafting depth before the next can enter the pipeline. We introduce LoopFill, a training-free speculative decoding algorithm for looped LLM that combines multi-token suffix drafts with shallow-loop predictions in a single draft tree. Both drafters contribute to one tree, so a suffix lookup can immediately extend a shallow prediction and several future tokens can enter the pipeline together. The shared block computes the highest-scoring draft candidates at different loop depths in one batch, and full-depth verification preserves the model's autoregressive output distribution. Across seven benchmarks, LoopFill's average speedup over greedy autoregressive decoding is $5.85\times$ on Ouro-2.6B and $5.13\times$ on Huginn-3.5B. The speedups exceed those of LoopAhead, the strongest baseline, by \(40\%\) on Ouro-2.6B and \(25\%\) on Huginn-3.5B.
  </details>

- **[Robust Well-Log Reconstruction Across Point Gaps and Whole Wells via Boundary-Gated Soft-Thresholding Kalman Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19625)**  `arXiv:2611.19625`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yongan Zhang, Zhihan Qiu, Yizheng Li, Youzhuang Sun_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Missing wireline logs hinder subsurface characterization. Deep reconstruction networks suffer from gap-boundary steps and cross-basin shifts, while interpolation and Gaussian smoothers struggle with long intervals and outliers. We propose boundary-gated soft-thresholding Kalman fusion (ST-KF), unifying multiresolution wavelet shrinkage, distance-gated residual prior learning, and closed-form robust Kalman smoothing with learning--inference decoupling. Across two public benchmarks totaling 263 wells in the Norwegian North Sea and New Zealand's Taranaki Basin, ST-KF ranks first over 0.15--100-m anchored gaps with 0.09% parameter overhead. Against the strongest of nine baselines, pooled RMSE falls by 5.0%--8.3% for bulk density and 6.4%--15.6% for compressional slowness; against the four strongest baselines, reductions are 0.7%--4.1% for neutron porosity and 1.3%--2.9% for photoelectric factor. Ablations and sensitivity analyses confirm operator synergy and stable performance: removing state-space fusion increases short-gap errors by up to 58.8%, while zero-shot transfer cuts anchored errors by 14%--68%. The results show that local borehole continuity transfers across basins whereas cross-property mappings shift with regional geology, supporting measurement-guided log reconstruction.
  </details>

- **[Well-Log Reconstruction with Pre-trained Geological Knowledge: A Knowledge-Conditioned Large-Language-Model Generative Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19624)**  `arXiv:2611.19624`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yongan Zhang, Yitao Yu, Yizheng Li, An Gong, Youzhuang Sun_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Missing well-log intervals make subsurface reconstruction ill-posed. Existing numerical regression models lack geological prior knowledge to constrain ambiguous inverse mappings. We propose GeoLog-Agent, a knowledge-conditioned generative agent using pre-trained large language models (LLMs) as a source of geological priors. A universal Geo-Harness translates LLM stratigraphic reasoning into formation-anchored petrophysical trajectories, feature-wise linear modulation, and conditional adversarial constraints. On the Norwegian North Sea benchmark, GeoLog-Agent achieves a mean $R^2$ of 0.9307 and a peak $R^2$ of 0.9713. It exceeds the nine-baseline average $R^2$ of 0.8387 by 0.0920, with a 30.97% average RMSE reduction, and exceeds the strongest baseline by 0.0274--0.0498 in $R^2$, with 16.25%--24.05% lower RMSE. Ablations confirm the effectiveness and synergy of geological prior reasoning, multi-scale dilated generation, and conditional adversarial modules. Transferring the coupling mechanism to six conventional deep learning models improves their $R^2$ by 0.0162--0.0789 with cross-well stability. These results demonstrate how foundation-model domain reasoning can constrain deep generative reconstruction of missing petrophysical measurements.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking the State of AI for Crop Yield Forecasting: A Global Assessment Across Modeling Paradigms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19330)**  `arXiv:2611.19330`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Michiel Kallenberg, Philip Janz, Christoph Jörges, Vageesh Saxena, Pratishtha Poudel, Mohammed Musthafa Rafi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurate crop yield forecasting is essential for food security, agricultural policy, and commodity markets. Although statistical, process-based, and machine learning approaches have all been proposed, their relative strengths remain poorly understood due to the lack of large-scale, standardized comparisons. We address this gap through a comprehensive benchmark of five modeling paradigms -- statistical baselines, process-based models, conventional feature-engineered machine learning, deep sequence models, and tabular foundation models -- using the CY-Bench benchmark spanning 63 country-crop datasets (>12,000 administrative regions) across six continents. Beyond conventional pooled accuracy metrics, we introduce a multi-dimensional evaluation framework that separately quantifies overall, spatial, temporal, and anomaly prediction skill. Across multiple crops and countries, feature-engineered machine learning and tabular foundation models achieve the strongest overall predictive performance. Data-driven models reduce NRMSE in 72% of countries growing maize and 78% of countries growing wheat, with median reductions of 8.8% and 11.9%, respectively. Across all modeling paradigms, spatial prediction is substantially easier than temporal and anomaly prediction. While data-driven models markedly improve temporal and anomaly prediction over statistical baselines, accurately forecasting year-to-year yield variability remains challenging. These findings suggest that future progress in large-scale crop yield forecasting will depend on the improved characterization of the environmental and management factors driving inter-annual yield variability, founded on effective representations for crop yield forecasting systems that support agricultural decision-making, early-warning applications, and food security planning
  </details>

- **[Neural Dual Bounds: Valid-by-Construction JGLP Warm-Starts for MAP and Constrained MAP](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19218)**  `arXiv:2611.19218`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Akshay Vyas, Shivvrat Arya, Brij Malhotra, Tahrima Rahman, Vibhav Gogate, Nicholas Ruozzi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present Neural Dual Bounds, a calibration-free framework that produces deterministic upper bounds for MAP inference in probabilistic graphical models using a single neural-network forward pass. The framework trains a network to output the edge messages of a mini-bucket style upper bound on a given join-graph decomposition in a self-supervised manner by directly minimizing a smooth dual objective. The output of this network can be used at test time to warm-start a standard JGLP optimizer. Across diverse benchmarks, this warm start yields dual gaps that are orders of magnitude tighter than those from zero initialization, with the largest gains in the early-iteration regime. We also apply our framework for the constrained MAP (CMPE) task, where neural warm starts with test-time adaptation are comparable to the adaptive baseline on Bayesian networks and achieve the tightest dual gaps, by one to two orders of magnitude, on the grid and segmentation families.
  </details>

- **[The Critic Can Hurt: On the Safety of Critics in LLM-Guided AutoML Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19037)**  `arXiv:2611.19037`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Gaoyuan Du, Xiaoyang Liu, Rex Zhou, Dengdu Jiang, Rong Fu, Meng Ding, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM critics increasingly steer iterative search in agentic AutoML, prompt optimization, and self-refinement--evaluated for whether they help, but rarely for whether they can hurt. We isolate the critic's marginal effect through a four-arm audit and characterize the loop's tolerance through controlled fault injection. We show a naive 8B critic drives search below the no-critic random-search floor on 7 of 8 tabular datasets, losing up to 0.245 AUC in the worst case; the magnitude varies strongly with dataset, frontier, and budget. When: stronger critics attenuate the loss on the evaluated suite, without establishing a universal capability threshold. Why: controlled fault injection shows that non-directional errors are nearly costless under best-so-far search, whereas directional errors consume finite search opportunities; an idealized effective-sample calculation yields a necessary condition, $q(m-1) > \beta$, not a deployment certificate. How: a simple observable validation gate and a learned detector can reduce realized loss, but neither is guaranteed to preserve the full-budget no-critic baseline. We will release CRITFAULT, to our knowledge the first benchmark for detecting harmful critic feedback in AutoML. A critic is not a free addition: audit before trusting, and gate when capability or verification is insufficient.
  </details>

- **[Self-Improvement Memory Enables Continual Recursive Self-Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18856)**  `arXiv:2611.18856`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Xin Yu, Fei Xia, Jiaqi Pan, Youbao Tang, Chengzhi Liu, Adams Yu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Harness optimization is often studied with a fixed policy model and benchmark. When either changes, the optimizer must decide which parts of an evolved harness to reuse or revise, but the harness alone may not explain why earlier edits were made or how they performed. In this work, we study Continual Recursive Self-Improvement (CRSI), a setting for continuing existing harness-optimization methods across compatible benchmarks or policy models. We propose Self-Improvement Memory (SIM) for the harness optimizer, rather than the task-solving policy. SIM groups edits by recurring harness problem and records their rationale, predicted and observed outcomes, and evaluation context. New attempts extend this history as optimization continues. In our experiments across four domains, SIM achieves higher final scores than harness-only and root-cause-only continuation in cross-dataset and pairwise cross-model comparisons. Across ten independent source runs in coding, paired continuations with SIM improve final scores by an average of 2.36 points over harness-only continuation ($p=0.003$).
  </details>

- **[What to Practice Next? Latent Profile-Guided Task Selection for Skill-Evolving LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18818)**  `arXiv:2611.18818`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Qijin Zhang, Hanzhen Wang, Yan Huang, Zhongxiang Dai, Shuang Qiu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Task selection shapes the experiences available for skill evolution in LLM agents, yet fixed practice schedules overlook differences in skill requirements and evolving task value. Most existing methods, however, rely on fixed schedules or scalar measures of progress, leaving nonstationary skill demands unaddressed. We propose SPG-Select, a framework for allocating limited practice budget using a multidimensional profile inferred from binary outcomes via MIRT. The framework predicts surrogate progress, defined as the difference between consecutive profile estimates, and prioritizes progress in dimensions with larger estimated proficiency gaps. Our SPG-Select-R policy combines these predictions with uncertainty-based exploration and periodic restarting to accommodate nonstationarity. We evaluate SPG-Select on ALFWorld and WebShop with three skill-evolution backends. In the reported runs, SPG-Select-R achieves higher mean held-out terminal success than uniform sampling in all six backend-benchmark combinations, with gains of 1.49-13.18 percentage points on ALFWorld and 0.93-3.13 points on WebShop. Under a predictable linear surrogate model and boundedness assumptions, we establish a high-probability dynamic pseudo-regret bound for surrogate task allocation in terms of parameter path variation.
  </details>

- **[LEAP-LLM: Preserving lead-time structure for question-conditioned ECG interpretation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18809)**  `arXiv:2611.18809`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Aakash Agarwal, Yidan Shen, Xin Fu, Renjie Hu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The electrocardiogram (ECG) records cardiac electrical activity across multiple leads and is used for detecting cardiac abnormalities. Recent ECG-language models enable large language models (LLMs) to interpret cardiac signals and answer clinically relevant questions. However, existing ECG-language models typically encode the ECG independently of the clinical question, forcing the ECG representation to retain broad and potentially irrelevant signal information. Moreover, these models often aggregate information across leads which can obscure lead-specific details needed for a particular question. To tackle these limitations, we introduce LEAP-LLM, an ECG-LLM architecture for Lead-aware ECG Alignment with Prompt conditioning. LEAP-LLM uses the clinical question to guide ECG feature extraction while preserving explicit lead and temporal structure. Task-trained instruction representations distill information from the question and guide two complementary branches. The Lead branch captures question-relevant temporal patterns within each ECG lead, while the Temporal branch summarizes relevant information across leads at each temporal position. Together, they provide the LLM with compact, question-relevant ECG information while preserving lead and temporal structure. LEAP-LLM reduces modality-token count by 89.0% and prefill FLOPs by 7.2x on average relative to other ECG-LLMs baselines. Across four ECG benchmarks, LEAP-LLM achieves better performance, including a 3.4-percentage-point improvement in G12EC over the strongest prior ECG-specific method. We further show that the model adaptively emphasizes the clinically relevant anatomical lead groups for different cardiac abnormalities, with lead importance patterns consistent with established diagnostic knowledge, providing interpretable evidence that the predictions are supported by clinically meaningful ECG structure.
  </details>

- **[RSIMed: Scaling Medical Foundation Models beyond Limited Clinical Data via Retrieval-Grounded Self-Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18646)**  `arXiv:2611.18646`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Wei Dai, Qianchu Liu, Guanghui Qin, Xing Han, Tristan Naumann, Sheng Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Rare diseases are hard to diagnose, and the labeled clinical records needed to train models for them are few, private, and slow to extend, since a specialist must read every case. A model that writes its own training tasks could learn beyond these records, but no program can check a model-written diagnosis, and a wrong answer key corrupts training. To tackle this dilemma between data scarcity and difficulty of data annotations, we introduce RSIMed, Recursive Self-Improvement in Medicine, one of the first self-evolving pipelines in which a task proposer co-evolves in context with a diagnostic solver on real clinical records. Seeded by training admissions, a frozen copy of the solver writes each clinical question from retrieved medical evidence, so its answer rests on a source, and steers topics and difficulty toward what the solver still gets wrong. On rare-disease diagnosis from MIMIC-IV admissions, the accuracy of RSIMed-27B grows linearly with training compute, while a static pool of generated tasks collapses. Without any external model, RSIMed-27B and RSIMed-9B reach 0.516 and 0.488 diagnostic accuracy, above every off-the-shelf GPT-5.6 setting, whose best reaches 0.399 at 38 times the inference cost of RSIMed-27B. Tasks written by four external teachers, including GPT-5.5, train a less accurate diagnostician than the solver's own. Trained only on diagnosis, RSIMed-RL-27B also improves on the physician-written tasks of HealthBench Professional, from 0.291 to 0.303 in adjusted score.
  </details>

- **[Minari: Infrastructure for Collaborative Reinforcement Learning Datasets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18644)**  `arXiv:2611.18644`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Omar G. Younis, Rodrigo Perez-Vicente, John U. Balis, Alex Davey, Will Dudley, Jordan K. Terry_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Offline reinforcement learning (RL) depends on shared datasets, but sharing them is still hard. Each benchmark ships its own format, documentation is thin, and most datasets cannot be regenerated once the environment code moves on. We introduce Minari, an open-source Python library for creating, sharing, and loading RL datasets. Its API follows Gymnasium, so recording a dataset means wrapping an environment and stepping through it as usual. From there, users can attach metadata, filter, combine, and shuffle datasets, and publish or download them with one command through the HuggingFace Hub. Every dataset stores the environment specification, seeds, applied wrappers, and pinned package requirements needed to rebuild the environment and regenerate the data. TorchRL, AgileRL, and d3rlpy already load Minari datasets natively, so switching datasets in those libraries takes one line of code. Minari also hosts 338 reproducible datasets (350K trajectories, 55M steps) covering robotic locomotion and manipulation, maze and grid navigation, language-conditioned instruction following, Atari, and web navigation, including a full regeneration of D4RL with public generation scripts. We report baseline scores for six offline RL algorithms on the regenerated D4RL and MuJoCo datasets.
  </details>

- **[FedFit: Federated Dynamic Sparse Training via Fisher Information scoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18643)**  `arXiv:2611.18643`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Meng Bi, Hong Huang, Jinlong Song, Charles Wang, Chengming Hu, Xi Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cross-device Federated Learning (FL) is frequently bottlenecked by the prohibitive memory and communication costs of training deep neural networks on resource-constrained edge hardware. While federated dynamic sparse training aims to alleviate these costs by adjusting sparse structures during training, existing methods rely on magnitude-based heuristics that are fundamentally ill-suited for the non-convergent, heterogeneous environments inherent to FL. To address this challenge, we propose FedFit, a federated dynamic sparse training framework that replaces simple heuristics with optimization-centric criteria for structure adjustment. By leveraging a second-order approximation of the loss landscape via the Fisher Information Matrix, FedFit enables precise and efficient structure adjustment without the overhead of explicit Hessian computation. Empirical evaluations across computer vision and natural language processing benchmarks demonstrate that FedFit significantly narrows the sparse-to-dense accuracy gap, outperforming state-of-the-art methods while maintaining high communication efficiency. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Channel Selection for Mixture-of-Experts Decoding Needs No Calibration Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37386)**  `arXiv:2610.37386`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Geunhyoung Kim, Sung Ju Hwang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models are bottlenecked by loading routed-expert weights from memory during interactive decoding at small batch sizes. Because most intermediate channels in a routed expert contribute little to any single token, channel selectors predict which channels matter and read only their weights, typically keeping channels above a threshold calibrated on offline data. We show that channel selection needs no calibration data. First, a calibrated threshold does not fix how much a selector reads, because the number of channels passing it varies across inputs; Prox with thresholds calibrated for an 18% read budget actually reads only 12.9% on average across our benchmarks. Second, top-k keeps each token's k highest-scoring channels and needs no calibration data. At the calibrated budget, a selector's top-k version matches or beats its threshold version on the same score in 40 of 43 comparisons at 13% and 18% budgets, including all 23 on generation tasks. The top-k versions also avoid the repetition-loop collapse that calibrated thresholds cause on Gemma 4. Calibrating a threshold thus provides neither of the two things selectors use it for: a known read budget and a consistent accuracy gain at that budget. We furthermore introduce a reader built entirely from the model's weights, which screens channels with a ternary sketch and recovers the skipped ones with a low-rank map. Across three post-trained MoE models, our reader retains 98.1% of full-model accuracy at 17.8% of routed-expert reads, with Triton kernels running MoE blocks 1.60 to 1.90x faster than optimized vLLM at batch one and up to 2.69x at larger batches on the same three models.
  </details>

- **[Constrained latent state modeling: A unifying perspective on representation learning under competing constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.15995)**  `arXiv:2605.15995`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Gwenolé Quellec_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning latent representations from temporal, multimodal, and partially observed data requires specifying what information a latent state should retain, discard, and organize. Existing approaches encode these requirements through heterogeneous objectives, making methods difficult to compare and learned representations difficult to interpret. We propose Constrained Latent State Modeling (CLSM), a conceptual framework that characterizes latent states through six complementary properties: predictive sufficiency, minimality, temporal coherence, observation compatibility, invariance to nuisance factors, and structural constraints. CLSM separates these properties from the surrogate objectives used to induce them and from the diagnostics used to evaluate them, and clarifies how combinations of constraints can improve identifiability by restricting the space of admissible representations. We reinterpret major representation-learning families through this common design space and illustrate the framework with a controlled synthetic benchmark. The experiments show how objectives produce distinct latent organizations and empirical trade-offs depending on the prediction target, surrogate formulation, parameterization, and optimization. Companion repository containing the reference implementation, reproducible experiments, documentation, and model cards:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Representation Before Training: A Practical Benchmark for Generative Medical Event Model Tokenization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.16775)**  `arXiv:2604.16775`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Inhyeok Lee, Luke Solo, Michael C. Burkhart, Bashar Ramadan, Sahil Sethi, Sarah Jabbour, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative medical event models use tokenized sequences of patient timelines as input, but practical guidance on the many decisions around tokenization is limited because most studies keep the tokenizer fixed while scaling architecture or data. We benchmark quantization granularity, reference-range anchoring, code--value fusion, numeric and temporal encodings, and schema harmonization across 156 models. Using both Llama-3.2-style and Qwen3-style architectures, all models were trained on full MIMIC-IV hospitalizations from three initialization seeds under a shared training recipe, and linear probes evaluated frozen 24-hour representations across eight binary and regression outcome families during hours 24--48. Fused tokens pairing codes with value deciles produced statistically significant gains in six of eight families over unfused deciles, including all four regression families ($+0.032$ to $+0.114$ Spearman correlation) and vital-signs classification ($+0.033$ AUROC), with 38 of 48 comparisons significant across all six quantization schemes (seven of eight families). Next-token training organized unfused and fused centile embeddings into ordered trajectories and made clinical reference ranges linearly decodable in unfused centiles (93--97\% accuracy). Neither reference-range anchoring nor finer quantization consistently improved 24-hour probes. Event order and admission-relative RoPE shortened inputs while marginally raising all eight family means over explicit time tokens (five and four statistically significant). Mapping native input to the Common Longitudinal Intensive Care Unit Data Format (CLIF) cut training tokens by 71.4\% and vocabulary size by 97.8\%, with statistically significant gains in four of eight families. These findings show that compact, expert-curated tokenizations can outperform more granular and continuous alternatives under a shared training recipe.
  </details>

- **[Revisiting Chebyshev Polynomial and Anisotropic RBF Models for Tabular Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.22422)**  `arXiv:2602.22422`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Luciano Gerber, Huw Lloyd_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Smooth-basis models such as Chebyshev polynomial regressors and radial basis function (RBF) networks are well established in numerical analysis. Their continuously differentiable prediction surfaces suit surrogate optimisation, sensitivity analysis, and other settings where the response varies gradually with inputs. Despite these properties, smooth models seldom appear in tabular regression, where tree ensembles dominate. We ask whether they can compete, benchmarking models across 55 regression datasets organised by application domain.We develop an anisotropic RBF network with data-driven centre placement and gradient-based width optimisation, a ridge-regularised Chebyshev polynomial regressor, and a smooth-tree hybrid (Chebyshev model tree), with all three released as scikit-learn-compatible packages. We benchmark these against tree ensembles, a bin-based additive model, a pre-trained transformer and standard baselines, evaluating accuracy alongside the train-test gap and prediction stability under retraining.The transformer ranks first on accuracy across most datasets, but its GPU dependence, prediction cost, and dataset-size limits constrain deployment in the CPU-based settings common across applied science and industry. Among CPU-viable models, smooth models and tree ensembles are statistically tied on accuracy at the medium data scale that characterises most of our datasets (up to roughly 50,000 samples and 50 features). In a full-scale check on eight datasets, XGBoost achieved the highest mean accuracy, with the Chebyshev model tree close behind. At matched accuracy, smooth-basis models tend to exhibit tighter train-test gaps, an empirical proxy for prediction stability under retraining. We recommend including smooth-basis models among the candidate models for tabular regression, particularly when downstream use benefits from smooth prediction surfaces.
  </details>

- **[Probing the Trajectories of Reasoning Traces in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.23163)**  `arXiv:2601.23163`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Marthe Ballon, Brecht F. Verbeken, Vincent Ginis, Andres Algaba_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) increasingly solve difficult problems by producing reasoning traces before emitting a final response. However, it remains unclear how accuracy and decision commitment evolve along a reasoning trajectory, and whether intermediate trace segments provide answer-relevant information beyond generic length or stylistic effects. Here, we propose a trajectory-probing protocol for evaluating how a reasoning model's predicted answer evolves along a partial reasoning trace. The protocol (1) generates a model's full reasoning trace, (2) truncates it at fixed token-percentiles, and (3) injects each partial trace back into the model, measuring the model's induced answer distribution. We apply the protocol to seven open-source reasoning models (Qwen3-4B/8B/14B, gpt-oss-20b/120b, DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B and Gemma-4-31B-it) across four benchmarks (GPQA Diamond, MMLU-Pro, Omni-MATH-2 and CompMath). The protocol reveals that accuracy and decision commitment generally increase as the percentage of provided reasoning tokens grows, and length, form, and token-identity controls confirm that gains stem from instance-specific semantic content rather than context length or generic reasoning style effects. Furthermore, cross-model probing shows that even stronger models frequently repeat the source model's errors when given its partial reasoning trace, while allowing further reasoning substantially improves recovery. Together, these results provide a reproducible, model-agnostic measurement of reasoning trace dynamics that generalizes across answer formats and can reveal model-specific failure modes invisible to aggregate accuracy.
  </details>

- **[Space Group Conditional Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.23822)**  `arXiv:2509.23822`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Omri Puny, Yaron Lipman, Benjamin Kurt Miller_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Inorganic crystals are periodic, highly-symmetric arrangements of atoms in three-dimensional space. Their structures are constrained by the symmetry operations of a crystallographic \emph{space group} and restricted to lie in specific affine subspaces known as \emph{Wyckoff positions}. The frequency an atom appears in the crystal and its rough positioning are determined by its Wyckoff position. Most generative models that predict atomic coordinates overlook these symmetry constraints, leading to unrealistically high populations of proposed crystals exhibiting limited symmetry. We introduce Space Group Conditional Flow Matching, a novel generative framework that samples significantly closer to the target population of highly-symmetric, stable crystals. We achieve this by conditioning the entire generation process on a given space group and set of Wyckoff positions; specifically, we define a conditionally symmetric noise base distribution and a group-conditioned, equivariant, parametric vector field that restricts the motion of atoms to their initial Wyckoff position. Our form of group-conditioned equivariance is achieved using an efficient reformulation of \emph{group averaging} tailored for symmetric crystals. Importantly, it reduces the computational overhead of symmetrization to a negligible level. We achieve state of the art results on crystal structure prediction and de novo generation benchmarks. We also perform relevant ablations.
  </details>

- **[quanda: An Interpretability Toolkit for Training Data Attribution Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.07158)**  `arXiv:2410.07158`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Dilyara Bareeva, Galip Ümit Yolcu, Anna Hedström, Niklas Schmolenski, Thomas Wiegand, Wojciech Samek, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training data attribution (TDA) methods estimate the influence of individual training samples on a model's predictions, offering a principled lens for interpreting neural networks. Despite rapid methodological progress, TDA evaluation remains scattered across papers and rarely reproducible, making fair comparison between methods challenging. We introduce quanda, a Python toolkit that standardizes TDA evaluation. quanda features a comprehensive collection of evaluation metrics and ready-to-use benchmarks spanning image classification, text classification, and language modeling, alongside a uniform interface for integrating diverse TDA implementations. We showcase quanda through a comparative study of existing TDA methods and find that no approach excels uniformly across metrics, with clear trade-offs between ground-truth faithfulness and downstream task performance. By consolidating the tooling around TDA, quanda equips the community to evaluate attribution methods rigorously and reproducibly. The toolkit is thoroughly tested, documented, and available as an open-source library on PyPI and atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[MegNIST, A Benchmark for Non-Invasive Inner Speech Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21399)**  `arXiv:2611.21399`  `cs.LG`  
  _Teyun Kwon, Pratik Somaiya, Gereon Elvers, Francesco Mantegna, Anna Camera, Sven Braeutigam, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  MegNIST is a benchmark dataset for developing and evaluating machine learning methods for inner speech decoding from magnetoencephalography (MEG). The dataset comprises 12,000 trials of a single participant imagining speaking the digits zero through nine, recorded across four sessions. Each trial contains 306-channel MEG recordings sampled at 250 Hz, preprocessed using signal space separation, filtering, and downsampling. Data are provided as pre-defined training (10,000 trials), validation (1,000 trials), and test (1,000 trials) splits, with entire experimental runs assigned to each split to preserve temporal independence. The dataset is available in both BIDS format for neuroimaging analysis and as serialised tensors for machine learning workflows. Baseline classification results demonstrate that imagined digits can be decoded significantly above chance. Standardised splits, validated baselines, example code, and a PyTorch-compatible loader in the pnpl Python library enable straightforward integration with machine learning frameworks, lowering barriers for researchers to rapidly develop and benchmark inner speech decoding methods for non-invasive brain-computer interfaces.
  </details>

- **[MiSTiQ: MicroScaling Trellis Quantization for Extreme KV Cache Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21216)**  `arXiv:2611.21216`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ofir Gordon, Lior Dikstein, Paul Balanca, Idan Achituve, Douglas Orr, Hai Victor Habi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern large language models (LLMs) increasingly support longer contexts and serve more concurrent requests, increasing both sequence lengths and batch sizes. Both trends increase KV-cache size and memory traffic during inference. KV-cache quantization reduces these costs, with recent approaches using codebook-based vector quantization to achieve extremely low bit widths. However, hardware constraints limit codebook size, and most methods reconstruct the cache in higher-precision floating-point formats for attention, limiting the computational benefits of quantization. To address these limitations, we introduce MiSTiQ, a drop-in replacement for codebook-based vector quantizers that combines trellis-coded storage with microscaling (MX) formats for low-precision computation. We combine trellis quantization with beam search to encode the KV cache over large reconstruction spaces without large codebooks. To support MX computation, we derive MX-Hash functions that generate reconstruction values directly in the target MX element format. These functions use the scale-conditioned marginal distribution of block-normalized Gaussian inputs. On Llama3 and Qwen3, MiSTiQ achieves lower WikiText perplexity and higher GSM8K accuracy than the evaluated baselines across several block-scale formats at approximately 1.25 effective bits per element. With NVFP4, it also achieves the highest LongBench average on both models.
  </details>

- **[Tight PAC-Bayes Generalisation Guarantees for Large Language Model Judges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20818)**  `arXiv:2611.20818`  `cs.LG`  
  _Tom A. Lamb, Philip H. S. Torr, Tim G. J. Rudner_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) judges are increasingly used to monitor the outputs of LLM-based AI agents, yet formal guarantees on the reliability of these judges remain largely absent, limiting confidence in their use for safety-critical oversight. We consider certifying such a judge and define its risk over safety classifications of prompt--response pairs drawn from a deployed generator. We apply a PAC-Bayes framework to this setting, refining existing compression-based methodology with the use of tighter bounds and more efficient coding. We show that standard parameter-efficient fine-tuning can yield non-vacuous and even tight certificates for safety judges, for both classification and Brier risk, without holding out data for validation. We introduce LoRA-GT, which shares a single quantisation scale across layers and achieves the tightest certificates with minimal degradation in predictive performance. Across five guardrail models and four benchmarks, we find that certificate tightness is governed more by adaptation complexity than by base model scale. Finally, we show how far quantisation can be pushed before the resulting increase in risk outweighs the bound tightening gained from shorter descriptions. Together, these results provide a practical route toward certifiable scalable oversight and offer insight into how the complexity of safety adaptations shapes generalisation.
  </details>

- **[Calibrate Once, Grow the Vocabulary: Candidate-Independent Scores for Conformal Risk Control in Open-Vocabulary Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20715)**  `arXiv:2611.20715`  `cs.LG`  
  _Md Tanveer Hossain Munim, Bijoy Ahmed Saiem, Muhammad Abdullah Adnan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Open-vocabulary segmenters accept class names at inference. Conformal risk control can bound how often their prediction sets miss the true label, but we show that this bound breaks when names are added after calibration. On ADE20K, a threshold calibrated for 10% risk at 150 names reaches up to 15.4% risk at 845 names in released CAT-Seg models. The risk exceeds the target on every calibration split, yet the prediction sets grow far less than the target requires. In SED they instead grow larger than needed. The cause is that pruning, cross-label attention and vocabulary normalisation make each label's score depend on the other requested labels. Scores can move either way, so a threshold recalibrated at a larger vocabulary need not hold at smaller ones. We remove this dependence without training by scoring each label against a fixed reference bank of names. A label's score then stays the same when names are added, so one calibration keeps its guarantee as the vocabulary grows. The guarantee holds whatever names are added, even ones chosen per image after seeing it. On three benchmarks and five segmenters, the converted models meet the target at every vocabulary size we evaluate. For CAT-Seg and SED at alpha=10%, they use at most one more label per pixel than the released models recalibrated at the grown vocabulary. Treating names instead of images as calibration samples also bounds the miss rate of added names. If the annotated names are a random sample of the names in the calibration images, an added name's expected miss rate on those images is at most the target. For harder added names, the excess is at most their gap in mean miss rate. Applied to Grounding DINO, which splits large vocabularies into chunks, the conversion also makes inference about six times faster.
  </details>

- **[A Temporal Convolutional Network with Multi-Head Attention Model: A Surrogate for Parallel DEVS Semiconductor Fab Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20493)**  `arXiv:2611.20493`  `cs.LG`  
  _Vamsi Krishna Pendyala, Hessam S. Sarjoughian, Edward J. Yellig, Bharadhwaj Balasubramaniam_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Semiconductor factories process many kinds of wafers organized into lots of various sizes and configurations. Different kinds of machines, each with its own operations, process wafers for hours to days, and the wafers undergo many incremental fabrication steps (with re-entry) and flows spanning days to weeks across connected machines. Fast and accurate predictions of throughput ($TH$) and turnaround time ($TA$) dynamics for individual machines and their combinations are essential for factory operations. To use machine learning methods for this purpose, this paper presents a Temporal Convolutional Network with Multi-head Attention (TCNA) architecture. It uses simulated datasets from a benchmark Parallel DEVS (PDEVS) semiconductor fabrication factory model. The factory processes configurable wafer lots with uniform and sinusoidal wafer arrival profiles and may undergo preventive maintenance. The wafer lot size, arrival profile, and factory maintenance policy are used as static covariates. TCNA is applied to a selected set of simulated discrete-event throughput and turnaround time trajectories. The $TH$ and $TA$ time series predicted by TCNA, evaluated with the common $MAPE$, $MSE$, and $R^2$ measures, show comparable or higher accuracy relative to other model architectures, with similar parameter counts and epoch times; the TCNA model size and execution time are similar to those of other architectures such as the Temporal Fusion Transformer. Although time series predictions are less accurate than high-resolution simulations, they can be deployed at a much lower computational cost. The TCNA achieves orders-of-magnitude speedup over PDEVS simulation with an acceptable loss of accuracy, enabling frequent product scheduling, capacity planning, and maintenance planning.
  </details>

- **[Reasoning Again with a Quarter of the Experts: Retry First, Then Train](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20419)**  `arXiv:2611.20419`  `cs.LG`  
  _Anik Jha_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Removing three quarters of the routed experts from a 122B-parameter reasoning mixture-of-experts model makes it fit in a 12 GiB file, and breaks it in a specific way: on LiveCodeBench it enters verbatim reasoning loops that run to the token limit on 44 of 120 development problems, where the unpruned model at the same 2-bit quantization loops on none. We study how much of that loss can be recovered without changing the kept experts' quantized bytes, in three steps of increasing cost. On development problems, a sampling penalty recovers part of it. Stopping a reply when it starts to loop and drawing again recovers more on top of it, with no weight change. Our method, B, adds rank-8 trained corrections to the kept experts, loaded as an adapter, and restores two experts per layer; it is fitted layer by layer to the unpruned model's expert block on the pruned model's own activations. In a pre-registered comparison on 130 easy and medium LiveCodeBench problems that no part of the study had touched, with both arms given the same sampling penalty and the same stop-and-redraw policy, B solves 116 problems against 104 (+9.2 pp, 95% CI [+3.1, +15.4]; 15 problems gained, 3 lost). A training-free weight edit aimed at the loops, tested the same way earlier, did not help. B removes more wrong answers than loops, and costs 1.7x the wall time. On two older benchmarks used as regression checks, B stays inside a pre-set 2 pp tolerance but shows no gain (HumanEval+ +0.6 pp, MBPP+ -1.9 pp). The evidence is one model, one quantization and one benchmark family; we state what it does and does not show.
  </details>

- **[EPI-SST: Renewal-Structured State-Space Transformers for Epidemic Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20352)**  `arXiv:2611.20352`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yiyun Chen, Zewen Liu, Shengpu Tang, Max S. Y. Lau, Wei Jin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Epidemic forecasting supports public health decisions ranging from capacity planning to intervention timing, for which both point accuracy and calibrated uncertainty are essential. Although neural models have advanced point forecasting, producing reliable probabilistic predictions over multiple future horizons remains challenging. Renewal models encode epidemic propagation, while neural forecasters learn flexible multi-horizon representations; integrating these strengths within a probabilistic future rollout remains underexplored. We propose EPI-SST (Epidemic State Space Transformer), a probabilistic forecaster centered on an evidence-driven renewal transition. At each horizon, neural evidence produces a bounded update to an internal multiplier and advances a finite-memory renewal state that generates the future mean trajectory. A separate heteroscedastic Gaussian head controls predictive scale using a validation-selected log-variance penalty. Across four epidemic surveillance benchmarks, EPI-SST achieves the lowest pooled RMSE on every dataset, reducing RMSE by up to 14.2 percent relative to the strongest baseline. It also achieves the lowest continuous ranked probability score (CRPS) at horizons h = 4, 5, 6 on every dataset, with reductions of up to 43 percent relative to the strongest probabilistic baseline. During the abnormal 2019-2020 influenza season, EPI-SST reduces CRPS by 16.9 percent and calibration error by 78.9 percent; a three-seed replication preserves its distributional lead. Ablation studies and frozen-path interventions show that the renewal transition, attention conditioning, horizon coupling, and uncertainty-scale control make complementary contributions to predictive performance.
  </details>

- **[MOBO-CAPS: Multi-Objective Bayesian Optimization with Cardinality-Aware Pareto Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19999)**  `arXiv:2611.19999`  `cs.LG`  
  _Hanyang Wang, Juergen Branke, Matthias Poloczek_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-objective Bayesian optimization (MOBO) traditionally aims to approximate the entire Pareto front, but, due to resource constraints, real-world deployment is often limited to testing a small, representative subset of solutions. Hence current methods first approximate the full front before selecting solutions, potentially wasting costly evaluations. We formalize this problem as Multi-objective Bayesian Optimization with Cardinality-aware Pareto Selection (MOBO-CAPS), where the goal is to identify a set of exactly M solutions with high hypervolume. We propose two practical cardinality-aware acquisition strategies: REHVI, a replacement-aware approximation to expected hypervolume improvement, and CHO-UCB, which constructs an optimistic M-point set by optimizing a collective UCB-based hypervolume objective. We prove a sublinear simple regret bound for the batched version of CHO-UCB, and empirically show that cardinality-aware acquisition design improves over standard MOBO followed by post-hoc subset selection across nine benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Foundation Pareto Flow Policy for Multi-Objective Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19825)**  `arXiv:2611.19825`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhanjiang Yang, Lijun Sun, Yueming Li, Meng Li, Yijun Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The overarching goal of reinforcement learning (RL) is to efficiently optimize the agent's policy for various environments, tasks, dynamics, and even user preferences, which often consist of multiple and potentially conflicting rewards/objectives. This necessitates the learning of Pareto-optimal policies that can navigate different trade-offs among objectives. However, existing multi-objective RL (MORL) methods, whether trained online or offline, are typically limited to task- or objective-specific policy learning and do not address generalization of learned trade-offs to unseen tasks/objectives. In this work, we propose the Foundation Pareto Flow Policy (FP2) for offline MORL in continuous robotic control, inspired by the pre-mid-post-training paradigm underlying recent foundation models. Specifically, we instantiate FP2 as a multi-modal diffusion transformer equipped with a likelihood-free flow matching loss, enabling unified, scalable, and instructable learning of diverse trade-off trajectories. We further develop a three-stage training pipeline to gradually optimize the policy: (1) pre-training on a large-scale MORL dataset, (2) mid-training on a carefully curated high-quality dataset, and (3) post-training on few collected trajectory data from new tasks and trade-offs. Empirical results demonstrate that FP2 outperforms previous state-of-the-art methods on most tasks of the D4MORL benchmark, achieving superior zero-shot interpolation and extrapolation on complex Pareto manifolds as well as efficient few-shot adaptation of the learned trade-off prior to new continuous-control tasks. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[The Sense Paradigm for World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19796)**  `arXiv:2611.19796`  `cs.LG`  
  _Haidong Hu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Latent world models predict the future in an embedding space rather than in pixels. An encoder trained only to predict collapses and maps every observation to the same point, so existing models add a mechanism against collapse, such as a target network, a variance penalty or a distribution penalty. These mechanisms keep the embedding from collapsing, but they leave its distances without meaning, and the model cannot tell a reversible transition from an irreversible one. We propose Sense, a single cross-entropy loss over the directed distance $d(x\to y)=\sum_i \mathrm{ReLU}(y_i-x_i)$ that replaces these mechanisms. The loss avoids collapse on its own, and since the encoder no longer needs the predictor, the predictor is fitted afterwards to fixed embeddings by plain regression. On four pixel environments, the return distance learned by Sense detects irreversible transitions with an AUC between 0.95 and 1.00, where LeWorldModel stays between 0.39 and 0.59. On TwoRoom, the navigation benchmark of LeWorldModel, planning with Sense reaches the goal in 100% of episodes against 91% for LeWorldModel. As the goal moves farther away, the planning failure rate of every baseline grows, while that of Sense stays near zero in Cup, Cliff and Sokoban and is the lowest of all methods in Pendulum.
  </details>

- **[Sample-Efficient Ensemble Reinforcement Learning at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19733)**  `arXiv:2611.19733`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yoav Feigenbaum, Dmitrii Krylov, Roy Fox_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Ensemble methods such as MeanQ have been shown to improve the sample efficiency of relatively small model-free deep reinforcement learning (RL) architectures, including DQN and similarly sized variants such as Rainbow. However, to our knowledge, the effects of ensembling on large-scale architectures such as Bigger Better Faster (BBF) have not been studied. In this work, we present MeanQ-BBF, a hybrid model-free algorithm that combines the principles of MeanQ and BBF. Through statistically rigorous evaluation on the Atari 100K benchmark, we show that in contrast to naive scaling of the network size of BBF, which degrades performance, MeanQ-BBF is able to leverage the increase in model capacity to improve performance over BBF. Moreover, MeanQ-BBF does this in a computationally efficient way by organizing the added parameters into largely independent learners. Finally, we discuss the performance-cost tradeoff in the context of practical sample-efficient RL.
  </details>

- **[With the Right Representation, Classical Machine Learning Rivals Deep Models for Olfactory Mixture Similarity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19650)**  `arXiv:2611.19650`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ze Dong, Yi Wang, Ambuj Tewari_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modeling olfactory mixtures remains a critical challenge in machine olfaction because the task requires reasoning over relationships between sets of odorants rather than over individual molecules in isolation. However, prior comparisons between deep and classical approaches also varied how the relationship between the two mixtures was represented, since the classical baselines primarily relied on weak representations that described each mixture independently and provided little direct signal about how the mixtures differed. In this work, we revisit classical learning using explicit symmetric comparison features on unordered pairs of mixtures, evaluating these representations with XGBoost and kernel ridge regression (KRR). We find that XGBoost with explicit comparison features achieves performance competitive with specialized deep mixture models on the main benchmark. Ablation studies show that coordinate-wise absolute differences alone provide strong predictive performance, while combining them with sums and products yields further improvements. KRR also benefits substantially from explicit comparison features, with further gains from appropriate kernel choice and preprocessing. Overall, these results suggest that explicitly encoding how two mixtures differ is an important ingredient for classical machine learning in low-data olfactory mixture prediction.
  </details>

- **[SLiDE: Structured Linear Dynamics for Forecasting with Exogenous Inputs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19586)**  `arXiv:2611.19586`  `cs.LG`  
  _Sebastian Pütz, Theodore Glavas, Benjamin Schäfer, Boris N. Oreshkin, Mark Coates_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurate time series forecasting with exogenous inputs is critical across domains, including energy and retail, yet modern deep learning models often overfit to correlations that do not generalize under shifts in these inputs. We propose SLiDE, a Koopman-inspired architecture for forecasting with exogenous inputs that imposes structure on latent temporal dynamics. SLiDE approximates nonlinear system evolution using a learned linear operator in a latent space, combining (i) a history encoder that reconstructs the current latent state from past targets and inputs with (ii) a shared linear rollout driven by future exogenous variables. By enforcing a common linear recurrence across encoding and prediction, SLiDE aligns how past information is encoded with how future states evolve, reducing parameterization and preventing reliance on spurious correlations in the input history. Empirically, SLiDE achieves state-of-the-art accuracy on real-world benchmarks, including electricity price forecasting and retail demand, while maintaining a computational footprint comparable to lightweight MLP architectures. SLiDE is especially effective at generalizing to shifted exogenous inputs, reducing MSE by 20% on electricity price samples with out-of-range exogenous values and MAE by 30% in a synthetic transfer setting where the same dynamical system is driven by an unseen exogenous process. Ablations confirm the importance of both the structured history encoder and the linear latent rollout, suggesting that structured latent linear dynamics provide a useful inductive bias for forecasting with exogenous inputs.
  </details>

- **[NetBreaker: Benchmarking Network Dismantling across Objectives, Scales, and Toplogoies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19298)**  `arXiv:2611.19298`  `cs.LG`  
  _Wenchuan Yang, Tianci Bu, Suoyi Tan, Changjun Fan, Zijie Zhou, Xin Lu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Network dismantling seeks to fragment a network with as few node removals as possible, which is widely applied in vulnerability analysis, epidemic containment, and infrastructure protection. Although learning-based methods have become increasingly prominent, their progress remains difficult to assess because existing studies differ in method coverage, datasets, evaluation criteria, and experimental protocols. Thus, we introduce NetBreaker, a comprehensive benchmark designed for the learning era of network dismantling. NetBreaker organizes 30 methods (18 non-learning and 12 learning-based) under a common taxonomy and evaluates them on 185 networks from eight unified domains, ranging from 14 to 1.69 million nodes. To enable fair comparison, heterogeneous method outputs are converted into node removal sequences and assessed under a unified execution protocol. The evaluation covers four complementary dimensions: target-based effectiveness, trajectory-based effectiveness, structural diagnostics, and applicability & computational efficiency. Extensive experiments show that current learningbased methods do not consistently outperform strong non-learning approaches, while exposing substantial differences in effectiveness, scalability, and efficiency across network conditions and evaluation perspectives. NetBreaker provides a reproducible basis for measuring progress and developing reliable, scalable, and computationally efficient network dismantling methods.
  </details>

- **[Where Mixture Recognition Fails: An Ingredient-Level Audit of Unseen Combinations in SmellNet](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19297)**  `arXiv:2611.19297`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zirui Liu, Zheming Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An electronic nose must name every ingredient of a mixture, including combinations it has never measured. SmellNet, a public benchmark for this task, scores ingredients with a hit rate that is told how many each sample contains. We audit what this protocol hides. We hold out ingredient combinations from the SmellNet training data, evaluate four architectures (MLP, CNN, LSTM and a Transformer encoder) without revealing the ingredient count, and repeat the evaluation once on the official unseen split. Under the count-informed protocol, 90-96% of mixtures receive an ingredient that is absent, on our folds and on the official split alike. On our folds, every model identifies one ingredient reliably but the second rarely, and it completes the pair with a familiar training partner about twice as often as chance. On the official split, even the first-ranked ingredient is correct in only 29-46% of binary mixtures, often because a frequent training ingredient that is absent from every test pair is ranked first, and on its combinations that are truly absent from training no model names both ingredients in more than 1.2% of them. The failures fall on the same ingredients for every architecture, and a frequency correction only moves errors between ingredients. Mixture recognition is therefore limited by ingredients the models cannot separate and by a preference for frequent ingredients, not by the architecture, and it should be evaluated with set-level errors and without oracle cardinality.
  </details>

- **[Advancing the Calibration-Compute Frontier of LLM Classifiers via Asymmetric Duos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19020)**  `arXiv:2611.19020`  `cs.LG`  
  _Berke Akturk, Ali Can Sahin, Rasit Cem Solatay, Emanuel Sommer, Yawei Li, David Rügamer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
