  The importance of reliable uncertainty estimation in large language models (LLMs) has become increasingly pronounced, yet modern models remain poorly calibrated and frequently overconfident. In this study, we address this by proposing an efficient method for LLM classifier calibration using the Asymmetric Duos framework. Our approach combines a strong base model with a smaller sidekick model via logit-space fusion, utilizing global scaling parameters optimized on a validation set. Across diverse benchmarks, this Asymmetric LLM Duo consistently achieves best or second-best performance on expected calibration error and negative log-likelihood, without degrading predictive accuracy. More importantly, these gains are realized at a fraction of the computational cost of more complex baselines, placing our method on the calibration-compute Pareto frontier. We further demonstrate that our method acts orthogonal to various established calibration-aware techniques, meaning it can be easily combined with them to consistently boost their performance. Finally, our analysis of the logit-space behavior illustrates how asymmetric fusion successfully mitigates base model overconfidence. Overall, our findings demonstrate that the benefits of an asymmetric fusion successfully transfer to the language domain, providing a simple, highly cost-efficient approach to improving uncertainty estimation in LLM classifiers.
  </details>

- **[Revealing the Biases of Large Language Models By Inferring Prior Distributions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18996)**  `arXiv:2611.18996`  `cs.LG`  
  _Joshua Ryo Cheng, Thomas L. Griffiths, Jake C. Snell_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) have been known to exhibit a wide range of social biases including name-nationality associations, occupation-gender associations and racial stereotypes. Existing approaches to evaluating these biases typically involve measuring surface-level behavior where a model is fed a dataset of prompts and its outputs are scored relative to ground-truth answers. Inspired by prior elicitation in cognitive science, we propose a different perspective that reconstructs latent bias as a prior underlying the model's behavior. We formulate the problem as a Bayesian inverse problem where, given prompt-response observations on a bias benchmark dataset, we infer a low-dimensional latent distribution that summarizes the model's stereotyping tendencies. This paper develops the mathematical foundation, a practical reconstruction algorithm, and experiments using the Bias Benchmark for QA (BBQ) benchmark to infer the prior distributions of LLMs over responses in ambiguous social contexts.
  </details>

- **[How Much Do Foundation Models Add to Gradient Boosting on Financial Event Sequences?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18914)**  `arXiv:2611.18914`  `cs.LG`  
  _Carlos Eduardo Martins Relvas, Gustavo di Giovanni Bernardo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Foundation models pretrained on raw transaction sequences are proposed for credit, fraud and churn modeling, with reported gains over gradient boosting, but the data and baselines behind these gains are rarely public. We measure how much the pretraining and adaptation recipes of these models add to a baseline of point-in-time window aggregates and LightGBM, and we propose TBS (Temporal Boosting-Sequence ensemble), which averages the ranks of a boosting model enriched with label-free event-transition features and of a pretrained sequence network fine-tuned jointly with the aggregates. On a synthetic credit-card dataset, the public MBD-mini banking dataset and two proprietary datasets of trade credit and B2B churn, we compare 27 methods at three label budgets with five seeds and paired bootstrap intervals, using sequence models of about 0.4 million parameters pretrained per dataset. Frozen self-supervised embeddings rarely improve the baseline, and a fine-tuned sequence network alone clears it in 9 of 21 target-budget cells. TBS clears it in 19 of 21 cells (18 after FDR adjustment), with a median gain of +1.8 AUC points, and the controls suggest that combining boosting with the pretrained network is the main source of the observed gain. Gains shrink as labels grow and with rich tabular data: with both, the gain on the proprietary datasets is +0.7 and +0.8 points, the latter with an interval including zero. We also re-examine two benchmarks. On IBM TabFormer, the test follows a change in the fraud pattern and the published baseline has no history; with the training period the vendor states, our history-aware LightGBM and our own foundation model stay below the published scores of a commercial model. On IBM AML, a simulation artifact lets a date rule reach F1 0.482, and a point-in-time LightGBM reaches the F1 of graph neural networks whose test graph includes later transactions.
  </details>

- **[SLICES: A Benchmark and Evaluation Framework for Self-Supervised Learning on Sparse ICU Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18868)**  `arXiv:2611.18868`  `cs.LG`  
  _Hannes Ill, Dimitrios Proios, Leo Anthony Celi, Peter J. Schüffler_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-supervised learning (SSL) results for intensive care unit (ICU) time series are hard to compare when data processing, encoders, and adaptation vary together. We present SLICES, a benchmark and evaluation framework for comparing and extending SSL methods under a shared clinical setup. It fixes 24-hour value and observation-mask inputs, a Transformer encoder, patient-level splits, four endpoints, two adaptation protocols, and five matched seeds across MIMIC-IV, eICU-CRD, and their pooled union. Masked autoencoding (MAE), joint-embedding prediction (JEPA), and contrastive learning provide reference implementations. Among these objectives, MAE leads mean AUPRC in all nine frozen classification comparisons, also under converged probes, and in seven of nine fine-tuning comparisons. Fine-tuned MAE leads hospital mortality at full labels and scores higher than a supervised Transformer of the same architecture at every label fraction, and SSL fine-tuning gives the lowest length-of-stay error. Supervised baselines remain strong elsewhere: GRU-D leads 24-hour mortality and XGBoost leads acute kidney injury in all three regimes. Rankings depend on the adaptation protocol, the comparator set, and whether the probe loss matches the metric. A two-seed MIMIC-IV rerun with the maintained code stays within two across-seed SDs of the reference in all 48 downstream runs. The code repository supports new objectives, shared downstream evaluation, and regeneration of the reference results, so new SSL methods can be compared with these references under the same conditions. Generalization to unseen hospitals is not tested here and is the natural next extension.
  </details>

- **[Clustering-Based Artificial Tree Ensembles for Random Forest Approximation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18803)**  `arXiv:2611.18803`  `cs.LG`  
  _Lea L. Kronziel, Maren S. Wieder, Silke Szymczak, Björn-Hergen Laabs_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Random Forests (RFs) offer strong prediction performance but are difficult to interpret. Most representative trees (MRTs) and artificial representative trees (ARTs) provide surrogate explanations, but a single tree may miss patterns within a forest. We investigate benefits and limitations of clustering-based representative tree ensembles for RF surrogate modeling and assess the contribution of similarity-based clustering relative to random tree partitions. Using linear and nonlinear simulations and regression benchmarks, we evaluate fidelity, prediction accuracy, complexity, and prediction stability against equally sized ensembles of top-ranked MRTs and randomly selected trees. Increasing ensemble size generally improves fidelity, accuracy, and prediction stability. ART ensembles show higher fidelity in simulations and often greater prediction stability for small ensembles. Compared with random tree partitions, similarity-based clustering generally improves ART fidelity in simulations and often on benchmarks. On benchmark datasets, MRT-based ensembles generally achieve higher fidelity and prediction accuracy, whereas ART ensembles have fewer terminal nodes. ART ensembles provide compact surrogates when reduced complexity matters and their fidelity and prediction accuracy meet application requirements. These findings clarify both the trade-offs of representative tree ensembles as RF surrogates and the contribution of similarity-based clustering beyond random clustering.
  </details>

- **[Actuarial-Informed Neural Networks: Constrained Deep Learning for Sex-Specific Multi-Population Longevity Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18802)**  `arXiv:2611.18802`  `cs.LG`  
  _Davide Rindori_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce Actuarial-Informed Neural Networks (AINN), a constrained deep learning framework for sex-specific multi-population longevity forecasting that embeds actuarial knowledge into LSTM training through differentiable penalty terms. Applied to a high-longevity 6-country cluster (Switzerland, Sweden, Norway, West Germany, Netherlands, Japan; 1956-2020), the framework is a challenger to the Li-Lee benchmark and unconstrained neural approaches.Our central finding is that monotonicity constraint intensity lambda functions as a continuous governance dial with a quantifiable accuracy-stability trade-off. At lambda = 0.01, 30-year forecast confidence intervals narrow by 6.5% at zero RMSE cost. At lambda = 0.1, they narrow by 25% at only +0.84% RMSE cost. This trade-off is invisible to standard hyperparameter optimizers, which select lambda by minimizing one-step RMSE, the wrong metric for structural constraints in recursive forecasting.The framework adds three contributions: (i) residual-calibrated process noise that eliminates double-counting in the dual uncertainty framework, reducing confidence intervals by 53-60%; (ii) a 5-seed model ensemble with a coefficient of variation of 1.06%; and (iii) a time-varying credibility weight Z(t) = exp(-alpha t) connecting to Buhlmann credibility theory for blending neural and actuarial predictions. A 10-year out-of-sample backtest (trained on 1956-2010, validated against observed HMD life expectancy for 2011-2020) yields a mean absolute error of 0.38 years, with 11 of 12 country x sex combinations within the 90% confidence interval. The model produces regulatory-grade outputs including Solvency Capital Requirements under the Swiss Solvency Test (ES 99.0%: +1.91 years, CHE Male) and Solvency II, reverse stress testing, and model-based stability verification (amplification ratio 1.02x).
  </details>

- **[Beveridge: Open Weight System One Decision Models with Calibrated Probabilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18655)**  `arXiv:2611.18655`  `cs.LG`  
  _Sathiskumar Jothi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many production systems need a model to make a narrow decision about a document (route a ticket, check a limit, refuse a request, pick the next step of a browser agent) rather than to write text about it. We present Beveridge, a family of open weight System One decision models that take a document and up to 15 typed questions (yes/no, choice among described options, or a level on an ordered scale) and return, in one forward pass without generating text, an answer with a calibrated probability for every option. Beveridge-Large-C (398M parameters) adds a 3.5M parameter decision network to ModernBERT-large: typed number and date features in the input, exact facts from a rule engine, question-to-document evidence attention, a three-step recursive core with learned halting and an exact yes/no path. Beveridge-Pro-C adapts Gemma 4 26B-A4B with LoRA to score option letters, and Beveridge-Pro-C-Web extends it to the next step of a browser agent. On held-out tests the 0.4B model reaches 93.0% on our operations-document benchmark against 91.1% for the best of five general LLMs (31B-675B) and 87.9% for a commercial System One API, and, because its confidences are calibrated (ECE 0.029 on that benchmark), it can hand over 76% of decisions automatically at 95% accuracy, where no general LLM we tested can hand over any. It answers in 4.9 ms per decision on one NVIDIA H200 GPU and 60 ms on the 14 core CPU of an Apple M4 Pro laptop. Beveridge-Pro-C-Web raises next-step accuracy on web pages from unseen site types from 49.4% to 98.2% and completes live browsing tasks without losing document accuracy. We describe the models, the training recipes and an evaluation on 19 held-out test sets against a commercial System One API and five general LLMs, including where the models are weak. Weights and inference code are released under Apache 2.0.
  </details>

- **[Not All Data Leakage Is Equal: A Controlled Benchmark of Evaluation Inflation in Tabular Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18598)**  `arXiv:2611.18598`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ankit Sharma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Data leakage is widely recognized as a threat to trustworthy machine-learning evaluation, yet the phrase covers mechanisms with very different statistical consequences. We present a controlled empirical benchmark that measures the inflation in held-out ROC-AUC caused by five common leakage mechanisms: global standardization, global median imputation, duplicate contamination, supervised feature selection before splitting, and target encoding before splitting. The benchmark spans six public datasets and two synthetic stress tests, three model families, and five repeated stratified train/test splits, yielding 600 paired clean-versus-leaked comparisons. To avoid pseudo-replication, inference is performed on dataset-level effects with cluster bootstrap confidence intervals and Holm-corrected one-sided Wilcoxon tests. Label-dependent or direct data-reuse mechanisms produced systematic optimism: global target encoding increased ROC-AUC by 2.42 percentage points on average (95% cluster-bootstrap CI 0.62--4.89), global supervised feature selection by 1.93 points (0.63--3.39), and duplicate contamination by 1.52 points (0.40--2.80). All three remained significant after multiplicity correction. In contrast, global scaling and global median imputation had near-zero average effects under the i.i.d. settings studied here. A controlled target-encoding stress test further shows that leakage becomes sharply worse as categories become sparse. The results support a severity-aware view of leakage: evaluation pipelines should prevent all held-out-data dependence, but label-dependent transformations and observation reuse deserve especially aggressive auditing.
  </details>

- **[Skill-Latent Rewards: LLM-Guided Reward Formulation for Hierarchical Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18597)**  `arXiv:2611.18597`  `cs.LG`  
  _Maryam Shoaeinaeini, Brent Harrison_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon reinforcement learning under sparse and delayed feedback remains an open challenge. Existing return-decomposition methods redistribute episodic rewards to ease credit assignment, but they operate on raw state--action transitions and disregard the subgoal structure that governs meaningful progress in complex tasks. We propose Skill-LR, a hierarchical reinforcement learning framework in which a vision--language model identifies the agent's active subgoal at each timestep and a large language model generates a matching latent reward formulation through a two-stage prompting process. By conditioning reward signals on the currently active subgoal, Skill-LR produces stage-specific credit that evolves with the hierarchical plan, reducing temporal variance and improving sample efficiency. On multi-stage MiniGrid and BabyAI benchmarks, including DoorKey and Unlock-to-Unlock, Skill-LR converges faster and more reliably than sparse environmental rewards and strong credit-assignment baselines such as RUDDER and LaRe, demonstrating that subgoal-aligned latent rewards offer a principled path toward stable, interpretable credit assignment in long-horizon tasks.
  </details>

- **[MORA: Correcting Time Series Forecasts with Coarse Future Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15413)**  `arXiv:2611.15413`  `cs.LG`  
  _Hu Li, Kenan Li, Haonan Xu, longlong, Shuqin Gao, Yucheng Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent architectural advances have improved time-series forecasting. However, our analysis of forecasters with diverse architectures reveals a common pattern: a substantial share of residual energy is concentrated in a small number of low-frequency components. Simple linear probes further show that the forecasters' historical inputs still contain predictive information about errors in the forecast mean. Building on these findings, we propose Model-Output Residual Adaptation (MORA), a model-agnostic residual adapter that refines the outputs of frozen forecasters without requiring additional inputs. MORA estimates segment-wise means of the target sequence through structural extrapolation and learned estimation, rather than directly predicting backbone-specific residuals. It then converts discrepancies between these estimates and the corresponding statistics of the backbone forecast into stagewise corrections, scaling each update according to estimation reliability. Extensive experiments on twelve benchmarks with six forecasting backbones show average MSE reductions of 7.2% for long-term forecasting and 28.1% for short-term forecasting, with lower MSE in 96.2% of the 288 evaluated cases. The benefits also extend to three time-series foundation models evaluated at different context lengths. Together, these results demonstrate that predictable coarse-scale residuals can be effectively exploited to improve existing forecasts. Code repository:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Continual-ARC: Measuring Skill Acquisition over a Working Lifetime](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14505)**  `arXiv:2611.14505`  `cs.LG`  
  _Daniel Lenton, Yusha Arif, Haris Mahmood_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent harnesses have emerged as a promising mechanism for continual learning, alongside test-time parametric learning. However, the current benchmarks have several limitations: they generally focus on a single applied domain, serve each instance only once, and assume a single family of learner, so memory-based and weight-based systems are rarely compared on the same scale. We propose Continual-ARC, a lifelong stream built from the three ARC-AGI editions, whose tasks need no domain knowledge. The same tasks return after dormant gaps with fresh instances, while new tasks keep arriving. On each instance, the learner may pay for demonstrations, submit answers, or act inside the harness, with retries permitted on incorrect submissions, which are paid for. Each instance is scored based on the share of this feedback budget consumed. Since a learner may keep anything between instances, memory-based and weight-based systems are scored on the same scale. After the stream, the learner is frozen and tested on fresh instances of the tasks it met, which measures memorization, and on unseen tasks, which measures generalization. We run an agent harness with frozen weights, with and without persistence, on Qwen3.5-9B and GLM-5.3 Flash, comparing them with two prompting floors, a program-induction method and Qwen3.5-9B's weights trained to imitate GLM-5.3 Flash. Persistence in the harness generally does not improve memorization unless provided with explicit expert guidance. Most harness-based methods still learn less than a floor that simply caches each task's demonstrations, across all models we tested. Harness-based methods gain little or nothing on unseen tasks, whereas weight-based learning shows substantial gains. This opens the door to future weight-based continual learning that could teach models to drive harnesses in new domains.
  </details>

- **[How Should Teachers Be Prepared? RL on Student-Induced States for On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04950)**  `arXiv:2610.04950`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xiaoyu Ma, Haoyue Liu, Zhichao Wang, Jionghao Zhu, Xiaoying Tang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation (OPD) improves the reasoning capabilities of small language models through token-level teacher supervision on student-generated trajectories. Yet can teachers that excel at solving problems independently also guide student reasoning effectively? Prior work shows that when student prefixes follow reasoning paths that differ from the teacher's own or contain errors, teachers can be less accurate when continuing from these prefixes than when solving problems independently. To this end, we propose Prep-OPD, which uses reinforcement learning (RL) before distillation to train the teacher to adapt to the student's existing reasoning state and correct course when errors arise. Training optimizes teacher continuations from fixed student prefixes using final-answer correctness as the reward. The prepared teacher then trains the student through trajectory guidance and token-level supervision. We evaluate Prep-OPD on eight mathematical reasoning benchmarks, using Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507 as the teacher and Qwen3-0.6B and Qwen3-1.7B as students. With the 4B teacher and 1.7B student, Prep-OPD improves average accuracy over standard OPD and the strongest baseline, Relay-OPD, by 8.28 and 2.30 percentage points, respectively. Controlled experiments further show that teacher RL conditioned on student-generated prefixes yields higher student accuracy than problem-start teacher RL with and without handoff on Qwen3-1.7B. Reusing the same prepared teacher also improves Qwen3-0.6B.
  </details>

- **[Shared Phase and Retention Control for Efficient Adaptive Spectral Recurrence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39082)**  `arXiv:2609.39082`  `cs.LG`  
  _Wentao Wang, Hengyu Zhong, Yunhan Jiang, Jialiang An, Meng Lu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fixed-state spectral recurrences offer constant-memory inference and strong long-range retention through heterogeneous complex modes, but their static transitions cannot selectively filter distractors or adapt to evolving dynamics. Existing input-dependent recurrences introduce selectivity, yet real-valued gates lack adaptive rotation, while fine-grained complex transitions incur substantial projection and data-movement overhead and can expose persistent modes to cumulative drift. To address these limitations, we introduce Shared Phase and Retention Control for Efficient Adaptive Spectral Recurrence (SPARC), which modulates a layer's heterogeneous complex memory through two input-dependent scalars. Each mode retains its learned baseline timescale, frequency, and write response, while a retention-coupled clock damps phase corrections in persistent modes. The same shared signals regulate incoming content through modal gates and retention-dependent normalization. The resulting diagonal affine recurrence supports parallel associative scans for full-sequence backpropagation through time (BPTT) and exact structured Real-Time Recurrent Learning (RTRL), with sensitivity storage and updates linear in the recurrent layer's parameter count. We evaluate SPARC on partially observable continuous control, POPGym memory benchmarks, sequence classification, and language modeling. SPARC places among the top two means across all ten reinforcement-learning tasks. Compared with RG-LRU, it improves permuted-MNIST and sequential-CIFAR-10 accuracy by $10.13$ and $10.66$ percentage points, respectively, reduces held-out language-model perplexity by $2.00\%$, and accelerates mixer forward--backward computation by up to $1.69\times$ at width $D=2048$. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Learning Chance-Constrained MDPs with Bellman Distributional Certificates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30856)**  `arXiv:2609.30856`  `cs.LG`  
  _Chenbei Lu, Hongyu Yi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Safe reinforcement learning (RL) commonly enforces expected-cost constraints, but such expectation-form safety may fail to control the probability of rare high-cost trajectories. Chance-constrained MDPs (CCMDPs) impose a stronger probability-level requirement, but are widely viewed as harder because the chance constraint itself is nonconvex and depends on the full trajectory rather than a Bellman-linear expectation. In this paper, we reveal that this computational difficulty does not necessarily imply a higher statistical price. For tabular discounted CCMDPs with bounded successor support and access to a certified planning oracle, we establish a model-based upper bound, with a matching lower bound up to logarithmic terms. Technically, our key idea is the \emph{Bellman distributional certificate}, which constructs a Bellman recursion for constraint violation probabilities before policy selection. The certificate reduces trajectory-level safety analysis to local transition uncertainty, avoiding a union bound over the potentially exponential number of candidate policies. For stochastic policies, we give a model-free variance-reduced policy-gradient algorithm with a finite-sample expected KKT-residual guarantee and independent validation of every accepted policy. Numerical experiments on synthetic CCMDPs and an IEEE 14-bus energy storage control benchmark illustrate the safety and mechanism behavior of the proposed algorithms.
  </details>

- **[Functional Degeneracy in Neural Networks: Measurement and Pruning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30741)**  `arXiv:2608.30741`  `cs.LG`  
  _Maria Matveev, Pascal Esser, Ayush Bharadwaj, Lucius Bushnaq, Gitta Kutyniok_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A central question in modern machine learning is how much a trained model can be compressed without changing its behavior, to reduce the memory, compute and energy required to deploy it. To study this, we quantify functional degeneracy through the behavioral recovery rank, the number of leading behavioral-Hessian eigendirections required to recover a model's performance to a given tolerance. Using it as a geometric benchmark for compression, we find that the rank flattens once the task is saturated, while the number of parameters retained by one-shot pruning keeps growing. This gap suggests that functional degeneracy is distributed across parameter directions and not exposed by individual weights or neurons.
  </details>

- **[An ECG foundation model for zero-shot transfer from hospital to point-of-care devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03690)**  `arXiv:2608.03690`  `cs.LG`  
  _Edoardo Coppola, Stefano Fiorini, Pietro Liò, Mattia Savardi, Alberto Signoroni_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Electrocardiogram (ECG) recording is moving from hospitals to point-of-care devices that record one or two leads, where public labelled data is too scarce to build foundation models (FMs) as for 12-lead ECGs. Whether existing 12-lead FMs transfer to these devices without retraining is unknown. We show that seven published FMs reach a zero-shot AUROC of 41-81% on two real point-of-care datasets and, when reduced to one or two leads, lose 12-35 AUROC points on average and gain 0.15-0.23 in mean absolute error across 29 benchmarks covering 1,673 clinical targets. We present RELAY, a 7-million-parameter FM that represents ECGs as graphs of segments from whichever leads are recorded, without filling missing ones or knowing their identities. Pretrained on 9.2 million 12-lead ECGs, RELAY is competitive with 12 leads, ranks first on both point-of-care datasets (AUROC 87.6% and 81.5%) and on 26 and 22 of 29 benchmarks with one and two random leads, and keeps the most informative operating point when 12-lead thresholds are applied to device recordings. Matched ablations show that omitting absent leads, rather than filling them, reduces what RELAY loses in transfer. ECG FMs should therefore be judged across all the acquisition settings, not on 12-lead benchmarks alone.
  </details>

- **[StableRCA: Mechanism-Level Root Cause Analysis without Global Graph Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05636)**  `arXiv:2606.05636`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xiaoyu Lin, Nicholas Tagliapietra, Kehan Li, Lavdim Halilaj, Juergen Luettin, Peng Cui_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Root-cause analysis (RCA) seeks to identify the true causal variables that are responsible for a system's abnormal behaviors. A central challenge in this task is to distinguish changes where the anomaly occurs from downstream propagated abnormal effects, particularly when the underlying causal structure is unknown. To address this challenge, we propose StableRCA, an RCA framework that helps distinguish root causes from variables affected by fault propagation by examining how each candidate's conditional distribution, given a selected set of other variables, changes between the normal and anomalous regimes, without requiring knowledge of the underlying causal graph. The method first identifies variables with marginal distribution shifts as root-cause candidates, then selects a local conditioning set for each candidate using stable learning, and finally ranks the candidates by the magnitude of their local conditional shifts, using an importance-weighted Relative Risk Variation (RRV) score as a proxy. Our theoretical analysis establishes sufficient conditions under which comparing the conditional distributions of candidate variables across the normal and anomalous regimes, given suitable conditioning sets, identifies the root causes. Experiments on synthetic benchmarks and four real-world application benchmarks demonstrate StableRCA's competitive performance across diverse domains.
  </details>

- **[Provable Joint Decontamination for Benchmarking Multiple Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.21543)**  `arXiv:2605.21543`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhenlong Liu, Hao Zeng, Hongxin Wei_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Benchmark data contamination has become a central challenge in LLM evaluation: when evaluation examples appear in the training data of one or more audited models, reported performance can be inflated and cross-model comparisons become unreliable. A broad line of training-data detection work designs scores to quantify how strongly a model memorizes a given data point, but these score-based methods lack theoretical guarantees. Recent conformal approaches provide provable false-identification control for a single model; however, applying them separately to each model can produce model-specific benchmarks, undermining fair comparison across models. In this work, we formalize multi-model benchmark decontamination as a joint selection problem and propose Joint Envelope Conformal Selection (JECS), a conformal procedure that enables global contamination rate (GCR) control under stated assumptions. Specifically, JECS computes per-model conformal p-values, aggregates them by the per-item maximum, and reconstructs a conservative envelope of the max-p null distribution from right-tail observations above a data-driven threshold. By applying the adaptive Benjamini-Hochberg (BH) procedure to the envelope-rescaled values, we select a benchmark with provable GCR control. Extensive experiments across various models and benchmarks demonstrate that JECS achieves higher power than the max-p baseline while consistently maintaining the target GCR control.
  </details>

- **[OmicsGBench: A Framework for Evaluating Graph Neural Networks on Omics Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.15511)**  `arXiv:2605.15511`  `cs.LG`  
  _Louisa Cornelis, Johan Mathe, Louis Van Langendonck, Guillermo Bernárdez, Nina Miolane_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) have become the dominant framework for inductive graph-level learning. Yet most benchmarks focus on the regime $n \gg p$, where the number of graphs $n$ greatly exceeds the number of nodes per graph $p$. This overlooks biological domains such as omics, which operate in the opposite $n \ll p$ regime, characterized by large graphs of genes, transcripts, or proteins across few patient samples. This raises the question: how do GNNs perform in this low-sample, high-node omics setting? We introduce OmicsGBench (Omics-Graph Bench), the first benchmarking platform for graph-level prediction on omics expression data, a domain whose defining characteristic is the $n \ll p$ regime. We provide a standardized, end-to-end modular infrastructure from raw omics data to families of featured graphs with varied structural properties. We benchmark classical GNNs, as well as GNNs designed for large graphs and omics applications, alongside MLPs and machine learning baselines to establish reference performances. Our results show that widely used GNNs often do not outperform simple MLPs and classical baselines. Ablation studies show that performance relies primarily on feature selection and whether the readout preserves node identity. Furthermore, graphs built from known biological interactions offer no consistent advantage over co-expression graphs. These findings challenge the prevailing assumption that graph structure inherently adds value in this domain, fostering a critical reassessment of current learning paradigms. Ultimately, by exposing these limitations, OmicsGBench provides the open-source ecosystem necessary for the community to develop and validate novel architectures explicitly tailored for biological graphs. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[The Challenge of Out-Of-Distribution Detection in Motor Imagery BCIs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.13324)**  `arXiv:2603.13324`  `cs.LG`  
  _Merlijn Quincent Mulder, Matias Valdenegro-Toro, Andreea Ioana Sburlea, Ivo Pascal de Jong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Machine Learning classifiers used in Brain-Computer Interfaces make classifications based on the distribution of examples on which they were trained. When exposed to EEG from unfamiliar classes, they can only make blind guesses. Instead of allowing such guesses, these Out-of-Distribution (OOD) samples should be detected and rejected to improve the robustness of BCIs against unfamiliar cognitive states.We study OOD detection in Motor Imagery BCIs by training a model on some classes and observing whether an unfamiliar movement class can be detected based on increased uncertainty. We tested seven different OOD detection methods and one more method that has been claimed to boost the quality of OOD detection.For many BCI users, the uncertainty for the familiar in-distribution classes can still be higher than for the out-of-distribution classes, due to the high intrinsic variability inherent in EEG signals. As a result, many OOD detection methods that have shown good performance in other machine learning domains prove to be ineffective at identifying unfamiliar motor imagery patterns in BCIs. However, we also found that OOD detection performance is correlated with on-task performance, and that Deep Ensemble models and MC-Dropout models were able to achieve on-task AUROC > 0.9, and OOD detection ability up to 0.7. This shows that for models and subjects where task performance is high, rejecting unfamiliar cognitive states becomes feasible.Our research demonstrates a Leave-One-Class-Out OOD detection setup as an experimental paradigm for studying whether models are robust against unfamiliar motor imagery actions that were not seen in the training data. This is the first benchmark in Motor Imagery BCI that evaluates this type of OOD data. Our results demonstrate how to improve the overall safety and reliability of BCIs by preventing erroneous actions.
  </details>

- **[Towards Generalist Models for Molecular Property Prediction: Distilling Verbalized Knowledge from Decision Trees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.12344)**  `arXiv:2603.12344`  `cs.LG`  
  _Khiem Le, Marcos Martínez Galindo, Vanessa Lopez, Sreejata Dey, Ting Hua, Nitesh V. Chawla, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Molecular Property Prediction (MPP) is a fundamental problem in drug discovery that has recently attracted growing attention. Large Language Models (LLMs), known for their impressive proficiency across domains, show promise as generalist models for MPP. However, their current performance remains below the threshold needed for practical adoption. To bridge this gap, we propose TreeKD for distilling the knowledge of tree-based specialist models into LLMs to complement the internal knowledge of LLMs and improve their predictive accuracy. For each property, we train a specialist decision tree using features derived from 40K functional groups in the input molecules. Then, the predictive rule learned by the decision tree, which encodes its knowledge, is verbalized and incorporated into the prompts for training LLMs. In addition, by replacing a single decision tree with a Random Forest, we introduce a test-time scaling technique called rule-consistency, which aggregates predictions generated from different prompts constructed with different rules. An extensive evaluation with two LLMs, Gemma-2-2B and Granite-3.3-2B, on the TDC benchmark with 22 prediction tasks shows that our method substantially enhances the performance of LLMs, advancing the development of generalist models for MPP.
  </details>

- **[Reasoning under uncertainty in humans and language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.08693)**  `arXiv:2602.08693`  `cs.LG`  
  _Gonçalo Guiomar, Elia Torre, Victoria Shavina, Pehuen Moure, Mario Giulianelli, Shih-Chii Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Do language models make decisions under uncertainty like humans do? And if so, what role does extended reasoning play in the underlying decision process? We answer this question by introducing an active probabilistic reasoning task that cleanly separates sampling (actively acquiring evidence) from inference (integrating evidence towards a decision). Benchmarking humans and a broad set of contemporary LLMs against an optimal reference policy reveals a consistent pattern: extended reasoning is the key determinant of strong performance, driving large gains in inference, while yielding only modest improvements in active sampling. To explain these differences, we fit a behavioral model that captures systematic deviations from optimal Bayesian behavior through interpretable parameter families, placing humans and models in a shared low-dimensional cognitive space. The resulting fits show how reasoning shifts models toward human-like regimes of evidence accumulation and belief-to-choice mapping, and yield testable predictions about the latent dynamics that might drive each decision. Probing residual-stream activations of an open-weight reasoning model, we find that the geometry of internal representations tracks these predicted dynamics, linking behavior to representational correlates of the fitted latent dynamics.
  </details>

- **[Flow Matching for Offline Reinforcement Learning with Discrete Actions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.06138)**  `arXiv:2602.06138`  `cs.LG`  
  _Fairoz Nower Khan, Nabuat Zaman Nahim, Ruiquan Huang, Haibo Yang, Peizhong Ju_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative policies based on diffusion models and flow matching have shown strong promise for offline reinforcement learning (RL), but their applicability remains largely confined to continuous action spaces. To address a broader range of offline RL settings, we extend flow matching to a general framework that supports discrete action spaces with multiple objectives. Specifically, we replace continuous flows with continuous-time Markov chains, trained using a Q-weighted flow matching objective. We then extend our design to multi-agent settings, mitigating the exponential growth of joint action spaces via a factorized conditional path. For an oracle single policy-improvement step from the behavior distribution, we theoretically show that, under idealized conditions, the Q-weighted objective recovers the KL-regularized Boltzmann policy. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method performs robustly across diverse settings and benchmarks, including high-dimensional control, multi-agent games, and dynamically changing preferences over multiple objectives, while achieving competitive performance and demonstrating advantages in multimodal decision-making and preference-conditioned policy representation. Our discrete framework can also be applied to continuous-control problems through action quantization, providing a flexible trade-off between representational complexity and performance.
  </details>

- **[Anomaly Detection via Autoencoder Composite Features and NCE](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.01920)**  `arXiv:2502.01920`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yalin Liao, Austin J. Brockmeier_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unsupervised anomaly detection is a challenging task. Autoencoders (AEs) or generative models are often employed to model the data distribution of normal inputs and subsequently identify anomalous, out-of-distribution inputs by high reconstruction error or low likelihood, respectively. However, AEs may generalize and achieve small reconstruction errors on abnormal inputs. We propose a decoupled training approach for anomaly detection that both an AE and a likelihood model trained with noise contrastive estimation (NCE). After training the AE, NCE estimates a probability density function, to serve as the anomaly score, on the joint space of the AE's latent representation combined with features of the reconstruction quality. To further reduce the false negative rate in NCE we systematically varying the reconstruction features to augment the training and optimize the contrastive Gaussian noise distribution. Experimental assessments on multiple benchmark datasets demonstrate that the proposed approach matches the performance of prevalent state-of-the-art anomaly detection algorithms.
  </details>

- **[Neural Graph Simulator for Complex Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.09120)**  `arXiv:2411.09120`  `cs.LG`  
  _Hoyun Choi, Sungyeop Lee, B. Kahng, Junghyo Jo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Simulating complex systems governed by coupled ordinary differential equations (ODEs) typically requires explicit governing equations and incurs high computational cost from iterative numerical solvers. We propose the Neural Graph Simulator (NGS), a general-purpose data-driven framework that learns multivariate continuous-time dynamics directly from noisy and incomplete observations, without prior knowledge of the equations. Unlike neural ODE-based approaches that depend on repeated solver evaluations, NGS approximates the state transition integral using elapsed time intervals as direct inputs. This eliminates numerical integration while naturally supporting irregular sampling and heterogeneous time scales. Through its graph network architecture, NGS generalizes to unseen graph structures and larger system sizes, as demonstrated on independently sampled Erdős--Rényi graphs. We evaluate NGS on comprehensive benchmarks covering linear diffusion, nonlinear and chaotic dynamics, systems with stiffness-like behavior, and real-world spatiotemporal datasets. NGS combines competitive accuracy across benchmarks with substantial speedups over numerical solvers in computationally demanding settings. Notably, NGS exhibits strong extrapolation to unseen graph sizes and longer time horizons, and supports non-uniform time steps. These results establish NGS as an efficient and scalable alternative to solver-dependent neural simulators.
  </details>

- **[MVSTF: Multi-View Spatio-Temporal Fusion for LiDAR-Based Moving Object Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20872)**  `arXiv:2611.20872`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Jiaoju Zhou, Chao Ye, Xuebo Yang, Weiyang Lin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous mobile robots confront significant chal lenges in recognizing the motion properties of objects in dy namic environments. However, existing methods are hindered by limitations such as boundary ambiguity, occlusion, viewpoint constraints, and training complexity. In this paper, we propose an innovative end-to-end Multi-View Spatio-Temporal Fusion network, named MVSTF, which utilizes sequential point clouds to generate multi-view images and feature differences, and designs a multi-branch network to extract spatio-temporal and dimen sional features in parallel, and realizes high-precision moving object segmentation. First, sequential point clouds are utilized to generate multi-view images and feature differences. Then, an end-to-end multi-branch network including Dual Down-sampling Blocks (DDB), Spatial Attention Blocks (SAB), Multi-Kernel Convolutional Neural Network blocks (MKCNN), Dual Feature Fusion Blocks (DFFB), and the Point Fusion (PF) module, is designed to extract motion features from feature differences and geometric and semantic features from multi-view images. Finally, an auxiliary semantic head is introduced to further improve the algorithm's robustness and the model's interpretability. This work is validated on the SemanticKITTI and KITTI-Road bench marks, achieving state-of-the-art results on the SemanticKITTI MOS validation set and the KITTI-Road validation set, and competitive results on the hidden SemanticKITTI-MOS test set. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[TRR: A Thermal Interaction Engine for Robot Simulation and RGBT Data Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20792)**  `arXiv:2611.20792`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Biwen Yang, Zhe Cao, Jin Zhang, Haoyang Sun, Zonglu Hu, Jintao Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A robot's actions change the thermal conditions of its next interaction: heat retained after release, carried with objects or stored beneath the surface can change a later contact's outcome despite similar current temperatures. Training trajectories must capture how these effects connect earlier actions to later observations and task outcomes. We present TRR, a \emph{Thermal Robot Renderer} that attaches spatial and depth-resolved thermal state to both contact partners, updates local heat exchange through contact, transport and separation, and generates synchronized RGB--thermal (RGBT) observations and event-local task checks on one execution clock. Controlled interventions show why retained heat, spatial transport and internal storage matter for subsequent contact outcomes. From the same state, TRR produces a paired six-task benchmark of 2,224 static--dynamic pairs, that is, 4,448 trajectories with synchronized dual-view observations, states and actions. Visible thermal history raises safe contact-site selection from 75.0\% to 91.7\% on a dedicated learning corpus, and training on dynamic histories raises simulated transfer completion by 50 percentage points under a new heating procedure with shared feedback execution. We demonstrate RGBT observation adapters on MuJoCo and Isaac Sim, report an engineering inventory of ten prepared robot configurations and six demonstrated camera viewpoints, and describe a developer workflow that carries an existing robot scene to exported RGBT records. The benchmark, the workflow and the platform resources described in Section 6 form the first items of a planned public release. Demonstrations and resource updates are available on the project page.
  </details>

- **[BEVCast: Path-Adaptive BEV for Efficient End-to-End Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08631)**  `arXiv:2611.08631`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Qiren Wang, Mingyu Guo, Zixuan Wang, Weisong Shi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Camera-based driving models build a dense bird's-eye-view (BEV) grid and spend the same computation on every cell of it, although the vehicle crosses only a few of those cells within the planning horizon. We present BEVCast, a two-stage BEV whose resolution adapts to the predicted driving path. A coarse pass encodes the whole scene on a grid of 4.096 m cells. A heat head trained on the recorded path assigns a heat to every coarse cell and the cells with the highest heat are refined into 1.024 m cells under a fixed budget. The remaining cells keep their coarse resolution, so the trajectory head reads a mixed-resolution BEV that still covers the entire scene and needs neither an HD map nor a navigation command. Against a full grid of 1.024 m cells with the same architecture and training recipe, BEVCast at a 10% refinement budget encodes 28% of the BEV queries and needs 66% of the BEV encoder time at batch size 1. On nuScenes its mean displacement error is 1.078 m against 1.149 m and its drivable area violation rate is 1.46% against 2.29%. On NAVSIM navtest its mean displacement error is 0.577 m against 0.598 m and its extended predictive driver model score is 82.51 against 81.28. Next to published planners BEVCast reaches a comparable error on both benchmarks without a navigation command. The resolution of a driving BEV can therefore follow the task instead of the grid.
  </details>

- **[HAVANA: A Hierarchical Video-Action Model for Vision-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24122)**  `arXiv:2610.24122`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Dongzhihan Wang, Yi Du, Chen Wang, Xiuxian Li, Liang Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language Navigation (VLN) requires long-horizon instruction-consistent planning and short-horizon path planning grounded in the currently observed geometry. Anticipating how future observations evolve along the instructed route provides predictive guidance beyond the current field of view, motivating video-action models (VAMs) that learn representations of future visual states. However, many existing VAMs couple future visual prediction with action generation during inference, despite their distinct temporal requirements: instruction-conditioned route guidance can be reused across multiple control steps, whereas local trajectories must continuously adapt to fresh observations. To bridge this gap, we introduce HAVANA, a dual-system VAM that decouples predictive reasoning from reactive local planning. A slow system produces a compact language-conditioned latent plan from observation history, while a fast system combines the latest plan with the current observation to generate local trajectories. Their asynchronous execution enables rapid adaptation without repeated future prediction, and no future video is generated during inference. Experiments on OmniNavBench demonstrate state-of-the-art navigation performance over prior baselines, while zero-shot real-world deployments further show robust generalization to unseen environments.
  </details>

- **[ActionUNet: Improving Robustness of VLA Models with Efficient Multi-scale Fine-tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34982)**  `arXiv:2609.34982`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Di Zhu, Ziheng Yan, Fang Wan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have shown great promise for robotic manipulation by mapping multi-modal semantics to physical actions. However, this mapping inherently struggles to align these coarse-grained semantics with fine-grained temporal execution. It leaves VLA models with limited generalization and insufficient robustness in cluttered environments. To overcome this issue, we propose ActionUNet, an efficient multi-scale fine-tuning framework that enhances pre-trained VLA models with minimal computational cost. ActionUNet first constructs a lightweight temporal U-Net within the temporal-aligned action feature space to fuse hierarchical structural priors, effectively bridging the scale gap between semantics and temporal executions. Recognizing that multi-scale modeling can disrupt microscopic temporal continuity and cause mechanical oscillations, ActionUNet then employs a conditional SIREN as a continuous action decoder. Equipped with explicit second-order smoothness constraints, this decoder guarantees temporal continuity and reduces high-frequency motion jitter. By smoothing temporal discontinuities from multi-scale fusion, this continuous formulation reduces mechanical execution failures while preserving the base VLA model's generalization and manipulation robustness. Extensive experiments on RoboTwin 2.0 and LIBERO-Plus benchmarks, together with real-world hard evaluations, demonstrate that ActionUNet significantly improves {\pi}0.5 success rates by absolute 9.8%, 6.1%, and 11.4%, respectively, while also generalizing to the regression-based OpenVLA-OFT backbone, highlighting its effectiveness and efficiency as a fine-tuning strategy. Code and implementation details are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[PRIME: Perception Feedback with Situational Memory Embeddings in VLA Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22040)**  `arXiv:2609.22040`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Erik Deinzer, Naya Baslan, Luca Paparusso, Narunas Vaskevicius, Peter Knott, Luigi Palmieri_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models for autonomous driving operate primarily through feedforward inference across the perception--reasoning--planning hierarchy. While modern architectures maintain temporal recurrence within the perceptual module, early perception remains blind to downstream reasoning and navigation goals, processing visual inputs agnostically without prioritizing cues informed by prior decisions. To bridge this gap, this paper introduces PRIME, a learned feedback mechanism that conditions the VLA perceptual queries on a novel Situational Memory. By aggregating latent representations of past perception, reasoning, navigation goals, and predicted behaviors across an L-step window via cross-attention, PRIME enables intent-driven perceptual attention at minimal computational cost, adding only a maximum of 29.7M parameters (0.41% of the 7.3B-parameter base model). Evaluated on the Bench2Drive closed-loop benchmark, PRIME achieves a state-of-the-art Driving Score of 82.47 (+4.73 over ORION) and a Success Rate of 60.00% (+5.38 percentage points), the highest reported Driving Score among published VLAs trained on Think2Drive demonstrations.
  </details>

- **[When Do Histology Foundation Models Need Geometric Test-Time Augmentation?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21565)**  `arXiv:2611.21565`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Lars Chen, Valentino F. Foit, Dorry Segev, Michal Mankowski_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Test-time augmentation (TTA) aggregates model predictions across multiple transformed views of an input during inference. Because histology images lack a canonical orientation, $D_4$ TTA---pooling across the 8 rotations and reflections of a square---is a standard default in computational pathology. However, the rise of self-supervised histology foundation models (FMs) with inherent rotational invariance calls its continued utility into question. We benchmark 9 histology FMs and 6 general-purpose models across four patch-level and two whole-slide image (WSI) datasets. We find that $D_4$ TTA remains a reliable post hoc improvement, but benefits diminish from $+1.98$ percentage points (pp) for general-purpose backbones to $+0.50$ pp for histology FMs on TCGA-UT, and shrink further for the largest models. To eliminate computational overhead, our experiments demonstrate that entropy routing---applying TTA selectively to uncertain predictions---recovers most of the gain while augmenting only ${\sim}13\%$ of samples. Finally, we show that patch-level TTA improvements do not transfer to slide-level multiple instance learning (MIL). Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[RAGE: Residual-Adaptive Gating for Flow-Based Image Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21177)**  `arXiv:2611.21177`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Ruofeng Mei, Xiangyu Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent inversion-free, flow-based image editing methods evolve the source image toward the target via a differential velocity field. We observe that this velocity field does not explicitly account for which regions should be preserved, leading to unintended drift in non-editing regions. To address this, we formulate source fidelity control as a stochastic optimal control problem in which a selective fidelity term on the editing residual is enforced along the editing trajectory. Solving this problem and approximating its solution yields the Residual Gating Principle: each latent component is pulled back toward the source with a gain that decays exponentially with the local residual magnitude, so that small deviations are suppressed while large, semantically meaningful edits pass through unimpeded. The proposed method, RAGE, requires only one lightweight gated interpolation per sampling step, involves no mask estimation, attention maps, retraining, or architecture-specific modifications, and is applied on top of FlowEdit-based editors. On PIE-Bench, RAGE achieves the best overall rank among 17 methods and substantially improves background preservation on both Stable Diffusion 3 and FLUX backbones while maintaining competitive editing quality.
  </details>

- **[Illusion of Explainability: A Sanity Check for Spectral Band Attribution in Satellite Time-Series Crop Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19889)**  `arXiv:2611.19889`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Sumit Tahiliani, Avi Mathur, Sandarbh Agarwal, Rakshith Sathish, Ashwathi Nambiar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Attribution methods are commonly validated through convergence across methods and consistency with domain knowledge, yet neither criterion establishes whether identified features are necessary for a task. This limitation is particularly relevant in multispectral remote sensing, where overlapping spectral information makes individual bands potentially substitutable. We investigate this gap in crop-type classification using GradientSHAP, Shapley sampling, and Occlusion on a U-TAE segmentation model trained on Sentinel-2 time series from the PASTIS benchmark. All three methods identify vegetation-sensitive spectral regions consistent with domain knowledge, while Shapley and Occlusion exhibit strong ordering sensitivity in deletion and insertion tests. However, these properties do not reliably predict retraining performance. Aggregate attribution consensus masks substantial per-class disagreement, with pairwise mean Spearman correlations ranging from 0.09 to 0.79. Models retrained from scratch using the five lowest-ranked bands perform comparably to or better than those using the five highest-ranked bands. Furthermore, attribution rankings from an independently trained model with identical configuration and seed identify a different top-five subset that yields better retraining performance. Analysis of all 252 five-band subsets reveals consistently low effective dimensionality (1.35 to 2.62), suggesting substantial spectral redundancy. These findings demonstrate that plausible and apparently faithful attribution rankings do not necessarily identify task-relevant bands for improved retrained performance, highlighting the limitations of band-level feature attribution in spectrally redundant input spaces.
  </details>

- **[Toward an Optimal Configuration for Tree Species Classification from Bark Images: A Leakage-Safe Ablation Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05680)**  `arXiv:2611.05680`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Maeve Côté_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Identifying tree species from bark photographs matters for forest inventory and the wood-processing industry, yet the design choices behind deep-learning approaches have not been studied systematically, and some reported results may be inflated by images of the same trunk appearing in both training and test sets. We present a systematic ablation on BarkNet 1.0 (20 species) under a tree-level, leakage-safe five-fold cross-validation, using ConvNeXt-V2 backbones with attention-based multiple instance learning (AMIL). We vary patch size, aggregation strategy, training regime, backbone capacity, the whole-image baseline, and test-time bag size, and we measure leakage directly with a matched image-level split. Per-patch accuracy rises with patch size (75.2% at 160 px to 89.3% at 512 px), and image-level accuracy plateaus near 94.9% at 512 px. AMIL exceeds majority voting by at most 0.7 pp; soft voting and mean pooling do no better than hard voting, so the gain comes from the learned attention weights and vanishes at 512 px, where attention is near-uniform. Backbone capacity changes accuracy by less than a point, and two-stage training gives no advantage. Whole-image classification (91.9%) trails patch aggregation, and accuracy saturates around 64 patches per image. Under an image-level split, the same configuration reaches 99.9% (+5.0 pp) and the whole-image baseline 99.2% (+7.3 pp), an inflation as large as the entire effect of patch size that also erases the differences between design choices. We recommend patches as large as possible while leaving enough to aggregate (about 512 px here), a nano backbone, and tree-level splits for bark benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[LatentUM: Unleashing the Potential of Interleaved Cross-Modal Reasoning via a Latent-Space Unified Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.02097)**  `arXiv:2604.02097`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Jiachun Jin, Zetong Zhou, Xiao Yang, Hao Zhang, Pengfei Liu, Jun Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unified models (UMs) hold promise for their ability to understand and generate content across heterogeneous modalities. Compared to merely generating visual content, the use of UMs for interleaved cross-modal reasoning is more promising and valuable, e.g., for solving understanding problems that require dense visual thinking, improving visual generation through self-reflection, or modeling visual dynamics of the physical world guided by stepwise action interventions. However, existing UMs typically rely on pixel decoding as a bridge when their visual representations for understanding and generation are disjoint, which is both ineffective and computationally cumbersome. In this paper, we introduce LatentUM, a novel unified model that represents all modalities within a shared semantic latent space, eliminating pixel-space mediation for model-internal reasoning over generated visual states. By making generated visual tokens directly interpretable to the model itself, LatentUM enables flexible interleaved cross-modal reasoning and generation. Empirically, LatentUM achieves state-of-the-art performance on the Visual Spatial Planning benchmark, pushes the limits of visual generation through self-reflection and demonstrates world modeling capability by predicting future visual states in the shared semantic latent space. Code and models are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Sfumato Supervision: Learning Instance Segmentation with Noisy Labels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.10891)**  `arXiv:2406.10891`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Moshe Kimhi, Ehud Rivlin, Chaim Baskin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Instance segmentation requires precise object masks, yet both human annotators and promptable models can produce unreliable contours. A high prediction loss near a contour does not necessarily indicate an annotation error: valid edges can also be uncertain. Inspired by Leonardo da Vinci's \emph{sfumato}, which softens transitions between a subject and its surroundings, we introduce \textbf{\texttt{CB-LNL} (Copula-Boundary Learning with Noisy Labels)}. Rather than judging each contour pixel alone, CB-LNL combines foreground evidence inside the mask with background evidence outside it to guide local supervision and annotation repair. We ground this approach in an audit of paired COCO and LVIS annotations, and introduce benchmarks covering human annotations, weak-prompt SAM2 masks, and controlled corruptions in natural and driving scenes. Controlled comparisons show improved training on procedural contour noise and fewer harmful edits than direct teacher-mask replacement. Paired-seed and frozen-prediction evaluations reveal how these benefits depend on the annotation source, supporting separate assessment of category and contour reliability.
  </details>

- **[Balanced Twin Weight Factorization for Efficient MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21459)**  `arXiv:2611.21459`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Bryan A. Plummer, Shan Lu, Keval Doshi, Noranart Vesdapunt, Wei Wang, Jim Thomas, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Weight Factorization (WF) methods aim to reduce a model's latency, memory, or both. These typically decompose a pretrained model into two or more factors that approximate the original model weights with fewer parameters and floating point operations (FLOPs). However, translating fewer FLOPs into reduced latency is challenging for WF methods in practice due to overhead introduced by replacing a single large weight matrix with multiple smaller ones. To address this, we propose Balanced Twin Weight Factorization (BTWF), which uses the unique structure of factorized models to simultaneously lower latency and increase learning capacity. Specifically, BTWF operates in the shared basis WF setting, where two or more layers are decomposed into a shared basis matrix and a layer-specific mixing matrix. If these shared layers have the same input (i.e., are twins), then we need only perform this shared forward pass once, reducing inference time. However, twin layers are often of varying sizes, and methods from prior work tend to favor the larger layer. In contrast, our approach boosts performance by combining Factor Component Balancing (FCB), which ensures every shared member is well represented in the selected factors, with Block Contribution Balancing (BCB), which considers downstream model effects. Further, we also show that an overparameterized BTWF, where some layer's factors result in higher FLOPs than the original during training, can boost task performance without additional resources at test time. Experiments on six models over nine benchmarks show BTWF obtains a 2-6% gain over the state-of-the-art without expensive finetuning (1-3.5% gain with it) at comparable computational costs.
  </details>

- **[AnachroBench: Evaluating Historical Consistency in Large Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21292)**  `arXiv:2611.21292`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Keunhyeung Park, Byeonggeuk Lim, Kahyeon Kim, Sunhee Jo, Hayeong Ryu, Hoejoon Kwon, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing Large Vision-Language Model benchmarks primarily evaluate visual understanding in general everyday scenarios, leaving historical consistency with a specified era and country largely underexplored. To address this gap, we propose AnachroBench, a controlled discriminative benchmark for evaluating historical consistency in visual scenes. It contains 1,600 image pairs from eight countries spanning the late 19th to the early 20th century, each pairing an original historical image with an anachronistic version containing a seamlessly inserted modern object. AnachroBench evaluates three aspects: Inserted Object Perception, which tests whether models detect the inserted modern object; Era-Country Consistency, which assesses whether the scene is consistent with the specified era and country; and Conditional Era-Country Consistency, which measures consistency judgments only when the inserted object is correctly perceived. A false consistency judgment occurs when a model perceives the modern object but still judges the scene as historically consistent. Overall, AnachroBench reveals a gap between inserted object perception and era-country consistency judgments, providing a targeted evaluation of this behavior.
  </details>

- **[Trait Collapse in Text-to-Image Generation: Evaluating and Mitigating Personality Inconsistency in Visual Storytelling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20381)**  `arXiv:2611.20381`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Yuanbo Tong, Haoyuan Li, Jibiao Jiang, Zirui Wang, Yuchen Li, Jiamou Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In visual storytelling, a believable character must not only look consistent across scenes but also \textit{behave} in ways that reflect their personality. Current text-to-image (T2I) models preserve visual appearance effectively, yet consistently fail to maintain personality-relevant behavior---a failure we term \textit{trait collapse}. We show that conventional metrics (CLIP Score, DINO similarity) are blind to this failure, and that abstract personality descriptors and scene-level emotion labels do not produce consistent improvements over neutral prompts across models. To systematically diagnose trait collapse, we introduce \textbf{ViStoryBench-Personality}, a story-level benchmark combining an expert-validated Visual Cues Lexicon (Fleiss' $\kappa = 0.757$) with a six-dimensional metric suite covering personality alignment, cross-scene stability, and behavioral coherence. Evaluating seven T2I systems, we confirm a persistent semantic gap between abstract trait labels and visual evidence, and find that visual identity consistency does not imply personality consistency. To bridge this gap, we propose \textbf{Trait-Behavior-Visual (TBV)}, a training-free prompting framework that decomposes personality traits into renderable visual constraints. TBV improves Personality Alignment Score by up to $+22.1\%$, with larger gains in models with stronger language-conditioning capacity.
  </details>

- **[HEED: Density-Weighted Residual Alignment for Hybrid Vision-Language Model Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17093)**  `arXiv:2605.17093`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Yihao Liang, Niraj K. Jha_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Distilling vision-language models into faster hybrid architectures, such as 3:1 Mamba-2/attention mixes, is now standard practice for making inference efficient. Aggregate benchmarks suggest that this works but they hide selective failures. When we distill Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct into a 3:1 Mamba-2/attention hybrid, student model stays within 2 points of the teacher across visual reasoning benchmarks like MMStar, MMBench, and MMMU-Pro, while dropping 13 points on optical-character-recognition and document tasks. The student can still understand the scene but loses the fine-grained text needed to answer. We localize much of the failure to a specific kind of position. In a high-resolution image, most patches are sky, wall, or smooth texture, while a small fraction carries text, edges, object boundaries, or other local details. In a token-level diagnostic, the top 10% highest-density patches have 3.6x larger residual drift than the bottom 10% lowest-density patches and 3.5x larger teacher-masking answer contribution. Uniform weighting devotes many loss terms to low-information background patches, whereas sparse answer bearing patches receive no special protection. The required intervention is minimal: we replace uniform residual alignment with density-weighted residual alignment, using patch self-dissimilarity as a training-free proxy for position importance. We call this HEED. Compared with normal end-to-end distillation, HEED increases performance by 8.7 points on OCRBench v2 and 5.13 points on a 10-benchmark average. The gain is realized on different teacher models. After standard post-training, the student reaches teacher-level performance on the 10-benchmark average. HEED adds no parameters and no inference-time cost. Hence, the student maintains the hybrid architecture's 4.12x throughput and 68% memory saving at 128k context. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ReactBench-AD: Do Driving VLAs and World-Action Models React to Imminent Hazards in Their Path?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20011)**  `arXiv:2611.20011`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _Nikos Theodoridis, Reenu Mohandas, Sushil Sharma, Ciarán Hogan, Ganesh Sistu, Anthony Scanlan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language-Action models (VLAs) and World-Action Models (WAMs) have emerged in autonomous driving with the promise of strong generalization to long-tail scenarios. Although these models achieve state-of-the-art results on autonomous driving benchmarks, most existing benchmarks are dominated by nominal scenarios and therefore cannot test this promise. To test one such long-tail capability that is critical for safety, we introduce ReactBench-AD, a benchmark that measures how driving models react to objects that appear in the ego vehicle's path. Built on real NAVSIM and PhysicalAI-Autonomous-Vehicles scenes, ReactBench-AD composites a crossing pedestrian, a standing pedestrian, or a stationary car consistently across all camera views and history frames. Each edited scene thus forms an exact, pixel-level counterfactual pair with its original, which allows us to isolate the causal effect of the inserted object on the predicted trajectory. Evaluating four VLAs and two WAMs, we find that they react weakly, often barely at all. Finally, we test how easily one of the evaluated VLAs, ReCogDrive, can be taught to brake hard for imminent hazards: a short fine-tuning stage on edited scenes substantially improves its reactions, with the learned behavior generalizing to unseen objects and fully real scenes, at minimal cost to nominal driving. These results call into question the out-of-the-box long-tail capabilities of these models, but suggest that they can be a strong foundation for acquiring them with little additional training, highlighting the value of real or synthetic data that demonstrates the long-tail behaviors nominal datasets rarely contain. Code and data will be made publicly available.
  </details>

- **[Orbis 2: A Hierarchical World Model for Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15898)**  `arXiv:2607.15898`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG` `cs.RO`  
  _Sudhanshu Mittal, Arian Mousakhan, Silvio Galesso, Karim Farid, Johannes Dienert, Rajat Sahay, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current world models operate at a single level of abstraction, with most prioritizing perceptual fidelity while lacking the spatial reasoning and semantic understanding required for real-world downstream tasks. We present a hierarchical driving world model that factorizes future prediction across two levels operating at distinct temporal and abstraction scales: a high-level predictor that forecasts coarse scene structure over extended temporal horizons, and a low-level generator that produces detailed predictions conditioned on the high-level output. This decomposition yields high perceptual fidelity while also capturing strong spatial and semantic representations. We further show that pretraining with a diffusion forcing objective yields substantially richer internal representations than the standard teacher forcing objective, while teacher forcing -- predicting only the next frame from clean context -- produces more stable autoregressive rollouts. We therefore introduce a generic two-stage training paradigm that pretrains the model with diffusion forcing and fine-tunes with teacher forcing, combining the representational benefits of the former with the rollout stability of the latter. Our approach achieves state-of-the-art results across the standard suite of driving world model evaluations on established benchmarks, including long-horizon generation fidelity, steering responsiveness evaluated on counterfactual scenarios, and internal representation quality. Project page with code, demo, checkpoints and qualitative results:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Unreasonable Confidence from Simulator Misspecification: Directional Robustness on the SPEED+ Sim-to-Real Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20321)**  `arXiv:2611.20321`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Nikolay Dolgov_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-based navigation systems for orbit must be trained almost entirely on synthetic imagery, because real labelled images of a given spacecraft in flight rarely exist. Benchmarks like SPEED+ are built for that situation: the training set comes from a simulator, and the test sets are real images of a satellite mockup under two kinds of lighting. That simulator approximates how light reflects off mylar and aluminium, and like every scientific simulator it is misspecified. Its error is radiometric, since it misses the specular highlights, hard shadows and sensor saturation common in real images. We probe the resulting synthetic-to-real gap, the central risk for such systems, with a deliberately simple monocular pose estimator. In a 2x2 study of backbone adaptation and photometric augmentation, trained on synthetic images and tested on both real hardware-in-the-loop domains, we find three things. (1) The model does not know it is failing: regions conformally calibrated to 90% on synthetic data cover only 9-15% of rotation errors on sunlamp and 21-38% on lightbox, so a wrong pose carries the confidence of a correct one. (2) Transfer is asymmetric: scaling data and features improved synthetic rotation error from 1.65 to 1.03 rad but moved the harshly lit sunlamp domain by only 0.05 rad, under twice its seed s.d., whereas adapting the backbone's last stage carried 63-83% of its extra synthetic gain to both real domains. (3) Once the backbone adapts, augmentation helps only in the direction it models: our photometric stack improved the diffusely lit lightbox domain from 1.17 to 0.99 rad on every seed but moved sunlamp no more than seed-to-seed variation, while with the backbone frozen it helped sunlamp more. Every post-review experiment was pre-registered, and the code, per-image predictions and pre-registration record are atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Self-supervised learning for zebrafish embryo phenotype evaluation: Systematic comparison of algorithms, label-efficient classification, and transfer learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20269)**  `arXiv:2611.20269`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Thomas Lautenschlager, Gaëlle Hayot, Quentin Brassart, Angelo Jovin Yamachui Sitcheu, Linus Schilpp, Tijana Geroski, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Zebrafish embryos are a prominent model organism for image-based phenotyping and especially suited for large-scale imaging studies, for which machine learning (ML) models can automate evaluation. However, training ML models usually requires large labeled datasets, and differences in imaging conditions often impede reuse across laboratories. Self-supervised learning (SSL) addresses both constraints by learning transferable representations from unlabeled images. Yet central aspects like the choice of algorithm remain underexplored for domain-specific applications. To close this gap, we benchmark popular SSL algorithms on the public EmbryoNet dataset, explore label-efficient learning, and test transfer to an unseen setting. Using representations from our strongest model, a ResNet50 network trained with SwAV, a simple linear classifier reaches 86.4% accuracy and a non-linear classifier 87.1%, rivaling the fully supervised EmbryoNet model (87.1%). The SwAV model decisively outperforms supervised training from scratch when labels are scarce (70.2% vs. 45.7% accuracy with 5% of labels). On a three-class problem (normal, dead, falsely cropped) with a small, previously unseen zebrafish toxicity dataset, recorded in a different setting, our model reaches 81.3% accuracy without retraining, while EmbryoNet achieves 75.9%. Overall, self-supervised models for zebrafish embryos seem to mostly facilitate label-efficiency and transfer to new settings rather than improving supervised results.
  </details>
