- **[Hard or Mislabeled? Failure Screens Enrich Vision Benchmarks for Label Errors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19044)**  `arXiv:2611.19044`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Haolin Guo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hard vision benchmarks often keep images that strong classifiers get wrong. Since a classifier is also marked wrong when the label is wrong, such failure screens may collect label errors. We measure this on ImageNet's twice-relabeled validation set with 103 classifiers. Among the 1,892 images that all 66 supervised classifiers get wrong, 61% have a wrong label, versus 11.1% overall. The fewer images a screen lets through, the more of them are mislabeled, along approximately one curve. Fit on one model family, this curve predicts the screens of two held-out model families with a mean error of 2.4 percentage points, and replays of ImageNet-D's and ImageNet-Hard's screens within 0.1 and 6.3 points. On new pools, ImageNet-V2 and CIFAR-10, enrichment at the strictest screens is stronger than the curve predicts: on ImageNet-V2, by human relabeling, 67% of the images that all 66 classifiers get wrong are mislabeled, against 46% predicted. Screens that let models choose among only some of the classes, as ImageNet-A's does (200 of 1,000), fall below the curve. By ReImageNet, 32% of ImageNet-Hard's ImageNet images have no correct label. ScreenAudit, which comprises an audit tool, a script that rebuilds corrected answer sets for the 5,464 decided ImageNet-Hard images (of 5,646) from ReImageNet's gated release, and per-image audits of 1,198 benchmark images, will be released later.
  </details>

- **[What Does Hard-Negative Fine-Tuning Teach Multimodal Large Language Models? Controlled Evidence from a Compositional Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18701)**  `arXiv:2611.18701`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Ranjith Chodavarapu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fine-tuning on hard negatives is a popular way to improve the compositional reasoning of vision-language models, but when accuracy goes up it is rarely clear what the model actually learned. We ask this question for multimodal large language models. We use CompHall, 2,860 forced-choice questions (spatial, attribute, counting and part-whole) with two graded hard negatives each, three model families (LLaVA-1.5, LLaVA-NeXT and Idefics2), and controls that such studies often lack: gray-image evaluation, three seeds, option-order tests, control arms trained on different question types, and independent benchmarks. We find four things. First, CompHall is not a pure test of vision: part-whole questions can be answered from the text alone (66-95% without the image), and per-image accuracy is unrelated to an object-hallucination probe (r = -0.05), so we see no evidence that compositional failures drive hallucination. Second, the original fine-tuning method scored mostly image-placeholder tokens and hurt both LLaVA models. With a corrected loss, fine-tuning raises accuracy on the spatial-relation questions of CV-Bench in all three families, by +3.2, +23.1 and +6.0 points (averaged over both option orders). Control arms show that the gain needs spatial training questions, and for LLaVA-NeXT about two-thirds of it appears even on relation words that were only wrong options in training. Third, the gain is not general: on CompHall itself it is significant only for LLaVA-NeXT (+6.9 points) and not detectable for Idefics2, it reaches about 3 points at most on true/false statements, and LLaVA-1.5 fine-tuned on other question types learns to answer "true" too often. Fourth, both losses raise the yes-bias of LLaVA-1.5 on POPE.
  </details>

- **[T2T Technical Report: Synthesizing GUI Agent Trajectories at Scale from Video Tutorials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18715)**  `arXiv:2611.18715`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Qingsong Xie, Yanlin Fei, Nazhou Liu, Jiacheng Tang, Madalina Ciobanu, Navan Preet Singh, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training generalist GUI agents requires large volumes of interaction trajectories comprising screenshot sequences paired with grounded UI actions, yet collecting such data through human demonstration is expensive and difficult to scale. Although public software tutorial videos contain rich operational demonstrations, their continuous pixel streams lack discrete action boundaries, semantic intents, and precise spatial coordinates, making them difficult to use directly for model training. To address this, we present T2T (Tutorial-to-Trajectory), a fully automated pipeline for synthesizing GUI agent trajectories at scale. T2T spans desktop, web, and mobile platforms to convert unannotated video tutorials into structured, executable interaction trajectories. To guarantee trajectory quality, T2T combines active segment filtering, keyframe identification via localized UI change, shortcut-free observation frame selection, presence-aware grounding, and two-stage action-space normalization. These refined trajectories are then converted into multi-task training views spanning navigation, planning, forward dynamics, and grounding. We fine-tune Qwen3-VL-8B on a subset of the more than one million human-annotation-free training samples generated by this pipeline. Evaluations show that the model achieves consistent gains across multiple benchmarks covering desktop, web, and mobile environments, including OSWorld-G, ScreenSpot-Pro, AndroidControl, and AgentNetBench, indicating improved core capabilities in grounding, step-level instruction execution, and trajectory-level decision-making. Notably, all gains are achieved without any in-distribution training data from target domains. Learning exclusively from trajectories extracted from public videos via the T2T pipeline, the model exhibits cross-application and cross-task generalization capabilities.
  </details>

- **[Seeing but Not Thinking: Routing Distraction in Multimodal Mixture-of-Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.08541)**  `arXiv:2604.08541`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Haolei Xu, Haiwen Hong, Hongxing Li, Rui Zhou, Yang Zhang, Longtao Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models have achieved remarkable performance on vision-language tasks. However, we identify a puzzling phenomenon termed Seeing but Not Thinking: models accurately perceive image content yet fail in subsequent reasoning, while correctly solving identical problems presented as pure text. Through systematic analysis, we first verify that cross-modal semantic sharing exists in MoE architectures, ruling out semantic alignment failure as the sole explanation. We then reveal that visual experts and domain experts exhibit layer-wise separation, with image inputs inducing significant routing divergence from text inputs in middle layers where domain experts concentrate. Based on these findings, we propose the Routing Distraction hypothesis: when processing visual inputs, the routing mechanism fails to adequately activate task-relevant reasoning experts. To validate this hypothesis, we design a routing-guided intervention method that enhances domain expert activation. Experiments on three multimodal MoE models across six benchmarks demonstrate consistent improvements, with gains of up to 3.17% on complex visual reasoning tasks. Our analysis further reveals that domain expert identification locates cognitive functions rather than sample-specific solutions, enabling effective transfer across tasks with different information structures.
  </details>

- **[ViRA: Toward Ultra-High-Resolution Visual Agents via Cross-Input Relative Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21605)**  `arXiv:2611.21605`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Huawei Lin, Jinting Liu, Tongyan Wang, Xuyang Chen, Ruihua Shi, Zhichao Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual agents answer questions about ultra-high-resolution photos by zooming into the relevant regions. However, existing agents often answer from common sense instead of reading the evidence. Reinforcement learning (RL) on the final answer cannot correct this, because a guess earns the same reward as reading. We propose Cross-Input Relative Policy Optimization (CrossRPO), which trains agents on contrast sets. A contrast set asks one question about several versions of an image that differ only in a small region with the evidence and have different answers, so only reading the evidence solves the whole set. CrossRPO rewards solving the whole set, and we prove that it does not reinforce a set answered from common sense. We build contrast sets for training (ViRA-Train) and evaluation (ViRA-Bench) by editing one small object in images of 21 to 120 megapixels, and we photograph further sets with real changes (ViRA-Bench-Real) to exclude editing traces. We train two Visual Reasoning Agents, ViRA-9B and ViRA-27B, with CrossRPO on only about 3,500 items of ViRA-Train each. CrossRPO achieves 63.3% set accuracy on ViRA-Bench at the maximum resolution, against 59.3% for GRPO. ViRA-9B is the most accurate open model of its size on all benchmarks, and ViRA-27B outperforms Claude Haiku 5.5 and GPT-6-Luna, both at their highest thinking level, on most benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[LynnReal-Hand: Unlocking World Interaction with Hand Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21479)**  `arXiv:2611.21479`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Shaohao Rui, Chaoyang Zhang, Xiaofeng Mao, Weijie Ma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Interactive video world models enable exploration through text and camera controls, yet direct object interaction requires finer control. Hands provide a natural interface, but translating continuous hand movements into coherent visual responses remains challenging because of complex hand-object dynamics and demanding real-time computation. To address these challenges, we introduce LynnReal-Hand, a hand-controlled video world model that unifies precise interaction modeling with efficient streaming generation. We curate 100K interaction clips spanning diverse real and synthetic environments and develop hand-aware supervision to improve fine-grained hand motions and object interactions. Building on this foundation, we learn a causal flow map for few-step generation and introduce region-aware score distillation that combines complementary video and image priors to refine interaction dynamics and visual appearance. To further reduce inference latency, we propose Ultra-Fast-VAE, a compact video decoder that preserves high reconstruction fidelity with substantially lower computational cost. Extensive evaluations on HandBench demonstrate superior hand-following accuracy and visual quality compared with existing approaches. Our full-sequence model achieves an FVD of 181.67 and a MANO Dice score of 93.02, while the three-step causal model maintains a MANO Dice score of 92.49. For real-time interaction, 540p streaming on a single H200 reaches 18.03 FPS with 741ms latency and an optimized peak GPU memory footprint of only 34.89GB. We will release the datasets, model weights, and the full training and inference code to support research on hand-controlled video world models.
  </details>

- **[Out of Sight, Into Memory: Compact Visual Latents Retain Local Evidence in Ultra-High-Resolution Remote Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21457)**  `arXiv:2611.21457`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Meng Tang, Siyuan Du, Yuxin Liu, Shuaishuai Cao, Di Wang, Haonan Guo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Question answering over ultra-high-resolution (UHR) remote sensing scenes poses a tension for multimodal large language models: global overviews obscure small objects and local attributes, whereas detailed crops consume substantial context. Compressing inspected crops into short sequences of continuous visual latents offers a compact memory once crop tokens are discarded, yet diagnostic studies on natural images find that models often ignore latent content, leaving unclear whether compact latents can retain fine-grained targets lost in downsampled UHR remote sensing scenes. To isolate evidence retention from region selection, we encode high-resolution crops into latent memory tokens for frozen backbones and evaluate both their predictive utility and content dependence. Across two generations of Qwen-VL on LRS-GRO, a 64-token memory achieves 7.6- to 9.9-fold compression of retained crop tokens and improves accuracy by 2.5 to 4.8 percentage points over the downsampled global image alone, recovering most of the high-resolution crop gain with consistent behavior on XLRS-Bench. Substituting memory from another image or another region of the same scene reduces accuracy by 5.8 to 7.4 percentage points and yields lower accuracy than removing memory altogether, demonstrating that predictions depend on the encoded regional evidence. Restoring the source crop within the same model reduces the accuracy loss caused by zeroing or removing memory. These findings establish that compact visual latents retain useful local evidence in UHR remote sensing and identify evidence availability as a key factor governing their contribution.
  </details>

- **[The Mask Is Not the Object: Volumetric Supervision for 3D Gaussian Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21383)**  `arXiv:2611.21383`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Jeonghwan Cho, Minsu Kim, Junyoung Hong, Seon Joo Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  3D Gaussian Splatting enables object-level operations on Gaussian primitives, yet existing segmentation methods are largely trained and evaluated through rendered views. This mismatch can produce accurate rendered masks from incomplete 3D selections since alpha-blended feature supervision concentrates learning on dominant contributors while leaving occluded or low-contribution Gaussians weakly supervised. We propose VODA, a plug-and-play supervision objective that exploits local depth structure to group per-pixel contributors and directly supervises all Gaussians in selected depth clusters, bypassing contribution-weighted gradient delivery. To evaluate volumetric completeness, we introduce Removal-3D, a synthetic object-removal benchmark, and the Removal Depth Error (RDE), a metric comparing predicted and ground-truth depth changes. Across four rendering-based baselines, VODA consistently improves volumetric segmentation on Removal-3D while maintaining or improving performance on LERF and ScanNet. Further analysis shows that VODA recovers lower-contribution Gaussians, while simple gradient reweighting and spatial propagation fail to reproduce comparable gains.
  </details>

- **[WeVisForensics: Self-Evolving Agent for Omni AI-Generated Video Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21373)**  `arXiv:2611.21373`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Yipeng Du, Yuxi Li, Ying Zhang, Chong Sun, Chen Li, Jing Lyu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in video generation call for reliable detection grounded in explicit forensic evidence. However, current methods primarily depend on single-pass chain-of-thought reasoning that overlooks brief or localized artifacts, or on predefined multi-agent workflows that offer limited flexibility in selecting relevant inspections. Even with adaptive investigation, optimizing the investigation policy alone does not directly improve the forensic capabilities of fixed experts. We introduce WeVisForensics, an agentic framework in which a main investigator agent coordinates a self-evolving library of forensic inspector sub-agents spanning visual and audio cues. The investigator learns which artifact type to examine, over which window, and when to conclude, and these interactions provide shared feedback for both levels of agents. Our dual-timescale policy optimization strategy, DTPO, closes this loop through fast reinforcement learning of the investigator and slower refinement of the inspectors, guided by annotated artifact intervals, to report subtle artifacts they once dismissed. Furthermore, we curate ForenCue-30K, combining role-specific forensic annotations and interaction trajectories to supervise individual inspections and their coordination, with an accompanying reinforcement-learning pool. Experiments show that WeVisForensics averages 95.2% detection accuracy across in-domain, out-of-domain and agentic benchmarks, 9.0% above the strongest prior detector, while grounding its verdicts in localized evidence.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Seeing: Fine-Grained Preference Validation and Severity-Aware Alignment for Mitigating Visual Hallucinations in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20478)**  `arXiv:2611.20478`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Abdulaziz Alqahtani, Alwaleed Alharthi, Muzammil Behzad_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large vision-language models can produce fluent responses containing objects, attributes, or relations unsupported by the image. We study an implemented alignment pipeline that normalizes released fine-grained hallucination supervision, validates chosen-rejected preference pairs with vision-language judges, repairs rejected chosen responses, and trains LLaVA adapters with standard or severity-aware direct preference optimization. The study uses 16,143 normalized feedback records and 8,386 released preference pairs, comparing direct training with zero-shot and two-shot judged severity-margin variants. The zero-shot variant reduces Object HalBench CHAIRs from 53.00 to 36.00 and CHAIRi from 15.72 to 10.41. Across aligned models, AMBER-Hal decreases from 35.90 to 25.10-26.90, accompanied by reduced visual coverage. In contrast, the base model retains the highest valid POPE adversarial F1, 84.18 versus 83.87-83.91 after alignment. These results support benchmark-dependent reductions in generative hallucination rather than uniform superiority of severity-aware alignment. We distinguish implemented pipeline components from the preference paths used in the reported experiments and discuss the limits of attributing gains to judging, repair, or the training objective individually.
  </details>

- **[Remember, Replay, Adapt: Learning from Past Experience in Online Vision-and-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19901)**  `arXiv:2611.19901`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Chenhao Ding, Jizhou Han, Lixin Xiu, Muyao Wang, Songlin Dong, Hideki Nakayama_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing feedback-based methods for online vision-and-language navigation (VLN) primarily learn from current episode outcomes, leaving previously acquired feedback underutilized. Inspired by human episodic memory, we propose R2A, an online VLN adaptation framework that continually reuses successful and failed navigation experiences for policy learning. Specifically, Success-Failure Memory (SFM) retains successful and failed navigation episodes with acquired feedback and retrieves task-relevant experiences based on incoming instructions, enabling historical feedback to guide subsequent navigation. Prior-Anchored Adaptation (PAA) learns from these experiences by reinforcing successful trajectories and suppressing failed ones relative to a frozen pretrained policy, while regularizing behavioral drift. Together, these modules enable continual feedback reuse and stable policy adaptation without source-domain data or expert supervision. Extensive experiments across three benchmarks, REVERIE, R2R, and R2R-CE, and five navigation backbones demonstrate consistent performance gains. On the REVERIE validation unseen split, R2A improves DUET's SR from 46.98% to 69.84% and SPL from 33.73% to 50.98%.
  </details>

- **[How Far Is Too Far? Object Recognition Declines Monotonically with Semantic Distance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19747)**  `arXiv:2611.19747`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Domenic Bersch, Hai Van Tran, Gemma Roig_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision models are sensitive to object-background context, yet it remains unclear how recognition changes as the semantic distance between objects and backgrounds increases. We introduce ImageNet-OOC1k, a benchmark enabling graded control of object-background relationships along a continuous consensus visual-semantic dissimilarity axis. This formulation reveals a highly consistent monotonic decline in recognition accuracy across 35 models spanning convolutional neural networks, Vision Transformers, and vision-language models. Internal analyses of Vision Transformers reveal patterns consistent with aggregation failure, where object evidence is present at the patch level but not consolidated into the final prediction. Using this characterization as a diagnostic probe, a simple spatial re-ranking strategy recovers a subset of these errors and improves in-sample accuracy by up to 5.7 percentage points. These results establish context sensitivity as a continuous and predictable property of modern vision systems, with Vision Transformer analyses suggesting that aggregation limitations may contribute to some failure types even when object evidence remains available.
  </details>

- **[DecoEdit: Beyond Flat Control for 3D-Aware Unified Image Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19121)**  `arXiv:2611.19121`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Xiaotang Gai, Jinbo Xing, Zijie Meng, Jiayi Yi, Jimin Xiao, Yixuan Qian, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While recent advances in multimodal image editing have achieved impressive results, current frameworks remain largely confined to ``flat" 2D controls (e.g., text prompts, bounding boxes, or masks). Precise 3D-aware geometric manipulation -- such as coordinating an object's location, physical scale, and 3D orientation while strictly preserving its fine-grained identity -- remains an elusive challenge, typically suffering from severe detail degradation or requiring expensive per-instance fine-tuning. In this paper, we present DecoEdit, an all-in-one image editing framework that goes beyond flat control by seamlessly integrating 1D textual, 2D spatial, and 3D volumetric inputs under a unified architecture. First, to power this unified model, we establish a data curation pipeline and curate a large-scale dataset, namely DecoDataset, containing 1.6 million samples, encompassing diverse multi-dimensional (1D/2D/3D) tasks and their complex compositions. Furthermore, we establish a comprehensive image editing benchmark named DecoBench to systematically evaluate multi-task editing performance. Finally, to address the fundamental trade-off between complex spatial transformations and identity preservation, we propose a novel decoupled pipeline: DecoEdit factorizes the editing process by utilizing structural guidance from SAM-3D to anchor the target geometry, scale, and spatial orientation, while concurrently leveraging a detail-preservation path to lock in high-fidelity textures. Extensive experiments demonstrate that DecoEdit achieves state-of-the-art results. Notably, on the challenging 3D spatial pose and orientation control tasks, DecoEdit significantly outperforms leading closed-source models such as Seedream 5.0 in both geometric accuracy and identity consistency.
  </details>

- **[Recognizing Events Is Not Enough: Why Streaming VLMs Struggle in Compositional Instruction Triggering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18743)**  `arXiv:2611.18743`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Yehua Huang, Penglei Sun, Xiang Li, Jinhao Zhou, Jiawen Wen, Qingqing Xiao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recognizing individual events does not guarantee correct execution of compositional instructions in streaming video. For example, given the request "Notify me when the pot boils after someone turns on the stove and leaves the kitchen", existing models may recognize the individual events yet notify too early or miss the intended moment, failing to track how their temporal conditions are satisfied over time. To evaluate this gap, we introduce CoTriggerBench, which composes annotated video events into temporally constrained instructions and verifies notification timing and silence through executable event plans and source-event replay. To address this challenge, we propose a real-time VLM agent, TEMPI. TEMPI decomposes instructions into atomic event queries and temporal constraints, and uses Scenario Memory to continuously maintain task progress as event evidence arrives, allowing earlier observations to support subsequent decisions beyond the detector's visual window. Even when no notification is required, relevant events continue to update the scenario state. On CoTriggerBench, TEMPI achieves 44.4% instruction-level success, outperforming the strongest closed-source and fine-tuned open-source VLM baselines by 15.6 (Qwen3.8-Omni-Flash-Realtime) and 18.1 (MOSS-VL-Realtime) percentage points, respectively. To support efficient on-device streaming inference, we further optimize the streaming inference pipeline by overlapping partial visual encoding and LLM prefix prefill with frame arrival. On an 8 GB RTX 5060, our optimized detector implementation achieves a 4.90$\times$ speedup.
  </details>

- **[Target-Free Autocalibration of Stereo PIV Camera Systems via Persistent-State Motion Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18741)**  `arXiv:2611.18741`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Nabil Madali_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Stereo Particle Image Velocimetry (PIV) recovers three-component velocity fields by triangulating tracer particles observed by synchronized cameras. It requires an accurate extrinsic calibration, which is still obtained by inserting a physical target into the flow and must be repeated whenever the rig is moved. We present a target-free method that calibrates a stereo PIV rig directly from particle image sequences. Because tracer particles are visually indistinguishable, we match motion rather than appearance: building on CUT3R, each camera stream is processed by a recurrent transformer whose persistent state accumulates particle motion over time. The state is decoded into per-camera optical flow and queried by a cross-view decoder that regresses bidirectional point correspondences, from which the relative pose is recovered with a five-point RANSAC solver. We train on a new synthetic stereo PIV dataset with ground-truth poses and evaluate on standard PIV optical flow benchmarks and on 200 real rig placements. Our model achieves lower flow error than existing PIV networks, more accurate cross-view correspondences than COTR, and higher pose accuracy than the strongest detector-matcher pipelines on both synthetic and real data, without any calibration target.
  </details>

- **[Acting by Intent, Reacting by Sight: Visual Conditioned Reflexes for Real-Time VLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18723)**  `arXiv:2611.18723`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Qiancheng Zhou, Wenhua Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language Models (VLMs) have shown strong capabilities as generalist agents that perceive, reason, and act in interactive environments. Yet real-time environments such as video games demand both deliberate control for exploration and split-second reactions to fast-moving hazards. Existing agents struggle to handle both: relying solely on direct VLM actions is too slow for sudden events, whereas delegating execution to fixed scripts leaves the agent blind at the moment of action, leading to frequent misfires whenever the scene shifts. Humans overcome this by pairing normal conscious control with conditioned reflexes-while the mind handles deliberate actions and shapes reflex rules, the reflex itself still relies on live vision to make the final instant decision. Emulating this mechanism, we propose ViCoR (Visual Conditioned Reflex). Alongside normal gameplay actions, the main VLM defines and updates lightweight situational guidance for recurring events, while a fast visual reflex branch (invoking the VLM in no-thinking mode) inspects live local views with motion cues to make the final sub-second action decisions. By offloading 67.8% of high-frequency reactions to the reflex branch (680 ms per check), ViCoR reduces average per-action decision latency by 2.6x (from 14.0 s to 5.4 s) and cuts token consumption by 36.9%. Across 17 diverse 2D and 3D games in the GameWorld benchmark, ViCoR achieves state-of-the-art performance, improving task success rate by +11.8% (from 52.9% to 64.7%) and average normalized progress by +13.9% (from 66.5% to 80.4%) over the strongest deliberative VLM and script baselines. In fast-paced real-time games, ViCoR refines its reflexes across episodes to boost progress by 1.67x, and even with a low-effort main planner, surpasses high-effort deliberative baselines by +8.1% at one-third of the token cost.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Answering: Learning to Speak First and Act in Streaming Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17048)**  `arXiv:2611.17048`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Shuhan Li, Huankang Guan, Xintong Guo, Yang Bo, Yuyang He, Dunyuan Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Most AI assistants respond only when prompted, remaining silent even when events in the video stream offer meaningful opportunities for conversation. To move beyond purely user-driven interaction, we present AURA 2.0, a streaming video large language model that introduces three core upgrades to AURA. The key advance is Model-Initiated Conversation (MIC), which enables the model to identify topics worth discussing in the evolving visual stream and initiate conversations at appropriate moments without a user query. Stream-Aware Tool Calling (SATC) lets the model act on what it sees by invoking external tools using the current visual and conversational context and incorporating returned information into the ongoing interaction. Interactive Visual Grounding (IVG) supports visual grounding in both user inputs and model outputs. The model can identify objects marked by users on video frames and produce frame-aligned bounding boxes to link its responses to specific objects and regions. To develop these capabilities, we construct a dedicated streaming interaction dataset and adopt a three-stage training scheme to integrate the new behaviors with AURA's existing streaming interaction abilities. We further incorporate Long-Horizon Interaction Memory (LHIM) to maintain contextual continuity over extended video streams. Evaluations on public streaming benchmarks show that AURA 2.0 achieves leading performance among open-source streaming models, while targeted evaluations further validate its new interaction capabilities.
  </details>

- **[PhysLoop: Harnessing LLMs' Physics Awareness for Self-Improving Video Generation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16748)**  `arXiv:2611.16748`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Yuxuan Chen, Xiaojie Li, Hao Chen, Juncheng Ma, Kewei Zhang, Zhongjing Du, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video generation models produce visually realistic videos but still struggle with physical dynamics and causal consistency. Learning from generated videos requires fine-grained physical supervision, yet reliable quality judgments, correction pairs, and controlled variations are difficult to obtain. Large language models (LLMs) possess physical knowledge, but struggle to reliably guide video generation models in producing their training videos. We present PhysLoop, a unified harness designed to support physically grounded self-improvement in video generation models. The harness guides and constrains LLMs throughout video generation, selection, and correction, distilling their physical knowledge into physically consistent videos. The framework supports controllable training-data construction and inference-time video repair. By identifying and correcting failures, PhysLoop marks a key milestone toward recursive self-improvement (RSI) in video generation models with limited human intervention. We release 100K videos spanning diverse, realistic indoor and outdoor scenes, along with generation tools and two evaluation datasets: PhysLoop-Gen and PhysLoop-Repair. We evaluate controllable generation and localized repair on these datasets, respectively, and assess physical behavior on the external Physics-IQ Verified benchmark.
  </details>

- **[PhysCapBench: A Cross-Domain Benchmark for Physically Grounded Video Captioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16424)**  `arXiv:2611.16424`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Haofeng Wang, Xuanyi Liu, Pengcheng Wen, Ruiqi Li, Yuhao Zhang, Tiange Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Physical AI aims to learn transferable physical knowledge from real-world experience to understand, predict, and act in the physical world. Internet videos and embodied demonstrations provide complementary observations of motion, interaction, and state evolution. A foundational data challenge is to measure and preserve this physical information as training supervision. In video-language pretraining, captions express observed processes in language, yet a semantically correct description can omit the interactions and transitions that define those processes. Rich physical experience in video does not automatically translate into sufficient language supervision. This gap calls for a yardstick to assess annotation quality, compare captioning strategies, and guide training-data refinement. Motivated by our data construction for world-model pretraining, we introduce PhysCapBench, to our knowledge the first unified physical-caption benchmark spanning all three domains: third-person scenes, egocentric manipulation, and handheld-gripper demonstrations. It provides human-verified annotations for 1,500 video sequences and evaluates accuracy and coverage through seven physical dimensions. We examine downstream utility through video reconstruction and manipulation-policy fine-tuning. Physical captions reduce reconstruction LPIPS by 8.6-9.5% on third-person videos and 26.9-30.9% on egocentric videos relative to metadata captions. Training-instruction enrichment improves both PhysCapBench scores and policy success, including a 7.5-percentage-point gain for pi0.5 on a RoboTwin 2.0 subtask. PhysCapBench makes the physical content of language supervision inspectable and comparable, providing a cross-domain basis for its evaluation and improvement.
  </details>

- **[Reframing Video Inverse Tone Mapping as Blind Posterior Inference via Latent Luminance Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15146)**  `arXiv:2611.15146`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Hanwool Sul, Chang-Hoon Jeong, Kisung Shin, Bomseok Park, Donghoon Cha, Hansl von Kwon, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  High-dynamic-range (HDR) displays are now common, but most video content, such as archival footage, broadcast material, and the output of generative video models, exists only in standard dynamic range (SDR). The need is sharpest for generated video, which has no HDR master at all and is graded alongside live-action and visual-effects material finished in HDR. Converting SDR to HDR means undoing a tone-mapping operation that was never recorded, and clipping destroys the radiance wherever it acts, so HDR recovery is a blind inverse problem. Methods built on generative video priors fill clipped regions with plausible structure, but their reconstructions are systematically biased: in every public method we test, saturated content leans too bright or too dark, and the direction of the lean moves with the tone operator behind the input. We call this failure the luminance error, measure both its magnitude and its sign, and make it the focus of this work. Reframing ITM as posterior inference under an unknown operator locates the failure in two places and yields two corrections. A fixed affine functional of the video-diffusion latent reads log-luminance without decoding; on that coordinate an asymmetric loss selects an expectile of the posterior, applied only where clipping leaves luminance unconstrained. We also synthesize training SDR through an explicit family of tone operators, so the model marginalizes over what the observation cannot show. Trained this way, our luminance error stays flatter across operators and smaller on average than any public method, and the full system achieves the best perceptual quality on all three benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[When the Editing Intent Is Split: A Cross-Modal Jailbreak Attack for Large Image Editing Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23909)**  `arXiv:2610.23909`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Xianmin Ye, Zhiyuan Fan, Tianju Liu, Yuhang Wang, Zhenxing Niu, Haichang Gao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large image editing models increasingly infer editing intent from both textual and visual inputs, creating an underexplored safety risk when harmful intent is distributed across modalities. In this work, we formalize this split-intent vulnerability and propose the Split-Intent Jailbreak Attack (SIJA), which asymmetrically distributes an unsafe editing intent by specifying the editing referent in text while concealing the safety-critical action and specification through graphical visual semantics in an auxiliary cue. To systematically study this threat, we introduce the Split-Intent Image Editing Safety Benchmark (SI2ESBench) for evaluating image editing models under split-intent inputs. Extensive experiments on representative commercial and open-source models demonstrate that SIJA effectively compromises state-of-the-art image editing systems, achieving ASRs of 68.28% on GPT Image 2 and 91.38% on Qwen-Image-3.0-Pro. Our results expose a mismatch between multimodal editing and safety assessment: the distributed intent can be recovered to execute the requested transformation, yet its harmfulness often remains undetected by existing safeguards. To mitigate this vulnerability, we propose editing-intent reconstruction, a defense principle that explicitly evaluates the complete transformation implied by the joint multimodal context and substantially improves rejection of split-intent attacks. Our findings expose a cross-modal safety gap in modern image editing systems and highlight the need to assess the jointly inferred transformation rather than individual inputs in isolation. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[EvoKnow: Continual Knowledge Evolution for AI-Generated Image Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11381)**  `arXiv:2610.11381`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhiheng Peng, Wenwei Jin, Yangshi Ge, Siyu Xia, Jiawei Li, Xu Tang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI-generated image detectors are commonly trained on fixed generator domains and become difficult to maintain as new generative models emerge. Continual adaptation is challenging because replaying historical generated images is costly, whereas updating shared parameters with limited current-domain data can overwrite prior forensic knowledge. We propose EvoKnow, a replay-free framework that formulates continual AI-generated image detection as forensic knowledge evolution. EvoKnow preserves a shared forensic basis learned from base domains, incrementally adds isolated residual experts for complementary generator-relevant evidence, and retrieves expertise through an Analytical Incremental Router (AIR) updated in closed form from current-stage generated images and accumulated sufficient statistics. Experiments demonstrate effective cross-generator generalization, few-shot expansion, and long-horizon continual adaptation. With ten generated images per arriving generator, EvoKnow achieves 96.70% average accuracy on non-base GenImage generators and 94.48% accuracy on Chameleon without target-benchmark adaptation. Under a strict replay-free continual learning protocol, EvoKnow achieves state-of-the-art continual learning performance, attaining 96.32% mean stage-wise accuracy and 4.32% average forgetting.
  </details>

- **[VAMR: Multi-Question Agentic Reasoning for Efficient Long-Form Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11171)**  `arXiv:2610.11171`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Runquan Gui, Hanzhu Chen, Zehao Wang, Hanxin Zhu, Xin Li, Zhibo Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-form video understanding often involves multiple questions about different aspects of the same recording. Yet existing video agents typically process each question through an isolated tool-use trajectory. This repeatedly restarts video exploration and memory construction, missing opportunities to acquire evidence jointly and progressively build a shared understanding that supports the complete question set. We introduce \textbf{VAMR} (\textbf{V}ideo \textbf{A}gent for \textbf{M}ulti-Question \textbf{R}easoning), which coordinates all questions about a video through one shared tool-use trajectory. At each round, a persistent policy model can invoke tools for one or more unresolved questions and submit answers for questions with sufficient evidence. Question-conditioned visual perception retrieves fine-grained clues for several questions in one call, while layered multi-question memory integrates reusable context into a shared video story and preserves separate evidence for individual questions. After supervised fine-tuning initializes this interaction protocol, we propose question-horizon policy optimization (\qhpo) to optimize shared trajectories in which questions progress and finish at different rounds. Specifically, a question-level critic estimates the value of each active question, while round alignment maps each question advantage to the rounds that directly serve it before the aligned advantages are aggregated to optimize the shared actor. Across LVBench, Video-Holmes, and LongVideoBench, VAMR achieves the highest accuracy overall and the fewest reasoning rounds among iterative methods. On LVBench, it reaches 62.1\% accuracy, exceeding VideoARM by \textbf{4.3} points while reducing reasoning rounds and processed frames by \textbf{85.9\%} and \textbf{61.4\%}.
  </details>

- **[GameReplica: A Benchmark for Black-Box Visual Game Replication by Vision-Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22308)**  `arXiv:2609.22308`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Boyu Qiao, Zixin Tang, Kaige Li, Xiaoshuai Hao, Dongyu She, Xiaochun Cao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Benchmarks for coding agents typically assess implementation from explicit specifications, leaving open whether agents can discover and reproduce systems whose rules are undocumented and accessible only through interaction. We study this capability in web games, which offer controlled environments with observable actions and outcomes, and introduce GameReplica, a benchmark for end-to-end black-box game replication. It comprises 125 instances spanning 25 web games and five core mechanism families, with each game instantiated at five difficulty levels. Under container isolation, an agent accesses the target game only through an action--screenshot interface, may iterate between probing the game, implementing a replica, and checking it, and delivers a self-contained runnable replica together with a rule checklist. A reference-grounded evaluation then scores the deliverables separately on rule consistency, implementation consistency, and visual fidelity. Experiments across six models and five agent runtimes show that black-box replication remains challenging: Claude Opus 4.8 achieves the highest Overall score, 71.6%, while the other models obtain 4.0%--43.0%. Every model scores highest on visual fidelity and lowest on rule consistency, suggesting that reproducing appearance is easier than recovering mechanics. Difficulty widens the gap: from L1 to L5, the four lower-scoring models show relative Overall score declines of 33%--63%, whereas the strongest model declines by less than 5%. In the evaluated configurations, outcomes vary more across models than across agent runtimes, and most attributed failures of weaker models arise during exploration, before replica code is written. Resources and interactive demonstrations are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Towards Clinically Interpretable Ophthalmic VQA via Spatially-Grounded Lesion Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.22414)**  `arXiv:2605.22414`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Xingyue Wang, Bo Liu, Meng Wang, Zhixuan Zhang, Chengcheng Zhu, Huazhu Fu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual Question Answering (VQA) holds great promise for clinical support, particularly in ophthalmology, where retinal fundus photography is essential for diagnosis. However, ophthalmic VQA benchmarks primarily emphasize answer accuracy, neglecting the explicit visual evidence necessary for clinical interpretability. In this work, we introduce FundusGround, a new benchmark for clinically interpretable ophthalmic VQA with spatially-grounded lesion evidence. Specifically, we propose a three-stage pipeline that collects 10,719 fundus images with 15,595 image-level meticulously annotated lesions. To ensure anatomical consistency and clinical validity, all lesions are spatially localized using the Early Treatment Diabetic Retinopathy Study (ETDRS) grid, enabling standardized mapping to nine clinically meaningful retinal regions. Built upon this structured lesion evidence, 72,706 questions are then generated spanning four formats: open-ended, closed-ended, single-choice, and multiple-choice. We further benchmark multiple general- and medical- large vision-language models using dual metrics for answer accuracy and lesion-level reasoning. The experiments demonstrate that incorporating lesion-level visual evidence consistently improves model performance and transparency, highlighting the necessity of explicit spatial grounding for reliable and explainable ophthalmic VQA.
  </details>

- **[VIOLA: Towards Video In-Context Learning with Minimal Annotations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.15549)**  `arXiv:2601.15549`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Ryo Fujii, Hideo Saito, Ryo Hachiuma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generalizing Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to novel video domains is essential for real-world deployment but remains challenging due to the scarcity of labeled data. While In-Context Learning (ICL) offers a training-free adaptation path, standard methods rely on large annotated pools, which are often impractical in specialized environments like industrial or surgical settings since they require the experts' annotations. To bridge this gap, we introduce VIOLA (Video In-cOntext Learning with minimal Annotation), a label-efficient framework that synergizes minimal expert supervision with abundant unlabeled data. First, to maximize the efficiency of a strict annotation budget, we propose density-uncertainty-weighted sampling. Unlike standard diversity or uncertainty strategies that risk selecting visual outliers, our method leverages density estimation to identify samples that are simultaneously diverse, representative, and informative. Second, to utilize the remaining unlabeled data without noise propagation, we construct a hybrid pool and introduce confidence-aware retrieval and confidence-aware prompting. These mechanisms explicitly model label reliability, retrieving demonstrations based on a composite score of similarity and confidence while enabling the MLLM to adaptively distinguish between verified ground truths and noisy pseudo-labels. Extensive experiments across nine diverse benchmarks using three MLLMs demonstrate that our framework significantly outperforms various baselines in low-resource settings, achieving robust adaptation with minimal annotation costs. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[EDVD-LLaMA: Explainable Deepfake Video Detection via Multimodal Large Language Model Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.16442)**  `arXiv:2510.16442`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Haoran Sun, Chen Cai, Huiping Zhuang, Kong Aik Lee, Lap-Pui Chau, Yi Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The rapid development of deepfake video technology has not only facilitated artistic creation but also made it easier to spread misinformation, which is increasingly difficult to identify. Traditional deepfake video detection (DVD) methods face issues such as a lack of transparency in their principles and insufficient generalization capabilities to cope with evolving forgery techniques. This highlights an urgent need for detectors that can identify forged content and provide verifiable reasoning explanations. This paper proposes the explainable deepfake video detection (EDVD) task and designs the EDVD-LLaMA multimodal, a large language model (MLLM) reasoning framework, which provides traceable reasoning processes alongside accurate detection results and trustworthy explanations. Our approach first incorporates a Spatio-Temporal Subtle Information Tokenization (ST-SIT) to extract and fuse global and local cross-frame deepfake features, providing rich spatio-temporal semantic information input for MLLM reasoning. Second, we construct a Fine-grained Multimodal Chain-of-Thought (Fg-MCoT) mechanism, which introduces facial feature data as hard constraints during the reasoning process to achieve pixel-level spatio-temporal video localization, suppress hallucinated outputs, and enhance the reliability of the chain of thought. In addition, we build an Explainable Reasoning FF++ benchmark dataset (ER-FF++set), leveraging structured data to annotate videos and ensure quality control, thereby supporting dual supervision for reasoning and detection. Extensive experiments demonstrate that EDVD-LLaMA achieves outstanding performance and robustness in terms of detection accuracy, explainability, and its ability to handle cross-forgery methods and cross-dataset scenarios. Compared to previous DVD methods, it provides a more explainable and superior solution.
  </details>

- **[BLIP-Live: Balancing Timeliness & Firing Precision in Proactive Streaming Video Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21553)**  `arXiv:2611.21553`  `cs.CV`  
  _Kanchana Ranasinghe, Honglu Zhou, Dominick Reilly, Le Xue, Shusheng Yang, Juan Carlos Niebles, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Streaming video understanding is becoming increasingly important for real-world interactive systems. Such systems must be proactive for natural and useful human interaction: they should respond as soon as sufficient evidence becomes available while avoiding premature, redundant, or incorrect responses. We present BLIP-Live, a framework unifying data, metrics, harness, and training around a shared formulation of proactivity as effective causal response decisions balancing the two competing objectives of responding vs silence. First, we construct BLIP-Live-780K dataset containing human annotations of proactive response content and timing, which defines this desired behavior. We then propose PAUC-F, a metric to effectively measure our desired proactive behavior. PAUC-F combines existing response timeliness metrics with firing precision to reward early useful responses without rewarding excessive firing. With our desired behavior defined and measurable, we finally investigate model-agnostic inference harnesses that draw out proactive streaming capabilities of offline vision language models (VLMs), together with training pipelines that learn to further improve their proactive responsiveness. Across both settings, we find that combinations of sliding visual windows, online interaction memory, and structured response outputs consistently yield strongest proactive performance. Our experiments result in two models, with our BLIP-Live-Qwen model achieving state-of-the-art performance across four proactive streaming benchmarks, with gains up to 44% over baselines and outperforming multiple closed API models. Our code and data will be released publicly.
  </details>

- **[DuoFlow: Towards Synchronized Speech and Motion Generation with Fine-Grained Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21530)**  `arXiv:2611.21530`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jun Dong, Ronghui Li, Zhongyuan Hu, Yunqi Zhao, Lijie Guo, Ruizhang Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An embodied speaker's expressiveness depends not only on what is said, but also on how the voice sounds and how the body moves. However, existing joint speech-motion generation models are largely driven implicitly by transcript semantics or intermediate speech representations, offering little explicit control over motion hierarchy, body parts, or vocal style. To bridge this gap, we construct MotionSpeech, a structured benchmark for controllable joint speech-motion generation that includes transcripts, voice-style descriptions, and motion conditions at two hierarchical levels. Building on this benchmark, we propose DuoFlow, a dual-stream latent flow transformer that jointly denoises speech and motion under a shared noise level and a unified second-based temporal coordinate. Modality-specific residual connectors inject structured conditions into the corresponding streams, layer-wise bidirectional cross-modal attention coordinates their generation, and a monotonic phoneme pathway combines normalized progress anchors with CTC supervision to strengthen speech temporal modeling. DuoFlow provides a unified interface for fine-grained motion and vocal-style control and demonstrates the ability to synthesize high-quality, synchronized, and instruction-consistent speech-motion sequences.
  </details>

- **[Lost in the Hallway: Benchmarking Persistent Spatial Memory Across Revisits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21485)**  `arXiv:2611.21485`  `cs.CV`  
  _Henry Zheng, Weibo Gong, Yang Yue, Rui Huang, Yang Zhang, Gao Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Persistent spatial understanding is essential for embodied agents to operate reliably in environments they repeatedly encounter. It requires recognizing revisited places despite viewpoint changes, distinguishing them from similar-looking rooms, and updating memories as environments change. Yet existing evaluations often leave physical place identity across separated visits implicit. We introduce ReSpaceBench, a benchmark comprising 3,244 questions across 590 synthetic and real-home scenes, organized into 9 tasks spanning room association, persistent state tracking, and downstream reasoning. Controlled walkthroughs define visits by physical room entry and deliberately include same-type room distractors and cross-session object changes. Our evaluation of 32 models and 3 agentic video-understanding frameworks reveals substantial limitations: the strongest evaluated zero-shot model achieves 50.5% overall accuracy and 52.2% on a 450-question subset, compared with 90.1% for human annotators. The evaluated agentic frameworks offer no overall improvement over direct inference with the same base model. Analysis of agent execution traces reveals evidence omissions and incorrect cross-visit room associations as major failure modes, suggesting that iterative evidence seeking alone does not ensure consistent spatial memory. Further analyses expose a gap between recognizing familiar rooms and identifying the correct visited room, while annotation-guided sampling highlights the importance of preserving intervening visits and transitions. We also provide RS-30K, a separate training dataset from synthetic sources with no template, scene, route, or video overlap with the benchmark. Fine-tuning Qwen3-VL-8B raises overall accuracy from 35.5% to 60.4%, with the largest gains in association and improvements extending to real-life scenes.
  </details>

- **[[CLS] and [EOS] Tokens are not Enough for CLIP-based Class-Incremental Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21440)**  `arXiv:2611.21440`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hao Sun, Tao Hu, Shixiong Kai, Siyuan Xu, Wenqian Zhao, Mingxuan Yuan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Class-Incremental Learning (CIL) aims to empower models to continuously acquire new knowledge while mitigating catastrophic forgetting. With the advent of vision-language models like CLIP, leveraging their cross-modal alignment capabilities to address CIL challenges has emerged as a dominant paradigm. Existing methods leverage CLIP's global representations (i.e., the visual [CLS] token and the textual [EOS] token) for cross-modal alignment. While achieving effective classification, they overlook the rich patch-level visual features and token-level textual semantics inherent in CLIP. For example, the [CLS] token aggregates local information to capture the general concept of "cat", while the patch-level details retain specific localized features essential for recognition, such as the tail, fur, and face. We propose PACE (PAtch-token Cross-modal Enhancement) to uncover the long-neglected local representations within CLIP. First, based on the global representations like [CLS] token and [EOS] token, we identify the top-k most crucial visual patches and textual tokens. Subsequently, to better utilize the extracted key local features, we design a cross-modal fusion module. This module leverages local textual semantics to refine visual representations, while augmenting textual semantics with local visual features. As the fusion module is shared across all tasks, directly fine-tuning it on new tasks would interfere with the knowledge learned from previous tasks, leading to catastrophic forgetting. To alleviate this, we employ a projection-based update strategy to effectively preserve the critical parameter space of previous tasks during the adaptation to new tasks. Extensive experiments demonstrate that PACE significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods across multiple CIL benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Flexible Multi-Stack Super-Resolution of Clinical Brain MRI Using Hetero-Orientation Variational Encoding: Expert Reader Evaluation in a Nigerian Cohort](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21412)**  `arXiv:2611.21412`  `cs.CV`  
  _Kabir Hamzah Muhammad, Lorena Garcia-Foncillas Macias, Soumya Snigdha Kundu, Emily Guilhem, Jon Cleary, Amal Saleh Nour, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  High-resolution brain MRI is important for neuroradiological assessment, yet routine clinical protocols often rely on fast, anisotropic 2D acquisitions, particularly in resource-constrained settings. These acquisitions may differ in orientation, alignment and anatomical coverage, resulting in partially overlapping image stacks. Existing learning-based super-resolution methods typically rely on single, under-constrained inputs or fixed multi-stack configurations with idealised acquisition geometry. We introduce MS-HVED, a sequence-agnostic framework for flexible multi-stack brain MRI super-resolution. Available axial, coronal and sagittal stacks are independently encoded into multi-scale Gaussian posteriors and fused using a field-of-view-aware Product-of-Gaussians formulation that limits contributions from unsupported regions. Physics-informed domain randomisation simulates heterogeneous clinical acquisition conditions, including obliqueness, partial coverage and imaging artefacts. MS-HVED achieved competitive or superior reconstruction fidelity across synthetic benchmarks and improved downstream tissue segmentation relative to low-resolution inputs. To assess generalisation beyond the training cohorts, we performed external clinical validation on 30 routine brain MRI examinations from a Nigerian cohort, representing an under-represented population in medical imaging research. These examinations formed the largest component of a blinded reader study involving 44 real cases and seven expert neuroradiologists. In the Nigerian cohort, MS-HVED was preferred over the supervised baseline for overall image quality in 187 of 210 paired assessments. Across all clinical cohorts, MS-HVED achieved higher overall-quality scores with all available inputs (5.93 vs. 4.49, p<.001), with the preference persisting when axial or coronal inputs were omitted (p<.001). Code atthis http URL
  </details>

- **[VideoNexus: Video Perception Beyond the Reasoning Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21392)**  `arXiv:2611.21392`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yancheng Long, Xuanyu Zheng, Wei Chen, Luoyuan Zhang, Haoyang Xu, Bin Wen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Understanding long videos requires locating evidence, resolving visual details, and integrating observations into an answer. Even when relevant evidence is present, performing these operations within long, dense visual sequences burdens both perception and reasoning. Video agents enable selective inspection, but returning frames to the planner still accumulates visual information in its reasoning context. Delegating perception reduces this burden, yet sparse sampling can miss decisive details, and per-frame descriptions can preserve context growth. We introduce VideoNexus, a training-free framework that separates detailed video perception from evidence-driven planning and reasoning. The planner controls where to inspect, what to resolve, and how densely to sample. Perception sub-agents process native audio-video in independent contexts and return compact, temporally grounded evidence for iterative search and verification. Using Gemini 3.8 Flash for both planning and perception, VideoNexus achieves 88.6% accuracy on full LVBench, exceeding same-backbone static ingestion and built-in agentic processing by 3.6 and 1.1 points, respectively, at 44% of the latter's measured API cost. A paired LVBench subset ablation shows lower planner input and higher accuracy. With Kimi K3 as the shared backbone, VideoNexus improves over the official Video-GAIA ReAct harness by 8.5 points in our evaluation. These results support compact evidence exchange for combining detailed observation with efficient planning and reasoning.
  </details>

- **[ALIVE: Making Streaming Video Models Interactive](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21366)**  `arXiv:2611.21366`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jinzhao Li, Yuan Xu, Jinxuan Wu, Yukun Wang, Yijia Lei, Hanyu Ji, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Streaming video LLMs can continuously observe the world, yet often handle user requests in isolation. We introduce ALIVE, an interactive streaming video model supporting proactive monitoring, streaming task coordination, and feedback-seeking interaction. We construct ALIVE-SFT-288K and ALIVE-RL-3K for two-stage training. Contrastive supervised fine-tuning teaches when and how to respond, while reinforcement learning with Behavior-Aligned Credit Assignment (BACA) combines trajectory-level and local advantages to refine decisions, responses, and silence across multi-turn interactions. We also introduce FSI-Bench to evaluate feedback seeking and subsequent task completion. ALIVE achieves the highest overall scores among the compared models on four public benchmarks and FSI-Bench, demonstrating strong streaming understanding and interactive capabilities.
  </details>

- **[MovieRoll: A Systematic Framework for Rolling Out Long-Form Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21339)**  `arXiv:2611.21339`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yunyang Ge, Weijie Kong, Ryan Xu, Yue Wu, Qi Tian, Jiangfeng Xiong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Streaming video generation must sustain quality beyond the training horizon while handling self-generated history and growing cache costs. To address these challenges, we present MovieRoll, a base-to-distilled framework that connects streaming fine-tuning, long-rollout distillation, and long-horizon inference. We first establish a robust multi-step base through Window-wise Teacher Forcing (WinTF), which jointly denoises chunks at progressive noise levels with bidirectional attention within each window and causal access to preceding history. To retain historical context efficiently, hierarchical KV-cache compression (HieraKV) exploits layer-wise attention patterns, retaining full history in shallow layers and sink--recent history in deeper layers. This reduces cache memory by up to 78% while improving generation quality. Building on this base, cumulative long tuning with distribution matching distillation (CuLT-DMD) holds parameters fixed throughout each long self-rollout and averages local gradients before updating. Each update therefore combines supervision across temporal positions while retaining only one local computation graph, enabling efficient long-rollout training of a few-step generator. To extend generation beyond the training horizon, KV recomputation bounds context for both models, while guidance annealing (AnnealCFG) reduces guidance toward the clean endpoint to stabilize MovieRoll-Base. Complementing these modeling advances, FBCA attention and PS-VAE decoding support efficient training and streaming output. On HeliosBench, MovieRoll-Base remains competitive at 30s after training on videos of up to 15s, and MovieRoll-Distilled achieves the highest total scores among the compared methods across single- and multi-event evaluations up to 60s, combining sustained naturalness with active motion.
  </details>

- **[Region-VAD: Video Anomaly Localization and Detection via Region Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21132)**  `arXiv:2611.21132`  `cs.CV`  
  _Giacomo D'Amicantonio_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Anomalies are local events, yet weakly supervised video anomaly detection scores them globally. Most methods pool an entire frame into a single vector, rank frames by that vector, and derive a location afterwards as a saliency map of the score. Full-frame pooling mixes the anomaly with its background, so a detector can reach competitive benchmark scores from scene context without being able to show where in the scene the anomaly occurs. We introduce Region-VAD, which first selects a region and then detects the anomaly from that region alone. Region-VAD selects an explicit rectangle over the tokens of a frozen foundation model and scores anomalousness only from the tokens inside the box, through attention pooling followed by a standard temporal head. The rectangle is not learned, it is the box in which the frozen tokens change most between consecutive segments, in all four feature projections of the encoder, and it is found by exhaustive search over the rectangles of the token grid. Only the pooling and the temporal head are trained, from video-level labels without boxes, masks or frame-level annotations, and no trained component reads a token outside the selected region. Experiments on five datasets, with one localizer configuration shared by all datasets and backbones, show that Region-VAD achieves state-of-the-art anomaly localization on UCF-Crime and MSAD at competitive detection performance.
  </details>

- **[IGRecon4D: Dynamic-Object-Aware Feed-Forward Reconstruction of Driving Scenes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21126)**  `arXiv:2611.21126`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yifei Tong, Ziyang Huang, Yizhao Han, Xiaoxiao Long, Hao Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Feed-forward 4D driving-scene reconstruction enables efficient modeling of dynamic autonomous-driving scenes, but current methods suffer from two complementary failure modes: semantic leakage of traffic participants into the static representation and geometric drift of Gaussian locations across viewpoints. We propose \textbf{IGRecon4D}, a feed-forward dynamic scene reconstruction framework that addresses these failures with training-time instance-prior distillation and LiDAR-anchored Gaussian positioning. Specifically, an instance-level semantic branch provides a potentially dynamic participant prior, which is distilled into dynamic-mask learning through the Instance-Dynamic Consistency Constraint to encourage high-recall localization of traffic-participant regions prone to dynamic-static leakage. In parallel, sparse LiDAR measurements guide dense depth estimation, and the resulting depths are back-projected to anchor Gaussian positions for improved multi-view geometric consistency. Experiments on the Waymo Open Dataset and zero-shot evaluation on nuScenes demonstrate improved full-image reconstruction on the main benchmark and stronger traffic-participant-region reconstruction under cross-dataset evaluation.
  </details>

- **[VAE Rhythm: Temporal Decoding Traces for AI-Generated Video Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21114)**  `arXiv:2611.21114`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ke Sun, Shuwei Li, Haiyang Mei, Mike Zheng Shou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visible artifacts are an unreliable cue for detecting generated videos, because newer generators learn to remove them. We instead detect a trace left by the generation pipeline itself. Most video generators decode latents with a 3D causal VAE that groups consecutive frames in time, so the fidelity of generated frames rises and falls periodically with their position in a group. We call this periodic trace VAE Rhythm. Real videos are captured frame by frame and do not have it. To reveal it without training, we propose a phase scan. The scan reconstructs a clip with an off-the-shelf VAE from successive start offsets and measures the periodicity of the reconstruction error across these offsets. We find VAE Rhythm in most of the 39 variants of 31 generators we test, including the closed-source Sora, Kling 3.0 and Seedance 2.0. We use the rhythm as a zero-shot score, as a plug-in for existing detectors and as a data engine that pairs real videos with their VAE reconstructions to train our detector CaDet. On DF26, the training-free rhythm score outperforms all 15 retrained baselines, and as a plug-in it improves all 19 tested detectors by 6.7% to 23.7% AUROC. CaDet achieves the best AUROC on AIGVDBench, DF26 and three 2026 closed-source models, and its variant trained without any generated video still exceeds 90% on AIGVDBench.
  </details>

- **[Source-Free Domain Adaptation for Multi-Label Image Classification: A Framework, Baselines, and Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21107)**  `arXiv:2611.21107`  `cs.CV`  
  _Salih Numan Büyükbaş, Erchan Aptoula, Mamatha Thota, Petra Bosilj_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Source-free domain adaptation (SFDA) adapts a model trained on a labelled source domain to an unlabelled target domain without access to the source data. Almost the entire SFDA literature assumes mutually exclusive classes, yet many deployed settings are multi-label. This paper formulates SFDA for multi-label classification and establishes the first benchmark for it: five adaptation tasks over six datasets spanning remote sensing, natural, clipart-style and driving imagery, with four single-label SFDA methods extended to provide the first baselines and a published multi-label method run alongside them, over three seeds. The benchmark shows that extending a single-label method is unreliable rather than merely suboptimal: half of the extended baselines fail to improve on the unadapted source model at all, and on two of the five tasks none of them does. The failures are not incidental. Nearest-centroid pseudo-labelling, softmax-based confidence, the diversity term of information maximization and confidence-weighted prototype construction each rely on mutual exclusivity, and each fails differently without it, so replacing the softmax with per-class sigmoids is a valid but insufficient extension. Multi-label Prototype Alignment with Contrastive Teaching (MPACT) is proposed in response, a mean-teacher framework whose objective, prototype construction and alignment term are each reconsidered for overlapping label sets. It attains the best average on every reported metric and is the only method that improves on the unadapted source model on all five tasks. The analysis further introduces a prototype-agreement diagnostic separating feature-space alignment from self-reinforcing agreement with the classifier, and reports a negative result for confidence-based pseudo-label filtering.
  </details>

- **[GRAZE: Text-Free Grasp Synthesis for Unseen Objects from Human Gaze](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21104)**  `arXiv:2611.21104`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jeongho Lee, Boeun Kim, Hyung Jin Chang, Jungchan Cho, Sang-Il Choi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-conditioned hand-object interaction (HOI) generation provides intuitive semantic control, but language is an indirect way to specify where an interaction should occur on an object. Naming the part to grasp requires object-specific words, and naming the approach side requires a reference frame that a prompt lacks. We propose GRAZE (GRAsp from gaZE), which generates hand-object grasp motion from recorded gaze instead of object and part names. Its Geometry-Aware Gaze Encoding (GeoGaze) maps gaze rays to cues on object geometry, and cross-attention conditions a diffusion Transformer on these cues. We also introduce HOT3D-Grasp, a grasp-centered benchmark with object-disjoint folds built on HOT3D recordings. On unseen objects, GRAZE grasps the target part more often than the strongest text-conditioned baseline (49.3% vs. 43.6%), achieves the highest grasp success (66.3% vs. 46.6%), and transfers without fine-tuning to GazeHOI (45.3% vs. 33.8%). Gaze conveys the approach side: when gaze is rotated around the object, the approach turns with it in 59.1% of cases, against 20.5% for a text model retrained with clock words. On the same backbone, a text prompt names parts better, but gaze adds the approach side. Given both, GRAZE keeps the prompt's part accuracy and follows gaze.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Family Labels: A Taxonomic Ornstein-Uhlenbeck Prior for Avian 3D Shape Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21102)**  `arXiv:2611.21102`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yunchan Jeon, SooGon Kim, Seungwook Kim, CheolWon Lee, Jongmin Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recovering 3D pose and shape of birds from single images is bottlenecked by the long tail of avian diversity: out of ~10,000 species, only a few dozen have 3D-labeled training data, and in-the-wild test sets routinely contain species never seen at training. Generalizing across clades is therefore a central problem. The current state of the art, AniMer+, mitigates it with a family-level contrastive loss that conveys only a binary same-family indicator per pair and discards the continuous structure of the species tree. We propose a taxonomic Ornstein-Uhlenbeck prior on the shape code of the AVES parametric bird mesh model: a multivariate Gaussian whose covariance decays exponentially with Linnaean rank distance, with a learnable selection strength and noise scale. The prior generalizes family contrastive into a continuous, generative form, exposing the graded cross- and within-family similarity that a binary indicator cannot, and brings continuous comparative phylogenetics in as a biology-grounded inductive bias for the long tail. Our method improves over AniMer+ on both metrics of the unseen-species CowBird benchmark and on CtrlAVES3D, while remaining comparable on seen-species CUB. A permutation ablation isolates taxonomic structure from generic Gaussian smoothing, and a rank-granularity ablation identifies cross-order distinctions (the structure beyond a family-vs-not indicator) as the dominant source of the unseen-species gain.
  </details>

- **[State-Message Interaction Adapter for Cross-Domain Few-Shot Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21085)**  `arXiv:2611.21085`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hang Xing, Yan Xia, Yongjiang Liu, Siqi Zhang, Xueming Ding_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cross-domain few-shot segmentation adapts natural-image pretrained encoders to medical or industrial domains from a few pixel-annotated support masks. Within frozen DINOv3, intermediate per-head attention messages already carry target-background distinctions that baseline final-layer similarities and predictions do not consistently reflect. We propose the State-Message Interaction Adapter (SMIA) to learn how per-head messages should update each receiving token representation while keeping pretrained attention and FFN parameters frozen. Inserted right after per-head aggregation, SMIA conditions the transformation of each head's aggregated output (message) on each patch's pre-attention feature (state), allowing similar contextual messages to produce distinct or opposing updates depending on the receiving position. Because scarce support masks cover only limited state-message combinations, a shared low-rank bilinear map confines the learned interactions to low-dimensional state, message, and output subspaces. Added before each adapted block's frozen FFN, these updates yield adapted features that are refined by frozen FFNs and deeper blocks for linear readout. With only 0.51M trainable parameters and single-pass, support-free inference, SMIA achieves six-dataset mean mIoUs of 77.40% at 5 shots and 80.99% at 10 shots across 20 medical and industrial tasks, surpassing the strongest prior methods by +3.61 and +3.93 percentage points. In controlled 10-shot comparisons across three benchmarks on the same DINOv3 backbone, SMIA also outperforms parameter-matched AdaptFormer, LoRA, and ParaX with a better precision-recall balance.
  </details>

- **[ScoutCache: Efficient Few-Step Autoregressive Video Diffusion via Conditional Readout and Selective Caching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21038)**  `arXiv:2611.21038`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hao Tan, Jinghui Qin, Xuming Hu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Few-step autoregressive video diffusion enables efficient long video generation, but inference still incurs substantial computational costs from repeated denoising and KV cache operations. Existing acceleration methods reduce parts of this overhead, while historical KV reading and cache construction remain costly across video chunks. To address these challenges, we propose ScoutCache, a training-free framework that jointly optimizes denoising, historical KV reading, and cache construction. We observe that shallow feature changes provide useful information for estimating clean latent updates, while historical KV dependence varies across Transformer layers and attention heads but remains relatively stable across video chunks. Based on these observations, we develop Conditional Denoising Readout (CDR) to estimate intermediate denoising updates from shallow feature changes and known noise variations. We further introduce Output Risk Guided Historical KV Reading (ORHR) to allocate historical reading budgets based on attention output perturbations, and Content and Coverage Guided Sparse Cache Construction (CCSC) to reduce cache construction costs by selecting informative spatial tokens while maintaining spatial coverage. Extensive experiments on Self-Forcing and LongLive using VBench and MovieGen demonstrate that ScoutCache achieves up to 2.28$\times$ and 1.86$\times$ speedups, respectively, while consistently improving generation quality without modifying pretrained model parameters.
  </details>

- **[MedJev: A System One Vision-Language Decision Model for Fast and Effective Medical Image Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20992)**  `arXiv:2611.20992`  `cs.CV`  
  _Fan Zhang, Hao Wu, Jinyang Wu, Hongyu Qu, Zheng Lian, Dongfu Yin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical image understanding with vision-language models (VLMs) typically relies on autoregressively generating reasoning and answer tokens. These unnecessary tokens make the inference cost scale with the response length and force the answer to be parsed from free-form text, incurring avoidable latency and decision errors. To this end, we introduce \textsc{MedJev}, a \emph{system one} vision-language decision model that maps medical images, questions, and candidate answers directly to decisions in a single multimodal forward pass, without any autoregressive generation process. For fast inference, \textsc{MedJev} removes the upper language layers and the generative output pathway, while keeping the full vision pathway and all visual tokens.
