We present DeepVFR, an initial controlled synthetic benchmark-not a new retouching architecture-for paired face video retouching. Starting from paired synthetic DeepFRR images, Wan2.2 generates mild-motion source clips, and backend-specific face transfer constructs synchronized proxy retouching targets, followed by automatic validity screening and manual visual inspection. DeepVFR contains 1,024 pairs of 150-frame clips at 30 fps, evaluated under an identity-disjoint protocol with explicit frame correspondence; release metadata will record the construction route. Within-benchmark baselines, external checkpoints applied to DeepVFR, qualitative analysis, and a 17-participant ranking study establish reference behavior in the controlled domain. The constructed targets exhibit a substantial low-preference tail in the human study. Because both streams inherit synthetic content and the targets additionally inherit commercial-API or face-transfer artifacts, the benchmark neither replaces artist-retouched captured video nor establishes synthetic-to-real generalization. DeepVFR provides a starting point for paired supervision and controlled evaluation. Data, individual study rankings, and training logs will be made publicly available upon acceptance. Broader motion coverage and real-video validation remain future work.
  </details>

- **[Agri-MRAG: Parameter-Enhanced Multimodal Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Agricultural Visual Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18771)**  `arXiv:2611.18771`  `cs.CV`  
  _Fuyong Liu, Xiantao Lei, Xining Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Retrieval-Augmented Generation (MRAG) has shown promise for Agricultural Visual Question Answering (Agri-VQA), but existing methods still face challenges in retrieving relevant knowledge while avoiding redundancy and in integrating retrieved evidence beyond simple prompt concatenation. To address these issues, we propose Agri-MRAG, a multimodal retrieval-augmented framework for agricultural VQA. Specifically, we first introduce a semantic-consistency-guided clustering mechanism that combines multimodal clustering and weighted relevance scoring to select high-confidence knowledge subsets and adaptively determine the retrieval size $k$. We then design a parameterized knowledge injection strategy, where retrieved agricultural knowledge is encoded into LoRA adapters and merged into the feed-forward network (FFN) layers of the MLLM, allowing external knowledge to influence generation through parameter-level adaptation. In addition, a perplexity-guided (PPL-guided) routing mechanism is employed to switch between implicit parametric reasoning and explicit retrieval-assisted generation according to model uncertainty, balancing inference efficiency and answer reliability. For evaluation, we use PlantVillageVQA as a broad agricultural benchmark and construct PlantVQA, a region-specific forestry and fruit crop VQA dataset containing 7,514 images and 15,028 expert-verified question-answer pairs. Experiments show that Agri-MRAG achieves ROUGE-L scores of 41.21 and 46.58 on the two datasets, outperforming mRAG by 11.70 and 4.51 points, and VisRAG by 8.80 and 3.39 points, respectively.
  </details>

- **[From Hosted Service to Field-Owned Infrastructure: Open, Self-Hosted Real-Time Camera-Trap Alerting with Regional Open-Weight Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18751)**  `arXiv:2611.18751`  `cs.CV`  
  _Paul Fergus_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cellular camera traps can alert field teams within minutes, supporting anti-poaching, threatened-species protection and human-wildlife conflict mitigation. Most systems use central servers, sending images of people and sensitive species locations outside the collecting organisation and often across national borders. We ask whether this capability can run on infrastructure owned by field organisations. Trap Tracker RT is an open-source, self-hosted application that receives images by email or locally via FTP/SFTP, classifies them with a project-specific model and sends alerts through a mail server chosen by the organisation. We evaluated DeepFaune-SSA v1.2, pairing MegaDetector v6 with a DINOv3-based classifier for 33 sub-Saharan taxa, against three open alternatives on 31,131 out-of-distribution images from Kenya, Namibia and Malawi, independent of its training data. DeepFaune-SSA achieved image-level species accuracy of 0.901 (95% CI 0.894-0.908), versus 0.530 for the global SpeciesNet classifier and 0.556 and 0.547 for YOLOv10x and YOLO26x detectors trained on the same regional data. DeepFaune-SSA had the highest recall for all 26 species evaluated. The single-stage detectors missed animals in about a quarter of images; SpeciesNet assigned species labels in only 57%, usually falling back to coarser labels otherwise. In bench tests of the fully local path, alerts were issued about 6 s after upload completion, including with slowed and interrupted uploads. The two-stage pipeline processed 19 images per minute on eight CPU threads without a graphics processor. Trap Tracker RT is used by five organisations in the United Kingdom and Kenya, including for human-elephant conflict mitigation. We discuss how data paths, rather than inference location, determine data sovereignty, the responsibilities of self-hosting and the ethics of real-time monitoring.
  </details>

- **[Feature-Space Latent Forecasting for Endoscopic Surgical Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18750)**  `arXiv:2611.18750`  `cs.CV`  
  _Songtao Hu, Hang Su_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Surgical workflow assistance is inherently anticipatory: systems must reason about how procedures evolve, not just recognize the current frame. However, many prior approaches perform prediction in pixel space by synthesizing future frames, which primarily focuses on appearance fidelity rather than the semantic representations required for downstream reasoning. To address this limitation, we propose a feature-space semantic forecasting framework that models temporal dynamics directly within the representations of a frozen Vision Foundation Model (VFM). Specifically, we build a hierarchical target representation from multi-layer VFM features, replace future-frame feature tokens with learnable \texttt{[MASK]} tokens, and train a masked transformer to forecast the corresponding future latent features from observed context, thereby formulating procedural evolution as masked future feature prediction. Experiments on public laparoscopic benchmarks demonstrate consistent improvements in phase anticipation, multi-label tool presence prediction, and remaining-time estimation over RGB-based forecasting baselines, while enabling a plug-and-play representation interface for lightweight downstream heads.
  </details>

- **[VTG-RAG: Long-Video Reasoning via Query-Adaptive Coarse-to-Fine Sampling and Temporal Graph Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18746)**  `arXiv:2611.18746`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yao Lu, Yunhao Wang, Yaxin Gao, Feifei Teng, Chenxiao Liu, Zhe Tang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-video reasoning requires a system to localize sparse evidence over extended temporal sequences, preserve temporal structure, and generate answers despite substantial multimodal distractors. Existing video retrieval-augmented generation methods typically build indexes from uniformly sampled video segments. In long-video settings, this design readily introduces irrelevant context and is unreliable for questions with explicit temporal constraints. We propose VTG-RAG, a framework for long-video question answering that combines query-adaptive coarse-to-fine sampling with temporal graph retrieval. First, a two-stage sampling strategy selects core segments with high mean query relevance or high intra-segment relevance variance. The framework then integrates subtitles, speech transcripts, visual descriptions, entities, and relations from these segments into a temporally ordered multimodal graph. Finally, it performs temporally filtered, two-pass retrieval and reasoning across frame, text, and graph evidence spaces. A lot of experiments have proven the effectiveness of the VTG-RAG. Specifically, we evaluate the framework on Video-MME and LongVideoBench. On LongVideoBench, the reported Qwen2.5-VL-32B and 72B results improve from 48.16% to 50.56% and from 52.13% to 53.32%, respectively, with 24 answer-input frames. These correspond to gains of 2.40 and 1.19 percentage points. The ablation experiments of VTG-RAG further support that the contributions of each component are very important.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Raw Similarity: Prior-Aware Fusion for Out-of-Distribution Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18692)**  `arXiv:2611.18692`  `cs.CV`  
  _Lakpa Tamang, Mohamed Reda Bouadjenek, Saman Forouzandeh, Kamal Berahmand, Mahdi Jalili, Sunil Aryal_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Out-of-distribution (OOD) detection in vision-language models presents unique challenges due to the reliance on cross-modal similarity. Even when embeddings are well separated for in-distribution (ID) data, similarity scores can remain overconfident for OOD inputs. A common remedy is to adapt the model on ID data, but adaptation discards the pretrained geometry that made the similarity meaningful. We propose multi-modal logit fusion (MM-Log), which keeps both by pairing a gradient-free zero-shot prior with a task-adapted branch in each modality and fuses their four cross-modal similarity channels into a single OOD score. Because the zero-shot priors are never adapted, the cross-channels act as a consistency check between the pretrained and adapted geometries: an OOD input that fools the adapted branch through a shortcut must additionally agree with frozen CLIP to retain a high score. We formalize this as a margin bound showing that the fused score is provably penalized by disagreement between the zero-shot and adapted branches, a signal to which adapted-only scores are structurally blind. MM-Log requires no auxiliary OOD data, negative prompts, or test-time adaptation, and trains on few-shot ID data alone. Across Far-OOD, Near-OOD, and Hard-OOD benchmarks, MM-Log consistently outperforms strong CLIP-based baselines.
  </details>

- **[ThinkWise: Learning When to Think in Large Multimodal Reasoning Models via Visual and Difficulty Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18685)**  `arXiv:2611.18685`  `cs.CV`  
  _Wenkang Zhou, Chen Yang, Jiaheng Peng, Xitong Li, Hongzhi Yin, Yunke Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Multimodal Reasoning Models (LMRMs) extend large language models to heterogeneous modalities, demonstrating strong potential across a wide range of open-ended tasks. Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has been widely adopted to improve reasoning performance in LMRMs; however, existing approaches overlook the critical role of visual perception uncertainty in guiding reasoning processes. We term this limitation Reasoning Allocation Misalignment, where the misalignment between visual perception uncertainty and reasoning depth leads to over-allocation of deep reasoning under uncertain visual grounding, resulting in unnecessarily long reasoning trajectories and degraded performance. To address this issue, we propose ThinkWise, a perception-aware RLVR framework that jointly models task difficulty and visual perception state to regulate reasoning depth. Specifically, we introduce an instance-adaptive visual perception modeling mechanism that enables context-aware calibration of perception uncertainty, leading to more accurate perception estimation. We further design a structured reward formulation that jointly considers perception, correctness, and task difficulty. In particular, an answer reward provides supervision for correctness, a perception- and difficulty-conditioned length reward adaptively regulates reasoning depth, and a perception reward encourages trajectories with stronger visual grounding. Extensive experiments on multiple multimodal reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that ThinkWise consistently improves the trade-off between accuracy and reasoning efficiency across both 3B and 7B models. These results highlight the importance of perception-aware reasoning and establish a principled framework for advancing LMRMs.
  </details>

- **[PolarGait: Constructing Complementary Views from a Single Human Sketch for Gait Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18682)**  `arXiv:2611.18682`  `cs.CV`  
  _Chao Zhang, Zhuang Zheng, Zhanyong Mei, Huayan Pu, Jun Luo, Jinglei Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing multimodal gait recognition methods exploit complementary information by fusing heterogeneous representations. Whether useful complementarity can be constructed from a single representation before fusion remains less explored. We propose PolarGait, which constructs complementary views of Human Sketch through filtering and spatial reparameterization. Filter-Oriented Sketch Decomposition (FOSD) uses Gaussian smoothing to extract coherent structures and absolute residuals to capture local response differences. Gait-Oriented Polar Transformation (GOPT) combines silhouette-guided sketch normalization, a gait-oriented center, and lower-body-biased non-uniform angular sampling to construct a polar view complementary to the Cartesian representation. Silhouette provides auxiliary global body-shape information. Experiments on CCPG, SUSTech1K, CASIA-B, and Gait3D validate the framework. Under the single-Sketch setting, FOSD+GOPT improves the original Sketch baseline by 6.6% on CCPG and 4.6% on CASIA-B. The complete PolarGait achieves Rank-1 accuracies of 87.9%, 95.2%, 96.6%, and 86.4% on the four benchmarks, respectively. These results show that complementary gait information can be constructed within a single structured representation and integrated for robust gait recognition.
  </details>

- **[Not All Steps Matter: Temporally Adaptive Spectral Control for Concept Removal in Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18678)**  `arXiv:2611.18678`  `cs.CV`  
  _Chanaka Thushitha Bandara, Lakmali Nadeesha Kumari, Sen-Ching Samson Cheung_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As text-to-image diffusion models see widespread deployment, removing unsafe or undesirable concepts without retraining has become a pressing need. Spectral methods are attractive because they are training-free and reversible, yet current variants apply the same output-value (OV) circuit intervention at every denoising step, assuming concept information is temporally uniform. This wastes intervention where concept energy is low while degrading image quality, an inefficiency that compounds across concept types. Through timestep-resolved spectral analysis, concept-specific energy is found to follow a consistent temporal hierarchy: content concepts decay fastest, object concepts are intermediate, and style concepts persist longest. This paper proposes Timestep-Adaptive Spectral Nullification (TASN), which adapts suppression strength and spectral components to the concept energy profile. Cross-attention at each timestep is decomposed into concept-aligned and concept-orthogonal components, and it is proved that when the orthogonal component is approximately stable, the collateral-minimizing schedule sets suppression proportional to concept energy. TASN implements this with a timestep-adaptive spectral subset tracking only active concept directions, strictly generalizing uniform Spectral Nullification (SN). On five red-teaming benchmarks, TASN lowers attack success rate below uniform SN while recovering the majority of its quality loss; ablations confirm gains arise from temporal targeting rather than reduced suppression, generalizing across concept categories and architectures.
  </details>

- **[LogicEye: Agent Driven Multimodal Dataset for Interpretable Ophthalmic Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18669)**  `arXiv:2611.18669`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jiajie Mu, Lu Chen, Zheyun Qin, Xiaoming Xi, Lishan Qiao, Xiushan Nie, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clinical adoption of ophthalmic Artificial Intelligence remains limited by low interpretability and a lack of finely annotated multimodal data. Most existing datasets provide only coarse disease labels without the granular pathological findings required for differential diagnosis. We present LogicEye$^{Agent}$, a dual-pool inference and verification agent that emulates clinical reasoning by jointly verifying positive inclusion signs and negative exclusion criteria. Using LogicEye$^{Agent}$, we curate LogicEye$^{Data}$, a large-scale multimodal dataset of 804,461 fundus and OCT images paired with structured pathological reports spanning 38 diseases and 29 subtypes, with quality assured through joint verification by multimodal large language models and clinical experts. Building on this dataset, we further develop LogicEye$^{Bench}$, which incorporates a category-constrained loss for hierarchical disease recognition and report generation. Grounding coarse labels into verified visual findings effectively curtails diagnostic hallucinations and establishes a solid foundation for interpretable vision-language modeling in ophthalmology. The code is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[UMM-JEPA: Generative Pretraining via Masked Next-Scale Representation Prediction for Unified Multimodal Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18668)**  `arXiv:2611.18668`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yucheng Zhou, Yunjia Xi, Di Yin, Jianbing Shen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unified multimodal models can support both visual understanding and generation, yet generative training does not necessarily improve understanding. We argue that this limitation may arise from a mismatch between the representation targeted by generation and the perceptual representation used for understanding. Conventional generation objectives primarily optimize synthesis-oriented spaces such as discrete visual codes or generative latents. We therefore introduce UMM-JEPA, which reformulates generation as progressive prediction of continuous visual-understanding representations. The model constructs a multi-scale representation hierarchy from coarse to fine, while pixel rendering is decoupled from representation pretraining and attached only afterward. Controlled comparisons show that the representation-prediction objective accounts for most of the understanding improvement, while a data-matched control leaves the group means nearly unchanged. UMM-JEPA reaches a 16-benchmark visual mean of 51.71, against 46.36 for the understanding-only Qwen-VL baseline, and the text mean also rises. Compared with discrete-code and latent-flow generation objectives, perceptual representation prediction yields stronger and more consistent transfer to understanding. Linear probes and visual-evidence interventions further reveal richer object and spatial structure and stronger use of image-specific information. These results suggest that generation can benefit understanding when it directly supervises the perceptual representations that understanding consumes.
  </details>

- **[Visual Cipher Bench: Benchmarking LLM Agents on Long-Horizon Visual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16861)**  `arXiv:2611.16861`  `cs.CV`  
  _Deyao Hong, Jiayi Jiang, Yuchun Chen, Yixuan Shi, Wenyi Li, Zheng Jiang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While language-model agents excel at long-horizon coding and terminal tasks, this success relies on two conditions: explicit text specifications and immediate execution feedback. However, neither condition holds in real-world problems where critical information exists only in images, leaving it unclear whether long-horizon capability transfers from text to visual reasoning. In such visual settings, because rules and clues resist text transcription and lack test-case feedback, agents must repeatedly inspect images, simulate spatial transformations, and autonomously cross-check clues. Despite this challenge, existing evaluations fail to capture such capability: visual reasoning benchmarks largely reduce tasks to single-turn, short-chain question answering, whereas agent benchmarks focus on text-based environments or interactive states that permit trial-and-error. To bridge this gap, we introduce Visual Cipher Bench, a collection of 40 original visual puzzles across four categories: spatial manipulation and simulation, logical constraints and planning, rule induction, and knowledge-based decoding. Each puzzle provides only one to three black-and-white line drawings and a minimal submission instruction, requiring agents to discover the underlying solving mechanism entirely from the images. Across 29 reasoning-effort configurations of eight frontier models, even the strongest model, GPT-6 Astra (max), achieves only 26.7% accuracy, while five of the eight models score below 1%. Yet on a ten-task subset, providing a single non-spoiler mechanism hint boosts accuracy from 12.2% to 51.1%. This sharp contrast shows that the primary bottleneck of current agents lies not in executing multi-step plans, but in autonomously discovering rules from visual evidence. Visual Cipher Bench thus provides a rigorous benchmark for measuring long-horizon visual reasoning in agents.
  </details>

- **[What Factors Matter for Collection-Level Counting with Human-AI Collaboration?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14380)**  `arXiv:2611.14380`  `cs.CV`  
  _Seoyun Jeong, Logan Lawrence, Owen L. Martin, Benjamin Koger, Daniel Sheldon, Subhransu Maji_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many scientific and policy decisions require estimating the total number of target objects across a large image collection. Exhaustive human labeling is often infeasible, while raw model predictions can be biased, especially under domain shift. Recent human--AI estimation methods combine imperfect model predictions with limited human verification to obtain reliable collection-level estimates. However, existing work provides little guidance on how practitioners should choose among models, estimators, and adaptation strategies for a new deployment. We study these choices within a common human-in-the-loop counting framework on CLC-12, a benchmark of 12 tasks spanning biomedical microscopy, ground-level and aerial wildlife surveys, and overhead imagery of urban structures. Our results show that modern multimodal models provide a strong, training-free starting point for many tasks, while fine-tuned detectors become more advantageous at larger labeling budgets and across repeated deployments. Estimation efficiency improves further when model predictions are used to prioritize which units humans label, rather than sampling uniformly. We also find that each model's ranking quality can be estimated with a small pilot set, yielding practical guidance for selecting strategies on new counting tasks. In a field deployment on aerial pronghorn surveys, a task-specific adaptation of these methods meets the survey's $\pm$ 5% tolerance after reviewing a median of only 3.2% of collected images.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Real or Fake: A Dual-Channel Authenticity and Reasoning Protocol for Photographic Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10908)**  `arXiv:2611.10908`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xiaoxiao Li, Ruinan Jin, Lili Meng, Miaosen Wang, Athula Balachandran, Pei Cao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Forensic verification has converged on a binary "real vs. fake" label that confates fully synthetic, tampered, and AI-retouched images despite their very different consequences. We argue that GenAI manipulations decompose along two complementary channels: a camera channel of sensor fingerprints (destroyed by neural processing), and a semantic channel of scene-level coherence (disrupted by content edits). Each manipulation type leaves a distinctive two-channel signature. We instantiate this taxonomy in 2CAP (2-Channel Authenticity Protocol), pairing a contrastively-trained camera encoder with a frozen semantic encoder to serve two applications on a shared backbone: (i) four-class authenticity classification via cross-attention fusion with learnable reliability weights; and (ii) evidence generation, where an authentic image serves as a reference for a query and the two encoders produce per-channel alignment scores together with a patch-level saliency map. These signals are supplied as privileged context to a frozen Vision-Language Model (VLM) through an Observe-Generate-Refine loop, yielding explanations with localization without forensic fine-tuning. On a multi-source benchmark, 2CAP attains the best classification performance, including strong retouching detection where existing methods fail, and improves explanation quality.
  </details>

- **[A Bridge from Paste to Place: Physics-Guided One-Step Image Composition via Object-Scene Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09387)**  `arXiv:2611.09387`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hongzhen Li, Youwei Pan, Yingfang Zhu, Sen Zhu, Fengjie Zhu, Leilei Cao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Realistic image composition makes a pasted object appear naturally present in a scene while preserving the pasted layout and fine foreground and background details. Reference-based insertion methods preserve overall object identity but can alter pose and fine details, often at substantial computational cost. Foreground harmonization and shadow generation address individual aspects of this task, but neither alone ensures natural integration. An efficient, unified solution remains an open challenge, and large-scale, high-quality datasets and benchmarks for this task are lacking. To address these challenges, we introduce Paste2Place, an end-to-end, physics-guided flow-based model that learns a direct latent bridge from pasted inputs to realistic composites, guided by image-space corrections analytically derived from predicted physical components of object and scene appearance changes. To support training and evaluation, we introduce P2P-Data with 86K manually reviewed synthetic and real-scene training pairs, including dense physical annotations from our Blender rendering and validation pipeline for the synthetic subset. P2P-Bench combines paired compositions and manually pasted inputs from collected images into 1.5K examples for evaluating composition under user-chosen placements. Extensive qualitative and quantitative experiments demonstrate that Paste2Place achieves substantial advantages in image composition quality and inference speed across diverse scenarios.
  </details>

- **[TwinPast: Benchmarking Historical Evidence Use in Streaming Video Understanding with Evidence Contrastive Video Pairs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41983)**  `arXiv:2610.41983`  `cs.CV`  
  _Haotian Yang, Yong Chen, Cheng Peng, Haobo Wang, Zhongle Xie, Xuanru Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  High accuracy on streaming video benchmarks does not show that a model uses visual history. Auditing OVO-Bench and StreamingBench, we find that recent frames alone suffice for 32% of sampled historical-task questions, and that the question and options alone also yield many correct answers. We introduce TwinPast, a benchmark of 350 human-reviewed video pairs spanning five historical tasks. The two videos in a pair share the recent video context, question, and ordered options, and differ in earlier events and therefore in the correct answer. Each video is evaluated independently and PairAcc requires both answers to be correct, so an answer determined by the shared inputs cannot solve a pair. Masking the annotated critical evidence lowers PairAcc from as high as 91% to at most 19%, whereas equal-area masking elsewhere changes it by at most 3.4 points. The strongest streaming system reaches 49% PairAcc, while general-purpose MLLMs given each complete video reach 67-91%, leaving substantial headroom. Streaming systems fail mainly by giving both histories the same answer. They give different valid answers on at most 57% of pairs, while the general-purpose MLLMs do so on 79-94%. Case studies further show wrong answers when the deciding evidence is still available at the final query.
  </details>

- **[When Watermarks Turn Adversarial: Exposing Vulnerabilities in Document Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15728)**  `arXiv:2610.15728`  `cs.CV`  
  _Qingzhe Han, Fan Dang, Shengqi Chen, Xinyi Sun, Haoyu Xiong, Lingkun Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visible watermarks are common in digital documents and persist during vision-language model (VLM) processing. We show that they can also serve as adversarial surfaces for document understanding. We propose Watermark-based Adversarial Surface Perturbation (WASP), a white-box attack that optimizes pixels inside fixed watermark strokes without requiring downstream questions, answers, or task-specific losses. We further introduce Balanced Aggregation Loss (BAL), which emphasizes visual token groups that remain highly similar to clean representations. Adversarial watermarks degrade performance substantially more than ordinary watermarks. On DocVQA, WASP achieves attack success rates of 96.81% and 71.93% against Qwen2.5-VL and InternVL2.5; BAL improves over a standard token hinge objective by 9.21 and 2.24 percentage points. On 1,651 OmniDocBench pages, WASP increases MinerU2.5's text edit distance by 0.7748 relative to ordinary watermarks. WASP also transfers within model families and across readers sharing a vision-tower architecture. Attack effectiveness depends on watermark geometry, model architecture, and preprocessing; in structured parsing, disrupted layout and routing can propagate errors downstream. These results expose an underexplored security risk in document understanding systems.
  </details>

- **[What Do Verifiable Rewards Teach Video-Language Models About Time? A Controlled Multi-Model Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03792)**  `arXiv:2610.03792`  `cs.CV`  
  _Avyay Sadhu, Patrick Cooper_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards (RLVR) has produced large reasoning gains in language models, and verifiable video benchmarks make the recipe directly applicable to causal-temporal video question answering. We present a controlled study of what RLVR actually teaches video-language models about temporal structure. We fine-tune four open models with group relative policy optimization (GRPO) under three data recipes using annotated synthetic questions, self-supervised real-video pretext tasks, and a one-to-one mixture, plus a targeted real-video augmentation arm. We evaluate the model-recipe grid on 105 CLEVRER questions with one correct option and on two real-video transfer sets. The 8B ordered gain is 4.8 points (95% video-bootstrap CI [-5.6, 15.1]), while blind accuracy increases 10.5 points ([0.9, 20.6]). The 4B model gains an accuracy advantage for ordered over shuffled frames. On the real-video sets, the 8B synthetic-only checkpoint loses 26.7 and 25.2 points, with smaller regressions under mixed and augmented recipes. In a separate scene-disjoint, single-gold Qwen3-VL-8B experiment, supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and GRPO each complete 1,200 updates at three fixed seeds. GRPO minus SFT is -3.89 points in ordered accuracy (nominal 97.5% video-bootstrap CI [-7.36, -0.45]) and -2.78 points in ordered-minus-shuffled accuracy ([-6.60, 0.87]). Transfer differences are sensitive to parseability. Intervals quantify test-video uncertainty conditional on the fitted runs. The results show why benchmark accuracy, transfer, and presented-order sensitivity need to be evaluated separately.
  </details>

- **[FlowTool: Controlling Tool Parameter in Image Retouching via Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35673)**  `arXiv:2609.35673`  `cs.CV`  
  _Thanh-Long V. Le, Steven Walton, Seunghyun Yoon, Branislav Kveton, Trung Bui, Eunho Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tool-based image editing (image retouching) is commonly formulated with autoregressive multimodal large language models (MLLMs) that sequentially generate reasoning, tool selections, and parameter values. In this work, we present a novel approach to tool-based image editing by framing the task as a flow matching problem. We introduce FlowTool, a framework that directly models the distribution of high-quality tool parameters conditioned on the input image and user instruction using conditional rectified flow. FlowTool combines a vision-language model backbone for multimodal understanding with a Diffusion Transformer parameter generator that transforms Gaussian noise into an editing plan. We train FlowTool with a two-stage supervised flow-matching curriculum, followed by reward-based post-training. Across four benchmarks, FlowTool significantly outperforms specialized MLLM agents and remains competitive with proprietary MLLMs, while reducing latency by at least $50\times$ and using nearly half the memory of the evaluated local baselines. These results demonstrate that tool-based image editing can be effectively modeled as conditional generation over structured continuous editing parameters, without autoregressive reasoning.
  </details>

- **[Object-Aware Background-Controlled Editing via Weighted Velocity Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06288)**  `arXiv:2609.06288`  `cs.CV`  
  _Wuji Wang, Yue Wu, Chenhao Yi, Shuhui Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training-free image editing steers diffusion or flow-matching generative models at inference time by modifying prompt-conditioned denoising velocities. Existing velocity-based editors often apply prompt-induced residuals globally over the latent space and rely on the model to localize semantic changes implicitly. For object-centric edits, these residuals are rarely zero outside the target object, so small non-target components can accumulate during multi-step integration, causing background drift and unstable object boundaries. We propose Object-Aware Velocity Control (OAVC), a training-free framework that introduces object-level control into the velocity-integration process. OAVC decouples where semantic residuals are allowed to act from how they are injected into the dynamics. It constructs a background-anchored reference interface under the source prompt and then performs object-localized safe semantic injection under the target prompt. A constrained injection operator suppresses drift-inducing velocity components, while time-adaptive spatial weighting stabilizes the transition near object boundaries. OAVC requires no training or modification of pretrained model parameters. Experiments on object-centric image and video benchmarks with image and video rectified-flow backbones show improved background preservation, structural fidelity, boundary stability, and temporal consistency while retaining effective localized editability.
  </details>

- **[CALE: Color-Adaptive Low-Light Enhancement via Learnable Intensity Coordinates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21847)**  `arXiv:2608.21847`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yi Ai, Zheng Chen, Yuanhao Cai, Yulun Zhang, Xiaokang Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Small brightness differences in dark regions are difficult to recover, and their spacing in the coordinates used for restoration depends on the intensity mapping. A globally learned Box--Cox exponent controls the full response, coupling different brightness intervals and applying the same mapping to every image. We propose Color-Adaptive Low-Light Enhancement (CALE), which combines an image-conditioned intensity representation with structure-conditioned rectified flow (\mbox{SR-RF}). The representation, Box--Cox Intensity Adaptation (BIA), refines a normalized Box--Cox transform using a monotone piecewise-linear curve. Compact statistics of the low-light observation determine the output widths of brightness intervals, allowing the response to vary across images. Positive, normalized slopes preserve intensity order and endpoints and give the intensity mapping an analytic inverse. Combined with polar chromaticity, BIA defines the coordinates for the \mbox{SR-RF} network. The mapping and restoration network are trained jointly so that the reconstruction objective shapes the intensity response. Experiments on paired benchmarks and unseen datasets show competitive restoration performance across diverse low-light scenes, with qualitative comparisons illustrating the recovery of scene colors, contrast, and detail. Coordinate ablations separate the effects of interval flexibility and input conditioning, while Full and Tiny models offer quality--computation trade-offs.
  </details>

- **[Peak-End-Net: A Peak-End Rule Inspired Framework for Generalizable Video Aesthetic Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13941)**  `arXiv:2607.13941`  `cs.CV`  
  _Geng Li, Haiwen Li, Rui Chen, Jing Tang, Lei Sun, Xiangxiang Chu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video aesthetic assessment (VAA) aims to predict how aesthetically pleasing a video is, yet remains far less explored than other visual assessment tasks. Its progress is hindered not only by the scarcity of large-scale benchmarks, but also by the intrinsic subjectivity of aesthetic judgment, which is shaped by human perception. In this paper, we revisit VAA from a psychological perspective and propose \textit{Peak-End-Net}, a lightweight and interpretable framework inspired by the \textit{peak-end rule}, which suggests that people tend to judge a temporal experience mainly according to its salient moments and the ending. Building on this intuition, we first transfer knowledge from image aesthetic assessment (IAA) to VAA by introducing a pretrained IAA head to produce frame-wise aesthetic priors, which serve as surrogate signals for identifying aesthetically salient moments and guiding \textit{peak-end rule}-based temporal aggregation. To further capture how a video evolves aesthetically over time, we design an aesthetic rhythm encoder that models temporal progression beyond isolated moments. Additionally, we refine the overall assessment through a dynamic gated fusion mechanism to improve robustness under distribution shift. Our method is built on a frozen vision transformer (ViT) and requires only a small number of trainable parameters, making it scalable and parameter-efficient. Extensive experiments on two existing VAA benchmarks, including in-domain evaluation on VADB and cross-domain testing on DIVIDE-3K, demonstrate that our approach achieves state-of-the-art performance, affirming the value of psychologically grounded modeling for VAA. Our code and models are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Breaking Déjà Vu: Independent Auditing of Visual Place Recognition through Vision-Language Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12818)**  `arXiv:2607.12818`  `cs.CV`  
  _Sania Waheed, Michael Milford, Sarvapali D. Ramchurn, Shoaib Ehsan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual place recognition (VPR) is a key enabler of accurate localization and long-term autonomous navigation in robotics applications, such as loop closure detection for simultaneous localisation and mapping (SLAM). However, real-world VPR deployment relies on selecting an image matching threshold that balances precision and recall. These thresholds are typically tuned using labeled validation data and fixed during deployment, making them unreliable under environmental changes where ground truth is unavailable. This is particularly problematic in safety-critical robotics, where accepting a false loop closure can corrupt the estimated trajectory and map. In this work, we introduce Visual Place Recognition Auditing, an independent post-retrieval verification framework that leverages Vision-Language Models (VLMs) to assess retrieved matches by reasoning jointly over query and candidate images. Unlike conventional verification methods, our approach performs instance-level verification without requiring architecture-specific confidence measures, dataset-dependent thresholds, or prior knowledge of the deployment environment. We evaluate our method on six benchmark datasets using five state-of-the-art VPR methods and four VLMs. Results show that VLM-based auditing improves recall@1 by 13.6% on average as compared to state-of-the-art methods while reducing false acceptance rates to 12%, maintaining precision above 95% and coverage above 75%.
  </details>

- **[Promptable Concept Segmentation from Above: Evaluating Zero-Shot Textual and Visual Prompting of SAM 3 in Remote Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09583)**  `arXiv:2607.09583`  `cs.CV`  
  _Mohammad Dabaja, Turgay Celik_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large-scale foundation models such as the Segment Anything Model 3 (SAM 3) promise a transition toward open-vocabulary, training-free computer vision. However, their capacity to generalize out-of-distribution to the top-down geometric structures of Earth Observation imagery remains largely unquantified. We present a multi-task empirical evaluation of SAM 3 across remote sensing scene classification, object detection, and instance segmentation, without any fine-tuning. For scene classification, we repurpose its decoupled binary presence head as a standalone zero-shot classifier. For detection and segmentation, we compare textual and visual prompts, where a visual prompt reveals one true instance of the target class in the image being segmented. Text alone performs poorly, while adding a visual prompt raises accuracy sharply, and combining both raises or lowers accuracy depending on the task. We also compare SAM 3's text-only performance against several general-domain, open-vocabulary detection and segmentation models under the same evaluation protocol, and find that it leads all of them on both benchmarks. Its accuracy without a visual prompt nonetheless remains limited, motivating parameter-efficient fine-tuning as a direction for future work.
  </details>

- **[UNIVERSE: Unified Video Action Models for Autonomous Driving with Flexible Mask-Modulated Modality Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05133)**  `arXiv:2607.05133`  `cs.CV`  
  _Mengmeng Liu, Diankun Zhang, Jiuming Liu, Jianfeng Cui, Hongwei Xie, Guang Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World Action Models (WAMs) can improve action generalization in autonomous driving by learning scene dynamics through dense future-video supervision. However, deploying such models often requires costly future-video generation, while architectures that bypass video rollout typically separate video and action parameters or alter their learned dependencies at inference. We introduce UNIVERSE, a unified video-action model built on a single mask-modulated Diffusion Transformer (DiT) that combines shared-parameter learning with modality-independent inference. Its Modality-Decoupling Visibility Mask allows future video and trajectory tokens to attend to a common historical context while blocking mutual attention between the two future modalities. As a result, future-video supervision directly updates the parameters used for trajectory denoising, yet video tokens can be omitted at test time without changing the trajectory branch's training-time attention dependencies. The same model supports trajectory-only, video-only, and joint video-action generation. Trajectory-only inference achieves a 4.3x speedup over coupled video-action rollout with comparable planning accuracy. UNIVERSE achieves 91.0 PDMS on NAVSIM, compared with 89.6 for a Two-DiT variant, and demonstrates strong zero-shot transfer to nuScenes and Bench2Drive without target-domain fine-tuning. Controlled ablations further support the complementary benefits of shared parameters, dense video co-training, and modality-decoupled visibility.
  </details>

- **[MACRO: Training-free Multi-plane Attention for Closeup Render Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03875)**  `arXiv:2607.03875`  `cs.CV`  
  _Nitzan Hodos, Roy Amoyal, Lior Fritz, Ianir Ideses, Sagie Benaim, Netalee Efrat_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Close-up rendering, zooming into a scene well beyond any training camera, is important for virtual production and interactive 3D content, yet remains an open challenge. 3D Gaussian splatting (3DGS) enables high-fidelity, real-time novel view synthesis, but its rendering quality degrades at close range. Recent diffusion-based methods that enhance the rendering by conditioning on reference images from the training set produce significant artifacts in this setting. We analyze this failure and identify its root cause: the scale gap between the close-up and reference views. We show that the features in reference-conditioned enhancement models are not scale-invariant, causing cross-view attention to retrieve incorrect correspondences when the same content appears at different scales, and that this mismatch cannot be corrected in latent space because the VAE encoder is not scale-equivariant. Building on this analysis we introduce MACRO, Multi-plane Attention for Closeup Render Optimization, a training-free method for high-quality close-up novel view synthesis from 3DGS. MACRO resolves the scale gap by leveraging the scene's known 3D structure: it decomposes the close-up into depth planes, crops and resizes references in image space to match the scale of each plane before encoding, and applies a depth-aware attention mask so each token attends only to scale-matched references. The method requires no architectural changes or additional training. We further contribute two new close-up novel view synthesis benchmarks, the first standardized evaluation protocol for this setting, and demonstrate state-of-the-art results on both, outperforming existing 3DGS and diffusion-based methods on both reconstruction and perceptual metrics. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[UnderOneFacade: Worldwide Facade Semantic Segmentation Benchmark Dataset](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02018)**  `arXiv:2607.02018`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yi Wang, Fan Wang, Prabin Gyawali, Ziyang Xu, Anna Klimkowska, Yixiong Jing, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Globally consistent semantic digital twins require centimeter-accurate and geographically transferable 3D facade segmentation. However, progress in facade parsing is limited by the lack of large-scale, standardized benchmarks for evaluating cross-domain generalization. Existing datasets are geographically narrow, semantically inconsistent, or insufficiently precise. We introduce UnderOneFacade, the largest cross-country and cross-continent 3D facade benchmark to date, comprising centimeter-accurate point clouds with hierarchical, harmonized, and architecturally grounded semantic labels totaling 2.7 billion annotated points. Through a systematic evaluation of representative point-, graph- and transformer-based architectures, we show that current methods struggle to recognize fine-grained architectural elements and degrade significantly across geographic domains, with the best models achieving only up to 33 IoU on the fine-grained LoFG3 benchmark. By combining geometric precision with standardized semantics at unprecedented scale, UnderOneFacade establishes a rigorous benchmark for developing robust and transferable 3D segmentation models. The dataset, evaluation scripts, and pretrained models will be released upon publication.
  </details>

- **[Perceive-to-Reason: Decoupling Perception and Reasoning for Fine-Grained Visual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01191)**  `arXiv:2607.01191`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hongxing Li, Xiufeng Huang, Dingming Li, Wenjing Jiang, Zixuan Wang, Haolei Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fine-grained visual reasoning remains challenging for vision-language models, especially when small but critical visual cues are buried in high-resolution images. Existing approaches rely on repeated cropping or test-time visual search to introduce local evidence, but they typically do not explicitly distinguish perception from reasoning. In this paper, we propose Perceive-to-Reason (P2R), a unified framework that formulates fine-grained visual reasoning as a two-stage process: the model first localizes question-relevant evidence as a Perceiver, and then answers the question as a Reasoner based on the annotated image and cropped regions. To better align training with this decoupled formulation, we further introduce Perception-Reasoning Alternating GRPO (PRA-GRPO), a role-aware reinforcement learning strategy that alternates between perception-focused and reasoning-focused updates using only final-answer supervision. Built on top of Qwen3-VL-Instruct-2B/4B/8B, P2R consistently improves performance across model scales. In particular, P2R-4B achieves 93.2% on V-Star, 81.9% on HR-Bench-4K, and 80.5% on HR-Bench-8K, substantially outperforming its corresponding backbone. Further experiments show that the benefits of P2R extend beyond high-resolution benchmarks to broader multimodal reasoning tasks. These results suggest that explicitly decoupling perception from reasoning provides an effective framework for fine-grained visual reasoning.
  </details>

- **[Caption Bottleneck Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00578)**  `arXiv:2607.00578`  `cs.CV`  
  _Seref Baris Cagliyan, Umut Ozdemir, Merve Tapli, Emre Akbas_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Concept Bottleneck Models (CBMs) provide interpretability by routing predictions through a layer of human-understandable concepts. However, defining an optimal concept set for a specific dataset remains an open challenge. Existing approaches rely on expensive expert annotations or LLM-generated lists based solely on class names. Even "open-vocabulary" variants typically depend on static concept sets, which restrict discovery and introduce label bias. Furthermore, traditional CBMs often suffer from information leakage, where unmodeled visual features bypass the bottleneck and compromise the integrity of the explanations. To overcome these limitations, we propose Caption Bottleneck Models (CaBM), a framework that circumvents the need for predefined concept sets by replacing rigid concept layers with free-form natural language. By representing images via LMM-generated captions and training a classifier strictly on this text, CaBM ensures a leakage-free architecture by construction. Additionally, by analyzing the text classifier post-training, CaBM autonomously discovers high-quality, dataset-specific concepts. Our results across fine- and coarse-grained benchmarks demonstrate that CaBM achieves competitive accuracy while preserving interpretability without the constraints of external dictionaries or manual labeling.
  </details>

- **[MOTOR: A Multimodal Dataset for Two-Wheeler Rider Behavior Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.22550)**  `arXiv:2605.22550`  `cs.CV`  
  _Varun A. Paturkar, Shankar Gangisetty, C.V. Jawahar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Two-wheelers account for a disproportionately high share of road fatalities in the Global South. Research on two-wheeler rider behavior, however, lags far behind four-wheelers, where multimodal datasets have driven major advances in Advanced Driver Assistance Systems (ADAS). To address this gap, we present the MOtorized TwO-wheeler Rider (MOTOR) dataset, the first large-scale, multi-view, multimodal resource dedicated to two-wheelers in dense, unstructured traffic. MOTOR comprises 1,629 sequences (25+ hours of video data) collected from 16 riders and integrates synchronized front, rear, and helmet videos, rider eye-gaze from wearable trackers, on-road audio, and telemetry (GPS, accelerometer, gyroscope). Rich annotations capture traffic context, rider state, 12 riding maneuvers spanning conventional and unconventional behaviors, and legality labels (Legal, Illegal, Unspecified). We benchmark rider behavior recognition and maneuver legality classification using state-of-the-art video action recognition backbones (CNN and Transformer-based), extended with multimodal fusion, and find that combining RGB, gaze, and telemetry consistently yields the best performance. MOTOR thus provides a unique foundation for advancing safety-critical understanding of two-wheeler riding. It offers the research community a benchmark to develop and evaluate models for behavior analysis, legality-aware prediction, and intelligent transportation systems. Dataset and code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[VISTAQA: Benchmarking Joint Visual Question Answering and Pixel-Level Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20676)**  `arXiv:2605.20676`  `cs.CV`  
  _Mozhgan Nasr Azadani, Yimu Wang, Yongpeng Zhu, Lihong Chen, Milan Ganai, Sean Sedwards, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Establishing a clear link between model predictions and the visual evidence that supports them is critical for transparency and reliability in multimodal reasoning, yet current multimodal large language model (MLLM) evaluations do not explicitly enforce this alignment. Existing benchmarks assess either textual answer correctness or pixel-level localization in isolation, leaving the coupling of reasoning and grounding an open challenge. We introduce VISTAQA, a comprehensive benchmark for joint evaluation of free-form answer correctness and pixel-level evidence grounding in visual question answering. VISTAQA comprises 1,157 expert-curated samples spanning six task types and six visual domains, ranging from direct perception to compositional and relational reasoning. VISTAQA requires models to not only answer correctly, but to also provide precise segmentation masks that support their answers. It also includes hallucination-aware examples where no valid visual evidence exists. To support this enhanced evaluation, we introduce GROVE, a unified evaluation metric that enforces joint correctness by combining textual accuracy and grounding quality via a per-sample geometric mean, ensuring neither dimension can compensate for deficiencies in the other. Comprehensive experiments across grounding-aware models and hybrid pipelines with general-purpose MLLMs reveal that even the strongest systems achieve limited performance under GROVE, highlighting a substantial gap between answer accuracy and visual evidence alignment. Project page can be found atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[EchoPrune: Interpreting Redundancy as Temporal Echoes for Efficient VideoLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10050)**  `arXiv:2605.10050`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jiameng Li, Minye Wu, Jiezhang Cao, Aleksei Tiulpin, Matthew B. Blaschko_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-form video understanding remains challenging for Video Large Language Models (VideoLLMs), as the dense frame sampling introduces massive visual tokens while sparse sampling risks missing critical temporal evidence and leading to LLM hallucination. Existing training-free token reduction methods either treat videos equally as static images or rely on segment-level merging heuristics, which weaken fine-grained spatiotemporal modeling and introduce additional overhead. In this paper, we propose EchoPrune, a lightweight and training-free token pruning method that improves temporal resolution under a fixed LLM-side visual token budget. Our core idea is to interpret redundant video tokens as temporal echoes: if a token is well reconstructed from the previous frame, it is merely a temporally redundant echo; otherwise, it may capture new events, motion, or query-relevant visual evidence. Based on this insight, EchoPrune scores visual tokens by (i) query-guided crossmodal relevance and (ii) temporal reconstruction error, measured by correspondence matching and echo matching across consecutive frames. The selected tokens preserve task-relevant cues and temporal novelty while suppressing predictable redundancy, allowing VideoLLMs to observe more frames without increasing the decoding budget. Extensive experiments on LLaVA-OV, Qwen2.5VL, and Qwen3VL across six video understanding benchmarks show that EchoPrune enables VideoLLMs to process up to $\mathbf{20\times}$ frames under the same token budget, yielding improved performance ($\mathbf{5\%^\uparrow}$) and inference speedup ($\mathbf{5.6\times}$ for prefilling) on LLaVA-OV-7B.
  </details>

- **[Reflection Anchors for Propagation-Aware Visual Retention in Long-Chain Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09614)**  `arXiv:2605.09614`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xuan Gong, Hanbo Huang, Hao Zheng, Yiran Zhang, Wenbin Dai, Weishu Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning improves large vision--language models, but visual information often fades during generation, limiting long-horizon multimodal reasoning. Existing methods either re-inject vision at inference or train policies for stronger grounding, but where to intervene relies on perception heuristics rather than principled gain analysis, and how local visual influence propagates remains implicit. We study this problem from an information-theoretic standpoint and derive a lower bound on the downstream visual gain of a one-step intervention, which suggests two factors: local branching room (token entropy) and downstream visual propagation potential (suffix divergence from a vision-marginalized reference). Guided by this analysis, we propose reflection-anchor policy optimization (RAPO), a GRPO-based policy optimization method that selects high-entropy reflection anchors and optimizes a chain-masked finite-window KL surrogate for downstream visual dependence. Experiments on reasoning-intensive and general-domain benchmarks show that RAPO delivers substantial gains over strong baselines across multiple LVLM backbones. Mechanism analyses further indicate that reflection anchors are enriched for visually sensitive decision points and that RAPO increases contrastive visual-dependence signals along generated trajectories.
  </details>

- **[EgoEMG: A Multimodal Egocentric Dataset with Bilateral EMG and Vision for Hand Pose Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05712)**  `arXiv:2605.05712`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ziheng Xi, Jiayi Yu, Yanbo Duan, Yitao Wang, Jinyang Yu, Zhanwei Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hand pose estimation from egocentric video is challenging under adverse visual conditions, such as occlusion. Wrist-worn surface electromyography (sEMG) measures muscle activity without requiring visual access to the hand, offering a wearable complement to egocentric cameras. Evaluating their combined use requires synchronized physiological and visual recordings with continuous pose annotations, which existing EMG-based hand pose datasets do not jointly provide. We introduce EgoEMG, a multimodal dataset pairing bilateral wrist sEMG with egocentric video and motion-capture-derived hand poses, supplemented by external multi-view RGB-D recordings. EgoEMG contains 25.9 hours of recordings from 68 participants, covering 60 bare-hand gesture classes and interactions with 10 everyday objects. We establish EMG-only, vision-only, and fusion benchmarks with shared 22-dimensional pose targets and evaluations on unseen users, gestures, and objects. For EMG-only estimation, EMGFormer achieves the lowest overall MAE among the compared methods on EgoEMG and competitive results on EMG2Pose. Our residual fusion model combines ViT and EMGFormer features. Adding EMG reduces error for both single-frame and temporal vision models, with MAE decreasing from $7.39^\circ$ to $7.06^\circ$ for ViT-L. The largest absolute reduction occurs when evaluating interactions with an unseen object. Generalization to unseen users remains challenging. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Visual Cues: Semantic-Driven Token Filtering and Expert Routing for Anytime Person ReID](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.15090)**  `arXiv:2604.15090`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jiaxuan Li, Xin Wen, Zhihang Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Any-Time Person Re-identification (AT-ReID) necessitates the robust retrieval of target individuals under arbitrary conditions, encompassing both modality shifts (daytime and nighttime) and extensive clothing-change scenarios, ranging from short-term to long-term intervals. However, existing methods are highly relying on pure visual features, which are prone to change due to environmental and time factors, resulting in significantly performance deterioration under scenarios involving illumination caused modality shifts or cloth-change. In this paper, we propose Semantic-driven Token Filtering and Expert Routing (STFER), a novel framework that leverages the ability of Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) to generate identity consistency text, which provides identity-discriminative features that are robust to both clothing variations and cross-modality shifts between RGB and IR. Specifically, we employ instructions to guide the LVLM in generating identity-intrinsic semantic text that captures biometric constants for the semantic model driven. The text token is further used for Semantic-driven Visual Token Filtering (SVTF), which enhances informative visual regions and suppresses redundant background noise. Meanwhile, the text token is also used for Semantic-driven Expert Routing (SER), which integrates the semantic text into expert routing, resulting in more robust multi-scenario gating. Extensive experiments on the Any-Time ReID dataset (AT-USTC) demonstrate that our model achieves state-of-the-art results. Moreover, the model trained on AT-USTC was evaluated across 5 widely-used ReID benchmarks demonstrating superior generalization capabilities with highly competitive results. Our code will be available soon.
  </details>

- **[AffectAgent: Collaborative Multi-Agent Reasoning for Retrieval-Augmented Multimodal Emotion Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.12735)**  `arXiv:2604.12735`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zeheng Wang, Zitong Yu, Yijie Zhu, Bo Zhao, Haochen Liang, Taorui Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based multimodal emotion recognition relies on static parametric memory and often hallucinates when interpreting nuanced affective states. In this paper, given that single-round retrieval-augmented generation is highly susceptible to modal ambiguity and therefore struggles to capture complex affective dependencies across modalities, we introduce AffectAgent, an affect-oriented multi-agent retrieval-augmented generation framework that leverages collaborative decision-making among agents for fine-grained affective understanding. Specifically, AffectAgent comprises three jointly optimized specialized agents, namely a query planner, an evidence filter, and an emotion generator, which collaboratively perform analytical reasoning to retrieve cross-modal samples, assess evidence, and generate predictions. These agents are optimized end-to-end using Multi-Agent Proximal Policy Optimization (MAPPO) with a shared affective reward to ensure consistent emotion understanding. Furthermore, we introduce Modality-Balancing Mixture of Experts (MB-MoE) and Retrieval-Augmented Adaptive Fusion (RAAF), where MB-MoE dynamically regulates the contributions of different modalities to mitigate representation mismatch caused by cross-modal heterogeneity, while RAAF enhances semantic completion under missing-modality conditions by incorporating retrieved audiovisual embeddings. Extensive experiments on MER-UniBench demonstrate that AffectAgent achieves superior performance across complex scenarios. Our code will be released at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[TinyNeRV: Compact Neural Video Representations via Capacity Scaling, Distillation, and Low-Precision Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.09220)**  `arXiv:2604.09220`  `cs.CV`  
  _Muhammad Hannan Akhtar, Ihab Amer, Tamer Shanableh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neural Representations for Videos (NeRV) encode video sequences within neural decoder parameters, enabling direct frame reconstruction from temporal indices. Deploying NeRV in resource-constrained settings remains challenging because reconstruction quality, model capacity, decoding cost, and numerical precision are tightly coupled. This paper presents TinyNeRV, a deployment-oriented framework for compact neural video representations based on capacity scaling, teacher-guided quality recovery, and low-precision inference. We introduce NeRV-T (0.80M parameters, 22.62 GFLOPs) and NeRV-T+ (1.68M parameters, 89.80 GFLOPs) as minimum-compute and balanced low-capacity configurations. To recover detail without increasing inference cost, frequency-focal knowledge distillation emphasizes high-frequency teacher-student discrepancies, improving PSNR on Big Buck Bunny by 0.84 dB for NeRV-T and 0.88 dB for NeRV-T+. We also evaluate weights-only INT8, INT6, and INT4 quantization using post-training quantization (PTQ), quantization-aware training (QAT), and distillation-assisted QAT. Across the seven-sequence UVG benchmark, frequency-focal distillation improves baseline PSNR by an average of 0.70 dB for NeRV-T and 0.78 dB for NeRV-T+. Across UVG and Big Buck Bunny, INT8 is nearly lossless; INT6 causes modest PTQ degradation largely recovered by QAT; and INT4 benefits from retraining-aware adaptation and teacher supervision. The results establish practical capacity-supervision-precision trade-offs for real-time, resource-constrained neural video reconstruction.
  </details>

- **[MediX-R1: Open Ended Medical Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.23363)**  `arXiv:2602.23363`  `cs.CV`  
  _Sahal Shaji Mullappilly, Mohammed Irfan Kurpath, Jaseel Muhammad, Omair Mohamed, Mohamed Zidan, Fahad Khan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this work, we introduce MediX-R1, an open-ended Reinforcement Learning (RL) framework for medical vision language models (VLMs) that enables free-form answers beyond multiple-choice formats. MediX-R1 fine-tunes a baseline vision-language backbone with Group Based RL and a composite reward tailored for medical reasoning: an LLM-based accuracy reward that judges semantic correctness with a strict YES/NO decision, a medical embedding-based semantic reward to capture paraphrases and terminology variants, and lightweight format and modality rewards that enforce interpretable reasoning and modality recognition. This multi-signal design provides stable, informative feedback for open-ended outputs where traditional verifiable or MCQ-only rewards fall short. To evaluate performance, we employ a unified evaluation framework for both text-only and multimodal (image+text) tasks that uses a Reference-based LLM-as-judge in place of brittle string-overlap metrics, capturing semantic correctness, clinical accuracy, and contextual alignment. Our results show that open-ended RL with comprehensive reward signals can effectively strengthen medical reasoning in multimodal models. Despite using only $\sim51$K instruction examples, MediX-R1 achieves strong performance across standard medical LLM (text-only) and VLM (image + text) benchmarks, outperforming existing open-weight medical VLMs, with large gains particularly on open-ended clinical tasks. These improvements remain consistent across multiple RL algorithms and VLM backbones and are further supported by blinded evaluations with medical experts. Our trained models, curated datasets and source code are available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[GRASP: Guided Region-Aware Sparse Prompting for Adapting MLLMs to Remote Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.17089)**  `arXiv:2601.17089`  `cs.CV`  
  _Qigan Sun, Chaoning Zhang, Jianwei Zhang, Xudong Wang, Jiehui Xie, Pengcheng Zheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In recent years, Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have made significant progress in visual question answering tasks. However, directly applying existing fine-tuning methods to remote sensing (RS) images often leads to issues such as overfitting on background noise or neglecting target details. This is primarily due to the large-scale variations, sparse target distributions, and complex regional semantic features inherent in RS images. These challenges limit the effectiveness of MLLMs in RS tasks. To address these challenges, we propose a parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) strategy called Guided Region-Aware Sparse Prompting (GRASP). GRASP introduces spatially structured soft prompts associated with spatial blocks extracted from a frozen visual token grid. Through a question-guided sparse fusion mechanism, GRASP dynamically aggregates task-specific context into a compact global prompt, enabling the model to focus on relevant regions while filtering out background noise. Extensive experiments on multiple RSVQA benchmarks show that GRASP achieves competitive performance compared to existing fine-tuning and prompt-based methods while maintaining high parameter efficiency.
  </details>

- **[Breaking the Visual Shortcuts in Multimodal Knowledge-Based Visual Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.22843)**  `arXiv:2511.22843`  `cs.CV`  
  _Dosung Lee, Sangwon Jung, Boyoung Kim, Minyoung Kim, Sungyeon Kim, Junyoung Sung, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing Multimodal Knowledge-Based Visual Question Answering (MKB-VQA) benchmarks suffer from "visual shortcuts", as the query image typically matches the primary subject entity of the target document. We demonstrate that models can exploit these shortcuts, achieving comparable results using visual cues alone. To address this, we introduce Relational Entity Text-Image kNowledge Augmented (RETINA) benchmark, automatically constructed using an LLM-driven pipeline, consisting of 120k training and 2k human-curated test set. RETINA contains queries referencing secondary subjects (i.e. related entities) and pairs them with images of these related entities, removing the visual shortcut. When evaluated on RETINA existing models show significantly degraded performance, confirming their reliance on the shortcut. Furthermore, we propose Multi-Image MultImodal Retriever (MIMIR), which enriches document embeddings by augmenting images of multiple related entities, effectively handling RETINA, unlike prior work that uses only a single image per document. Our experiments validate the limitations of existing benchmarks and demonstrate the effectiveness of RETINA and MIMIR. Our project is available at: Project Page.
  </details>

- **[Automatic Intermodal Loading Unit Identification using Computer Vision: A Scoping Review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17707)**  `arXiv:2509.17707`  `cs.CV`  
  _Emre Gülsoylu, Alhassan Abdelhalim, Derya Kara Boztas, Ole Grasse, Carlos Jahn, Simone Frintrop, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Background: The standardisation of Intermodal Loading Units (ILUs), including containers, semi-trailers, and swap bodies, has transformed global trade, yet efficient and robust identification remains an operational bottleneck in ports and terminals. Objective: To map Computer Vision (CV) methods for ILU identification, clarify terminology, summarise the evolution of proposed approaches, and highlight research gaps, future directions and their potential effects on terminal operations. Methods: Following PRISMA-ScR, we searched Google Scholar, dblp and OpenAlex for English-language studies with quantitative results and charted the included studies across methods, datasets, failure modes, and evaluation metrics. Results: We reviewed 95 studies published between 1990 and 2026. A shift from static (e.g., OCR-gates) to vehicle-mounted camera setups is observed. Only 26 studies report an end-to-end accuracy (median 94.5%, range 19-100%). Eight datasets are publicly available, yet none is commonly used as a benchmark, and six studies describe a deployed system and seven a field test. Conclusions: We propose the term ILU identification for this broader task, which includes finding the unit and its code and covers semi-trailers and swap bodies. The literature still reflects its origin at the gate: challenges typical of moving cameras receive the least attention, and no study reports separate results for semi-trailers or swap bodies. Evaluation requires public benchmarks that cover these conditions and all ILU types, with end-to-end accuracy results. Promising directions are synthetic data, unified end-to-end models, recognisers built for codes without linguistic context, and mission planning with automatic coverage path planning for vehicle-mounted cameras. Addressing these challenges could reduce human errors, turnaround times and operational costs and increase resilience against disruptions.
  </details>

- **[Rollback the World, Keep the Reflection: Rollback-Induced Reflection for Long-Horizon LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18304)**  `arXiv:2609.18304`  `cs.CL` `cs.RO`  
  _Yi Yu, Liuyi Yao, Yaliang Li, Enshu Wang, Libing Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly tackle long-horizon tasks through multi-step environment interaction, yet a single erroneous action can alter subsequent states and observations, causing errors to compound over time. Existing methods either correct the context without repairing altered environment states or restore earlier states while discarding useful experience, making it difficult to both eliminate failure conditions and avoid repeating past mistakes. We argue that reliable recovery should instead be treated as a rollback-boundary control problem that jointly determines when to intervene, where to resume, and what information should survive recovery. Based on this view, we propose Rollback-Induced Reflection (RIR), a unified recovery framework that restores execution to a selected prior state while carrying forward reusable knowledge distilled from the abandoned trajectory to guide subsequent decisions. We further characterize recovery through a unified operator over rollback depth and retained memory, providing a general view of state restoration and knowledge retention. Experiments on three long-horizon benchmarks show that RIR consistently improves average task performance across multiple LLM backbones, with structured reflection memory preserving useful experience and selective rollback enabling efficient recovery.
  </details>

- **[When Should Decision Models Compare Candidates?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20335)**  `arXiv:2611.20335`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Fangzhi Li, Quan Shi, Wenting Yang, Yueqiao Chen, Jiayu Chang, Shuting Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language-model agents frequently select among candidate actions, such as tools and web elements. Should decision models score candidates independently or allow their scores to depend on other candidates? We investigate this question by comparing three approaches: Pointwise scoring, Setwise scoring, and Candidate Attention. Across five agent-decision benchmarks, Pointwise scoring remains competitive, while Setwise scoring performs better on some tool-selection and binary-decision tasks. Candidate Attention does not consistently improve upon Pointwise scoring, despite allowing cross-candidate interaction. Using two controlled synthetic tasks, we further examine whether models learn to use candidate-set information. With targeted relational training, Setwise models learn candidate dependencies and generalize to shifted inputs, while Candidate Attention shows weaker transfer. Our results show that access to other candidates does not guarantee effective comparison, and that its benefits depend on both task requirements and training.
  </details>

- **[A Comparative Evaluation of BERT-Based and LLM-Based Models for Indic Sentence Similarity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19356)**  `arXiv:2611.19356`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Aishwarya Gunjal, Kanchan Khemnar, Pawan Poojary, Pranav Kulkarni, Raviraj Joshi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The relative effectiveness of BERT-based encoders and large language model (LLM)-based embeddings for Indic semantic textual similarity remains insufficiently characterized. We compare eight BERT-based and five LLM-based models on the 1,500-pair STS Benchmark development set translated into ten Indic languages and on the native Marathi MahaSTS test set. IndicSBERT achieves the highest Pearson correlation in all ten translated language sets, with a macro-average of 0.843, followed by BGE-M3 at 0.792. Harrier-270M is the strongest LLM-based model on translated STS-B, with a macro-average of 0.751. On MahaSTS, IndicSBERT and Bhasha-Embed-v0 achieve Pearson correlations of 0.901 and 0.900, respectively, while EmbeddingGemma leads the LLM-based models at 0.871. The highest-scoring BERT-based model thus exceeds the highest-scoring LLM-based model in both settings, although the magnitude of the difference varies across benchmarks. These findings indicate that LLM-derived embeddings do not uniformly improve Indic sentence similarity over established encoders and motivate evaluation that accounts for language coverage, task supervision, and benchmark construction.
  </details>

- **[FlashREINFORCE: Critic-Free Single-Rollout Asynchronous RL for Agentic Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18627)**  `arXiv:2611.18627`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Jian Hu, Yifan Zhang, Hao Zhang, Binfeng Xu, Shaokun Zhang, Hongqing Peng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Asynchronous reinforcement learning suits long-horizon agents with irregular rollout times, but group-relative updates require repeated rollouts of each prompt. However, group-based sampling limits prompt coverage at a fixed rollout budget and introduces group synchronization barriers. One rollout per prompt avoids these constraints but removes the variance-reducing group baseline, making updates noisier and more prone to instability on stale, variable-length trajectories. We present FlashREINFORCE, a critic-free, one-pass framework built on One-Batch REINFORCE, a Sequence Trust Region, and Sample-Mean Optimization. These components preserve prompt coverage and signed feedback while controlling policy drift and length-amplified negative updates. In our experiments, FlashREINFORCE remains stable through 6,000 updates on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-1.5B at a policy lag of approximately four updates. On Qwen2.5-Math-1.5B, it reaches 38.0 mean accuracy across five benchmarks, outperforming the reported GRPO baseline by 1.7 percentage points with half the rollouts (256k versus 512k). Python-tool experiments extend to the 30B MoE model Qwen3-30B-A3B, with stable training at policy lag eight. On Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, FlashREINFORCE maintains tool use where GRPO stops making tool calls and achieves 98.3%/96.5% seen/unseen ALFWorld success.
  </details>

- **[Write Facts the Way Models Read Them: Lifting the Two-Hop Curse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01519)**  `arXiv:2611.01519`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhiyun Shi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A language model that learns a new fact by fine-tuning can repeat it but cannot reason with it: taught that a person is a citizen of a new country, it still cannot say, without chain-of-thought, who the head of state of that person's country is. This two-hop curse is widely treated as a reasoning deficit, and the strongest knowledge editors answer it with synthesized reasoning data. We show that the reasoning is intact and the writing is wrong.
