Fine-tuning writes a new fact as an answer to be said, whereas the second hop reads the bridge ("that person's country") as an entity, from the middle layers on: patching the model's own representation of the entity into the bridge at those layers largely restores the second hop, yet after fine-tuning the bridge there is no closer to the new entity than before the edit. We therefore propose read-equivalent writing (Legible): one loss term makes the bridge phrase, at the layer where the second hop finishes reading it, match the model's own reading of the new entity, and keeps the same relation's other subjects unchanged. Phrases and targets come from the edited model itself, and no multi-hop training data is used. Across Qwen2.5, Llama-3 and Qwen3, Legible (I) composes learned facts more often than the model composes its own knowledge, on MQuAKE's two-hop-curse questions; (II) reaches 1.8-2.6x the bridge accuracy of CaKE, whose reasoning data composes no better than plain fine-tuning, and has the best average over composition, accuracy and locality against four families of writers; and (III) lifts the curse on RippleEdits, on the benchmark of Balesni et al., who named the curse, and at 14B.
  </details>

- **[3D-MoE: Towards Spatial Intelligence with Mixture-of-Experts for 3D Reasoning and Action Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.16698)**  `arXiv:2501.16698`  `cs.CL` `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Yueen Ma, Zenglin Xu, Irwin King_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spatial intelligence, encompassing 3D perception and reasoning, is the essential next frontier of AI. Scaling current 3D vision-language models (VLMs) that rely on dense Transformers for spatial tasks incurs prohibitive computational costs. In this paper, we introduce 3D-MoE, a 3D VLM leveraging an efficient mixture-of-experts architecture with a modality- and spatial-context-aware probabilistic routing scheme, stably cultivated by a novel routing curriculum. To seamlessly extend 3D-MoE to embodied AI, we integrate a diffusion-based action head, Pose-DiT, transforming 3D-MoE into a 3D vision-language-action (VLA) model. By employing a rectified flow framework, Pose-DiT generates precise 6D pose actions in a single sampling step. Extensive experiments demonstrate that 3D-MoE achieves superior performance on diverse 3D vision-language benchmarks with drastically fewer activated parameters and yields higher success rates while enabling real-time inference for robot manipulation tasks. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Nemotron 3.5 Bolt E4B 4GB: An Open and Efficient Small Language Model for Fast Conversational Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20771)**  `arXiv:2611.20771`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _NVIDIA, Adi Margolin, Aditya Malte, Aishwarya Chhabra, Alex Hodson, Ameya Sunil Mahabaleshwarkar, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce Nemotron 3.5 Bolt E4B 4GB, an open and efficient small language model further post-trained from Gemma-4-E4B-it and optimized for speed and intelligence per GB of memory used for fast conversational agents. The model carries forward Gemma's strong conversational capabilities and further improves multi-turn tool use, instruction following, and input grounding for fast, local voice/device assistants, interactive personas, and embedded/Edge interfaces. The model combines natural conversation with multi-turn agentic capabilities to power voice/device assistants, interactive characters and robotic/IoT interfaces running on GeForce RTX, Jetson, and RTX/DGX Spark. Experiments on 21 benchmarks show that Nemotron 3.5 Bolt E4B 4GB achieves the highest average accuracy while generating the fewest output tokens in both reasoning and non-reasoning modes, compared with Gemma-4-E4B-it and Qwen3.5-4B. The model is released with a DFlash drafter. With DFlash, Bolt E4B achieves $2.74\times$ the per-user decode speed of inference without speculative decoding at concurrency~1, and $1.21\times$ the generation throughput of Gemma-4-E4B-it with MTP at batch size 1.
  </details>

- **[Safety-Preserving Medical LLM Fine-Tuning via Bilevel Data Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20643)**  `arXiv:2611.20643`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Ruoxuan Shi, Sheng Yang, Zhengxing Su, Xiaoyang Hou, Yating Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As general large language models (LLMs) are increasingly adapted for patient-facing medical applications, improving medical capability while controlling patient-facing risks remains a critical challenge. Large-scale medical instruction data are essential for acquiring domain knowledge through supervised fine-tuning (SFT), but unsafe responses can undermine existing safety alignment. To address this problem, we propose Scalable Bilevel Data Selection (S-BDS) for safety-preserving medical SFT. By controlling selection-weight concentration, S-BDS enables reliable sample ranking over large candidate pools and identifies training examples that improve medical capability while controlling patient-facing risks. We combine clinician-validated safe data and selected data with unscreened medical instruction data for SFT, and jointly evaluate medical capability and patient-facing safety on HealthBench and PatientSafetyBench, respectively. Experiments across Llama and Qwen models show that S-BDS achieves the highest safe rates among all compared methods, outperforming the strongest baselines by 4.64 and 3.00 percentage points, respectively, while maintaining competitive medical capability. Further experiments show that varying the safety-oriented weight during SFT controls the trade-off between medical capability and patient-facing safety.
  </details>

- **[EfficientSkill: Learning Skills for Token-Efficient Multi-Turn Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19823)**  `arXiv:2611.19823`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Deep Shah, Sanket Badhe, Nehal Kathrotia, Priyanka Tiwari_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reflective skill optimizers improve multi-turn LLM agents by revising instructions from failed and successful rollouts, yet accumulating edge-case rules bloats prompts, inflates token use, and pushes agents into long, error-prone loops. We introduce EfficientSkill, a two-step framework that first discovers a reliable skill without token penalties, and then warm-starts accuracy-guarded pruning from that skill to compress instructions, bound agent turns, and offload reusable code into workspace utilities with zero implementation prompt cost. Across four frontier, mid-tier, and open-weight models on MATH-500, ALFWorld, and SpreadsheetBench, EfficientSkill cuts output tokens over unconstrained EvoSkill on 11 of 12 configurations (by up to 80%) while raising mean accuracy on every benchmark, including +14.3 and +17.5 percentage-point gains on SpreadsheetBench with Gemini 3.8 Flash and Grok 4.1 Fast by replacing stalled loops with compact workflows. By shrinking both generated tokens and replayed history, EfficientSkill lowers total token usage and serving cost across every evaluated configuration, with or without full-prefix KV caching.
  </details>

- **[SG-LegalClarify: A Large-Scale Benchmark for Legal Reasoning and Ambiguity Recognition in Singapore Law](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19643)**  `arXiv:2611.19643`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Ziwei Gu, Michael S. Lee, Drew Rein, Yash Maurya, Vikneswaran Kumaran, Darshini Ramiah, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Singapore offers a distinctive testbed for legal reasoning by large language models (LLMs). Its legal system combines inherited English common law with jurisdiction-specific statutes, requiring models to distinguish local doctrine from generic common-law intuition. This setting also highlights the challenge of recognizing underspecification in lay legal queries, because the facts material to a legal outcome depend on the governing doctrine. Yet legal benchmarks rarely cover Singapore law or test this form of ambiguity recognition. To address these gaps, we introduce SG-LegalClarify, a large-scale benchmark for open-ended legal reasoning and ambiguity recognition in Singapore law. SG-LegalClarify contains 1,100 lawyer-authored source questions spanning 10 legal domains, including 340 fully specified and 760 underspecified questions. Each underspecified question has a fixed hidden-fact assignment and a fully specified counterpart, yielding 1,860 evaluation instances. Responses are graded with lawyer-authored Issue, Rule, Application, and Conclusion (IRAC) rubrics. We evaluate 19 LLMs, closed-book and with web search, using a multi-turn pipeline in which models decide whether to answer or clarify, and a user simulator reveals hidden facts only when asked. We find the benchmark far from saturated: the best models satisfy only 69.8% of mandatory rubric items closed-book (average 53.8%) and 77.8% with web search (average 63.0%). Models also struggle to recognize when clarification is necessary. We will release the public split of our benchmark, the model outputs on it, and the evaluation pipeline to support future research.
  </details>

- **[Recursive Semantic Divergence for LLM Agent Consistency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33012)**  `arXiv:2610.33012`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Harshavardhan Abichandani, Penny Chong, Atin Ghosh, Daniel Dahlmeier_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Model (LLM) agents with tool use can behave inconsistently across independent runs from identical states. They may call different tools, produce contradictory outputs, or follow divergent strategies. This variability compounds over time and undermines deployment reliability. Existing evaluation metrics primarily measure task correctness via completion-based scores and do not capture consistency in agent behavior across executions. Separately, agent behavior is analyzed either at the token level using probability distributions or at the single-response level using semantic clustering, but neither captures how inconsistency propagates across future turns of a trajectory. We introduce Recursive Semantic Divergence (RSD), a trajectory-level consistency metric adapted from bisimulation metrics in reinforcement learning. RSD measures the expected cumulative semantic disagreement between two independent rollouts of the same agent from the same state, using natural language inference to detect logical inconsistency. It is defined recursively over future states and approximated by a neural network trained on independent rollouts. The learned metric estimates future divergence from the current state, providing a consistency score at each turn without requiring the full trajectory to complete. When used as a reward signal for fine-tuning, RSD reduces AUC coefficient of variation (CV) by up to 30.9% on tau2-bench and 50.5% on ToolSandbox. Across Qwen-2.5 and Qwen-3.5 models, RSD reduces AUC CV in seven out of eight model-benchmark settings, while improving the final task progress in all eight settings.
  </details>

- **[ReVision: Selective Intrinsic Reasoning for Tool-Augmented, Reasoning-Intensive VQA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18637)**  `arXiv:2611.18637`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Ningxin Pan, Guochen Yan, Jialong Wu, Wentao Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  External tools substantially expand what multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can retrieve and compute on reasoning-intensive visual questions, and aggregate accuracy typically improves when tools are available. Our empirical study across six VQA benchmarks shows that aggregate accuracy gains under tool access can coexist with answer reversals: questions answered correctly without tools but incorrectly with tools. Trajectory analysis reveals substantially shorter reasoning turns under tool access, suggesting a shift from intrinsic visual reasoning toward reliance on external outputs. We propose ReVision, a training-free test-time method that registers intrinsic reconsideration as an optional action alongside search and code execution. When invoked, a separate tool-free multimodal call re-examines the original image, integrates accumulated evidence, and returns a revised analysis. The MLLM chooses if and when to use it, without fixed reflection stages or paired rollouts. ReVision improves over standard tool use on all six benchmarks (1.28--7.51\%) and outperforms Dual-Rollout Combine on five, with reasoning-token use comparable to standard tool use.
  </details>

- **[Isolating Perception for Multi-View Planning under Occlusion-Induced Partial Observability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19179)**  `arXiv:2611.19179`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Kevin Nguyen, Ceyhun Efe Kayan, Feng Liu, Li Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) are beginning to saturate visual planning benchmarks, yet they fail to resolve depth ambiguity and degrade sharply with problem size under occlusion-induced partial observability (PO). We introduce BW-PO based on the Blocksworld domain, a controlled benchmark that reduces existing VLM planners and formalizers to near-zero performance, and propose VLM-as-perceiver, a paradigm that narrows the VLM's role to local perception while delegating cross-view evidence reconciliation and plan generation to external solvers. Across model-specific challenging subsets of BW-PO, this raises plan success from 1% to 97% for Gemma-4-31B, 0% to 95% for Qwen3.5-122B-A10B, and 3% to 84% for Gemini-3.1-Pro. Isolating perception in this way separates two bottlenecks that are otherwise entangled: reconciliation under depth ambiguity, and completeness of visual extraction under scene density.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking Large Language Models for Linguistic Annotation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21291)**  `arXiv:2611.21291`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Ondřej Tichý, Anna Marklová, Barbora Bulantová, Magdalena Titlbachová, Jiří Milička_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Assessing large language models for linguistic annotation requires task-specific evaluation under documented conditions. We present dhAIBench, a Python benchmarking and annotation tool with a web interface for exploring and sharing results. It records prompts, configurations and predictions, enabling comparisons of annotation quality, confidence calibration, repeatability and estimated cost. We demonstrate the framework and the associated methodology through 293 runs of 48 models on nine linguistic annotation tasks spanning morphology, syntax, semantics, discourse-pragmatics, spelling normalization and learner-language correction. Models achieve high agreement with reference annotations on several tasks, including those on non-standardized, historical varieties as well as requiring contextual interpretation. The results support evaluating models against specific linguistic tasks, rather than relying on general rankings. To this end, we make the current and future benchmarks publicly available atthis http URL.
  </details>

- **[A Generative AI-Driven Architecture for Whole-Home Intelligent Services: Edge Models, Memory Management, and Household Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21232)**  `arXiv:2611.21232`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Mo Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative artificial intelligence is entering the home. Cloud-based assistants face governance limitations in data transmission, continuous availability, and the reliability of physical actions, which are especially salient in households where devices remain online, data accumulate over time, and commands trigger real-world actions. Using the home local server as a unifying organizing axis, this article develops a design-oriented review framework. Sources from arXiv, CNKI, ACL Anthology, and official documentation were searched through November 2026 and coded into five evidence categories: reviews, system prototypes, benchmarks, HCI and eldercare studies, and official documentation. System designs were mapped onto shared architectural modules, identifying nine recurrent responsibilities of the home local server. Generative models support perceptual interpretation, intent understanding, and candidate planning, while deterministic validation, knowledge-graph constraints, and authorization leases confine candidate actions to an inspectable space. Home control, emotional companionship, and intelligent guarding can be compared within a common framework: they share state and memory but differ in latency, false positive and negative rates, proactivity, and permissions. Existing benchmarks rely on virtual environments and cannot substitute for real-deployment evaluation. Local deployment is not inherently safer, and technical continuity alone does not establish the social or medical effects of companionship and guarding. The article proposes a five-dimensional evaluation framework covering architectural capability, system resources, privacy, safety and reliability, and human experience, together with six design principles and a future research agenda for local-first intelligent home systems.
  </details>

- **[EDITORS Know Your Style! Editing LoRA Subspaces for Stylistic Attribution and Imitation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20846)**  `arXiv:2611.20846`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Zixuan Wang, Gregory Kang Ruey Lau, Xiao Tian, Jue Fan, Caroline Chaux, Bryan Kian Hsiang Low_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Disentangling writing style from semantic content is a fundamental challenge in literary text modeling. Style-content entanglement causes models to rely on semantic content for authorship attribution (AA) tasks and memorize author-specific content for style imitation (SI) tasks. We propose EDITORS (EditingLoRA Subspaces), a diagnose-then-deflate framework that adapts pretrained large language models and prioritizes stylistic features, making it effective for both AA and SI tasks. EDITORS first trains a diagnostic LoRA adapter using style-neutral statements derived from the training corpus to identify a content-oriented subspace. It then trains the final style adapter under activation-space deflation that projects input activations away from the identified content-oriented directions. We empirically demonstrate that EDITORS is effective. On three AA benchmarks spanning literature, social media, and news, EDITORS achieves state-of-the-art accuracy, outperforming the strongest existing baseline by up to 29%. On SI, EDITORS generates stylistically faithful and diverse passages, outperforming baselines in achieving style similarity while adhering to content specifications.
  </details>

- **[MemEmo: Evaluating Emotion in Memory Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20733)**  `arXiv:2611.20733`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Peng Liu, Zhen Tao, Jihao Zhao, Ding Chen, Yansong Zhang, Cuiping Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Memory systems address the challenge of context loss in Large Language Models during prolonged interactions. However, as an essential tool for warm companionship and personalized services, the effectiveness of memory systems in processing affective information remains underexamined. To bridge this gap, we propose an emotion-enhanced memory evaluation benchmark to assess the performance of mainstream and state-of-the-art memory systems in integrating and harnessing emotional information. First, we design a benchmark called \textbf{MemEmo} to assess the affective reasoning and tracking capabilities of memory systems across four dimensions: emotional information extraction, emotional memory updating, emotional memory question-answering, and emotional conflict detection. Second, we construct the \textbf{H}uman-\textbf{L}ike \textbf{M}emory \textbf{E}motion (\textbf{HLME}) dataset to provide an empirical foundation for scrutinizing memory systems. Finally, We evaluate five representative memory systems on the HLME dataset, reporting 11 performance metrics across four dimensions. Experimental results reveal that aggregating scores for all tested memory systems is up to 45\%, far below 100\% scale, exposing a profound disparity between memory system and affective intelligence.
  </details>

- **[IndustrialKnowEval: A Benchmark for Evaluating Multi-Level Industrial Knowledge in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20695)**  `arXiv:2611.20695`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Haiteng Wang, Haoze Li, Jingwei Guo, Jingbo Zhou, Si Liu, Lei Ren_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly explored for industrial tasks, yet few benchmarks evaluate their mastery of industrial knowledge across different types of cognitive demand. We introduce IndustrialKnowEval (IndKnow), a benchmark for industrial knowledge and engineering question answering. It contains 43,282 samples covering 21 task types across seven industrial sectors, including aerospace, electric power, and metallurgy, organized into four levels of cognitive demand: memory, comprehension, reasoning, and engineering application. Enterprise-based domain specialists and experienced researchers participate in all stages of dataset construction and quality control. We conduct a series of experiments on 18 representative LLMs, with domain experts deeply involved in the evaluation. Building upon IndKnow, we propose the Industrial Cognitive Capability Gap (ICCG) metric to characterize the performance differential between foundational industrial knowledge and higher-order reasoning and application, and we analyze it alongside absolute performance to provide a more complete view of model capabilities. We anticipate that IndustrialKnowEval will support the benchmarking of LLMs in industrial scenarios and promote the development of robust industrial intelligence. Code is available at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[ROUNDS: Source-Framed Diagnostic Revision after Multi-Turn Clinical Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19636)**  `arXiv:2611.19636`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jinrui Fang, Runhan Chen, Yining Wu, Jiawei Xu, Xu Yang, Jian Yu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) achieve high scores on medical benchmarks, but does this performance translate into reliable diagnosis as a conversation unfolds? In diagnostic conversations, users can provide clinical findings across multiple turns, ask for a diagnosis without supplying answer options, and introduce opinions from others. Models must therefore form diagnoses from the available evidence and decide how to respond to subsequent advice. We introduce ROUNDS, a framework that evaluates these abilities after a fixed sequence of clinical evidence delivered over multiple turns. ROUNDS compares diagnosis with and without answer options, presents the same incorrect diagnosis under clinician and lay framing, and varies support for competing diagnoses in written five-clinician panels. Across seven models and 1,035 cases from five datasets, average accuracy was 76.9% with answer options and 57.0% without them. Clinician-framed misleading advice produced lower final accuracy than lay-framed advice in every model, with differences of 4.6 to 38.2 percentage points. Initially correct diagnoses more often became incorrect answers than abstentions, and many adopted the suggested disease. Conversely, accuracy increased as more panel members supported the correct diagnosis, including when the majority opinion remained unchanged. These findings reveal a gap between medical benchmark performance and reliable diagnosis in conversation: models respond to external opinions, but remain vulnerable to abandoning correct diagnoses without new clinical evidence.
  </details>

- **[KUDEX: A Zero-Shot Decompositional Framework for Logical Analysis in Natural Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18851)**  `arXiv:2611.18851`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Zixu Zhao, Tianhang Zhang, Tong He, Zheng Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While Large Language Models (LLMs) excel at generating fluent text, they often struggle with multi-step logical reasoning with inferential connections among statements. We introduce KUDEX (Knowledge-Upward-Downward Reasoning Extension), a novel zero-shot, decompositional framework for analyzing and verifying logical relationships in natural language. Unlike traditional approaches that rely on surface-level entailment checks, KUDEX leverages LLMs to decompose reasoning into atomic primitives and constructs transparent reasoning graphs that serve both as execution traces and audit logs. Our framework includes a general prompt adaptation pipeline that adapts to diverse reasoning tasks without additional fine-tuning or domain-specific pipelines. Through comprehensive experiments on benchmarks across four domains, we demonstrate that KUDEX provides superior performance compared to existing methods, offering a more granular and robust framework for assessing logical relationships between statements.
  </details>

- **[Skill Transfer: Adapting Agent Skills from Strong to Weak Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18624)**  `arXiv:2611.18624`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yifan Lan, Hanyu Wang, Lu Lin, Jinghui Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent skills are modular bundles of instructions, code, and resources that an agent loads on demand, increasingly authored and validated on frontier models and shared as an open standard. The same skill behaves differently across models: a strong model turns it into a large performance gain, while a weak model realizes little of that gain and sometimes does worse than with no skill at all. Cost and resource constraints, however, often restrict real deployments to exactly these weaker agents. We hypothesize that the strong model silently supplies procedural steps the skill leaves implicit, which the weak model omits, and formalize closing this gap as \emph{Skill Transfer}: holding the skill's capability ceiling and the model's parameters fixed, we adapt the skill so a specific weak agent can execute it well. Our method abstracts strong and weak trajectories into typed execution structures, localizes their structural divergence with a graph edit distance (GED), and repairs the skill through an iterative diagnoser-patcher loop. Across three benchmarks and two model families, Skill Transfer raises pass rate over the unmodified skill significantly and well over other skill-evolution baselines. The adapted skill also improves the strong agent it was never adapted for, and transfers out of distribution to a structurally related, unseen task. Code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Evaluating the Robustness of Fact-Checking LLMs on Multilingual Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18623)**  `arXiv:2611.18623`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Radhika Shah_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated fact-checking benchmarks typically pair claims with clean, curated evidence in high-resource languages, but real-world evidence is often incomplete, misleading, or written in a language where models are weaker. We evaluate the robustness of fact-checking LLMs for different languages. Building on EuroVerdict, we construct gold evidence sets for 190 claims in each of English, German, Greek, and Romanian, then apply four corruption strategies at two severity levels. We test LLMs on a set of claims, providing them with evidence from the sets above. A rubric-based LLM-as-a-judge scores reasoning quality. Verdict accuracy proves highly sensitive to both factors: Misleading edit cut Llama 3.1 8B's English accuracy from 80.00% to 16.84%, and losses widen as language resource level decreases. Machine-translating Greek and Romanian claims into English recovers much, but not all, of the low-resource gap. Judge scores decline more slowly than accuracy, suggesting that automated reasoning evaluation underestimates degradation. These results caution against deploying LLM fact-checkers on noisy multilingual evidence, and we release our evidence datasets and evaluation code to support future work.
  </details>

- **[When Helpful Agents Cause Financial Harm: Evaluating LLM Agent Safety in Executable Economic Worlds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18612)**  `arXiv:2611.18612`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yuzhu Ling, Jiale Han, Benyou Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As LLM agents become increasingly capable of making consequential economic decisions, their safety under economic pressures remains poorly understood. We introduce EconSafeBench, the first benchmark for systematically evaluating the safety of LLM agents in executable economic environments. EconSafeBench contains 828 cases across five economic scenarios and four categories of safety violations. A key design is the matched evaluation of three sources of safety pressure, including explicit user requests, pressure from other actors, and pressure arising from the agent's own pursuit of a performance objective. We further evaluate safety at both static and dynamic scales through 30-period interactions with persistent memory and matched controls. Across eight LLMs, all evaluated models exhibit safety violations on the static benchmark, with overall violation rates ranging from 8.5% to 40.6%. We further find that violations can occur even without an explicit unsafe request, and that initially compliant agents may begin to violate as performance pressure increases over time. These findings highlight the need for stronger safety alignment and rigorous evaluation before LLM agents are deployed in high-stakes economic environments.
  </details>

- **[CardioLM: A multilingual suite of small language models for the cardiology domain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07940)**  `arXiv:2611.07940`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Manuela-Daniela Danu, Miguel Rodríguez-Ortega, Alberto Becerra-Tome, Pablo Arancibia, Fernando Gallego-Donoso, Lorenzo Pratesi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Background: Cardiovascular care generates large volumes of clinical free-text documentation in local languages. However, clinical natural language processing (NLP) resources remain concentrated in English, and no existing benchmark is cardiology-specific, multilingual, and broad in entity coverage.Methods: We present two contributions. The first is CardioCCC, a corpus of 508 cardiology case reports originally published in Spanish and extended to Czech, Dutch, English, Italian, Romanian, and Swedish through machine translation, annotation projection, and clinician review, with annotations covering diseases, symptoms, procedures, and medications. The second is CardioLM, comprising CardioBERTa, a set of language-specific encoders obtained by continued pretraining on cardiology and biomedical corpora, and CardioNER, a set of named entity recognition (NER) models fine-tuned on CardioCCC.Results: Cardiology-specific pretraining reduced pseudo-perplexity by up to a factor of eight, yet changed strict span-level F1 by at most 0.04, with benefits concentrated in languages whose backbones lacked biomedical coverage. The jointly trained multilingual XLM-RoBERTa-large models achieved the best strict F1 in nearly every language and entity type, exceeding the strongest monolingual system by up to 0.13 F1. A large share of residual errors concerned boundary detection: relaxed matching recovered 0.10-0.23 F1 for disease, procedure, and symptom, but only 0.01-0.07 for medication. Clinicians rated CardioNER well above a rule- and dictionary-based baseline, with the mean rating across entity types rising from 26% to 73%.Conclusions: Specialty-specific pretraining is not the decisive factor for downstream entity recognition, whereas multilingual joint training yields the strongest results in nearly all settings and a simpler deployment path for multi-country clinical information extraction.
  </details>

- **[Form Over Content In Gradient-Based Data Attribution Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19589)**  `arXiv:2609.19589`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Sunwoo Kim, Seokwon Jung, Sohyung Kim, Seong Joon Oh, Alice Oh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Data attribution methods using gradient similarity are widely used to analyze and select training data for large language models, but what gradient similarity actually measures is debated. Some interpret it as identifying task-relevant skills, while other work reports that surface form is the main factor. We resolve this debate for supervised fine-tuning examples by varying task and answer format independently. Specifically, we render benchmarks in different answer formats, such that datasets can share a task without a format or a format without a task. We find that gradient alignment follows the answer format, as benchmark pairs sharing an answer format align strongly (disattenuated cosine near 0.4), while same benchmarks rendered with different answer format classes show no alignment (near 0.0). We demonstrate that this ordering holds from the earliest pretraining checkpoints through post-training, and across model scales and families. We then analyze the released selections of LESS, a gradient-based data selection method for instruction tuning, and find that each target's selections over-represent the target's own answer format. Hence, we demonstrate that gradient-based attribution methods track format similarity more than task semantics, meaning that such methods, as well as the semantic interpretation of the gradient, should be tested on data where answer format and task vary independently for greater robustness and reliability.
  </details>

- **[Pass the Baton: Trajectory-Relayed On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26057)**  `arXiv:2607.26057`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Haolei Xu, Xiaowen Xu, Haiwen Hong, Zixuan Ni, Hongxing Li, Yiwen Qiu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation (OPD) grounds token-level supervision in the student's own trajectory, yet suffers from prefix failure: once the student commits to a wrong reasoning direction, all subsequent generation builds on this deviation, producing misdirected continuations that elicit unreliable supervision and waste compute. We identify a teacher-student continuation asymmetry on failed prefixes, where the teacher tends to redirect while the student continues along the original direction, and convert it into a label-free handoff trigger in Relay On-Policy Distillation (Relay-OPD). During training, Relay-OPD constructs relay trajectories by letting the teacher briefly take over at detected trigger points to produce a teacher leg, after which the student resumes and is optimized on the resulting trajectory. A limited relay budget concentrates intervention on critical early positions while limiting departure from the student policy. With a Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507 teacher and Qwen3-0.6B/1.7B-Non-Thinking students on eight mathematical reasoning benchmarks, Relay-OPD achieves the best or second-best results on every benchmark, outperforming standard OPD by +5.73% and the strongest baseline FastOPD by +1.49% on average for 1.7B, with consistent gains at 0.6B. Training trajectory length is reduced by over 50%.
  </details>

- **[CRADLE-Dialogue: Expert-Level Crisis Detection in Mental Health Conversations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.10380)**  `arXiv:2606.10380`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Grace Byun, Abigail Lott, Rebecca Lipschutz, Sean T. Minton, Elizabeth A. Stinson, Jinho D. Choi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world crisis intervention is inherently conversational, yet existing research largely focuses on static texts. When applied to multi-turn dialogues, current models exhibit significant performance degradation, struggling to track risk signals that emerge as context evolves. To address this gap, we introduce CRADLE-Dialogue, a clinician-annotated benchmark for turn-level crisis detection in conversational settings. The dataset features 600 dialogues with multi-label annotations across clinically grounded risks, including suicide ideation, self-harm, and child abuse, distinguishing past from ongoing risk. We further propose an Alert-Confirm evaluation protocol that distinguishes early warning signals (Alert) from turns where a specific crisis becomes explicitly identifiable (Confirm), reflecting the clinical need to intervene before risk becomes explicit. Experiments show that identifying when risk emerges is much harder than recognizing that it exists: models achieve only mid-40% to high-60% Micro F1. Additionally, we release a synthetic training corpus and a 32B-parameter model that substantially outperforms existing open-source models and achieves competitive or superior results against proprietary models across turn-level, dialogue-level, and confirm-only evaluation settings. We further validate our model on real patient interviews, demonstrating its ability to generalize beyond synthetic conversations.
  </details>

- **[BioELX: Context-Aware Cross-lingual Biomedical Entity Linking without Task-Specific Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27380)**  `arXiv:2605.27380`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yi Wang, Corina Dima, Liangyu Zhong, Steffen Staab_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cross-lingual biomedical entity linking (BEL) maps mentions in any language to unique identifiers in a biomedical knowledge base, supporting clinical and biomedical NLP applications. We identify two issues affecting current systems. First, the UMLS (Bodenreider,2004) aliases used to train cross-lingual BEL retrievers are heavily skewed toward English, so retrievers generalize poorly to non-English mentions. Second, although context is often necessary for disambiguation, naively injecting context into retrievers trained only to align aliases severely degrades retrieval. We propose BioELX, a retrieve-rerank framework that addresses both issues. For retrieval, we continue training SapBERT_multi (Liu et al., 2021b) using Wikidata-derived cross-lingual alias supervision, forming shared concept neighborhoods across languages. For reranking, we adapt pretrained LLM rerankers to entity linking through mention-anchored prompting, which marks the target mention so that rerankers score candidates with respect to the intended mention rather than other salient tokens in the context. Experiments show that BioELX achieves new state-of-the-art results on four cross-lingual BEL benchmarks, improving Recall@1 by 4.8 to 18.2 percentage points over prior best results, without any task-specific BEL annotations. Our code and resources are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[JuICE: A Benchmark for Evaluating LLM-Judge in Identifying Cultural Errors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26955)**  `arXiv:2605.26955`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiho Jin, Junho Myung, Juhyun Oh, Junyeong Park, Rifki Afina Putri, Sunipa Dev, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed to users around the world, they are integrated into everyday tasks across diverse cultural contexts, from drafting personal communications to brainstorming creative ideas. These tasks are inherently cultural: they require contextual appropriateness, symbolic resonance, and tacit cultural expectations that native speakers draw on instinctively, meaning that a response can be factually plausible yet unmistakably wrong to a local reader. Existing cultural benchmarks have treated culture as a flat set of facts via fact verification or norm entailment methods, and have adopted LLM-as-a-Judge without examining whether they can capture such thick cultural errors. To address this gap, we present JuICE (Benchmark for LLM-Judge in Identifying Cultural Errors), a multilingual dataset of 7,470 span-level annotations of cultural and linguistic errors in long-form LLM responses. It covers 1,050 query-response pairs from four countries (the United States, South Korea, Indonesia, and Bangladesh), in both English and their countries' main languages. Using JuICE, we find that even the strongest LLM-judge achieves only an F1 of 0.51 in the erroneous span detection task. Furthermore, LLM-judges consistently miss thick cultural errors that local residents readily identify. Our findings suggest that robust cultural evaluation must move beyond surface-level detection toward frameworks that account for the depth and situatedness of cultural meaning.
  </details>

- **[Verbal tics in frontier language models: A critical review of current releases, research evidence, and public discussion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.19139)**  `arXiv:2604.19139`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Shuai Wu, Xue Li, Zhijun Wang, Bolun Liu, Weilin Cai, Zihao Su, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Repeated praise, canned reassurance, formulaic contrasts, and conspicuous vocabulary figure prominently in discussions of large language models. Context governs how these habits should be read. A conventional phrase can orient a reader, whereas a fluent answer can reinforce a false belief. This critical review examines linguistic habits and sycophancy across eight developer families. We verify public offerings from OpenAI, Anthropic, Google DeepMind, xAI, ByteDance, Moonshot AI, DeepSeek, and Xiaomi against official release and API documentation through 1 October 2026. We then synthesize empirical research on lexical overrepresentation, stylistic variation, social warmth, and agreement alongside benchmark methods and dated public discussions in English and Chinese. Published studies document recurring linguistic patterns and agreement that distorts human judgment, but comparable measurements for the newest releases remain sparse in the retrieved material. Current user accounts report both persistent complaints and clearer prose, and experiences vary by task and prompt design. We propose separate measures for recurrence, contextual appropriateness, and belief distortion paired with exact service records and language-specific annotation. This framework keeps claims about writing quality and conversational reliability testable as model services change.
  </details>

- **[Feedback Forensics: A Toolkit to Measure AI Personality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.26305)**  `arXiv:2509.26305`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Arduin Findeis, Timo Kaufmann, Eyke Hüllermeier, Robert Mullins_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Some traits making a "good" AI model are hard to describe upfront. For example, should responses be more polite or more casual? Such traits are sometimes summarized as model character or personality. Without a clear objective, conventional benchmarks based on automatic checks struggle to measure such traits. Evaluation methods using human feedback such as Chatbot Arena have emerged as a popular alternative. These methods infer "better" personality and other desirable traits implicitly by ranking multiple model responses relative to each other. Recent issues with model releases highlight limitations of these existing opaque evaluation approaches: goblin-obsessed, sycophantic and generally over-the-top personalities have resulted in model rollbacks and extensive media coverage. Despite these known issues, limited public tooling exists to explicitly evaluate model personality. We introduce Feedback Forensics: an open-source toolkit to track AI personality and related behavioural traits, both those encouraged by human (or AI) feedback, and those exhibited across AI models trained and evaluated on such feedback. Feedback Forensics is adaptive and can automatically find new traits appearing in models. We demonstrate the toolkit's usefulness in two steps: (A) first we analyse the personality traits encouraged in popular human feedback datasets including MultiPref, PRISM and data from Chatbot Arena; and (B) then use our toolkit to analyse how much popular models exhibit such traits. We release (1) our Feedback Forensics toolkit alongside (2) a web app tracking AI personality in popular models and feedback datasets and (3) the underlying annotation data, seethis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Breaking the Silence: A Dataset and Benchmark for Bangla Text-to-Gloss Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02293)**  `arXiv:2504.02293`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Sharif Mohammad Abdullah, Abhijit Paul, Shubhashis Roy Dipta, Zarif Masud, Shebuti Rayana, Ahmedul Kabir_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Gloss is a written approximation that bridges Sign Language (SL) and its corresponding spoken language. Despite a deaf and hard-of-hearing population of at least 3 million in Bangladesh, Bangla Sign Language (BdSL) remains largely understudied, with very limited prior work on Bangla text-to-gloss translation and no publicly available parallel dataset at the time of this work. To address this gap, we construct the first publicly available Bangla text-to-gloss dataset, consisting of 1,000 manually annotated and 4,000 synthetically generated Bangla sentence-gloss pairs, along with 159 expert human-annotated pairs used as a test set. Our experimental framework performs a comparative analysis between several fine-tuned open-source models and a leading closed-source LLM to evaluate their performance in low-resource BdSL translation. GPT-5.4 achieves the best automatic metric scores, while open-source models perform competitively despite being much smaller. This work introduces the first publicly available expert-annotated Bangla text-to-gloss dataset, benchmark and trained model for Bangla text-to-gloss translation. It also demonstrates the effectiveness of systematically generated synthetic data for addressing challenges in low-resource sign language translation.
  </details>

- **[Jetriever: Shifting Agent Memory Retrieval from Similarity to Utility](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21478)**  `arXiv:2611.21478`  `cs.CL`  
  _Ruizhe Li, Licheng Zhang, Mingxuan Du, Zhendong Mao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A useful memory need not resemble the question it helps answer. Jetriever ranks each stored memory by Jev's decision score for its usefulness to the current question, replacing similarity search with full-pool utility scoring. On InMind's 125 indirect-query tasks, it places the gold exchange first in 84.8% of cases; matched BM25 and MiniLM controls reach 1.6% and 0.0% when indexing user turns. A 20-question comparison separates finding evidence from ranking it: Jev and Qwen3.5 9B each find 90% of gold exchanges by rank 50, while Laya finds 30%; at rank one, Jev reaches 85% and Qwen3.5 9B 40%. On a dimension-proportional random sample of 60 MemoryAgentBench questions, Jetriever's four-dimension score is 55.4%, against 44.3% for ReFind on the same questions. Jev retrieval costs USD 0.00834 per InMind question and USD 0.03103 per MAB question on average. These results show the value of world knowledge, scores that rank decisive memories first, and affordable full-pool scoring.
  </details>

- **[Latent Bridge: Asking for Help Without Words](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21301)**  `arXiv:2611.21301`  `cs.CL`  
  _Qichao Wang, Tingyu Qu, Pengcheng Wu, Yiran Zhong, Feida Zhu, Zhiqi Shen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-based model collaboration requires a large model to generate intermediate responses before a smaller model can use its guidance, adding large-model decoding cost to inference. We introduce POND (Prefill-Only, No-Decode), a method that uses a frozen large sender for prefill and a small receiver for all autoregressive decoding. A trainable bridge maps the sender's hidden states to additional attention keys and values, allowing the receiver to use these representations throughout generation. POND also supports periodic review, in which the sender processes the receiver's partial response and updates its guidance through additional prefill passes while preserving the receiver's existing KV cache. Across reasoning and coding benchmarks, POND improves receiver performance with senders from different model families. In particular, GLM-5.3 provides gains comparable to those of Qwen3.8-Max when paired with a Qwen receiver, supporting the effectiveness of representation transfer between independently trained model families. POND further demonstrates its ability to generalize across domains. When trained on math and coding tasks, it transfers directly to general knowledge tasks without additional domain-specific training. These results demonstrate that large frontier model prefill representations can improve small-model generation without requiring large-model autoregressive decoding.
  </details>

- **[LATTE-WordSeg-Lite: Multi-Granularity Thai Word Segmentation on a CPU, with a Reproducible Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21300)**  `arXiv:2611.21300`  `cs.CL`  
  _Thodsaporn Chay-intr, Kobkrit Viriyayudhakorn, Thanaruk Theeramunkong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models read Thai through subword tokens, without a word segmenter. Yet Thai treebanks, speech metrics and morphological resources are built on words, often at several granularities, and word boundaries resolve ambiguities that subword tokens leave open. LATTE (Lattice ATTentive Encoding) reads a text at several granularities at once, through a lattice of dictionary words, but it is a large GPU model. We distil it into LATTE-WordSeg-Lite, a CPU segmenter of 1.17M parameters that keeps the lattice as features. It outputs compounds, words and morphemes in one pass, and learns only from annotated data and text whose licences allow commercial use. To compare it with other tools, published Thai scores do not suffice, as they come from different files, splits and scorers. We therefore rebuild seven test sets from pinned sources and score twelve systems under one protocol. With the unit that fits each test set, LATTE-WordSeg-Lite scores above DeepCut on five sets, four of them from corpora that its teachers learnt from. Called line by line on one CPU thread, it runs 23 times as fast as DeepCut. We release the model, the benchmark scripts and all predictions.
  </details>

- **[Seeing Isn't Resolving: Visual and Linguistic Disambiguation with Conversational Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21183)**  `arXiv:2611.21183`  `cs.CL`  
  _Edoardo Cecchinato, Shun Inadumi, Angel García Contreras, Giuseppe Riccardi, Koichiro Yoshino_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual grounding benchmarks usually do not consider the contextual uncertainty of real-world scenarios. In this study, we construct a novel benchmark, the Japanese evaluation for Ambiguous visual Grounding with Dialogue (JAG-D) dataset. It consists of human-robot conversational multimodal interactions covering different types of ambiguity, arising from visual and linguistic categories. We evaluate the capabilities of different state-of-the-art Visual Language Models (VLMs) for ambiguity recognition and resolution. We compare dialogue context grounding performance for visual and linguistic ambiguity resolution. Our experiments show that VLMs can substantially improve the resolution of linguistic ambiguity, especially Japanese zero anaphora, by using dialogue context. We further conduct a comparative analysis to understand whether grounding improvements arise from the semantic content of the dialogue or from its conversational structure.
  </details>

- **[From Repositories to Training Environments: Scaling Long-Horizon Coding Agents with Environment Evolution Loop](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21152)**  `arXiv:2611.21152`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yang Tian, Tianyu Zheng, Xiyuan Yang, Yaxin Du, Chengran Yang, Bo Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training coding agents for long-horizon tasks requires environments with verifiable feedback. Existing pipelines often follow a linear process that ends with validation, establishing usability but providing little guidance on improving training value. Recursive self-improvement (RSI) often requires model rollouts or model training followed by downstream evaluation, with costs and feedback delays amplified on long-horizon tasks. How can we use timely, reliable proxy signals to improve training value and synthesize environments at scale? We present RepoFactory, an automated pipeline that converts real repositories into verifiable environments where agents build complete projects from scratch given a language specification. Its Environment Evolution Loop optimizes a multiplicative objective combining validity, structural coupling, and implementation scope through proxy feedback. RepoFactory produces 10,159 environments across 17 software domains. Training Qwen3.5-35B-A3B on these environments yields RepoHorizen-35B-A3B, which achieves gains of 22.6 percentage points on NL2Repo-Bench and 8.5 points on average across five benchmarks covering long-horizon coding and agentic tasks in real-world production settings, compared with 17.5 and 6.4 points without environment evolution. These results highlight the training value of RepoFactory environments and the additional gains from the Environment Evolution Loop.
  </details>

- **[Medical question answering collapses in Hausa: a pre-registered pilot on three small open models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20823)**  `arXiv:2611.20823`  `cs.CL`  
  _Abbas Sani_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A system that reads nothing and always answers the most frequent letter scores \BaselineP{} on our Hausa medical multiple-choice set. \NBelowBaseline{} of the \NModels{} small open models we evaluate do not beat it. We lead with that because the usual summary of a multilingual result -- a percentage-point drop from English -- does not say whether what survives the drop is performance at all.On a paired English--Hausa set of \NEval{} MedMCQA items, three instruction-tuned models of 3--4B parameters lose between \GapMedGemma{} and \GapQwen{} points of accuracy, every gap significant after Holm correction. Back-translating the Hausa items into English recovers between half and two-thirds of the gap for the two general-purpose models but not demonstrably for the medically adapted one, so the deficit is substantially, though not wholly, a matter of the language of presentation. Answers are also far less stable: agreement across three option orderings falls from \ConsEnQwen{} to \ConsHaQwen{} in the worst case. An exploratory error analysis of \ENRows{} discordant items attributes most of the damage to medical vocabulary lost in translation (\ETerm{}, 95\% CI \ETermLo{}--\ETermHi{}), usually in the question stem.A fourth registered hypothesis proposed closing the gap with self-generated preference pairs. Its pilot gate found nothing to train on: asked to reason in Hausa rather than to pick a letter, the model echoes the prompt back, switches into Swahili, or simply stops without answering; only \GateParse{} of chains are parseable. We report that intervention as not run, which is itself the sharper result -- a forced-choice benchmark cannot see a model that has no generative command of the language at all.All hypotheses, thresholds and decision rules were registered before any model was run, and all deviations are logged.
  </details>

- **[CuRSE-Bench: A Benchmark for Probing Cultural Blind Spots in Text-to-Image Safety Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20821)**  `arXiv:2611.20821`  `cs.CL`  
  _Minsuk Jang, Minseok Son, KiHyun Seol, Yeongeun Shim, Changick Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-image (T2I) safety evaluation typically assigns each prompt or image a single culture-blind harm label, so content that is harmful only within a particular culture can pass as benign.We introduce CultureCraft, an agentic framework that constructs T2I prompts that appear benign under a general-purpose rubric yet carry a latent harmful meaning in a target culture, and use it to build CuRSE-Bench: 10,709 culture-specific prompts and 2,648 culture-general controls over six regions and ten harm axes, each drawn from a predefined knowledge base of documented harm vectors and traceable to at least two independent sources.Scoring every prompt under six region persona judges and a paired culture-blind judge, we find that the culture-blind judge under-rates culture-specific latent harm while matching the persona judges on the controls.Each persona judge, in turn, chiefly decodes its own region's latent harm under our instrument: a selective cultural blind spot that persists in deployed moderators.
  </details>

- **[GPRM: Global Perspective Process Reward Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20755)**  `arXiv:2611.20755`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zifan Zhang, Zhengbo Jiao, Shaobo Wang, Jing Zhou, Guoyi Li, Jiayi Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Process Reward Models (PRMs) are widely used for step-level evaluation of multi-step reasoning in large language models, but existing methods are often limited by weak historical dependency modeling and insufficient global awareness. They typically fail to leverage prior step evaluations for coherent judgment and lack visibility into subsequent reasoning, leading to suboptimal assessment of intermediate steps. To address this issue, we propose the Global Perspective Process Reward Model (GPRM), which jointly models the problem, previous reasoning steps and their evaluations, the current step, and future steps to enable history-aware and globally informed process evaluation. We further introduce a structured evaluation framework along four dimensions---look back, look ahead, self-check, and goal alignment---to assess prior consistency, future plausibility, step validity, and task alignment. Based on this framework, we construct datasets for supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning (GRPO). Experiments show that, despite using less training data and smaller model sizes, GPRM outperforms strong baseline models by 7.8 and 5.4 points on PRMBench and ProcessBench, respectively. GPRM also generalizes well across tasks, surpassing the GPT-4.1 baseline on Agent Error Bench, improving strong baselines by around 4 points under test-time scaling (TTS), and achieving competitive performance on PPE.
  </details>

- **[ANCHOR: Cluster-Grounded 5W1H Extraction via Large-Small Model Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20728)**  `arXiv:2611.20728`  `cs.CL`  
  _Hongyu Jiang, Chenang Cai, Lei Gao, Zhao Kang, Ling Tian, Jinchuan Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep semantic understanding of events requires recognizing related events and distinguishing why an event occurs from how it is carried out. 5W1H extraction provides a structured foundation for such understanding by organizing event information around six fundamental questions. However, single-event slot filling provides limited support for these distinctions. We propose ANCHOR, a cluster-grounded framework that combines a large language model (LLM) with a small language model equipped with discriminative heads for accurate 5W1H extraction. The LLM proposes candidate events and generates their initial 4W records, which guide the selection of an event cluster and the subsequent anchoring of its main event. A shared document encoder provides evidence for joint 4W refinement, and two separate sets of boundary and role-scoring heads then extract why and how under event-specific constraints, respectively. Document representations are reused across events and stages, and unsupported roles can remain empty. Following the same principles, an agent-and-skill annotation workflow constructs a benchmark of 26,110 documents from ten source corpora covering three languages and seven domains. We select nine of the resulting datasets to evaluate ANCHOR against strong baselines using four open-weight LLMs. ANCHOR achieves competitive performance. The datasets and code are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[LEAF-OPD: Learning to Forget from Privileged Contexts via On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20674)**  `arXiv:2611.20674`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zirui Pang, Zhijie Deng, Yuancheng Wang, Qihan Yu, Xin Li, Jiaheng Wei, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM unlearning aims to erase targeted knowledge from large language models while preserving their utility. However, many existing methods suppress target knowledge without explicitly teaching the model how to respond to queries about it. We propose LEAF-OPD (LEArning to Forget from Privileged Contexts via On-Policy Distillation), in which a frozen teacher provides token-level supervision on student-generated responses. On forget data, the teacher uses privileged contexts (instructions not to reveal knowledge about forget targets) to guide forgetting. On retain data, the teacher provides supervision without privileged context to preserve model utility. The student operates without privileged context in both cases, learning how to respond when encountering knowledge that should be forgotten. A key challenge is that the teacher often provides effective supervision only at the first few tokens of student trajectories. We introduce Supervision Signal (SS) to measure corrective supervision over a specified token interval, evaluating the entire trajectory (SS1) and its second half (SS0.5) to assess overall strength and late-stage persistence. When supervision is weak, lightweight SFT strengthens the teacher's context-conditioned refusal behavior, substantially increasing both scores. We evaluate LEAF-OPD on three widely used benchmarks: TOFU, RWKU, and MUSE-News. On TOFU, LEAF-OPD achieves the best Forget Quality, Model Utility, and Forget ROUGE-L while maintaining competitive retain performance. On RWKU, it outperforms the second-best method by 34% in Forget Quality while maintaining competitive Retain Quality. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of privileged-context supervision with on-policy token-level distillation for LLM unlearning.
  </details>

- **[MetaAct: Self-Evolving Executable Action Constraints for Embodied Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20655)**  `arXiv:2611.20655`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jiaxing Zhao, Hanwen Wang, Di Liu, Shaoteng Liu, Jing-Ran Su, Yanheng Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-evolving language agents have emerged as a promising paradigm for continuously improving agent behavior through experience. However, most existing approaches evolve textual artifacts, which can influence action preferences but cannot reliably enforce action admissibility in embodied environments with discrete and often irreversible actions. We propose MetaAct, which shifts self-evolution from textual guidance to executable action constraints by synthesizing GEAR (Grounded Executable Action Rules) from execution feedback. Each rule is represented as a typed specification, compiled into a deterministic guard, and validated offline through trajectory replay without additional model calls or environment interactions. Across four benchmarks, MetaAct improves task performance; on ScienceWorld and WebShop, violations drop from 70% to 0% and 40% to 1%, with score gains of +10.1 and +8.0. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Do We Still Need Fine-Tuned Classifiers? Swapping Typed Decision Models into 24 ACL-Venue Papers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20191)**  `arXiv:2611.20191`  `cs.CL`  
  _Marek Šuppa, Jaroslav Kopčan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Typed Decision Models such as TypeSafe's Jev, also called System One or System 1 models, answer typed questions about an input with a probability for each option, and they need no training. Many NLP papers still fine-tune a task-specific classifier, and prompted large language models (LLMs) trail such classifiers on most text classification benchmarks. We ask how many of these fine-tuned classifiers a Typed Decision Model can replace under each paper's own evaluation, and what is needed where it cannot. We swap the predictors of 24 ACL-venue papers for Jev, keep each paper's splits, label definitions and metric, and check the results with Cloudflare's Clef, Microsoft-Decision-1 and OpenAI's GPT-6 Luna Decisions. The resulting 46 English test sets, with their questions and published scores, form the TDM-Gauntlet benchmark. With a zero-shot prompt, Jev directly replaced the paper's predictor on 12 of the 46 test sets, mostly single judgments with plain labels, but rarely where the paper's method was trained on its labels. Where Jev scored lower, we most often attributed the shortfall to an annotation convention that the label name does not state. With 500 training labels plus validation and no user-managed GPU, calibration and GEPA-style prompt optimization closed a median 72% of the gap to the paper's method, about twice what off-the-shelf GEPA closed, for a median $1.66 per search. On the same labels, fine-tuned encoders such as RoBERTa and DeBERTa closed 59%, and LoRA fine-tuning of the open LLM Qwen3.8-27B closed 97%. Fine-tuning remains the most accurate option, but a zero-shot prompt is often enough for plain labels, and prompt optimization closes most of the remaining gap.
  </details>

- **[A Corpus-Driven SentencePiece Unigram Tokenizer for Sindhi: Design and Benchmark Against Fourteen Existing Tokenizers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20126)**  `arXiv:2611.20126`  `cs.CL`  
  _Ali Nawaz, Muqadas Fareed, Soonh Sadaf_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sindhi is a low-resource Indo-Aryan language written mainly in an extended Perso-Arabic script. Several Sindhi tokenizers have been released on public model hubs, but they have not been compared with one another on shared data. We train SentencePiece Unigram tokenizers with byte fallback at four vocabulary sizes (32k to 150k) on about 1.10 million Sindhi sentences and a smaller, repeated Urdu, Persian and Arabic slice, from four public sources, and compare them with fourteen existing tokenizers on four evaluation sets: FLORES+, Sindhi Wikipedia, a news-derived sentiment corpus, and a contemporary news corpus. The metrics are fertility, single-token retention, UNK rate, byte-fallback rate, and round-trip character loss. At 64k, our tokenizer averages 1.147 tokens per word, against 1.208 for the best existing tokenizer and 1.512 for XLM-RoBERTa, and keeps 88.6% of words as single tokens. On fertility, single-token retention and UNK rate it is best or tied for best in all four evaluation sets at 64k and above; at 32k it trails one baseline on FLORES+. Margins over the best Sindhi-adapted baselines are small (0.002 fertility on FLORES+ at 64k), and we report no significance tests. A round-trip check finds no character loss for our tokenizer and silent character deletion (up to 0.85% of characters) in two existing ones; seven others lose less than 0.1%. We evaluate intrinsic metrics only, so effects on downstream tasks remain untested. Code and tokenizer files are public (DOI:https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23962474).
  </details>

- **[A Dataset for Misogyny Detection in Polish Social Media: Transferability of Hateful Speech Detection from General to Specific Types of Hate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20044)**  `arXiv:2611.20044`  `cs.CL`  
  _Ewelina Gajewska_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Harmful language detection is commonly evaluated on broadly defined hate speech and toxicity benchmarks, yet it remains unclear whether models that successfully detect harmful language in general can transfer this capability to more specific forms of hate. We investigate this problem for misogyny detection in Polish, an area for which dedicated resources remain scarce. We introduce a new dataset of Polish social media comments annotated for misogyny, gender-directed hateful speech. Using this resource, we first assess the reliability of LLM-assisted annotation and then evaluate general-to-specific transfer by comparing models trained on general harmful language data, safety-specialized models, and models fine-tuned directly for misogyny detection. Our results show that strong performance on general harmful language detection does not transfer uniformly to misogyny; safety-specialized models demonstrate substantially stronger generalization ability. The study contributes a new Polish resource for gender-directed harmful language and demonstrates that the scope and granularity of prior safety training are important factors in transferring harmful language detection from general to specific forms of hate.
  </details>

- **[Offline-First Quantized AI: Design and Evaluation of an On-Device Maternal-Health Assistant for Low-Connectivity Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20012)**  `arXiv:2611.20012`  `cs.CL`  
  _Okenla AbdulQahhar Abimbola, Maurice Abraham John, Adepoju Jeremiah Moyondafoluwa, Omolaiye Samuel, Oko-Oba Sandra Chioma, Owoo Oluwamayowa Emmanuel, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Offline language models could extend access to maternal-health information where internet connectivity is unreliable, but deployment requires both clinically appropriate responses and feasible local execution. This study presents an offline Android assistant integrating ready-made quantized language models, a clinically validated local knowledge package, conversational context, and model-initiated local retrieval. Gemma 4 E2B and Qwen3.5 0.8B were compared across 45 conversations and 312 turns per configuration, including a dedicated offline agentic benchmark. Gemma achieved higher response-quality, context-retention, and safety scores; Qwen offered faster generation and lower memory use. Clinician assessment of 150 Gemma responses across nine clinical domains yielded a mean quality score of 3.80/4, with 147 responses meeting the acceptance criterion. A paired RAG-on/off experiment evaluated 74 questions: retrieval improved 11 responses, left 56 neutral, produced four adverse response changes, and left three pairs unassessable. RAG-on had descriptively higher mean usefulness, safety, correctness, and actionability in the conditional 70-pair subset excluding the four flagged pairs from both conditions. Physical-device testing demonstrated offline responses on a 6 GB Android handset, with a 14.3 s median latency, alongside successful reconnection synchronization in the integrated application. Together, the findings document clinical response quality and practical performance of the integrated offline maternal-health assistant.
  </details>

- **[Omni-Workspace: Benchmarking Omni-Modal AI Assistants on Multimodal Workspace Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19202)**  `arXiv:2611.19202`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yuhao Wang, Yucheng Zhao, Yiran Zhong, Feida Zhu, Hongcheng Liu, Qichao Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Omni-modal agents are expected to move beyond multimodal understanding toward completing complex tasks in realistic multimodal environments. In these settings, information is distributed across heterogeneous files and modalities, with cross-file and cross-modal dependencies requiring agents to continually coordinate perception, reasoning, and tool use as newly gathered evidence informs subsequent actions. However, existing omni-modal benchmarks primarily focus on multimodal perception and understanding, leaving complex task completion through sustained interaction with multimodal workspaces underexplored. We introduce Omni-Workspace, a benchmark comprising 380 deep-research-style tasks across four multimodal workspace types and two difficulty levels. These tasks require agents to inspect and process workspace files, retrieve external information when needed, integrate evidence across files and modalities, and produce artifacts satisfying explicit requirements. Evaluating 18 representative models reveals limitations in long-horizon multimodal workspace execution, with the best model scoring only 65.93 on average. Further analysis identifies multimodal context inflation and task-state drift as recurring challenges. Motivated by these findings, we further propose OmniFocus, a lightweight approach combining multimodal working-memory management with explicit task-state tracking to improve performance and efficiency. Omni-Workspace advances the evaluation and development of omni-modal agents toward autonomous completion of complex tasks in multimodal workspaces.
  </details>

- **[What Transfers Across Cuneiform? Script-Level Substrate and Language-Specific Regularities via Segmentation and Representation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19094)**  `arXiv:2611.19094`  `cs.CL`  
  _Chuanjun Zhou, Adam G Anderson_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cuneiform recorded typologically unrelated languages for more than three millennia, raising a natural question: which of its statistical regularities are properties of the shared writing system, and which belong to individual languages? We answer this with two probe pairs targeting the same script-versus-language decomposition. The first compares two unsupervised, dictionary-free segmentation methods: transitional probability (TP), grounded in infant statistical-learning research, and Morfessor. To our knowledge, this is the first systematic evaluation of dictionary-free segmentation on cuneiform; the only prior published baseline relies on a hand-built dictionary (Max-Match, F1 = 0.74). Both our methods reach F1 > 0.97 within language (Morfessor reaches F1 > 0.99 on Akkadian and Sumerian), exceeding MaxMatch by more than 23 F1 points, but diverge sharply in cross-language transfer: TP holds F1 > 0.95 across all six language pairs, while Morfessor collapses to F1 = 0.27 from the 13K-token Elamite source. A learning curve and a Hittite stress test (34.7% foreign-script logograms, where TP drops 35 points and Morfessor only 8) further isolate the two underlying mechanisms. The second probe pair extends the same decomposition to representations: concatenating Unicode and Latin features improves classification in 11 of 12 morphosyntactic tasks (p < 0.01). We release CUNEI-TOOLS and a three-language benchmark.
  </details>

- **[GPBam: A Retrieval-Aware German Public Law Exam Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18981)**  `arXiv:2611.18981`  `cs.CL`  
  _Abdullah Al Zubaer, Lorenz Wendlinger, Simon Alexander Nonn, Michael Granitzer, Jelena Mitrović_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Legal reasoning in civil-law systems is not rule-lookup: the German state examination asks for a structured opinion (Gutachten) that names the governing norms, applies them to the facts, and argues to a conclusion. We introduce GPBam (German Public Law Bar-style Exam), 81 public-law examination cases with reference solutions, paired with a recitation task over 200 statutory provisions. Drawn from state examination preparation, the cases are broad and rarely repeat, and are not publicly available - we find no evidence of exact-solution memorisation in the models we evaluate, and keeping them private limits contamination. Because each reference solution's cited norms form a retrieval gold set over the very instances the essays are graded on, GPBam scores retrieval and long-form generation jointly, which makes it a benchmark for legal retrieval as well as for legal writing and lets us ask how much retrieval quality a legal opinion actually needs. Across 32 open-weight and proprietary models evaluated without retrieval, scores span 4.81-76.42 out of 100. Supplying the law helps modestly: across seven models and eight retrieval configurations, retrieval performance varies nearly threefold and buys at most 4.1 essay points, and nothing significant for three of the seven, while an oracle with 91% gold-norm recall improves scores by 2-6 points. Essay quality strongly tracks prior knowledge: verbatim statute recall, measured in a separate task, correlates at $\rho = 0.92$ over 32 models.
  </details>

- **[Criterion-Activated Preference Synthesis: Attribution-Preserving Preference Data from Explicit Standards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18663)**  `arXiv:2611.18663`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zian Wang, Pu Lu, Caishuang Huang, Wenxi Wu, Meng Zhou, Xiku Du, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) is notoriously sensitive to attribution ambiguity: when chosen and rejected responses differ along multiple entangled dimensions, the model cannot identify which behavior the preference label teaches. We propose Criterion-Activated Preference Synthesis (CAPS), a criterion-first framework that turns explicit quality standards into attribution-verified preference pairs. CAPS synthesizes contexts and responses from a target criterion, then applies dual-signal scoring: a rubric-bound judge measures criterion compliance, while a blind general judge measures holistic quality. Two filters close the attribution loop: the Empirical Activation Test (EAT) keeps criterion-context pairs where satisfying the criterion improves blind quality, and the Attribution Ratio (AR) keeps pairs whose quality gap is dominated by the target criterion. Instantiated with general rubrics, medical rubrics, and Chinese legal statutes, CAPS shows strong data efficiency and domain transfer. With 20k general synthetic pairs, it outperforms 35k human-annotated HelpSteer3 pairs on IFEval, IFBench, AlpacaEval, and Creative Writing v3. In domain settings, CAPS improves HealthBench with 2k medical pairs and improves Qwen3-8B/14B by +4.9 to +6.6 points on legal benchmarks using only public statutes, without human preference labels. We additionally construct the CAPS-Legal preference dataset.
  </details>

- **[Decision 1.0: Towards Open Decision Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18629)**  `arXiv:2611.18629`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xunzhuo Liu, Haichen Zhang, Andy Luo, Huamin Chen, Xue Liu, Bowei He_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Intelligent software needs reusable judgments inside its control flow: selecting an action, testing a condition, or assessing evidence against a rubric. We present Decision 1.0, an open-weight family that turns textual state and runtime-defined questions into typed probability distributions. Its Choice, Noul, and Score interface lets applications specify outcomes at inference time and compose predictions into agents and workflows. Each question produces its candidate distribution without autoregressive text generation. Six models cover bidirectional encoder and causal decoder architectures, including a workflow specialist. Training draws on verifiable tasks, natural-language annotations, and workflow adaptation. On a common decision benchmark, Lux-9B achieves 84.17\% on explicit decisions and 91.25\% on inference, with a weighted overall score of 77.22\%. Results also expose gaps in composition, transfer, and probability calibration. Released weights and inference code provide a basis for developing decision models as reusable components of software.
  </details>

- **[EmoTrace: Recursive Trace-Factorized Generation for Emotional Support Conversation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18628)**  `arXiv:2611.18628`  `cs.CL`  
  _Mingwen Zhang, Minqiang Yang, Yang Yu, Haobo Liang, Yongfeng Tao, Changsheng Ma, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fundamental to effective emotional support is the iterative interpersonal dynamic wherein a supporter intervention continuously shapes the psychological trajectory of the seeker. While contemporary models capture variables at the turn level, including transient emotions and discrete strategies, they inherently treat these factors as isolated events. By retaining strictly literal dialogue histories while discarding the underlying decision logic, such architectures remain fundamentally incapable of sustaining coherent extended support. Here we introduce EmoTrace, a recursive method utilizing trace factorization to preserve explicit decision continuity throughout interactions spanning multiple turns.
