Departing from others, our approach formulates each conversational turn as a structured interaction increment encapsulating the evolving state of the seeker, the selected strategy, the affect of the supporter, and the ultimate textual realization. Accumulated sequentially, this trajectory of increments functions as an explicit structural trace informing subsequent interventions. Optimization of this factorized generation is achieved through two complementary mechanisms. Specifically, a value guidance mechanism aligns discrete factor selection with interaction values over an extended horizon, whereas a trace conditioning mechanism refines textual realization contingent upon the fixed strategic trace. Empirical evaluations across standard benchmarks demonstrate that EmoTrace yields substantial improvements in both strategic planning and response generation. Crucially, rollouts spanning multiple turns reveal that these retained structural traces confer compounding benefits over extended dialogues, establishing a robust mechanism for sustained affective alignment in human-machine interaction.
  </details>

- **[CondRelBench: A Mixed-Domain Benchmark for Condition-Scoped Relation Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18620)**  `arXiv:2611.18620`  `cs.CL`  
  _Sydney Anuyah, Bofu Dong, Sudhasmita Swain, Sunandan Chakraborty_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Relation extraction systems often identify what a relation is, but not the condition under which it holds. This can collapse condition-bound claims into unsupported generalizations, a failure we call relation overgeneralization. We introduce CondRelBench, a mixed-domain benchmark for evaluating condition-scoped relation understanding. Each claim is represented as (E1, R, E2 | C), where C is the condition licensing the relation. Using 3,000 synthetic paragraphs, 200 PubMed full-text papers, and 695 Reddit posts, we construct five probe variants per tuple that preserve, replace, remove, or contradict the condition, or contradict the relation itself. Models classify each probe as Supported, Not Supported, or Not Enough Evidence. We also introduce Overgeneralization Rate (OGR) and Scope Preservation Score (SPS) to measure false support for altered claims and consistency across probe families. Across prompted LLMs, fine-tuned LLMs, and encoder models, we find that Accuracy and Macro F1 often hide scope failures. Models frequently support the original scoped claim but fail when its condition is removed or altered. Domain-specific encoders achieve the strongest scope preservation, while instruction-tuned LLMs often show higher overgeneralization. Synthetic supervision helps but does not close the gap to real-world PubMed and Reddit performance.
  </details>

- **[Assisting Patients with Reliable Information Gathering: A Guide to Building Efficient QA Systems for Medical Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18619)**  `arXiv:2611.18619`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zhipeng Yin, Zichong Wang, Min Chen, Ian Stockwell, Xin Ning, Jun Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The application of question-answering (QA) systems in the medical domain has rapidly advanced, significantly improving patients' access to reliable health-related information. However, current approaches face notable challenges, including the difficulty in obtaining large-scale and unbiased medical datasets, significant privacy concerns, and inefficiencies due to manual dataset annotation. To address these issues, This study introduce a novel methodology leveraging publicly accessible online health forums to systematically build an unbiased, privacy-conscious QA dataset, and it employs Topic-guided Semantic Modeling (TGSM) for automated topic identification, enabling efficient and targeted annotation of relevant patient-generated content. Subsequently, this study propose a two-stage QA pipeline based on a Retriever-Reader architecture, which is further enhanced through fine-tuning state-of-the-art transformer-based models on the constructed domain-specific QA dataset. Experimental results demonstrate that our fine-tuned BioBERT significantly outperforms existing benchmarks, offering accurate patient-derived insights and providing a replicable framework for building efficient medical QA systems.
  </details>

- **[NYUAD at ImageEval Shared Tasks: A Multimodal LLM for Audio-Visual Question Answering and Hallucination Detection in English and Modern Standard Arabic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18610)**  `arXiv:2611.18610`  `cs.CL`  
  _Nouar AlDahoul, Yasir Zaki_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The increasing adoption of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) has enabled more natural interaction with visual and spoken information, creating new opportunities for intelligent audio-visual question answering (AVQA) and hallucination detection systems. AVQA extends traditional visual question answering by requiring models to jointly understand an input image and a spoken question to generate accurate, context-aware responses. Despite recent advances, AVQA and hallucination detection tasks remain challenging for modern standard Arabic (MSA) due to the limited availability of multimodal datasets and the need to reason across Arabic speech and text modalities alongside visual inputs. In this paper, we present an MLLM-based framework for AVQA that takes an image and a spoken question as input and generates answers. In addition, we utilized this framework to detect hallucination in text-image pairs. The proposed approach is evaluated on the ImageEval 2026 Task (Ayn-VQA) benchmark, which focuses on culturally grounded Arabic AVQA. Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed framework effectively understands spoken queries, grounds their responses in the visual content, and produces accurate answers. The results underscore the capacity and limitations of different LLM architectures for both English and MSA and under various conditions.
  </details>

- **[ScopeBench: Evaluating Cultural Norm Scope Awareness in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18600)**  `arXiv:2611.18600`  `cs.CL`  
  _Haojun Liang, Ziwei Zhao, Chenyu Li, Qiyue Zhao, Rongduomijia Mu, Yulan Shen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Safety evaluation in large language models treats harm as culturally invariant, yet a model cannot correctly apply a safety norm without knowing whose norms bind. We introduce ScopeBench, a benchmark that treats cultural scope as a first-class safety dimension. It comprises 3,323 norms spanning 49 countries across nine cultural clusters, each annotated as Universal or Specific. We define three scope error types identically across four evaluation tasks: Scope Inflation, Scope Deflation, and Scope Displacement. Evaluating 21 frontier LLMs across seven model families, we surface three structural failures. First, the dominant error type inverts with prompt format: Scope Deflation reaches 70% in single-answer classification, while Scope Inflation reaches 33% when each candidate culture is judged independently. Second, scope-classification accuracy is empirically decoupled from behavioral safety: the six classifiers achieving at least 97% Task 1 accuracy still produce harmful responses to 40-42% of scope-violating statements. Third, no model is safe in interaction, with harmful-response rates ranging from 29% to 51% across the cohort, excluding one model that systematically refuses the task. We release all data, code, and model outputs to establish cultural scope as a distinct alignment axis.
  </details>

- **[Endogenous Preferences as Vulnerability Signals: Discovering Universal Adversarial Triggers for LLM Utility Degradation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18592)**  `arXiv:2611.18592`  `cs.CL`  
  _Qingyu Wu, Zeyu Feng, Yuzhe Luo, Hua Cheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deployed large language models must remain useful when untrusted text enters their prompt context. Adversarial prompting and red-teaming methods typically define the failures they seek through task labels, harmful targets, judge feedback, or downstream utility scores. We ask whether a frozen model's clean behavior can reveal a reusable context vulnerability when its task-level form is unknown, and downstream task feedback is withheld. To study this question, EndoUT uses a Siamese-style design that compares the same frozen victim on clean and span-modified versions of each unlabeled instruction. Without a task-level target, the clean pass supplies the victim's preferred response-onset token as an instruction-conditioned reference; Endogenous Preference Disruption (EPD) measures how insertion weakens that preference. An auxiliary generator supplies bounded-span proposals; EPD guides its update and ranks the pool without access to downstream information. The highest-scoring victim-specific span is fixed before evaluation and reused unchanged across all held-out inputs. Across four instruction-tuned models and nine benchmarks, fixed triggers reduce benign-task utility by 40.2 percentage points on average, with negative mean change on every benchmark and greater average degradation than every evaluated baseline under the reported protocols. Matched protocol ablations show stronger transfer for source-side selection followed by fixed reuse than for null-context fixed reuse or per-input generation. Transfer recurs across proposers with proposal-dependent strength; output-level analyses identify victim-specific associations between trigger families and downstream failure patterns. Thus, clean behavior can expose context vulnerabilities that transfer across inputs yet manifest through structured, victim-specific patterns of utility degradation. The code and data will be released upon acceptance.
  </details>

- **[Read, Route, Patch: Robust Test-Time Evolution for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18585)**  `arXiv:2611.18585`  `cs.CL`  
  _Kaijie Chen, Liang Yan, Yilan Wei, Jiachen Luo, Zhihao Dou, Jiashu Qu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Model (LLM)-based agents can improve long- horizon interactive decision-making through test-time evolu- tion, yet existing methods face three information bottlenecks: memory retrieval overemphasizes textual similarity, candi- date harnesses are often behaviorally redundant, and whole- harness rewriting may overwrite useful evidence or introduce cross-component inconsistency. We introduce Trident, which improves experience utilization, behavioral exploration, and harness evolution through boundary-aware memory reading, diversity-aware candidate routing, and order-aware insertion- based updating, respectively. We evaluate Trident on five benchmarks spanning text games, web interaction, and cod- ing tasks. Across qwen-32B, mistral-small-3.2-24B, and gpt- oss-20B, Trident achieves the best average performance and consistently outperforms non-learning, memory-based, reflection-based, and automated prompt optimization base- lines. Compared with the strongest baseline, APEX, Trident yields relative average gains of 30.7%, 30.8%, and 38.3% on the three backbones, respectively. Ablation and sensitivity stud- ies further show that the three designs provide complementary gains and remain robust to parameter variations.
  </details>

- **[GT-FENG: General Theory of Factual Evaluation and Normative Grounding in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18279)**  `arXiv:2611.18279`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jiayu Feng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Whether a language model's answer is correct often depends on context: the same value can be right under one jurisdiction, date, or legal definition and wrong under another. We introduce GT-FENG (General Theory of Factual Evaluation and Normative Grounding), the first general framework to unify contextual/temporal correctness and rule-based error attribution across law, policy, and other rule-governed domains. GT-FENG checks answers against the applicable rules and identifies documented rules that match incorrect answers. It shows that when multiple rules give the same value, the answer alone cannot tell which rule the model used. In Medicaid income-limit answers from two models, documenting the asked state's own records reduces the number of wrong answers that match only other states' values by 68-78%, and more than half of stable wrong answers then match two or more documented sources. GT-FENG provides a foundation for evaluating language models in rule-governed domains and for future benchmarks and evaluation toolkits. We release GT-FENG as an open-source Python package, gtfeng.
  </details>

- **[We Are What We Remember: Mental-State-Driven Memory for Long-Term Character Dialogue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16223)**  `arXiv:2611.16223`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zhicheng Jia, Shuchen Wu, Zhuoshi Tong, Haisen Liao, Sicheng Zhou, Chao Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Shared history in human dialogue is not merely a repository of facts, but the foundation of evolving relationships and mutual understanding. However, mainstream conversational memory systems rely on passive semantic similarity over external fact stores, frequently retrieving topically similar records while missing relationally pivotal experiences. Inspired by cognitive psychology's Self-Memory System (SMS), we propose MindMem, a mental-state-driven memory framework that asynchronously consolidates shared experiences into source-linked mental states of the character, the user, and their relationship. At each turn, a specialized mental-state reasoner (Mind) contextualizes these persistent states to anticipate latent user needs, form proactive intentions, and recover supporting episodic evidence that surface-level matching overlooks. To evaluate history-sensitive adaptation beyond factual recall, we introduce MemReal-Bench, a longitudinal benchmark grounded in month-long real-user interactions across dyadic and group scenes. By pairing Direct Question Answering (DQA) with Situated Memory Realization (SMR), it tests whether agents bridge the gap between knowing past facts and naturally using shared history, world rules, and relational trajectories. On MemReal-Bench, MindMem reaches 59.03% SMR and 82.58% DQA, exceeding the strongest memory baseline by 24.19 and 9.19 percentage points, respectively, and outperforming oracle target-memory injection on SMR. Code and dataset are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Truncation: When Can LLMs Safely Omit Generated Text?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08975)**  `arXiv:2611.08975`  `cs.CL`  
  _Mahmoud Alyosify, Mirna Imbabi, Eman Elkhamisi, Rahatara Ferdousi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Instruction-tuned large language models (LLMs) answer at greater length than a request needs. We ask whether a training-free controller inside the decoding loop can remove that surplus while preserving content, judged against the simplest possible baseline: truncating the model's own answer to the same length. An entropy-gated logits processor removes 25.5-34.9% of tokens on MT-Bench development questions and 19.9-34.2% on a sealed test split within pre-registered BERTScore non-inferiority margins, yet we prove and confirm by audit that its output is always a prefix of the unmodified answer: the saving is truncation. A survey of 23 training-free mechanism families finds none that selects content better than truncation without oracle labels. We therefore introduce Entailment-Licensed In-Decoding Elision (ELIDE), which, at each sentence boundary, withholds a span from the reader only when a natural-language inference model judges it entailed by the text already shown, while leaving the model's generation unchanged. ELIDE beats matched-length truncation on three model families, but reading 936 of its elisions on 670 public prompts shows that 59% harm the answer, a failure that no automatic measure detects. Scoping the licence to its discourse unit reduces the harmful share to about one elision in ten on two fresh tests.
  </details>

- **[NitiBench+: A Temporal Thai Legal Question Answering Benchmark Across 17 Domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03576)**  `arXiv:2611.03576`  `cs.CL`  
  _Pawitsapak Akarajaradwong, Sarana Nutanong, Chompakorn Chaksangchaichot_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Law changes over time. A court decides a case under the law in force on that day, and legal QA benchmarks grade answers against today's law. We checked 156,650 decisions of four Thai courts. Up to 67% of them cite law that has since been amended or repealed. We introduce Nitibench+, the sequel of our NitiBench, a Thai legal QA benchmark with 1,670 items across 17 domains, built from decisions of three courts. For each cited section it holds both the text in force on the decision date and the current text, and its retrieval corpus holds every dated version of every Thai statute section. Answers are graded on the Thai Bar Council rubric, the Bar-standard score, and on the overlap between the cited sections and the court's. The report has two parts. Part I measures the cost of the wrong text on 13 open-weight models. Reading the current text of amended law drops the Bar-standard score by 0.25 and missing repealed law drops it by 0.79. The penalty comes from rule changes, 0.45 on the 428 rule-changed items and none on the 331 wording-only items, and Citation F1 does not see it. The text in force is the fair setting. Part II benchmarks today's models on that setting. A public leaderboard ranks 33 models on a held-out test split of 500 items stratified by domain, with a validation split of the same shape released for public use, from memory alone, with the court's sections provided, and as a retrieval agent over the law in force, with the cost per item and the hallucinated citations. The best model scores 7.78 out of 10 and the best open-weight model 6.39. Without a search tool only five open-weight models recall any Thai law, and with it 27 of the 33 models gain and six lose. Leaderboard will take new models as they are released. Legal benchmarks must track dates, and on the right text today's models still leave a wide gap on Thai law.
  </details>

- **[Unreliable Narrator Framing as a Cost-effective Mechanism for Satire Explanation of Australian and American Satirical News](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12863)**  `arXiv:2610.12863`  `cs.CL`  
  _Kanay Gupta, David Y. Liu, Aditya Joshi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Satirical news is a unique form of misinformation: it is intended to be humorous by the original publication source but can be misinterpreted. Explanation of satirical news articles is challenging because recent and local context needs to be incorporated. This is a pilot study that explores explanation generation for satirical news articles from Australia and compares them alongside those from USA. We show that framing explanations with unreliable narrator theory on open-weight models can be a feasible, cost-effective alternative to frontier models. We find that models identify the satirical tropes more accurately on the American source than the Australian one. This work results in a novel dataset and an evaluation benchmark for satire explanation.
  </details>

- **[Vishing-Tactics-Bench: Forecasting Exploitation Trajectories in Voice Phishing Calls](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07151)**  `arXiv:2609.07151`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jeongmin Lee, Dongmyung Sul, Seung Yun, Jinxia Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Voice phishing (vishing) unfolds in real time; by the time a call has ended and post-hoc classification is possible, the harm has already been done. The more actionable question is which concrete harm (Information Gathering or Financial Exploitation) an ongoing call is tactically progressing toward. We present Vishing-Tactics-Bench, a benchmark grounded in Endsley's situation-awareness (SA) framework that recasts vishing defense from after-the-fact fraud classification to harm projection: predicting at each turn whether the call will reach either terminal harm. We adapt MITRE ATT&CK to vishing as a 6-tactic taxonomy (Vishing-Tactics) and label 35,340 scammer utterances across 5,645 synthetic Chinese calls. We define Exploitation Trajectory Forecasting, a survival-style protocol over the two terminal harms with three metrics: AP@k, C-index, and divergence error. Baselines ranging from a Markov heuristic to fine-tuned LLMs show that the tactical trajectory serves as an interpretable representation of the call's tactical state, supporting harm-specific forecasting, which can then be used for the downstream application of intervention selection; a stratified lead-time analysis at a tight false-alarm budget further identifies at what point in a call the trajectory signal yields early warning.
  </details>

- **[IndicQE-APE: A Consolidated Benchmark for Quality Estimation and Automatic Post-Editing over Indic Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16344)**  `arXiv:2608.16344`  `cs.CL`  
  _Diptesh Kanojia, Archchana Sindhujan, Sourabh Deoghare, Daria Sokova, Shenbin Qian, Girish Koushik, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Indic quality estimation (QE) and automatic post-editing (APE) data is spread across separate releases, so no single resource supports training and evaluation across tasks and language pairs on one footing. We consolidate the WMT 2020-2024 shared-task lineage with an extended English-Malayalam resource into IndicQE-APE: $126{,}754$ instances over nine directional pairs, with up to four label types aligned on the same segment, a direct assessment, a human post-edit, word-level tags and an error explanation, and a test set stratified over four difficulty axes. We benchmark six prompted LLMs and three COMET metrics on segment-level QE, and three systems on APE. Two of the axes are defined partly on direct assessment and select a compressed slice of it. Segments whose segment-level and token-level signals disagree are ranked below equally scored segments of the same language. Four-shot prompting costs every model at or below $3.4$B both correlation and output-format compliance. Unedited MT beats every APE system we run on three of the four pairs. The benchmarkthis https URLand codethis https URLare released.
  </details>

- **[Uncertainty Is Not a Safety Net for Clinical VQA, but Can It Anticipate Model Failure?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16583)**  `arXiv:2606.16583`  `cs.CL`  
  _Arnisa Fazla, Alberto Testoni, Ameen Abu-Hanna, Barbara Plank, Iacer Calixto_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Safe deployment of clinical vision-language models (VLMs) requires reliable uncertainty estimation (UE): a signal indicating when predictions should be trusted or escalated to a clinician. We test whether current UE methods actually deliver this signal. Benchmarking 8 methods across 12 VLMs on clinical visual question-answering (VQA), we find that UE quality is not an intrinsic property of the UE method: it tracks model accuracy, degrading precisely where the model performance is weakest, and therefore where reliability is most needed. When we stress-test models by hiding the correct option among the multiple-choice answers (NOTA perturbations), accuracy collapses while uncertainty barely changes, leaving models systematically miscalibrated. Yet, we find that uncertainty on the unperturbed input reliably anticipates which predictions will collapse under NOTA, indicating that UE in current VLMs carries diagnostic information about model fragility. Our results position UE as a diagnostic tool for identifying fragile predictions and motivate perturbation-based evaluation as a path toward safe clinical deployment.
  </details>

- **[K-BrowseComp: A Web Browsing Agent Benchmark Grounded in Korean Contexts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02404)**  `arXiv:2606.02404`  `cs.CL`  
  _Nahyun Lee, Dongkeun Yoon, Guijin Son, Geewook Kim, Dayoon Ko, Jeonghun Park, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Frontier model evaluations are shifting from foundational capabilities (e.g., instruction following and reasoning) toward compositional, agentic ones, but Korean agentic benchmarks remain scarce. We introduce K-BrowseComp, a web-browsing agent benchmark grounded in Korean contexts, consisting of 400 problems. The 300-problem K-BrowseComp-Verified subset is manually constructed and validated by native Korean speakers. On this subset, frontier LLMs, including GPT-5.5, DeepSeek-V4-Pro, and GLM-5.1, reach only 30.00--45.67\%, a substantial drop from BrowseComp, while Korean LLMs released through Korea's Proprietary AI Foundation Model program obtain only 0.00--10.33\%. We further construct a 100-problem synthetic split using hard few-shot exemplars and failure-mode-targeted generation to exploit the asymmetry between solving and creating web browsing problems. On the adversarially filtered synthetic diagnostic split, the strongest model reaches only 26.00\%, and we report this split separately as a targeted stress test. We publicly release our data and code.
  </details>

- **[SimCT: Recovering Lost Supervision for Cross-Tokenizer On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07711)**  `arXiv:2605.07711`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jie Sun, Mao Zheng, Mingyang Song, Qiyong Zhong, Yilin Cheng, Bichuan Feng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation (OPD) is a standard tool for transferring teacher behavior to a smaller student, but it implicitly assumes that teacher and student predictions are comparable token by token, an assumption that fails whenever the two models tokenize the same text differently. Under heterogeneous tokenizers, exact shared-token matching silently discards a large fraction of the teacher signal at precisely the positions where vocabularies disagree. We propose \textbf{\underline{Sim}ple \underline{C}ross-\underline{T}okenizer OPD (SimCT)}, which restores this signal by enlarging the supervision space: alongside shared tokens, SimCT compares teacher and student over short multi-token continuations that both tokenizers can realize, leaving the OPD loss form itself unchanged. We show that these units are the finest jointly tokenizable supervision interface, and that coarser alternatives remove teacher-student distinctions that are useful for on-policy learning. Across three heterogeneous teacher-student pairs on mathematical reasoning and code-generation benchmarks, SimCT shows consistent gains over shared-vocabulary OPD and representative cross-tokenizer baselines, with ablations confirming that the improvements come from recovering supervision discarded by exact shared-token matching. Code is available at \href{this https URL}{this https URL}.
  </details>

- **[PersonaFuse: A Situation-Aware Post-Training Framework for Adaptive LLM Behavior in Enterprise Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.07370)**  `arXiv:2509.07370`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yixuan Tang, Yi Yang, Ahmed Abbasi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in enterprise settings to support task execution and workflow automation. Despite the strong performance of general-purpose models on standard benchmarks, organizations often need to adapt them to domain-specific tasks and operational constraints in practice. However, existing post-training approaches introduce capability trade-offs and lack mechanisms for situational adaptation. In this work, we propose PersonaFuse, a post-training framework that enables situation-aware behavioral adaptation in LLMs. Grounded in Trait Activation Theory and operationalized through the Five-Factor Model, PersonaFuse dynamically adjusts model behavior based on task context using a mixture-of-experts architecture with trait-level experts and a context-aware routing mechanism. We evaluate PersonaFuse at three levels. At the capability level, it achieves balanced improvements across general reasoning, interaction quality, and safety without introducing trade-offs. At the workflow level, it improves task completion in enterprise scenarios involving tool use and structured execution across both B2B and B2C tasks. At the deployment level, we analyze model selection under cost and safety constraints using a capability-cost frontier framework, showing that models adapted with PersonaFuse achieve more favorable trade-offs and are consistently selected as optimal across deployment scenarios. PersonaFuse provides a practical framework for developing LLMs that are not only capable, but also adaptable and deployable in real-world enterprise environments.
  </details>

- **[Autonomous Multi-modal Agents for ITOps Observability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19982)**  `arXiv:2611.19982`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Gunjan Siddharth, Sanjana Garai, Utsav Shekhar, Prakhar Jain, Sarvam Maheshwari, Balaji Ganesan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  ITOps observability products must integrate data from different tools and systems. We present ITOps Observability Studio, a multi-modal multi-agent system that orchestrates observability workflows through collaborative AI agents. Its three-tier architecture features inter-agent communication, specialized LLM-powered agents for data access, schema operations and visualization, and unified tool integration. The system supports natural language to GraphQL translation, GraphQL schema generation, schema mapping, trace data lifecycle management and multi-tool workflows for reliability engineering. Its agents are instrumented with agent analytics, so that their model and tool calls can be traced. We evaluate each agent on its own benchmark, scored against gold answers where they exist and by a model judge for schema mapping and chart quality. Query generation on a public benchmark and routing work well with a current open model, and the data lifecycle management agent solves most of its scenarios. The evaluation exposed defects that only appear when prompts, parsers and serving stacks are tested together, and we report them together with their fixes.
  </details>

- **[LLM Trading Agents Earn Beta, Not Alpha: A Look-Ahead-Free Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19530)**  `arXiv:2611.19530`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Jackson Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) trading agents now trade stocks for their users, and benchmarks rank models by their agents' returns. Yet a single run's return says little about skill. In our study, two runs of the same model that differed only in the random seed ended 5.2 points apart on average, so telling skill from luck takes many backtests across markets, models and seeds. However, live arenas trade each day only once, and backtests either use the few months after a model's training cutoff or run on years the model may remember, often with news joined to prices by calendar date. In this paper, we present rta-bench-1, a backtest over three US markets (a rally, a bear market and a crash). It shows each headline only after its publication minute, plays each period blind and named, and checks every run for look-ahead. It also splits each return into factor exposure and alpha and scores every decision against random choices of the same day and sector. In 91 runs of six models from four vendors, no alpha was significant, market and style exposure accounted for a median 78% of the return, and the decisions did no better than random stocks of the same sector. The agents bought the stocks with the most positive news, yet in ablations they earned as much without their headlines. Still, the agents carried out rules reliably, since a single cash rule in the prompt cut a GPT-based agent's share in stocks by 30.3 points in all six pairs. Builders should therefore supply the edge themselves and report exposures and alpha, and benchmarks should replay news by publication time.
  </details>

- **[SWE-OPD: On-Policy Distillation for Agentic Coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21020)**  `arXiv:2611.21020`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Yuhang He, Yanyu Chen, Chuanzhe Guo, Jie Zhu, Zichen Tian, Hongyu Ge, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this work, we study on-policy distillation (OPD) for transferring agentic coding capabilities from a large teacher model to a smaller student model. Prior work has focused primarily on reasoning and short-horizon agentic tasks. We present SWE-OPD, a minimal OPD study for software engineering, where the student interacts with executable repositories and the teacher supervises the prefixes visited by the student. Across three teacher--student pairs, two training sources, and three SWE benchmarks, we find that on-policy supervision transfers coding capability: supervision from Qwen3.6-27B increases the SWE-bench Pro resolve rate of Qwen3.5-9B from 35.9% to 39.4%. Refreshing trajectories is not strictly required either, as an offline variant with cached teacher scores still improves over the student baselines while cutting end-to-end GPU-hours by 59.4%--72.6% and halving peak GPU allocation, at benchmark-dependent accuracy. Furthermore, we extend the offline variant to corss-tokenizer scenarios: distilling GLM-4.5-Air from DeepSeek-V4-Flash-0731 raises its SWE-bench Pro and Multilingual resolve rate from 35.8% to 39.7% and from 41.8% to 46.2%, around 4% and 4.6% improvement, respectively. We also find that a decreasing loss, however, does not necessarily indicate improved agent performance: under top-$K$ reverse-KL loss, one student exhibits severe repetition despite a decreasing loss. We identify a support-mass gradient term as a potential mechanism underlying this failure.
  </details>

- **[GRACE: Parallel Best-of-N Multi-Agent Analysis of Closed-Form Questions over Dataframes with Guardrail Selection and Adaptive Retry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20983)**  `arXiv:2611.20983`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Fabio Picariello, Salvatore Cipolla, Susanna Bonura, Vito Morreale_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents answer closed-form questions over a tabular dataframe by writing and executing Python, almost always as a single rollout: one interpretation, one script, one result, accepted without comparison. The few pipelines that produce several candidates reconcile them by answer-agreement voting, which needs a single comparable scalar and always returns one, even when every candidate is wrong. We present GRACE (Generative, Ranked, Adaptive Code Ensemble), a production-oriented pipeline that instead runs parallel best-of-N selection specialised to dataframe code. N agents run concurrently under a strict, machine-checkable output contract, diversified by persona hints, per-retry rotation, and a two-regime temperature schedule. Rather than vote, GRACE judges the executed artefacts: a pairwise tournament pre-filters the pool and a domain-specialised guardrail either selects the best candidate or, when none is defensible, rejects the whole pool and abstains with a diagnosis (an option voting structurally lacks), which in turn drives an adaptive retry with per-agent feedback. On InfiAgent-DABench (DAEval, M = 246), using Qwen3.6-35B-A3B as the backbone, GRACE raises answer match from 39% for a single-rollout baseline to 71% (+32 pp, 1.8x), recovering 53% of previously wrong answers. On the same DAEval task, the smaller dense Qwen3.5-9B lifts answer match from 38% to 63% (+25 pp, 1.7x). A controlled 3 x 3 study that sweeps the number of parallel agents against the retry budget finds, on the 35B backbone, the two levers largely substitutable: once either one is generous, the other adds little, identifying a cheap operating point for deployment. The same directional pattern replicates on the independent DataBench benchmark, again on the Qwen3.6-35B-A3B backbone, where answer match rises by 15 percentage points (from 70% to 85%) across 400 sampled questions.
  </details>

- **[AgentFaultBench: State-Verified Evaluation of Tool-Fault Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20711)**  `arXiv:2611.20711`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Sachin Gupta_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Does recovery advice help an agent finish a task after a tool fails? AgentFaultBench evaluates LangGraph and CrewAI agents performing specified tasks in an isolated GitLab installation. We inject tool errors and check both the resulting files and issues and the required reads, verification and final response. The prompt supplies the expected issue and report text, so the task tests execution rather than content generation. In the primary comparison of 1,944 planned runs, advice changes success by -8.33, 0.00 and +1.06 percentage points relative to a neutral reminder; missing outcomes leave the latter two comparisons inconclusive. Later experiments test a revised recovery policy. GPT-4o mini improves, while Sol and Claude already complete the recoverable cases. In a separate 624-run study on six related tasks not previously used by that policy, the guided condition records 161 correct final states and one unknown outcome in 192 planned runs, versus 142/192 with neutral feedback. The gain lies between +9.90 and +10.42 pp when missing outcomes are included. Removing issue IDs gives 47/48 successes versus 46/48 with IDs; other execution information remains. A second checker agrees on 1,879 observed primary outcomes and 241 constructed test cases. No human validation was collected. The findings support testing advice against explicit recovery controls rather than assuming it helps.
  </details>

- **[GEM-MM: Reducing VLM Hallucination with Entropy-Guided Preference Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20016)**  `arXiv:2611.20016`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Yuanshan Chua, Xuejiao Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) are routinely aligned with offline pairwise RLHF objectives, but multimodal preference data is scarce and treats every response token as equally informative. Under a small preference budget, pair construction becomes nearly irrelevant: standard DPO-style, image-conditional, and self-improvement objectives all perform within 1.1 points on MM-RLHF metrics and 0.5 points on hallucination rates, matching the SFT baseline. We call this entropy-blind preference optimization: the learning signal ignores policy uncertainty, which in chain-of-thought VLMs concentrates on specific fork tokens where visual claims are made. We introduce GEM-MM, extending entropy-guided preference modeling to VLMs. It samples on-policy candidates, rewards high-entropy reasoning forks, penalizes uncertain final answers, and updates the model via group-normalized policy gradients. On a 3000-prompt MM-RLHF budget with Qwen3-VL-4B-Instruct, GEM-MM achieves the best entropy-depth preference score (61.7% vs. 57.3-58.4% baselines) and implicit-reward accuracy (41.4%). Crucially, it reduces AMBER hallucination from 23.4-24.0 to 20.6 and CHAIR from 5.0-5.1 to 4.6 at matched caption coverage. It also ranks best on all three HallusionBench granularities, with a conservative operating point raising the near-chosen rate to 62.4%. An off-the-shelf multimodal reward model ranks GEM-MM first, and two such models independently confirm our main ablations (p < 0.001). We report confidence intervals, paired significance tests, metric-reward functional overlaps, and document a collapse mode when exceeding the fair budget. Code and evaluation harness:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Interactive Structured Environment Scaling for Large Language Model Agentic Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19871)**  `arXiv:2611.19871`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Peng Yu, Luoyi Fu, Xinbing Wang, Ying Wen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning via environmental interaction is a cornerstone post-training paradigm for evolving large language models into autonomous agents capable of multi-turn tool use and exploration. Because the environment defines what the policy observes and how actions are verified, its structure directly bounds the capabilities an agent can acquire. Yet existing structured and retrieval environments rely on upstream pre-filtering---extracting a compact subgraph around the gold answer or retrieving fixed passages before the policy acts. We show that such pre-filtering introduces a fundamental measurement confound: because per-question subgraphs enclose ground-truth paths, they compress the candidate space by orders of magnitude and leak answer locations via local topology, allowing a question-blind degree heuristic to surface gold answers far above chance. To restore path selection, candidate pruning, and optimal stopping as genuine policy decisions, we propose RISE (Interactive Structured Environment), which transitions structured reinforcement learning from static subgraphs to full-scale interactive databases along two coupled axes. First, Decision-Space Scaling exposes the unpruned knowledge graph via a five-stage graph-agnostic typed intent adapter under a bounded call budget and a multiplicative grounding reward gate. Second, Learning-Frontier Scaling overcomes within-group variance collapse under group-normalized objectives by uniting model-independent structural tier progression with a self-evolving competence-frontier bandit. Across nineteen benchmarks and three model backbones, RISE substantially advances in-domain and unseen-schema knowledge-graph reasoning, transfers zero-shot to open-domain passage retrieval, and significantly enhances out-of-domain mathematical and logical deduction.
  </details>

- **[PMR: MoE Routing with Shared Prototypes and Congestion Pricing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19413)**  `arXiv:2611.19413`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Wending Wang, Binqi Huang, Ju'an Li, Yu Guo, Lei Duan, Yuqi Ouyang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Conventional linear routers in sparse mixture-of-experts models select experts directly from token-expert matching scores, coupling input matching with expert allocation. We propose Prototype-Mediated Routing (PMR), a two-stage router with shared prototypes. PMR first computes each token's distribution over prototypes, then uses it to mix prototype-conditioned expert distributions according to the law of total probability. This formulation models input matching and expert allocation separately, explicitly represents many-to-many associations between prototypes and experts, and provides separate interfaces for context adaptation and load regulation. Within this framework, short-term states summarize preceding tokens to adapt the prototypes, while congestion prices updated from dispatch feedback achieve loss-free load balancing by adjusting the conditional expert distributions. We use a two-pass blockwise approximation to accelerate short-term state processing, achieving over 20 times the training throughput of the eager tokenwise PMR implementation while maintaining high agreement in routing decisions. Across three pretraining configurations with 0.43B, 1.18B, and 0.48B total parameters, PMR improves mean zero-shot accuracy on seven benchmarks over linear routing baselines with matched expert architectures by 1.54, 2.65, and 1.45 percentage points (pp), respectively. The corresponding gains on ARC-Easy are 6.71, 5.80, and 3.92 pp. In parameter-efficient fine-tuning of 7B-8B backbones, PMR's improvements over MixLoRA vary across backbones, with gains of up to 1.76 pp in mean accuracy across four tasks.
  </details>

- **[SpecRelay: Amortize or Avoid the Round Trip in Edge-Cloud Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18963)**  `arXiv:2611.18963`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Jai Jain, Chihyo Ahn, Yong Liu, Charles Chen, Duc N.M Hoang, Ajay Kumar Jaiswal, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Edge-cloud speculative decoding moves autoregressive drafting to an edge device while a larger cloud model verifies draft tokens in parallel, so that edge drafting and cloud verification overlap under a synchronous draft-verify schedule. Beyond low-latency local networks, however, the next verification request of that schedule can be sent only after the previous reply arrives, so every round still waits out a full round trip, and throughput falls as the network RTT grows. To recover this loss, we present SpecRelay, an edge-cloud LLM inference system that addresses this cost at two levels: it amortizes round trips when cloud verification is necessary and avoids them when local reasoning suffices. For cloud-bound queries, an asynchronous verification pipeline keeps fixed-budget draft trees in flight while earlier replies are still outstanding, and the cloud rejects stale requests before the target-model forward pass (the asynchronous schedule). At the query level, an RTT-conditioned router (the Latency-Aware Router) estimates quality and completion latency for three on-device modes (direct generation, Chain-of-Draft, and full reasoning) and cloud speculative decoding, selecting among them under a configurable accuracy-latency objective without adding latency during inference, since the router runs concurrently with the draft-model prefill. The asynchronous schedule alone improves decoding throughput by up to 50% over a reproduced synchronous baseline across multiple benchmarks. At matched target-model accuracy and 250 ms of added round-trip latency, the full system with the router delivers 1.95x and 1.55x the throughput of the same baseline on SPROUT and OlympiadBench, respectively, while sending only 47% and 39% of the queries to the cloud.
  </details>

- **[LLMs Can Design Near-Optimal OR Algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27296)**  `arXiv:2608.27296`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Jackie Baek_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We ask whether large language models (LLMs) can design effective algorithms for well-specified operations research (OR) problems. We study inventory control, queueing network control, and assortment optimization, using benchmark instances from recent papers in each area. We evaluate two levels of LLM use: at level 1, the model receives one problem instance and returns a solution for that instance; at level 2, it receives only the problem class description and broad parameter ranges, and returns an algorithm that maps instance parameters to solutions. Human input is minimal: we give one untuned prompt that describes the problem, and the model has access to a Python sandbox tool with a fixed compute budget.The strongest model we test, gpt-5.6-sol, matches or outperforms the best method reported in those papers on almost all evaluated instances. This holds even at level 2, where the returned algorithm is fixed before seeing the evaluation instances. Performance also improves sharply across models released less than eight months apart, suggesting that this capability is moving quickly. Thus, for the well-specified operations problems we study, a single untuned LLM query can already produce algorithms competitive with specialized methods. These results suggest that frontier LLMs can be a serious empirical baseline for algorithm design in well-specified OR problems.
  </details>

- **[SkillBoost: A Constrained Exploration-Exploitation Process for Mitigating Skill Overfitting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26643)**  `arXiv:2607.26643`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Hongqiang Lin, Chao Liu, Xiaofan Bai, Xuan Jin, Yuhong Li, Nenggan Zheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Enabling large language model (LLM) agents to accumulate and reuse experience from past interactions remains a central challenge in real-world applications. A promising solution is to treat skills as trainable states and optimize them in the same way as model parameters in neural network training. However, data-driven skill optimization is prone to overfitting to the limited trajectories collected from real environments. Overexploiting these trajectories overfits the current batch, while unconstrained exploration causes regression on previously solved cases. This tension motivates a constrained search view of skill self-evolution, governed by an exploration--exploitation trade-off. We propose SkillBoost, a three-stage framework that mitigates both risks: structured exploitation localizes observed failures to editable skill components, prior-guided exploration draws on prior knowledge in the LLM to generate diverse repair candidates, and verified acceptance commits a candidate only when it improves performance within a regression bound. Experiments across 23 model--benchmark configurations show that SkillBoost achieves state-of-the-art performance while mitigating overfitting, outperforming both human-crafted and LLM-generated skills. Transfer experiments further show that optimized skills can be reused by other agents on similar tasks. The code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[AgentGA: Evolving Code Solutions in Agent-Seed Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.14655)**  `arXiv:2604.14655`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _David Y.Y. Tan, Kellie Chin, Jingxian Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present AgentGA, a framework that evolves autonomous code-generation runs by optimizing the agent seed: the task prompt plus optional parent archives that initialize a fresh workspace. The outer loop searches over reusable starting conditions rather than editing code directly. Each generation launches a fresh autonomous run in an isolated workspace, while selected parent archives provide inherited artifacts that descendants can inspect and reuse. AgentGA couples a population-level genetic algorithm with long-horizon agents, deterministic 1:1 elite tournaments, and online operator allocation through a modified Hedge controller. We evaluate the approach for tabular AutoML on the 16-competition Weco-Kaggle Lite benchmark, including model-matched AIDE and AutoGluon comparisons. AgentGA with DeepSeek V4 Flash (0731) achieves 71.13% mean Exceeds % of Human, compared with 57.61% for model-matched AIDE and 57.89% for AutoGluon, outranking AIDE on 14/16 tasks. A control that removes inheritance, in which every agent starts from scratch, reaches 65.76% under AgentGA's stopping rule and 67.25% at the full budget, above a single long-lived agent (61.20%) and below AgentGA. These descriptive results support agent-seed optimization as a practical design choice for autonomous code-search systems, without isolating the causal contribution of inheritance.
  </details>

- **[ReST-RL: Reinforcing LLM Reasoning through Unified Self-Training and Value-Guided Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.19576)**  `arXiv:2508.19576`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Sining Zhoubian, Dan Zhang, Jie Tang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  With respect to improving the reasoning accuracy of LLMs, the representative reinforcement learning (RL) method - Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) - has achieved critical success, yet it still suffers from the issue of insignificant reward signals. This paper introduces ReST-RL, a unified Reinforced Self-Training (ReST) policy-value framework that reconnects policy optimization and value-guided search to improve LLM reasoning ability. Firstly, ReST-GRPO adopts an optimized ReST-style algorithm to reshape the policy-induced trajectory distribution by increasing the reward variance of GRPO sampling and exposing the policy to more informative partial states, thereby improving training efficiency and effectiveness. Then, we further introduce a decoding optimization method, VM-MCTS, which trains a Value Model (VM) from self-collected Monte-Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) targets and deploys it through an adapted MCTS algorithm to provide precise process signals and verification scores, further enhancing reasoning accuracy. These two stages are internally dependent - ReST-GRPO yields higher quality trajectories for value learning with VM-MCTS, which in turn enables more effective inference-time search. We validate our framework on multiple coding benchmarks (e.g., APPS, BigCodeBench, and HumanEval), where it significantly outperforms other reinforcement training baselines (naive GRPO, DAPO, and ReST-DPO), as well as decoding and verification baselines (e.g., PRM-BoN and ORM-MCTS), indicating its power to strengthen LLM reasoning capability. Moreover, we further evaluate ReST-RL on out-of-domain math and science reasoning tasks, where it achieves improved performance without target-domain tuning and favorable end-to-end efficiency trade-offs, providing preliminary transfer evidence beyond our primary coding domain.
  </details>

- **[CivilBench: Benchmarking Vision-Language Models for Multimodal Information Integration in Civil Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21105)**  `arXiv:2611.21105`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Dan Yuan, Yue Wen, Zhili He, Zhenkui Zhou, Shicen Liu, Ziqiang Zheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Artificial intelligence (AI), particularly large language models (LLMs) and vision-language models (VLMs), is increasingly applied in civil and structural engineering for disaster assessment, structural analysis, and engineering decision support. However, the extent to which current VLMs possess the reasoning capabilities required for reliable engineering judgments remains unclear. In this work, we introduce CivilBench, a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating VLMs in civil engineering and disaster-related scenarios. CivilBench covers three major task families: Sequential Reasoning, Image-based Structural Understanding, and Open-world Understanding, encompassing numerical reasoning, structural damage assessment, risk inference, causal analysis, and repair planning. The benchmark is constructed through a systematic pipeline of domain-specific data collection, engineering-oriented captioning, expert annotation, question design, and quality verification. Evaluation of representative state-of-the-art VLMs reveals substantial limitations in domain-specific reasoning, with Sequential Reasoning emerging as a major bottleneck, particularly when models must infer numerical relationships from sparse structured observations. Further experiments show that numerical priors and external knowledge can alleviate these limitations, although their effectiveness depends on the model's ability to reliably interpret and use the available evidence. CivilBench provides a capability-oriented testbed for identifying reasoning limitations and evaluating progress toward reliable AI-assisted engineering decision-making. The benchmark will be publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[MindTopo: Can Foundation Models Reason in Topological Space?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11900)**  `arXiv:2609.11900`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Yunfei Ge, Anbang Liu, Qineng Wang, Johnalbert Garnica, Jianwen Lyu, Zihan Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spatial reasoning depends not only on metric properties such as distance, angle, and shape, but also on topological relations that remain invariant under continuous deformation. Cognitive science identifies these relations as foundational to spatial understanding, yet foundation-model evaluations largely focus on metric or viewpoint-dependent relations. We introduce MindTopo, a benchmark of topological intuition across five properties grounded in cognitive science and formal topology, namely continuity, separation, order, enclosure, and knots. MindTopo evaluates each property at two cognitive levels. Reasoning asks a model to identify topological relations or infer how they change. Planning instantiates a foundation model as a closed-loop agent whose policy selects environment actions. MindTopo provides a Core Set of 3,900 instances across 13 procedurally generated task types, split into an Easy and a Hard level, and an Extended Set of 11,035 original instances. On the Core Set, we benchmark 13 multimodal large language models (MLLMs) and study agent configurations augmented with image and video generation, covering seven video generative models in planning settings. Every MLLM performs better on reasoning than on planning. On the Easy level, where human scores are available, the strongest model trails humans in both reasoning and planning. On Qwen3-VL-2B-Instruct, supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning improve reasoning more than planning. Generated observations retain local cues and reach solved endpoints, yet none of the 1,890 evaluated video rollouts passes all process-validity checks.
  </details>

- **[LatentRouter: Can We Choose the Right Multimodal Large Language Model Before Seeing Its Answer?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11301)**  `arXiv:2605.11301`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Xueqi Cheng, Yushun Dong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have heterogeneous strengths across OCR, chart understanding, spatial reasoning, visual question answering, cost, and latency. Effective MLLM routing therefore requires more than estimating query difficulty: a router must match the multimodal requirements of the current image-question input with the capabilities of each candidate model. We propose LatentRouter, a router that formulates MLLM routing as counterfactual multimodal utility prediction. Given an image-question query, LatentRouter extracts learned multimodal routing capsules, represents each candidate MLLM with a model capability token, and performs latent communication between these states to estimate how each model would perform if selected. A distributional outcome head predicts model-specific counterfactual quality, while a bounded capsule correction refines close decisions without allowing residual signals to dominate the prediction. The resulting utility-based policy supports performance-oriented and performance-cost routing, and handles changing candidate pools through shared per-model scoring with availability masking. Experiments on MMR-Bench and VL-RouterBench show that LatentRouter outperforms fixed-model, feature-level, and learned-router baselines. Additional analyses show that the gains are strongest on multimodal task groups where model choice depends on visual, layout-sensitive, or reasoning-oriented requirements, and that latent communication is the main contributor to the improvement. The code is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Jev-as-a-Reward: Validating Probabilistic Safety Judges for Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20955)**  `arXiv:2611.20955`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Eunkyeong Lee, Junhyung Moon, Seunggwan Hong, Dongsuk Oh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Probabilistic safety judges return the probability of a safety label and cheaply score reinforcement learning rollouts. A confidence gate sends only uncertain verdicts to a stronger LLM judge, which leaves confidently passed harm in a blind spot. Policies can exploit this blind spot, and commercial judges release new versions. We propose a protocol that validates such a judge before and during training, separates the share of confidently passed harm from its amount, and re-validates it after each judge change. We apply it to two versions of Jev, a commercial decision-only judge, and to an open guard classifier. Under the probability reward of Jev, passed harm shrinks in all 11 runs under the primary reference, mostly more slowly than caught harm, and concentrates in the blind spot. Binarizing this judge meets the escape criterion on Llama, and the open guard classifier shows an escape signal in one seed under a second reference. Guard classifiers share this blind spot, and even frontier LLM judges catch at most one of 10 human-labeled confident misses. Without a trained reward model, the probability reward with a helpfulness term lowers HarmBench attack success on Llama-3.1-8B from 0.287 to at most 0.033 at the final checkpoint.
  </details>

- **[DetourBench: Trajectory-Level Evaluation of Interactive Agents with Exact Minimum-Action Bounds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20844)**  `arXiv:2611.20844`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Hanlin Tian, Sihan Zhu, Zhao Yang, Yuxiang Wang, Zhiquan Hu, Yu Mi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Benchmarks judge language-model agents mainly by whether they reach the correct final state, not by how they got there. Endpoint scoring cannot separate an agent that retrieved exactly the evidence a decision required from one that read most of the environment, nor an agent that never located the evidence from one that stopped a single action short. Settling this needs a reference cost, and authored solutions establish only that a task is solvable, not that no shorter route exists.We present DetourBench, which constrains the environment until that minimum is computable. Each task is a finite, partially observable transition system with unit-cost accesses whose resources become globally addressable after discovery. Exhaustive search yields the full-information minimum action count $L_0$ with a replayable witness; from graph-recoverable terminal states it also yields exact residual work $R_T$, which separates an episode that stopped one access short from one that wandered, while an irreversible wrong commitment is recorded as having no finite residual distance rather than assigned one. Success, attainment, residual progress, and normalized detour are reported separately. Across 375 scored episodes --- five model endpoints, 25 integrated tasks, three repeats --- success ranges from 58.7% to 96.0% while conditional attainment ranges from 0.803 to 0.890 and orders the models differently. Among recoverable failures, residual work separates near-complete trajectories from costly incompletion and from irreversible commitment. We open-source the instances, oracle witnesses, and evaluation harness atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Objectify, Don't Delete: Complaint-Derived Evaluation of Ambient Clinical Notes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19525)**  `arXiv:2611.19525`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Nikita Gupta, Narayanan Unny Edakunni, Vikram Okaly, Suranjana Datta, Navya Penubadi, Karl Eric Swanson, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating the quality of AI-generated clinical notes is hard: standard metrics like ROUGE measure lexical similarity to reference notes, while expert checklists encode what committees believe matters. Neither captures what clinicians actually complain about. Recent work has shown that evaluation criteria can be mined from user feedback and filtered to criteria that LLM judges can reliably enforce. We argue that this is where much of the value is lost. The criteria that fail enforceability are disproportionately the ones clinicians raise most often, because high-frequency complaints are stated as several questions glued together. No yes/no judgment can grade such composites as posed. We introduce an objectification step that rewrites such criteria into sets of scoped, determinate decisions. We demonstrate the approach through an evaluation pipeline that mines criteria from complaints, objectifies them, and deploys LLM judges against both planted defects and production notes. Across eleven defects planted into ACI-bench reference notes, objectified judges localize violations with median recall of 0.95, while reference metrics change by at most 0.037. Given the complaint as one broad question, the same judges still find every planted defect, but they also flag 70% of the unperturbed reference notes; one section-pair question per call raises Youden's J from 0.30 to 0.72 (by 0.19 to 0.26 with prompt effort matched). We release ACI-Defect, a perturbation benchmark; clinician-reviewed instance-level labels showing quality gaps in the ACI-bench gold standard (32 of 40 notes contain hallucinations; 40 of 40 omit discussed facts); and grounding-judge verdicts. On 1,446 production notes, mean instance-rate burden falls at every clinician-rating step (rho=-0.15), a small association. Defect counts have to be kept per span rather than collapsed into one pass/fail bit per note.
  </details>

- **[Large Language Models for AI Infrastructure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19061)**  `arXiv:2611.19061`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Bolin Shen, Shaoyi Huang, Yushun Dong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to design, optimize, and operate the infrastructure behind AI training and serving, yet the literature remains fragmented across hardware design, machine learning systems, electronic design automation, and high-performance computing. We present a comprehensive survey of LLM-assisted AI infrastructure across its lifecycle, from accelerator design and kernel engineering to compilation, deployment, operations, data management, and energy optimization. We organize the literature by the primary resource the LLM manages: artifact-centric methods for computational artifacts, operational-centric methods for deployment context and system state, and resource-centric methods for data and energy. These categories span the lifecycle and cover AI-specific concerns such as distributed-training configuration, inference serving, GPU-cluster scheduling, and data loading. Building on this taxonomy, we conduct a cross-study quantitative synthesis of reported results. Because studies adopt heterogeneous benchmarks, baselines, hardware, and metrics, we compare methods only where evaluation conditions permit and align results along common dimensions: functional correctness, execution performance, throughput, resource and energy consumption, operational outcomes, and optimization cost, keeping each result's native metric, baseline, and setting rather than collapsing them into a single score. This comparison reveals where LLM-driven methods yield consistent gains, how reported gains in workload orchestration depend on the evaluation environment, from simulators to real clusters and production, and where evidence is still lacking. Together, the taxonomy, lifecycle perspective, and quantitative evaluation provide an evidence-grounded reference for LLM-driven engineering of AI infrastructure.
  </details>

- **[Infrathon: Benchmarking Language Agents for Real-World, Long-Horizon AI Infra Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11995)**  `arXiv:2611.11995`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Zhengyu Hu, Zheyuan Xiao, Yike Wang, Hamish Ivison, Haoyu Wang, Yige Yuan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this work, we study whether frontier agents can continually improve real-world AI infrastructure over long horizons. We introduce Infrathon, a benchmark of diverse, long-horizon AI infrastructure optimization tasks across different levels. We evaluate six models across 150 runs with 24-hour budgets, totaling over 10,000 GPU-hours. Our results show that model rankings vary with the evaluation horizon, while 135 of the 150 runs achieve further improvements during the second half of their budgets. These findings indicate that shorter evaluations can overlook both optimization gains and changes in relative model performance. We also find agents can discover solutions that outperform human-designed baselines: Claude Opus 5.5 increases the mean KV-cache compression ratio from $6.82\times$ with the human-designed Quant-VideoGen codec to $19.86\times$. Analysis of agent trajectories identifies task violations, unreliable testing, and premature completion as barriers to sustained improvement. Together, our Infrathon framework provides resources for constructing long-horizon tasks, evaluating frontier agents, and discovering good infrastructure solutions. Code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[SmartThinker: Learning High-Density Reasoning via Step-Level Token Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.04348)**  `arXiv:2507.04348`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Xingyang He, Xiao Ling, Jie Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large reasoning models (LRMs) have exhibited remarkable reasoning capabilities through inference-time scaling, but this progress has also introduced considerable redundancy and inefficiency into their reasoning processes, resulting in substantial computational waste. Previous work has attempted to mitigate this issue by penalizing the overall length of generated samples during reinforcement learning (RL), with the goal of encouraging a more concise chains of Efficient reasoning is critical for large reasoning models (LRMs) deployed in agent systems with strict context constraints. Existing methods predominantly rely on global length penalties that overlook step-level structure, inadvertently compressing critical derivations while retaining low-value detours. Hence, we argue for a principled, step-aware alternative that preserves critical steps and compresses low-value detours. However, it raises three fundamental challenges: the difficulty of obtaining step-level supervision, the need for reward signals that induce differentiated length pressure, and the requirement to balance training stability with exploration on complex problems. We propose SmartThinker, a step-level token allocation framework that learns to selectively preserve or compress individual reasoning steps. At its core is Step-Level Length Control Policy Optimization (SCPO), which provides label-free feedback by online estimation of each step's contribution from the change in correct-answer likelihood after removing it. We further introduce reasoning density, defined as contribution per token, to relax length pressure on high-value steps while intensifying it on low-density ones. Finally, SCPO stabilizes training through length-bias-corrected advantages and difficulty-adaptive clipping, achieving superior accuracy-efficiency trade-offs across nine in-domain and out-of-domain benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Learning to Reason with Predictive Gains for Generative Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21441)**  `arXiv:2611.21441`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhuoyang Li, Teng Shi, Jiakai Tang, Weijie Yu, Guofu Xie, Xiao Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reasoning-enhanced generative recommendation (GR) has attracted increasing attention for leveraging the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) to improve next-item prediction. However, existing methods primarily optimize reasoning through Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) with rule-based rewards determined by final recommendation performance, which faces two key limitations: (i) unmeasured predictive contributions within reasoning, where the predictive contributions of individual reasoning steps are not directly reflected in optimization signals, and (ii) weakly discriminative learning signals within groups, where different reasoning trajectories within the group often receive indistinguishable rewards, limiting effective policy optimization. In this paper, we propose GainReason, a simple yet effective training framework that optimizes reasoning through Predictive Gain (PG). Specifically, PG measures changes in target Semantic ID (SID) log-likelihood induced by individual reasoning sentences, providing contribution-aware and discriminative rewards. We further theoretically establish the alignment between expected PG and the recommendation ranking objective. Building on this signal, we develop PG-based Reasoning Optimization (PGRO) to optimize reasoning generation with sentence-level rewards, supported by a custom causal attention mechanism for efficient PG computation. Additionally, an auxiliary PG-based Reasoning Utilization (PGRU) task adaptively strengthens reasoning-conditioned prediction to complement PGRO. Extensive experiments on three public benchmarks demonstrate that GainReason consistently outperforms competitive baselines and achieves substantial performance gains from reasoning.
  </details>

- **[VisualArtifactSpec 1.0: Evaluating Visual Artifacts under Task Specifications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21382)**  `arXiv:2611.21382`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shuyi Ouyang, Xiaojun Tang, Zijian Zou, Xintong Zhang, Mingli Song, Guannan Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual artifact tasks test agents' ability to translate task specifications and source materials into outputs that meet practical needs. Evaluation across artifact families helps distinguish broadly applicable authoring capabilities from strengths confined to a particular medium. We introduce VisualArtifactSpec 1.0 (VAS), a benchmark of 211 creation and modification tasks spanning presentations, static graphics, animations and videos, and web interfaces. Each task specifies authoring objectives, source materials, constraints, and deliverables. Existing benchmarks provide limited support for systematically evaluating how authoring choices suit different audiences, purposes, and media across artifact families. VAS evaluates two aspects separately: Task Fulfillment verifies explicit requirements and applicable functionality, while Artifact Performance assesses Content, Organization, Visuals, and Delivery. Task-specific rating anchors instantiate shared criteria for the intended audience, purpose, and medium, with judgments grounded in source materials and artifact inspection records. We evaluate nine model-harness configurations on all 211 tasks. Overall scores range from 42.12 to 70.52, with Artifact Performance accounting for 73.4% of the gap between the highest- and lowest-scoring configurations.
  </details>

- **[Benchleaking Is Accelerating: Benchmark contamination has worsened sharply in the past month -- from post-training down to pre-training base models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21315)**  `arXiv:2611.21315`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yiwei Qin, Muhang Xie, Xuefeng Li, Mohan Jiang, Dayuan Fu, Tiantian Mi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Benchmark contamination is not new, but recent model releases show it in a sharper form. We use the term benchleaking for the case in which a benchmark's own test examples enter training data, so that a score reflects recall rather than capability. This differs from benchmaxxing, which optimizes toward a benchmark without seeing its test set. We document two escalations over the past month. First, in post-training, the traces have become explicit: recent audits show an agent stating hidden test requirements it was never given in 23% of its attempts, where its predecessor did so in none, and continuing to follow them after the task is changed to contradict them (Schaeffer and Räuker, 2026; Lo and Lo, 2026). Second, the problem has reached pre-training. Using few-shot continuation on 20 benchmarks across four public base models, we find that some base models write out other test examples---complete questions, options, and answers---that appear nowhere in their input. For the recently released ERNIE-5.5-Flash-Base, 96% of HumanEval problems leak with their reference implementations and 85% of OpenBookQA questions leak with their answers, against 9% and none for DeepSeek-V4-Flash-Base. To quantify what a reported score owes to such examples, we define an Anti-Hack score: the benchmark score recomputed with every leaked example marked wrong and the number of examples unchanged. Averaged over the suite, it costs DeepSeek-V4-Flash-Base 1.5 points and Hy3-preview-Base 1.3, against 5.0 for MiMo-V2.5-Base and 16.6 for ERNIE-5.5-Flash-Base. Open base models are the foundation of much downstream research, so benchleaking now affects more than leaderboard rankings. We call for decontamination reports and contamination-adjusted scores to become standard practice.
  </details>

- **[Turing Agent: Agentic Formal Verification for Mathematical Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21299)**  `arXiv:2611.21299`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yang Chen, Qianyi Wang, Ronghui Yang, Zhanhao Li, Yu Cong, Chen Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are rapidly advancing mathematical discovery, moving beyond benchmark-level problem solving to tackle frontier research problems. This progress is ushering mathematics into an era of proof abundance, raising a fundamental challenge: how can we ensure the correctness of the ever-growing volume of AI-generated proofs and transform them into trustworthy, machine-verified mathematical knowledge? We introduce Turing Agent, an agent harness designed to orchestrate end-to-end formal verification of research-level mathematics. Its wo-scale architecture integrates theorem formalization and proof formalization agents, empowered by structured proof decomposition, parallel verification, accumulated proof experience, and selective semantic review. Leveraging our collaborations with theoretical computer science (TCS) researchers, we focus our evaluation on TCS, a particularly demanding domain characterized by intricate quantification, complex dependencies, and stringent semantic requirements. Against direct generation and general-purpose coding harnesses, including Codex CLI and Claude Code, Turing Agent achieves approximately 57-62% faithful formalization success on Formal TCS Bench and 83% overall pass@1 on a TCS-focused subset of RLMEval, using GLM-5.3 and DeepSeek-V4-Pro. Notably, Turing Agent, built on DeepSeek, successfully completes the formal verification of three previously unresolved TCS tasks from OpenAI's October 2026 collection. One particularly challenging TCS problem required about a hundred formally verified auxiliary claims. Moreover, by actively exploring and refining the formalization process, Turing Agent can uncover simpler alternative proofs, expose unnecessary dependencies in original proofs, and generate reusable mathematical knowledge as a byproduct of verification.
  </details>

- **[CAE-Bench: Benchmarking AI Agents for Reusable CAE Solver Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21273)**  `arXiv:2611.21273`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhiyu Zhou, Tianyou Liu, Xinping Yang, Yiyao Liu, Jiayi Dong, Yihan Lin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We developed CAE-Bench, a benchmark to evaluate AI agents on Computer-Aided Engineering (CAE) solver development. A CAE solver is more than a PDE solver; it must be able to generalize across various inputs, such as different boundary conditions, geometries, and material properties. CAE-Bench evaluates the generalizability of AI-developed CAE solvers by providing the same task, such as a heat transfer problem, multiple cases, with each case specifying an input. The AI agent develops a solver for a fixed task using only a few public cases, and the resulting solver is then tested on held-out cases, i.e., cases it has never seen before. We evaluate 12 models across a range of reasoning settings on 80 computational tasks. Our experiments show that even if a solver developed by an AI agent performs well on the public cases, it may even get 0 score on held-out cases. For tasks such as fluid dynamics problems, even the solver developed by GPT-6 Astra performs poorly on held-out cases. A data format and an SDK for task registration are provided, so the researcher can use the benchmark on their own infrastructure. We hope that CAE-Bench will help the community to develop AI agents that can develop reusable CAE solvers that generalize to various inputs.
  </details>

- **[InclusionMed: Benchmarking AI for Inclusive Healthcare](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21218)**  `arXiv:2611.21218`  `cs.AI`  
  _InclusionMed Collaboration_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Advancing medical AI for inclusive healthcare requires evaluation across diverse healthcare roles, tasks, and care settings, but benchmark coverage remains uneven. We introduce InclusionMed, an open medical AI evaluation initiative for inclusive healthcare. The InclusionMed Atlas organizes benchmarks across three healthcare roles and three AI capabilities.
