For the first release, we selected four public benchmarks from 388 candidates through structured audits and independent reproduction. With InclusionMed-DxFiles, our private diagnostic reasoning benchmark, the Atlas contains five benchmarks covering three of its nine role--capability cells. We evaluated frontier large language models on the Atlas and aggregated their results into the InclusionMed Index, with overall scores ranging from 17.5 to 49.1 out of 100. The Index also reports scores by healthcare role and AI capability, showing that a model's rank varied with the role or capability considered. A within-benchmark analysis using task-level tags showed that the ten highest-ranked models in the Index all scored higher on patient communication than on clinical decision-making. Models with similar Index scores also differed substantially in estimated inference cost. These findings highlight the need for evaluation covering more diverse healthcare needs and for context-specific model selection. Through open collaboration and community contributions, InclusionMed seeks to ensure that diverse healthcare needs worldwide shape how medical AI progress is defined and measured.
  </details>

- **[Sage S2.1 Flash Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21072)**  `arXiv:2611.21072`  `cs.AI`  
  _Kun Cai, Haodong Chen, Guangran Cheng, Qingxiu Cheng, Xu Dai, Guanting Dong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce Sage S1.5 Flash, a 320-billion-parameter general-purpose mixture-of-experts model designed to reason over scientific evidence and act in interactive environments. Sage Sci-Perception uses dedicated encoders for molecular graphs and biological sequences and represents spectra as ordered patches of measurements. Modality-specific projectors map these representations into the input space of a shared backbone, which processes them alongside text and images. To develop the model's agent capabilities, we construct verifiable environments for tool use, search, and coding, as well as specialized workflows involving machine learning experiments and scientific software. Task completion is checked against execution results, changes in environment state, or submitted artifacts. We evaluate Sage S1.5 Flash on 33 benchmarks across seven domains. In the primary comparison against five baselines, it ranks first on OfficeQA Pro (72.2), MLE-bench Lite (90.3 HumanRank), Harvey LAB, and SpreadsheetBench~2, and second on OSWorld-Verified (85.2), WebArena-Verified (67.6), and DeepResearch Bench~II (54.2). These results show particular strengths in using documentary evidence, producing verifiable workbooks, and carrying out machine learning tasks, alongside competitive performance in desktop and browser workflows that require the model to act on visual feedback.
  </details>

- **[K2-Horizon: A Frontier Fleet of Open Source Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21003)**  `arXiv:2611.21003`  `cs.AI`  
  _K2 Team, Zhengzhong Liu, Haonan Li, Xuezhe Ma, Alexander Moreno, Subham Sekhar Sahoo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present K2-Horizon, a fleet of six fully open language models, from a 0.9B dense model to a 375B-A23B MoE, built for general-purpose reasoning and agentic work across math and science, software engineering, deep research, office automation, and beyond. The models lead or are competitive with strong open models of their size on agentic and coding benchmarks. On the modeling side, to strengthen reasoning, we introduce Mixture-of-Value Attention (MoVA), applying MoE to attention value projections, and softplus attention gating, a more flexible, theoretically motivated gate. Our 36B-A4B model uses both and nearly matches a 32B dense model trained on the same data, with one-seventh the active parameters. We also introduce Uno, lossless diffusion adapters that speed up decoding by up to 2.2x without a separate draft model. Our release follows the LLM360 definition of open source for transparency and reproducibility: the full development history, including intermediate checkpoints from every training stage, pre-training data, training logs, and all training, evaluation, and infrastructure code, notably our production-scale xLLM framework for dense and MoE training and the RL360 reinforcement-learning stack. Beyond the data itself, we release and document in detail every pipeline that built it, the part of model development that open releases most rarely disclose. These pipelines produced roughly 10T synthetic pre-training tokens, showing that synthetic data can be a major ingredient of pre-training at scale. Four of the models share one 22T-token pre-training corpus and recipe, revealing consistent scaling behavior and enabling controlled studies of scale and sparsity. All artifacts are available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Razavi-Bench: Tracking AI Progress in Analog Circuit Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20935)**  `arXiv:2611.20935`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhishuai Zhang, Oscar Toomey, Taekwang Jang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We propose Razavi-Bench, a benchmark built from the 50 questions in Razavi's two Analog Design Experiments With AI articles and graded by GPT-6 Luna against reference answers and rubrics, and use it to track how fast AI models improve at analog circuit analysis. Across 73 multimodal large language models released between May 2024 and November 2026, the top score rose from 28.83% to 98.33% in 30 months, mostly before the reference answers were published. The leading models now answer almost every textbook question correctly; the hardest questions show a circuit that resembles a textbook one but works differently, or ask about behavior the obvious analysis misses. Within two and a half years, a set of questions that exposed the limits of AI models has become one they answer almost perfectly.
  </details>

- **[Multi-LLM Agentic AI at the Edge: Joint Agent Scheduling and Resource Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20915)**  `arXiv:2611.20915`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yuanyan Song, Kezhi Wang, Xinmian Xu, Yongmin Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this paper, an edge-enabled multi-large language model (LLM) agentic AI architecture is proposed, where one supervisor AI agent and multiple worker AI agents cooperatively execute users' requests at the edge. The supervisor AI agent decouples the user request into a series of sub-tasks and dynamically dispatches them to LLM-equipped worker AI agents via two agentic workflows, i.e., parallel and serial workflows, with a two-level retry mechanism to enhance execution reliability. To prolong the service duration of worker AI agents, the energy minimization problems are formulated by jointly considering AI agent selection and resource allocation, subject to task execution latency and quality of service (QoS) constraints. A resource allocation scheme based on alternating optimization (AO) is proposed. Specifically, for the parallel workflow, the scheme decouples the original problem into bandwidth allocation, retrieval selection and AI agent selection sub-problems, which are iteratively solved by the proposed successive convex approximation (SCA), Lagrangian relaxation (LR), and Gale-Shapley (GS) or LLM-based algorithms, respectively. For the serial workflow, the problem is similarly decoupled into service time allocation, retrieval selection and AI agent selection sub-problems; the optimal service time allocation is obtained via Karush-Kuhn-Tucker (KKT) conditions, while the latter two sub-problems are addressed by extending the proposed algorithms to the serial scenario. Simulation results show that the proposed resource allocation scheme can reduce system energy consumption compared with other benchmarks, while experiments on a multi-LLM agentic AI system based on Qwen and Llama demonstrate improved task execution success rates.
  </details>

- **[Accelerate Bench: Evaluating Agents for Application-Level Program Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20882)**  `arXiv:2611.20882`  `cs.AI`  
  _Bowen Liu, Yuhan Li, Nuo Chen, Weile Luo, Miao Peng, Qintong Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Application optimization requires coding agents to discover useful changes and coordinate them across a complete program while preserving the required computation. We introduce Accelerate Bench, a benchmark of 32 CPU optimization tasks adapted from scientific applications and supercomputing competitions. Each task provides a working program and specifies the workload, correctness requirements, timing boundaries, and permitted edits, leaving agents to choose where and how to optimize. Public testing supports iterative development, and a separate verifier scores the final submission. On benchmark version 1.1, we evaluate 16 coding agents, each pairing a model with a harness. Their Score@1 ranges from 25.5% to 67.4%. Most of their programs are correct, and the size of the speedup separates them: the two leading agents are at least 2x faster on 22 or more of the 32 tasks, while the other fourteen reach 2x on 8 to 17 and leave 125 of their 412 correct outcomes below 1.25x. Controlled replays show why coordination across an application matters: identical source code runs between 0.29x and 2.12x depending only on build and runtime settings, and one code change is worth 1.01x or 6.4x depending on another. Accelerate Bench pairs executable workloads with agents' programs and trajectories to evaluate both their gains and the process behind them.
  </details>

- **[Routing Beyond Task Categories: A Cost-Aware LAB-Bench Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20732)**  `arXiv:2611.20732`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ahmed Tariq, Leo Guinan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We tested whether features of biology questions or of a model's first answer can improve accuracy within an inference budget. In a LAB-Bench pilot, we compared a category router with one that adds six structural features. Neither reached the primary 80% accuracy target on development. At the 50% and 60% secondary targets, both chose Haiku for all 120 held-out questions and scored 71/120. We then froze a new comparison before collecting responses on 144 additional questions. Semantic routing scored 86/144. A policy that could defer after reading Haiku's answer scored 87/144. Both stayed within the inference budget of USD 0.678 per 1,000 queries. Against a 95% Haiku and 5% GPT mixture, the accuracy gains were 0.66 percentage points for semantic routing and 1.35 points for deferral. The paired 95% intervals were [-0.28,2.25] and [-0.14,3.47] points. A simple rule that escalated Haiku nonanswers scored 92/144 at USD 0.710 per 1,000 queries, above the budget. A retrospective nonanswer rule with a calibration-selected escalation probability gave an expected 85.63/144. At the learned deferral policy's observed inference cost, post hoc interpolation gave an expected 86.14/144. In a later development check, Astra and Gemini each scored 30/40. The best tested policy in each router family matched that score. The pair covered 33/40 in hindsight. The routers missed the 32/40 criterion, so the proposed 120-question evaluation did not start.
  </details>

- **[GEAR$^2$: Pruned Experts Live On through Gated Reuse of Activated Experts and a Missing-Mass Residual](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20616)**  `arXiv:2611.20616`  `cs.AI`  
  _Linliang Chen, Yan Zhong, Wang Kang, Sai Li, Ji Wu, Ji Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) Large Language Models (LLMs) activate only a few experts per token while generally keeping all the experts in GPU memory for high efficiency. Expert pruning reduces this GPU memory footprint cost, yet discards the contribution of pruned experts. Existing remedies either rely on static substitutes, merge experts at the cost of altering their functions, or require costly recovery training. In this paper, we find that the functionalities of pruned experts can be efficiently reconstructed from the activated remaining experts. We propose GEAR (Gated Expert Activation Reuse), which reuses the scaled outputs of reliable activated experts without any training or additional expert computation. In addition, we propose GEAR$^2$ to further recover what reuse cannot express with an ultra-lightweight residual. On five MoE LLMs from 16B to 106B parameters with 25% and 50% of experts pruned, GEAR improves over its training-free counterparts in nearly every setting (up to 4 pp.) in terms of accuracy, and GEAR$^2$ outperforms baseline approaches (up to 2.75 pp.) in every setting on nine zero-shot benchmarks, with larger advantages (up to 2.72 pp. higher) under more aggressive pruning (up to 50%).
  </details>

- **[BiGSyn: A Bi-Level Heterogeneous Graph Contrastive Learning Framework for Interpretable Drug Synergy Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20603)**  `arXiv:2611.20603`  `cs.AI`  
  _Senquan Wang, Hui Fang, Haoran Nan, Xingwei Wang, Qiang He_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Drug combination therapy has become a mainstream of modern medicine, with the potential to enhance efficacy, reduce toxicity, and overcome drug resistance. However, identifying synergistic drug combinations remains challenging, as it requires modeling complex interactions across multiple biological scales---from atom-level molecular structures to entity-level relations in biological networks. Existing approaches typically model these levels in isolation, overlooking complementary signals and long-range dependencies. To address these limitations, we propose BiGSyn, a Bi-level heterogeneous Graph contrastive learning framework for drug Synergy prediction. Specifically, BiGSyn leverages heterogeneous graph transformers to extract high-order dependencies from molecular graphs and biological networks, and introduces a bi-level contrastive learning objective to bridge the cross-level semantic gap. Extensive experiments on two benchmark datasets show that BiGSyn consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines. Moreover, its attention mechanism highlights key molecular substructures and biological interactions driving synergy, providing interpretable insights.
  </details>

- **[ToolGraph as a Q-Function for Test-Time Scaling of Tool-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20569)**  `arXiv:2611.20569`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ke Shen, Ankita Bhaumik, Li Dong, Shengjie Liu, Shuzhen Li, Naveed Ahmed Janvekar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tool-use agents must chain interdependent calls, yet which outputs satisfy which later inputs in a deployed catalog is not something a general-purpose language model reliably infers from tool descriptions alone. We compile that structure once into a reusable external representation. ToolGraph is a directed, weighted dependency graph over a tool catalog: parameter matching and interventional probes, which execute a candidate chain with and without its proposed predecessor, propose edges whose evidence is combined into an edge confidence. Prior work uses such graphs to retrieve tools, search for tool paths, or score the next call. We ask instead whether the same structure can supply a \emph{multi-step} value by composing graph paths into a goal-conditioned action value \(Q(s,a,g)\). We study three estimators (dynamic programming over entity sets, a GraphSAGE estimator distilled from it, and an LLM scorer conditioned on graph evidence) plus a verbalizer that returns action--goal feedback to the agent, which keeps final action selection. Across two tool-use benchmarks and three backbones, we compare value ranking against personalized-PageRank retrieval over the identical graph, and against direct and reflective agents. Composition pays off only when the estimator also reads the concrete state: the LLM estimator improves on PageRank in every benchmark--backbone setting, by \(8.3\) points on average, while the two purely structural estimators fall below it, because a value defined over tool types cannot separate a correct tool called with a wrong argument. Graph construction is a one-time cost; we report it separately rather than assume it amortized away (at our scale it is comparable to request-time cost) and sweep four knobs controlling how much graph evidence to expose against success and tokens.
  </details>

- **[VAHBench: A Harness Benchmark for Agentic Long-Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20535)**  `arXiv:2611.20535`  `cs.AI`  
  _Hongbo Zhang, Liuyang Song, Hang Zhou, Xinchen Liu, Wei He, Kai Han, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI agents are commonly used for everyday information retrieval and coding. We ask whether they can also interpret human requests and turn them into complete videos. We introduce VAHBench, a benchmark that tests this ability through five short-drama briefs specifying story, characters, dialogue and duration. Agents plan and carry out production using shared generation tools. Evaluation combines video completeness and basic quality with checks of production reliability and reporting accuracy. Across 240 runs covering 48 configurations of general-purpose and video-specific agents, higher quality scores do not necessarily mean more complete delivery. Missing episodes, repeated footage and static filler reveal gaps between producing a video file and fulfilling the brief. Added composition tools help some agents but do not consistently improve performance. VAHBench measures how well agents translate user intent into finished video content and helps identify where that process breaks down.
  </details>

- **[Agent-Guided Configuration Tuning for SMT Solvers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20484)**  `arXiv:2611.20484`  `cs.AI`  
  _Cong Wang, Qiaoyi Zheng, Zhaoguo Wang, Fan Long_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  SMT solvers expose many configuration choices that determine how well their existing algorithms perform on a workload. We present, to our knowledge, the first workflow that uses general-purpose LLM agents to tune SMT solver configurations. Agents read solver documentation, source code, and persistent experiment records to propose option changes, and a deterministic harness measures each proposal, validates the returned models, and selects changes separately for each competition Division. Over roughly nine weeks, our tuning campaign recorded 4,545 proposals for cvc5 across 13 Divisions and 36 logics.We evaluate the tuned configurations on the 2026 and 2025 SMT-COMP ModelValidation benchmarks under a 24-second cutoff. On problems not used for training, they validate more problems than official cvc5 in ten of the 13 Divisions in 2026 and in eight in 2025, with geometric-mean gains of 7.8% and 6.8% across Divisions. On the complete official selections, which include training problems, the tuned floating-point counts exceed those of every individual competition participant in both years. Further studies show how configuration changes make existing algorithms effective by changing access to strategies, scheduling, and option compatibility, and how benchmark families trade off against one another. The agents' experiment traces contain hypothesis-based proposals in every tuning group, and 64.3% of the extracted hypotheses draw on prior measurements. These results show that agent-guided tuning can specialize a mature SMT solver and that accumulated experimental evidence informs sustained search.
  </details>

- **[The Logic Chain Breaking Point: A Controlled-Variable Benchmark for Reasoning Degradation in Open-Weight Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20447)**  `arXiv:2611.20447`  `cs.AI`  
  _Akif Islam, Mohd. Ruhul Ameen, Md. Ekramul Hamid_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce The Logic Chain Breaking Point, a controlled-variable benchmark for deductive reasoning in open-weight language models. It spans three phases: (i) pure modus-ponens chain scaling, (ii) distractor resilience with a fixed core chain, and (iii) contradiction detection under primacy versus recency placement. Independent variables follow a powers-of-two sequence $v \in \{1,2,4,8,16,32,64,128\}$, with ground truth known by construction. We evaluate sixteen open-weight models across instruction-tuned and reasoning-tuned panels, including a small-model suite and a larger local-model extension. All models are evaluated with greedy decoding under model-appropriate context and output budgets, while context- and length-limited trials are quarantined from the valid accuracy denominator. Our results reveal a sharply non-monotonic difficulty surface: pure deductive chains up to $L=128$ remain near saturation for most models, and distractor noise causes only model-specific degradation at high $D$, while the inconsistent premise condition has the lowest accuracy. Instruction-tuned models, including larger 24B--27B baselines, score essentially zero on inconsistent premise sets, while reasoning-tuned models recover only partially and remain below reliable accuracy. Within this benchmark, inconsistent premise sets are associated with the strongest degradation under the benchmark's abstention-based scoring rule.
  </details>

- **[FinVerify: A Verification Framework for Financial Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20423)**  `arXiv:2611.20423`  `cs.AI`  
  _Mao Guan, Qian Chen, Ge Meng, Xiao-Yang Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Financial agents produce numerical claims that can be wrong even when their calculations execute correctly, due to mismatched reporting periods, unsupported sources, incompatible units, or incorrect financial definitions. We introduce FinVerify, an evidence-grounded framework for verifying financial agents' numerical claims. Based on an analysis of 190 incorrect trajectories from the Agent Finance Reasoning dataset, we define five error categories--temporal integrity, source fidelity, comparability, accounting integrity, and financial-model fidelity--and operationalize them as verification protocols. An LLM structures claims and supporting evidence, while deterministic validators check financial requirements; when authoritative formulas are available, Z3 checks consistency with grounded inputs. FinVerify accepts only if all applicable checks pass, rejects proven violations, and abstains when evidence is insufficient. We evaluate the framework against a Direct baseline and an LLM judge across five fixed-candidate benchmarks. On Agent Finance Reasoning, accepted-claim precision increases from 56.4% for the LLM judge to 76.7% at 81.2% coverage. On XBRLFiling, FinVerify achieves 99.8% accepted-claim precision and 97.0% decision accuracy; on FinanceBench-67, it reaches 87.9% accepted-claim precision versus 69.7% for the judge. Improvements are smaller on FinQA and TAT-QA, where authoritative evidence is limited. These results suggest that explicit, evidence-backed verification can improve financial-agent reliability while exposing trade-offs between correctness, false rejection, and abstention.
  </details>

- **[Deploying a Control-Logic LLM Assistant in Air-Gapped Power Plants: Lessons in Source Authority and Bounded Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20398)**  `arXiv:2611.20398`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yiwei Fu, Nicholas Gabriel_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deploying Large Language Model (LLM) assistants in industrial operational technology environments poses constraints largely absent from cloud applications: site policies restrict engineering and operational data from leaving the plant, on-premises compute is limited, and incorrect causal attribution can mislead incident response. We report lessons from deploying a control-logic evidence layer that underpins a broader on-premises operator assistance system at two gas power plants. Two prototypes revealed where generative models added avoidable uncertainty and cost. The first used a Vision Language Model (VLM) to reconstruct control topology from PDF printouts routinely exchanged by engineers. Across the full 6,979-page document, extraction reached 0.51 connection precision and required 3.1 hours on 4xH200 GPUs; consultation with controls engineers then revealed a native structured export from the authoring system. The second used an LLM tool-calling agent to plan repeatedly over eight predefined graph operations. In a controlled benchmark of 40 template-generated questions, replacing this agent with explicit execution plans reduced mean token use by 20x and median latency by 3x, with identical retrieved node sets across repeated runs. The deployed design ingests the authoritative export into a versioned knowledge graph, executes bounded graph operations in fixed software, and confines the local LLM to fallback intent classification and evidence-grounded explanation. At one site, the graph contains 221K nodes across seven controllers. Our experience suggests a practical division of labor for high-consequence AI: LLMs interpret natural-language requests and explain results, while deterministic software constructs and retrieves technical evidence grounded in authoritative sources. Abstention, entity disambiguation, graph versioning, and read-only integration provide additional safeguards.
  </details>

- **[When Facts Change, Which Decisions Break? Dependency-Aware Context Graphs for Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19859)**  `arXiv:2611.19859`  `cs.AI`  
  _Paul Deepakraj Retinraj_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When an observed fact changes, an agent must identify and repair the decisions that depend on it while preserving unaffected outputs. We investigate the reliability-cost trade-off of field-level dependency graphs extracted from actual agent read traces. A controlled benchmark spans three executable workflow domains and four successive updates, with independent imperative and expression-based references. Across 120 workflows, two models and two repeated runs, we compare full recomputation, rolling summary memory, lexical retrieval, trace-extracted dependencies and verified dependency upper bounds. Trace-derived graphs reduce reviewed decisions and estimated total cost, but omitted alternative supports and missing repair context can reduce complete-plan correctness. Paired ablations isolate the effects of edge deletion, spurious edges, transitive propagation and context scope. An exploratory enhancement supplies the existing record ID/field directory during extraction without revealing unread values or reference edges. We evaluate this enhancement on 60 fresh workflows with two repeats per model. On this fresh set, the directory-aware graph matches observed full-context complete-plan correctness for gpt-6.1-sol (100%) with an estimated 4.0% total-cost reduction, but reaches only 84.8% versus 99.6% for gpt-6-luna. The results distinguish potential dependency impact from actual decision invalidity and show why selective repair must be evaluated with both executable correctness and full memory-processing cost. The evidence concerns explicit procedural workflows; broad real-world agent-memory generalization remains untested.
  </details>

- **[When AI Agents Should Repair, Replan, or Abandon: A Threshold Theory of Plan Commitment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19663)**  `arXiv:2611.19663`  `cs.AI`  
  _Gaurav Tewari_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI agents often discover mid-execution that their plan is broken, for example when an action fails or a tool is withdrawn. Deployed systems use conventions such as retry loops or "always replan," and a recent benchmark finds no fixed strategy dominates. This paper derives the optimal policy. In the model, a disruption destroys the causal support of part of a plan; repair is a partial reset that preserves the plan's structure, replanning is a full reset, and changing a plan is costly because others have conditioned on it. The optimal policy is a threshold in effective disruption depth: repair shallow damage, replan after deep damage, and abandon the goal when even a fresh plan is not worth its cost. Depth depends on plan structure. In expectation, a single broken action forces 1+R(G)/n actions to be revalidated, where R(G) counts the pairs of actions joined by a dependency path; to first order, a fully serial plan switches to replanning at raw damage about w times smaller than a plan of w parallel chains. A closed-form stability-price threshold determines when a worse repaired plan should be kept over a better fresh one. As language-model planners push replanning costs toward zero, the stability price becomes the only force sustaining plan commitment. Under repeated disruptions, revision frictions make commitment hysteresis optimal: the agent keeps executing a plan it would never choose fresh, and each repair adds patch debt until the debt outweighs repair's cost advantage and the agent rebuilds. A value-of-diagnosis result states when measuring damage is worth its compute and shows that "always replan" is optimal when damage is predictably deep and replanning is the better fallback. A separately implemented numerical checker tests each proposition (zero violations; 1.6 million static-model evaluations), and the model's regime map reproduces that benchmark's regime flips qualitatively.
  </details>

- **[Data-Driven Heuristic-Guided Business Process Redesign](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19623)**  `arXiv:2611.19623`  `cs.AI`  
  _Nan Sai, Karolin Winter, Remco Dijkman_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Business process redesign (BPR) has a long-standing catalog of best-practice redesign heuristics, yet, applying them remains a mostly manual task that requires extensive domain knowledge. Despite recent advances using Large Language Models (LLMs) for conversational process model redesign, limitations remain. For example, redesign is user-driven instead of data-driven and is not guided by best practices such as the redesign heuristics. Moreover, only one change can be handled at a time. To address these limitations, given an event log and a BPMN model, we present the DH-BPR framework to realize data-driven heuristic-guided BPR using LLMs. To design DH-BPR, we first conducted an analysis of which redesign heuristics can be automatically detected and applied given information contained in an event log such as the fraction of cases that bypass an activity. Based on this, DH-BPR detects and validates heuristic candidates through a combination of rule-based techniques and an LLM. After that, DH-BPR jointly designs and records an application plan for valid candidates, before finally yielding a redesigned BPMN model and an updated resource model (the way resources execute the process). Evaluation based on simulation using a synthetic benchmark and two real-world event logs shows that the framework's redesign reduces the mean case completion time by up to 61.5%.
  </details>

- **[Program Distillation: Transferring LLM Capability from System 2 to System 0](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19461)**  `arXiv:2611.19461`  `cs.AI`  
  _Pujun Zheng, Wanying Ren, Guoxiu He_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent studies leverage large language models (LLMs) to construct executable programs through environment interaction, feedback analysis, and code revision. However, prior work has mainly treated LLMs as optimizers, overlooking their ability to transfer knowledge across system levels. We propose Program Distillation (PD), a framework that formalizes the transfer of abilities from System 2 to System 0. During learning, the LLM provides task knowledge and draft strategies; at deployment, a student program executes the resulting policy. We show that this approach can improve performance while reducing inference time. We introduce PDBench, a benchmark comprising 27 discrete- and continuous-control tasks with fixed initial policies, and PDMetaHarness, a model-harness-independent meta-system for monitoring and guiding the learning process. The strongest model achieves a normalized mean score of 0.728, surpassing PPO (0.656) and Jev-like decision models (0.239), while running exclusively on CPUs and reducing inference costs. Our analysis shows that performance is largely driven by the first breakthrough during learning, and structural modifications have a slightly lower success rate than parameter modifications but typically yield larger gains. We further introduce style distillation, which enables interpretable stylistic control over the learning process. Overall, we present an analytical framework for PD and demonstrate its practical viability.
  </details>

- **[Does a completed simulation run mean a usable urban wind result for AI agents?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19385)**  `arXiv:2611.19385`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhongke Qu, Yunwei Zhang, Junwei Su, Yabin Zhang, Chengwei Li, Qingxiang Shui, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI agents based on large language models (LLMs) can now configure and run computational fluid dynamics (CFD) solvers, but a completed run does not ensure that delivered metrics can be recomputed from raw outputs. This gap was examined for urban wind simulation, where multi-case comparisons must hold non-target conditions fixed and reported values must be traceable to raw samples. UrbanWind-AgentBench, comprising 200 tasks and 367 cases in 10 engineering task families, was constructed. Public task contracts specify variable factors, fixed conditions, numerical thresholds and deliverable requirements, and two-layer scoring separates a structural score for deliverables, run provenance and raw evidence from a numerical score obtained by independent recomputation against references. WindPilot was then proposed as an execution framework that incorporates contract parsing, execution provenance, deterministic verification and bounded acceptance review into the LLM execution loop. On 30 OpenFOAM tasks with independent references, at least the structural maximum of 60 points was reached by a base agent on 28 tasks, but numerical credit was obtained on only 6. With the same model name and budget, WindPilot increased the number of tasks with numerical credit to 17, full-score tasks from 0 to 3 and the mean task score from 62.5 to 72.5. Mean action steps fell from 130.4 to 104.9, and mean token consumption decreased by 12.9%. The mean gain for atmospheric boundary layer (ABL) tasks was 18.5 points. A completed simulation run is therefore not sufficient for a usable urban wind result; usability depends on checks that tie reported metrics to raw evidence during execution.
  </details>

- **[Zero-Shot Weight-Space Programming for Prediction and Classification of Target Variables in Structured Datasets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19292)**  `arXiv:2611.19292`  `cs.AI`  
  _Eduardo C. Garrido-Merchán_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Weight-space programming fixes every parameter of a feed-forward network by hand, with no gradient descent anywhere in the construction. Every demonstration so far starts from a target function handed to the model as a rule system, making it a compilation problem. A supervised dataset withholds that specification, so the procedure must do the statistical inference itself and then express the result in the parameterisation it was given. We ask whether a large language model can do both. It is shown a small dataset in full, with the architecture of a feed-forward network, and writes out every weight and bias as a literal number, with no optimizer, no gradient, no code execution, and no chance to evaluate it first. Each construction is sealed and pre-registers its expected score. The graded suite runs from two toy problems, through nine synthetic problems disguised by a hidden rotation, to the classical benchmarks and one real engineering regression. The frozen network matches the closed-form optimum wherever that optimum is linear, reaches the irreducible error of the generating process on three rungs where it is not, and beats every fitted baseline on two of those, including the same architecture trained by gradient descent. It fails on two rungs, both times because the structure to be found is oblique to the coordinates it analysed. Three further constructors separate two failure modes a single score conflates: one returns no parameter vector at all on the hardest rungs, another delivers everywhere while writing networks worse than predicting the mean. One of those failures proves avoidable: another constructor solves the oblique-hinge rung by a margin that survives multiple-comparison correction. What predicts whether the hidden layer earns its keep is the training error of a linear separator, which the composite complexity index misses.
  </details>

- **[Agentic On-Policy Distillation Implicitly Reshapes LLMs' ReAct Patterns](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19244)**  `arXiv:2611.19244`  `cs.AI`  
  _Deyang Kong, Zhaoyi Li, Sijian Ren, Yuan Wei, Ying Wei, Defu Lian, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation (OPD) transfers specialized capabilities to language-model agents, but its cross-domain effects on interaction remain unclear. We investigate how OPD reshapes ReAct interaction patterns across mathematics, ALFWorld, and $\tau^2$-bench, focusing on reasoning, actions, and observation acquisition. General-reasoning supervision reduces mathematical tool attempts from 70.4% to 14.4% while increasing accuracy from 16.7% to 38.0%; code-interpreter supervision improves household actions and retail queries. In contrast, ALFWorld supervision reduces $\tau^2$-bench accuracy from 41.5% to 6.6%, accompanied by frequent tool calls that violate user-information dependencies. To address negative transfer using only source-domain training trajectories, we explore selective OPD guided by teacher preference changes before and after domain-specific RL. Retaining the top 95% of positions raises $\tau^2$ accuracy from 11.5% to 29.9% with a source-learning trade-off.
  </details>

- **[CompoundingKernel: Self-Improving from Zero to First](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19230)**  `arXiv:2611.19230`  `cs.AI`  
  _Qian Qu, Hongyang Wang, Wenbo Chen, Yichuan Li, Jingguo Liu, Xian Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present CompoundingKernel, a self-improving agent system that writes GPU kernels by search. As of 2026-11-11, its generated kernels rank #1 on the public L1, L2 and Quantization boards of SOL-ExecBench (NVIDIA B200). They have held #1 on L1 since 2026-10-27, albeit a short loss on 2026-11-07. All 235 problems have a site-verified correct kernel; on the 209 public-board problems they are 4.40x faster than the benchmark's reference baseline. The search starts from zero: agents get each problem's PyTorch definition but no reference kernels or official scores. A per-problem archive outside the model holds every correct kernel and its lineage. An LLM controller reads it, picks a parent or a new design, and gives each parallel child agent one direction. Checks outside the model decide correctness. At equal wall time, it beat a coded UCB style tree on 35 of 38 problems by making 2.4x as many correct attempts. Over a thousand agents ran at once, and one warm restart fix raised their evaluation rate 3.6x. We find that the rate of reliable measurements, not the number of agents, limits how far such a search scales. Our code and kernels are available on URL.
  </details>

- **[What Moves a LoCoMo Score? Memory Context, Answer Prompts and Judges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19199)**  `arXiv:2611.19199`  `cs.AI`  
  _Nikhil Kumar Jha, Amitoj Cheema_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LoCoMo is a widely reported benchmark for long-term conversational memory, but published scores mix memory quality with choices of answer prompt, judge and baseline layout that are rarely reported together. We measure how much these choices move a score, and evaluate Becos, a team memory system for coding agents, as a black box under all of them: ten conversations (1,540 questions), two answer models (gpt-4o-mini, gpt-4.1-mini), two answer prompts and three LLM judges, against a whole-conversation baseline rerun under identical conditions. Changing only the judge moves a score by up to 7.2 points and changing only the answer prompt by up to 9.3. A blind human check of 150 answers finds the judges reliable when they agree, and the primary judge's errors on disputed answers working against Becos rather than for it. Becos answers from a context with 90% fewer characters than the whole conversation. Its configuration was developed on LoCoMo itself, so there is no held-out set. With gpt-4o-mini answers it scores clearly higher than the whole conversation (79.7 vs 73.0 with the short prompt, paired 95% interval for the difference +3.8 to +9.5; 88.8 vs 77.5 with a step-by-step prompt). With gpt-4.1-mini answers the two score about the same (83.2 vs 83.1; 92.5 vs 92.1). We release every answer and verdict and the scoring script.
  </details>

- **[Learning inadequacy of Bayesian neural operators for PDE-constrained inference and design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19115)**  `arXiv:2611.19115`  `cs.AI`  
  _Pratyush Kumar Singh, Lianghao Cao, E. Bruce Pitman, Danial Faghihi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Partial differential equations (PDEs) underpin predictive modeling across science, engineering and medicine. Their use in digital twins, parameter inference and optimal design requires repeated solutions across parameters and scenarios. For nonlinear, transient and multiphysics systems, these calculations can make numerical simulation prohibitively expensive. Here we introduce the residual-informed Bayesian neural operator (RI-BNO), which learns operator inadequacy using a posteriori error estimates from the governing PDEs and incorporates its correction and uncertainty into Bayesian training and prediction. We establish local error bounds for the residual-informed estimates under stability and smoothness assumptions. We demonstrate RI-BNO on a non-linear reaction-diffusion benchmark with DeepONet and latent-subspace architectures, Markov chain Monte Carlo inference of glioma parameter fields from magnetic resonance imaging, and Monte Carlo risk-averse optimization of three-dimensional thermal breaks in building envelopes under manufacturing uncertainty. Across the evaluated examples, RI-BNO improves agreement with finite element (FE) reference predictions, parameter posteriors and optimized designs while using half as many full nonlinear or transient training simulations as the vanilla Bayesian neural operator (vBNO). For thermal break design, online optimization time decreases from 178 hours with the FE model to 18 minutes with RI-BNO. These results demonstrate the value of explicit inadequacy modeling in neural operators for reliable PDE-based inference and design.
  </details>

- **[Assume/Guarantee Contracts for the Compositional Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning of Heterogeneous Teams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19078)**  `arXiv:2611.19078`  `cs.AI`  
  _Dominic Klukas, Cyrus Neary_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) problems for heterogeneous teams require agents to collaborate to complete team tasks while making the best use of diverse agent capabilities. We introduce assume/guarantee contracts for heterogeneous multi-agent systems (AGC-MAS) and Iterative Heterogeneous Assignment for MARL (IHA-MARL). AGC-MAS contracts are compositional objects that encode the assumptions provided to, and the guarantees required of, each subtask contributing to the overall team task. We formulate contracts as tuples that specify a subtask, an automaton, timeout and success-probability requirements, and dispatch behavior. The automaton tracks an agent's high-level progress through the subtask associated with its contract and provides a natural mechanism for synchronization across contracts. Our abstraction enables robust interaction across heterogeneous low-level subtask implementations, allows heterogeneous agents to substitute for one another in satisfying contracts, and supports reuse of previously satisfied contracts in new team contexts. Having defined our theoretical framework, we develop a decomposition algorithm that breaks down a team task into a set of contracts. We then propose an iterative algorithm for task assignment and MARL that alternates between assigning contracts representing subtasks to individual agents, training the assigned agents to satisfy their contracts, and assessing their resulting capabilities to inform the next round of task assignments until the team's success probability meets the desired threshold. Our experimental results demonstrate the versatility of contracts in enabling agent substitution and contract reuse. Across three benchmarks with five seeds each, IHA-MARL achieves observed $100\%$ final evaluation success. In CPU timing pilots, comparable baselines required approximately $7$--$11\times$ more training-loop time.
  </details>

- **[SPLICE: Structured Prompt Local Iterative Combinatorial Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18883)**  `arXiv:2611.18883`  `cs.AI`  
  _Anish Acharya, Phillip Studans, Amit Dhanda, Sina Amini Niaki, Ninad Vivek Rao, Vishal Amarlal Khatri, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Natural language optimization (NLO) improves an agent by having a model critique and rewrite its natural language policy parameters: system prompts, tool instructions, reasoning directives, few-shot demonstrations. These artifacts are multi-section documents in which some sections encode contracts (safety clauses, tool schemas, output formats) that must not drift, while others are legitimate optimization targets. Most iterative NLO methods treat the artifact as a single editable string, producing three recurrent failure modes: unsafe global rewrites, beam collapse onto a single lineage, and unchecked bloat, none addressed by formal guarantees. We introduce SPLICE, a unified NLO framework that poses optimization as constrained combinatorial search over an edit graph of block-structured, selectively-mutable states. We map each failure mode to a matched guarantee: (i) per-block mutability preserves contract text verbatim, with a price-of-freezing bound; (ii) elitist beam search with diversity slots never lets the leader regress on validation, bounds regression under noisy re-evaluation by an exponential tail, and keeps a competitive candidate from a different parent whenever one exists; (iii) a ratio length regularizer comes with an explicit accuracy-length bias bound. Candidates are proposed from dual section-local textual gradients; under a proposer-competence condition whose proxy we measure, edit-graph reachability and a convergence rate bound how quickly improving children arrive. Across seven public benchmarks under five reproduced protocols, SPLICE wins on four and matches the strongest prior method on three. On a production-style contract stress test it keeps the contract clauses that unconstrained optimizers trade away to raise the proxy metric, and ablations attribute gains to critique and elitist search.
  </details>

- **[Typing Matters: Muscle, Language and Typing Behaviour in Live Keystroke Decoding from sEMG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18464)**  `arXiv:2611.18464`  `cs.AI`  
  _Abhilash Durgam, Nyle Siddiqui, Mubarak Shah, Qiushi Fu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Surface electromyography (sEMG) from the wrists can recover typed text without a keyboard. It is usually posed as a signal-inference problem: characters are read out of the muscle signal and character error rate is reported offline. We argue that this framing removes most of the problem. The decoder is constrained simultaneously by the muscles, by the language being typed, and by typing behaviour such as typos, corrections, backspaces and line breaks, and a usable system must predict keys live while the hand keeps moving. Existing systems fall short on two counts: (1) they process sEMG in fixed windows and carry nothing from one window to the next, discarding context accumulated over a session, and (2) they lack any understanding of the linguistic structure that drives typing, so every keystroke is inferred from the muscle signal alone. We propose a fully causal, zero-lookahead sEMG typing decoder built on a state space model (SSM), in which a single model decodes a whole session without windowed partitions, conditioned on what has already been typed. We achieve this by fusing a traditional typing decoder with an efficient autoregressive language model, instilling both sEMG and linguistic understanding in one model. To train such a model for per-character typing rather than whole-word text, we introduce a retokenization method that rewrites ordinary prose as human-like typing, with keystrokes, typos, backspaces and line breaks included, converting WikiText into a large typing corpus where none existed. Our model, having never seen a single sEMG-to-character transcript in typing, achieves a state-of-the-art character error rate of 25.13% on emg2qwerty's cross-user benchmark, a 5.4% relative improvement over the previous best with 6.7 times fewer parameters, while running strictly causally with no future context.
  </details>

- **[Skill Adapter: Personalized Modification and Online Routing of General-Purpose Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17007)**  `arXiv:2611.17007`  `cs.AI`  
  _Qijia Zhuang, Yanyin Gou, Wenbin Ma, Yuxiang Ren_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents use external skills to reuse task procedures and experience with tools. General-purpose skills describe shared execution procedures, while specific tool choices, operating practices, and output requirements vary across users. In long-horizon tasks, subgoals, available inputs, and environmental states change as execution progresses. Agents must select currently applicable skills from an expanding skill pool and prepare candidates for subsequent steps.We propose Skill Adapter, which enables personalized modification and online routing of general-purpose skills. The system distills local adjustments from user requirements and execution feedback and stores them as Adapters linked to general-purpose skills. The routing agent selects general-purpose skills and applicable Adapters based on the current subtask, execution progress, and environmental feedback, incrementally maintaining a task-level online skill library that the main LLM reads on demand. The system preloads candidates for subsequent nodes and rechecks their applicability once actual inputs are determined. Reinforcement learning optimizes the two types of decisions using modification gains and routing outcomes, respectively.We evaluate Skill Adapter on ALFWorld, WebShop, and SkillsBench. Compared with SkillRL, it improves task success rates by an average of 11.43 percentage points and reduces mean execution time on all three benchmarks. Skill Adapter retains its performance advantage when the skill pool grows to 1,000 skills.
  </details>

- **[Singularity-Bench: Can Agents Build Agents?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16591)**  `arXiv:2611.16591`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yang Zhou, Krish Agarwal, Zhuoming Chen, Zerui Wang, Zhenyu Zhang, Wenshuang Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Frontier AI agents increasingly succeed on realistic, long-horizon tasks and are beginning to improve the systems through which they operate. Yet existing benchmarks often restrict agents to narrow action spaces, use simplified environments, or rely on verification methods that are unreliable for evaluating arbitrary agent-generated systems. We introduce Singularity-Bench, an open-ended benchmark for autonomous AI development with six end-to-end tasks spanning pre-training, mid-training, post-training, inference, the agent harness, and cluster orchestration. Each task requires agents to implement core systems from a minimal starting point and evaluates them against mature human-engineered baselines on identical hardware. Additionally, through extensive red-teaming covering 9 of the 10 categories in the OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications, we expose reward-hacking vulnerabilities in the conventional Harbor framework and develop Breakwater, an extension of Harbor that blocks all attacks tested in our study. Across 11 agents built from 7 frontier models and 2 harnesses, Opus 5.5 with Claude Code matches or outperforms the human baseline on all six tasks with a Singularity-Index of 1.25, suggesting it may have the skills needed to reach the singularity. The remaining agents average only 0.60. Our vision is for Singularity-Bench to track how much of AI development agents can already automate and when they may transition from assisting to taking end-to-end responsibility for building stronger successors.
  </details>

- **[ReA-Bench: Benchmarking Responsible Agent Auditing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16493)**  `arXiv:2611.16493`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zipeng Ye, Shenyu Dai, Yong Xie, Kun Wang, Jiyang Guan, Zhikai Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As agents perform tasks autonomously in local and cloud environments, auditing their execution trajectories can help service providers identify risks and improve agent harnesses and foundation models. Our analysis of production and red-team trajectories shows that some risks are repeatedly missed across auditing configurations. Overall recall obscures which risks are missed and whether higher recall comes with more false positives on legitimate behavior. Existing benchmarks offer limited insight into these failures. Generated trajectories can contain inconsistent tool calls and responses, conspicuous risky actions, or repetitive attack patterns. Grouping results by risk type also makes it difficult to tell whether auditors overlooked evidence, accepted an unsupported conclusion, or lacked information needed to make a judgment.We introduce ReA-Bench, a diagnostic benchmark for auditing responsible agent behavior. It organizes auditing challenges into six tiers: internal safety knowledge, contradictions within trajectories, evidence retrieval in long contexts, verification of sources and reasoning, indirect steering, and missing information. It includes challenging benign cases in which actions resemble risky ones but are consistent with the task and its authorization. It also provides paired trajectory versions to test whether auditors detect the same risks more reliably when the trajectory is shortened or missing information is supplied. Most samples are drawn from real production trajectories, with scenarios manually selected and adapted to preserve realistic tasks and risks that could plausibly arise during execution.
  </details>

- **[Can MLLMs Reason over Surround-View Driving Videos? A Multi-View Autonomous Driving QA Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15225)**  `arXiv:2611.15225`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shunming Liu, Jianyi Peng, Cheng Li, Lu Hou, Pei Huang, Yajing Sun, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Surround-view video understanding in autonomous driving is a crucial capability for Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to achieve reliable driving reasoning. Real-world driving often requires information beyond the front-view camera. Existing MLLMs primarily focus on front-view information, while their spatio-temporal understanding and reasoning abilities remain unknown. To address this gap, we propose MVDriveBench, a high-information-density video QA benchmark for autonomous driving, characterized by synchronized surround-view inputs, spatio-temporal understanding, and reasoning-chain-oriented evaluation. We design 12 categories of QA tasks that cover the complete reasoning pipeline from low-level visual perception to high-level driving decision-making, enabling a systematic evaluation of MLLMs surround-view spatio-temporal understanding and reasoning capabilities in complex driving environments. We conduct a comprehensive evaluation of 23 open-source, closed-source, and expert MLLMs on MVDriveBench. The results show that current MLLMs still exhibit insufficient surround-view spatio-temporal reasoning ability. Meanwhile, closed-loop evaluations of specialist models in AlpaSim reveal a correlation between surround-view spatio-temporal reasoning capabilities and closed-loop driving performance, highlighting the importance of comprehensive multi-view understanding for reliable vehicle control.
  </details>

- **[Early Signals, Strong Decisions: Prefix-Guided Sampling for Parallel Test-Time Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14965)**  `arXiv:2611.14965`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jie Ren, Jonathan Rosenfeld, Neil Thompson_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Parallel test-time scaling (TTS) has been shown to enhance reasoning in large language models by generating and aggregating multiple independent reasoning paths. Internal confidence scores can further improve this process by identifying paths of varying quality. However, existing methods remain computationally inefficient, as low-quality paths are fully expanded before being evaluated. In this work, we aim to improve the use of internal confidence signals for better model performance and computational efficiency. We begin by offering a new perspective on the role of confidence in aggregation, showing that it is most impactful for questions near the decision boundary. Notably, when amplified by multi-sample aggregation, even small confidence signals derived from short prefixes can meaningfully influence outcomes in these boundary cases. Building on this insight, we introduce Prefix-Guided Sampling (PreG), a method that reallocates computation by first generating short prefixes, ranking them using internal confidence scores, and then completing only the most promising candidates. This strategy reduces token usage while maintaining gains in path quality. We provide theoretical analysis demonstrating that, under a fixed compute budget, PreG strengthens the effective signal by widening the decision boundary margin. Empirically, our method consistently outperforms standard parallel TTS approaches across benchmarks, while significantly improving token efficiency and reducing overall computation.
  </details>

- **[GenPFN: An In-Context Tabular Foundation Model Approach for Augmenting Small Datasets in Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14804)**  `arXiv:2611.14804`  `cs.AI`  
  _Noah J. Bagazinski, Cyril Picard, Faez Ahmed_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Costly simulations and experiments limit the data available for engineering design. This paper proposes GenPFN, a training-free candidate generator guided by a pretrained tabular Prior-Data Fitted Network (TabPFN) classifier with fixed weights. The context contains evaluated designs labeled desired (Class 1) or undesired (Class 0). GenPFN interpolates between desired designs and refines candidates using gated classifier gradients and Gaussian noise. Each candidate is evaluated using the problem's constraints and objective functions. Evaluated desired designs and their objective values augment the context for regression training.Evaluation spans fourteen selected problems, including a two-parameter benchmark and a 44-parameter hull problem. In paired comparisons, GenPFN with TabPFN v1 achieves higher class satisfaction and lower realism distance than the tested adversarial, variational-autoencoder and diffusion generators. The variational-autoencoder generator provides greater coverage and diversity. Relative to the synthetic minority over-sampling technique (SMOTE), GenPFN provides lower coverage distance and greater diversity. Among observed paired regression fits, augmentation reduces estimated AutoGluon validation mean squared error by 62.1% relative to context-only training. A hull-stern case study yields 99.3% propeller-compatible candidates versus 1.4% among randomly sampled context designs. Generation followed by evaluation produces sample sets that augment limited datasets and support training more accurate predictive models.
  </details>

- **[Personalized Memory Rubrics: Learning What to Remember per User via Rubric-Guided Credit Assignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14017)**  `arXiv:2611.14017`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yupeng Han, Kai Zhang, Shuochen Liu, Ze Liu, Xianquan Wang, Zhihong Pan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon personalized agents maintain an evolving memory by continuously deciding what to add, modify, or discard. However, learning an optimal update policy remains challenging. Conventional training relies solely on terminal question-answering accuracy, yielding a coarse feedback signal that treats all intermediate updates identically. Furthermore, update utility is inherently user-specific, as identical memory actions may yield opposite effects across different user personas. Existing methods fail to address this heterogeneity, relying on globally shared evaluation standards with at most a single scalar intermediate reward. To address these challenges, we propose \textbf{Personalized Memory Rubrics} (\textbf{PMR}), a framework that translates terminal feedback into fine-grained, per-step, and per-user credit assignment. PMR decomposes evaluation into a universal layer capturing cross-persona failure patterns and a per-user adaptive layer modeling individual traits as a specialized delta over the universal baseline. Through \textbf{Rubric-Guided Credit Assignment}, PMR redistributes credit at the token level, selectively amplifying validated decisions on successful trajectories while falling back to standard policy optimization on failed ones to avoid unreliable attribution. The rubric and memory policy \textbf{co-evolve} in a closed loop with anti-circularity guarantees. Crucially, PMR incurs zero inference overhead, operating without any rubric or external judge during deployment. Extensive experiments across four benchmarks demonstrate consistent gains over strong memory-augmented baselines.
  </details>

- **[From Questions to Decisions: Benchmarking How LLM Agents Acquire Decision-Relevant Information from Human Beings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13015)**  `arXiv:2611.13015`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yuhan Tang, Ke Fan, Xinkai Zou, Baichuan Mo, Kangxin Cui, Ao Qu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents are increasingly asked to make plans for people, such as scheduling drivers or assigning nurses. However, people's requests are often incomplete, and the supporting information for decision-making may be outdated, requiring agents to ask for clarification. Existing benchmarks typically provide fully specified problems or rely on uncalibrated LLM-simulated users, leaving this ability insufficiently tested. We introduce Ask2Decide, a benchmark of 43 planning tasks that evaluates whether agents ask for missing information that matters for their decisions. An operator holds facts absent from the agent's request and records, and optimization solvers determine which facts change the optimal plan. We also include similarly worded facts that leave the optimal plan unchanged to test whether agents distinguish useful information from irrelevant details. We collect 79 interaction sessions from 19 participants and find that, after an incorrect plan, human replies omit the correcting fact far more often than replies from an uncalibrated simulated user (43% versus 7%). Using these interactions, we calibrate a simulated operator that more closely matches human reply patterns. Evaluating four frontier LLMs, we find that their initial plans are infeasible on 58-98% of tasks when information is missing, compared with 5-28% when all facts are provided upfront. Agents uncover facts that change the optimal plan only slightly more often than facts that leave it unchanged (39-52% versus 32-41%). These results show that obtaining decision-relevant information through interaction remains a major bottleneck for LLM planning. This is ongoing work and will be updated. The date of this version is shown on the first page.
  </details>

- **[BiVerify: Closing the Loop Between Backward and Forward Verification for Mathematical Self-Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11368)**  `arXiv:2611.11368`  `cs.AI`  
  _Rui Zou, Xin Zhao, Mengqi Wei, Ji-Rong Wen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have made substantial progress in mathematical reasoning but still produce incorrect solutions to complex problems, indicating the need for more reliable self-correction mechanisms. Mathematical self-correction typically identifies errors through verification, which can proceed backward or forward. The two directions provide complementary verification capabilities. Backward verification checks a candidate answer against the original constraints, bypassing complex intermediate reasoning, but cannot ensure correct reasoning or objective completeness. Forward verification follows the problem conditions and current solution, enabling it to locate reasoning errors and assess objective completeness, but may overlook key constraints in complex solution chains. We propose BiVerify, a dynamically scheduled framework for bidirectional mathematical verification. BiVerify begins with backward verification to screen the answer against the original constraints, followed by forward verification of the reasoning process; subsequent direction switching, state resetting, and re-verification are governed by verification outcomes. Upon failure, the system revises the candidate or verification flow until the same candidate passes both directions. We also introduce shared mathematical tools that separate logical reasoning from precise computation. We systematically evaluate BiVerify on five challenging mathematical reasoning benchmarks using multiple mainstream DeepSeek, GPT, and Gemini models. BiVerify exceeds the strongest representative public baseline evaluated in this work by 13.5 percentage points in average accuracy, with the margin reaching 31.2 percentage points on GPT. Further analysis shows that BiVerify's overall performance exceeds that of either verification direction alone.
  </details>

- **[How Finite Brains Generalize Without Bound: Spatial Memory as a Universal Computer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07920)**  `arXiv:2611.07920`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yuhang He, Si Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A child who learned to add small numbers adds numbers of any length. LSTMs and Transformers trained on short sequences fail on longer ones. We trace this to what learners read when deciding. A position, count or time takes new values as input grows: it is a coordinate, and a learner that reads one meets in every long input values its experience never covered. Whether two things are the same has the same two answers at every size: it is a coincidence, and a learner that reads only coincidences finds in a long input nothing that short inputs lacked, so finite experience fixes its rule for every size. The memory carries the size of the input and the rule stays finite, and this determines the memory a generalizing brain can have: a memory that grows while what the learner reads of it stays finite has to look the same from every place, so it is made of cycles, and the past is reached by asking what happened when the cycles last stood where they stand now. The cycles are the grid modules, the cells that detect their return the place cells, the records the hippocampal index; a prefrontal controller moves the cycles and never sees them. Reading only coincidences costs no power: what finite experience fixes in such a learner is what Turing machines compute in step with their input, which is why a circuit that evolved to find the way home can learn arithmetic and grammar. In a network of neurons this machine of cycles is the dynamics seen through its symmetry, and symbols are its invariants. Trained on strings up to 40 symbols, the machine solves five benchmark tasks that no LSTM or Transformer solves, among them duplication and binary addition, and beyond the reach of its cycles it fails on the curve the theory predicts. Given larger cycles after training, a machine that saw no string longer than five symbols reverses a string of a million without error. Its controllers run unchanged on neurons.
  </details>

- **[PatentAgentBench: Benchmarking LLM Agents with Professional Patent Task Suites](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35031)**  `arXiv:2610.35031`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yijie Hu, Wei Li, Junjie Zhou, Zhiqi Jiang, Yi Jin, Licong Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Patent practice lies at the intersection of technical, legal, and linguistic work, making it challenging yet crucial to technological innovation. Recently, rapid advances in large language models (LLMs) and agentic systems have demonstrated the potential to complete realistic business workflows and even patent works. This raises a question: can current AI systems perform patent work reliably? Existing benchmarks cover a limited range of patent tasks and simplify evidence acquisition by providing reference materials or candidate documents. In this paper, we introduce PatentAgentBench, a benchmark comprising 510 instances across eight task suites: novelty search, freedom-to-operate (FTO) search, design FTO, invalidity search, claim charting, patent drafting, office-action response, and patent translation. Curated from real patent records and professional materials, the benchmark combines task-specific metrics with expert-reviewed annotations and rubrics. Retrieval tasks such novelty search, FTO, etc., require agents to search and compare patent collections without a supplied candidate pool. The drafting and OAR tasks assess the quality of agents' work given technical disclosure and office action notice. We evaluate seven LLMs across four agent harnesses on PatentAgentBench, finding that current systems still struggle to identify novelty, assess risk, and understand practitioner intent throughout patent workflows.
  </details>

- **[ARCANA: A Benchmark for Abstraction and Analogical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29271)**  `arXiv:2610.29271`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ruqi Chen, Zilei Wang, Wei Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Analogical reasoning is widely considered a fundamental component of human intelligence. Few benchmarks are designed to explicitly test far analogical reasoning - reasoning between domains that are superficially dissimilar yet share deep relational or structural similarities. In this work, we introduce an analogical reasoning benchmark called Abstraction and Reasoning Corpus of ANAlogy (ARCANA). The tasks of ARCANA require performing far analogical mappings from real-world objects or physical phenomena to abstract grid-based transformations, different from the well-known ARC-AGI benchmark which primarily emphasized innate human priors. We validate ARCANA through a human study, demonstrating that humans solve these tasks reliably. In contrast, evaluations across a range of large language models show a distinct performance gap compared to humans, revealing systematic failure modes that remain challenging for current models. To address this gap, we propose ConceptSIR, a reasoning framework based on concept slippage, introspection, and reflection. Experiments demonstrate that ConceptSIR enables standard LLMs to solve several challenging ARCANA tasks that were unsolved by direct prompting in our evaluation. Overall, our results suggest that far analogical reasoning remains an open problem for contemporary LLMs. All the materials are available atthis http URL. The complete codebase, including benchmark, and ConceptSIR, is publicly available atthis https URL. All ARCANA data and task instances can be accessed and explored atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Autonomous Science Bench: Evaluating AI Agents for Autonomous Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18449)**  `arXiv:2610.18449`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yibo Wen, Han Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI agents for autonomous scientific discovery must decide which experiments to run under a limited budget. Most benchmarks for science agents provide fixed datasets, while others that support active experimentation largely rely on simulation. We introduce Autonomous Science Bench, an initial release comprising six budgeted design-build-test-learn campaigns grounded in published experiments across biology, chemistry, and materials science. Agents select batches of designs, receive measurements from a lab service, and use the results to guide subsequent experiments before delivering a predictive model. Success requires passing two calibrated gates: a discovery gate, which evaluates the designs the agent measured, and a learning gate, which evaluates its model's ranking of held-out designs. In a pilot evaluation of ten agent-model configurations and 180 trials, the best configuration succeeds in 72% of trials, while the hardest campaign is solved in only five of 30 attempts. The two gates reveal different weaknesses: some agents build accurate rankings but find too few promising designs, while others find promising designs but fail to predict which unseen designs will perform well. These differences are obscured by overall success rates alone. We release version 0.1 of the campaigns, lab runtime, and public leaderboard atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Memadapter: Counterfactual Adaptation Against Memory-induced Sycophancy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05162)**  `arXiv:2610.05162`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ruqing Ning, Haibo Meng, Zhishang Xiang, Zerui Chen, Jinsong Su, Xin Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-term memory enables LLM-based agents to retain and reuse information across tasks and sessions, supporting personalization and long-horizon interactions. However, persistent memories can also induce sycophancy, causing agents to over-align with users' historical beliefs even when they are inaccurate, outdated, or inconsistent with objective evidence. Existing mitigation methods assume that memory-induced sycophancy originates from biased or incorrect memories and attempt to reduce this risk by filtering such memories at different stages of the memory pipeline. However, in the real world, objective and correct memories can still induce sycophancy, and the same memory can warrant different influence across different contexts. To this end, we propose MemAdapter, a novel framework that adaptively integrates retrieved memories to support objective and reliable reasoning. Specifically, MemAdapter consists of three components: (i) Counterfactual Induction, which leverages counterfactual reasoning to uncover the potential risk of retrieved memories; (ii) Context-Aware Reflection, which calibrates the inferential influence of each retrieved memory in light of the current task via self-reflection; and (iii) Evidence-Based Reasoning, which grounds the final response in appropriate evidence while preserving the legitimate influence of memory. Extensive experiments on three benchmarks demonstrate that MemAdapter consistently improves memory reliability across diverse scenarios. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Build2SPARQL: A Large-Scale Text-to-SPARQL Benchmark Dataset for Building Knowledge Graph Querying](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00224)**  `arXiv:2610.00224`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wooyoung Jung_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Building automation systems are increasingly represented as semantic knowledge graphs (KGs) using ontologies such as Brick and ASHRAE 223P, creating a machine-readable substrate for artificial-intelligence applications. One promising application is translating natural-language questions into SPARQL (text-to-SPARQL), which would let building operators query these graphs through language agents, but progress is limited by the scarcity of large natural-language/SPARQL benchmarks. This paper presents Build2SPARQL, a large-scale benchmark for building KGs generated by a KG-grounded pipeline: SPARQL queries are produced and validated entirely by graph-traversal code, while large language models generate only the natural-language questions, keeping query correctness independent of model behavior. The pipeline mines six query-pattern families -- linear chains, branching, UNION, aggregation, OPTIONAL, and attribute-filtered -- and phrases each query across five vocabulary registers. Applied to 201 building KGs (180 Brick, 21 ASHRAE 223P), it yields 6,136 executable SPARQL queries and 30,680 questions. A two-rater human validation of 300 questions found 98.8% semantic fidelity, 98.8% naturalness, and 84.0% operational plausibility. A retrieval-augmented evaluation across three open-weight language models raised exact-match accuracy from 0.2-20% (zero-shot) to 56-65% (three-shot retrieved).
  </details>

- **[Vestrum: Learning the Verification, Retrieval and Structure of Agent Harnesses from a Few Long Episodes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33822)**  `arXiv:2609.33822`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jayant Parashar, Eugene F. Douglass, William C. Bastian, Suchendra M. Bhandarkar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent harnesses decide what the model retrieves, decomposes, remembers and verifies, and most people use harnesses that are pre-built and fixed, such as Claude Code or a published memory system. Harness optimizers rewrite their own harness code rather than editing pre-built systems, and they learn from tens to hundreds of tasks, while long-horizon work offers a handful of episodes, each hundreds of tool calls long. We present Vestrum, which improves existing harnesses component by component. It reads full raw traces, states each failure as a recognizable class, and changes the one component where the error arose (a self-verification, a retrieval view, a decomposition step or a memory operation). Each change targets a failure that recurs across tasks of the same kind. Before anything runs, a generalization filter refuses changes that copy facts from the development tasks. Vestrum learns from one to ten long development episodes and improves tasks it has never seen in all five settings. On Claude Code, Vestrum raises Terminal-Bench 4 Hard from 63.7% to 70.3% of test checks at 1.03x the test cost. It raises expert cell-type agreement from 67.5% to 77.8% on held-out sections of one slide. On the GAM memory system, it raises UltraHorizon from 47.6 to 59.8, AMA-Bench from 64.2% to 74.2% and LoCoMo from 71.5% to 74.9%. It beats the GEPA prompt search we ran on the same data in every paired evaluation, and these gains hold under a test model different from the one Vestrum's search used. Across the searches, every self-verification the loop kept acts early: it checks an intermediate result against recorded evidence before the result is committed, or, on Terminal-Bench, requires an invariant and a probe before the first edit. In development, auditors that re-examined every finished answer broke more correct answers than they fixed, and the loop did not keep them.
  </details>

- **[Externalized CPDAG Summaries Improve LLM Causal Deduction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31071)**  `arXiv:2609.31071`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wentao Sun, João Paulo Nogueira, Dominique Verchere, Mathieu Acher, Alonso Silva_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Observational data rarely identify a single causal structure. Such structures are typically modeled as directed acyclic graphs (DAGs) whose arrows point from direct causes to their effects, and observed correlations and conditional independencies usually leave a whole family of DAGs that fit the data equally well.
